# Pacifica — Master Storyline

**Status:** Working canonical synopsis for the second draft · **Established:** 16 October 2026 · **Updated:** 28 October 2026  
**Coverage:** Elias Thorne's life (born c. 1824) through the establishment of the dominant Pacific order in 1932.  
**Reading order:** Primarily chronological. Retrospections are deliberately selective.  
**Canonical authority:** [CR-031-R1](CR-031-R1.md), the author’s correction to Japanese construction and labour organisation, supersedes the earlier Kamchatka bonded-labour proposal. The author-approved [change register](change_requests.json), [CR-021 revision 9](CR-021.md), and historical background in [historical_background.md](historical_background.md). CR-021 supplies the constitutional order, Pacific Concord and dynastic identities; CR-020 governs the productive economy, Gulf challenge, Depression and islands settlement except where CR-021 expressly amends it. Numerical results remain **MODEL** estimates; engineering, demographic and financial evidence is tracked in [war_base_assumptions.md](war_base_assumptions.md).
**Characters:** See the separate [canonical character bible](character_bible.md) and [stable identity registry](character_registry.json) for genealogy, motivation, voice and independent perspectives.

> This is the **complete working storyline**. Undecided narrative details are identified as **[OPEN]**; approximate years are marked c. Dates and characters that depart from actual history are developed in the separate [historical background](historical_background.md). Changes to the story are governed by the authorial approvals maintained in [change_requests.json](change_requests.json).

**CR-016 integration:** [The managed succession](CR-016.md) replaces the inheritance war and transfers the Polytechnic leader’s role to an unrelated Pacific-war commander, Rafael Soberanes. Julian is adopted and raised as successor while his mother lives; Kiyoshi is Elias’s eldest son by a Satsuma mother. The separate author decision [CR-016-R1](CR-016.md#additional-author-decision--trans-siberian) makes Japanese prevention of Russian Trans-Siberian completion canonical; CR-018 specifies Japanese eastern-link and terminal control through Chinese-owned Manchuria. It also establishes the stakeholder empire’s endpoint: Pacifica retains the common institutions, Japanese imperial elites share their commanding heights, and the Concord holds.

**CR-019 integration:** [The brokered peace and creditor order](CR-019.md) supplies the European and Union half of the ending. The Union stays neutral, Pacifica brokers the peace, and the 1932 rescue brings the Union into its order through association. The sealed archive preserves the triumph's hidden cost. CR-020 replaces the labor-charter strand with the Lands and Waters settlement; other undecided CR-019 mechanics retain their qualifications.

**CR-018 integration:** [The stakeholder empire](CR-018.md) governs the Pacific arc: Julian succeeds Victoria (dated 1907 by CR-040); Kiyoshi becomes Kuroda; the Siberian coalition wins; the 1927 Concord survives the crises of 1931–1932. Approved outcomes and proposed constitutional, diplomatic and character mechanics retain their source labels. CR-017 was folded into CR-018 and was never separately merged.

**CR-020 integration:** [Fleet, scale, Gulf admission, the Depression and Keawe](CR-020.md) is integrated under its 19–20 October decisions. B1–B15 are approved, with B4 superseded; the decisions in section E govern older draft wording. All twenty-three OPEN-020 entries are decided or superseded. Narrative choices are distinguished from MODEL figures, and unrelated earlier open questions remain open.

**CR-025 integration:** [Wartime credit, Japan's boom and who pays](CR-025.md) explains how the Entente loans of 1914–18 build Japanese factories and shipyards on short foreign money, how peace and the 1923 earthquake leave those debts unresolved, and who finances the champions' growth abroad. Its amounts are MODEL estimates.

**CR-024 integration:** [Japan's 1927 rescue](CR-024.md) makes the bank crisis the Concord's decisive negotiation: Japan rebuilds its banks with its own money, and Pacifica supplies the foreign exchange nobody else will. Its amounts are MODEL estimates. The 1927 provisional application, 1928 indemnity and 1932 renewal remain governed by CR-021 revision 9.

**CR-021 integration:** [The constitution of Pacifica and the Pacific Concord](CR-021.md), revision 9, incorporates the direct author corrections to the approved constitutional framework. The Constitution of the Realm, counties, languages and plural franchise, the Act of Settlement (dated 1907 by CR-040), 1927 provisional application and 1928 indemnity govern the chronology below. The two crowns of the Concord are co-equal; Julian is styled Sovereign of Pacifica and Mōʻī of Hawaii, and from 1907 also Rey de Filipinas (CR-029, CR-040). Kaʻanoe Ward is his birth mother; Kahekili is his heir. All seventeen OPEN-021 entries are decided. The remaining uncertainty over Noe's death concerns culpability, not her identity or the recorded circumstances.

**CR-022 integration:** [Silas, Abigail Merritt and Donner Pass](CR-022.md), revision 3. Silas is Elias's eldest son, born in 1851 to Abigail Merritt, the pastor's daughter; Kiyoshi becomes the second son, reversing CR-016's seniority. At Donner Silas fires at Soberanes and Caleb dies in his place; the lie holds. Silas confesses to Victoria in 1912 and dies that winter. Where CR-016's summary above calls Kiyoshi the eldest son, CR-022 governs.

**CR-026 integration:** [Kiyoshi's bid for the crown](CR-026.md), revision 6. Kiyoshi claims the crown and renounces it in exchange for the Kuroda queen (in 1913–1914 in this note; early 1907 since CR-040); the Act of Settlement names him as Elias's son. He founds his own Kuroda branch and is made a count in 1907; Kiyotaka's son Kiyonaka keeps the main house.

**CR-027 integration:** [Victoria Kamāmalu, the Puʻuloa lease and the Five](CR-027.md). The historical Victoria Kamāmalu, Kaʻahumanu IV (born 1838), replaces the fictional Kaiulani in every role; earlier change requests that name Kaiulani refer to her. The historical Princess Kaʻiulani (born 1875), spelled with the ʻokina, is a different person (CR-043). She marries Elias in November 1858, becomes Queen of Hawaii in December 1872, leases Puʻuloa to the Confederation in 1874 and from 1887 governs Pacifica as Regent while remaining Queen, until Julian succeeds her in 1907. A February rising with disputed deaths replaces the 1874 massacre, and the Hawaiian throne is never in abeyance. Kalākaua serves her as mythmaker and envoy; the Five at the head of the chartered estates run the technocratic state under an unwritten arrangement with the Crown.

**CR-028 integration:** [The bargain of 1847–1848](CR-028.md). Washington accepts California's independence in three steps: the suspension of April 1847, Mexico's recognition at Querétaro in December 1847, and Polk's acceptance of Trist's treaty and the Buchanan–Vallejo Convention in February 1848. California gives a non-alienation pledge, port and commercial privileges and the Basin; Washington takes independence for the Texas road. Ratified treaties, British and French recognition, the bondholders and the Senate's sectional lock hold the result until secession.

**CR-029 integration:** [Manila, Puʻuloa and the Philippine crown](CR-029.md). The Manila Bay action of August 1873 comes first. Neutrality rules send the damaged squadron to Honolulu, and the Manila station makes Puʻuloa urgent. Prim refuses a sale in 1869–70; the Republic borrows in 1874; Cánovas sells in 1876 to settle the Republic's debt and save Cuba. The Philippines is a confederal dependency until 1887, a dependency of the Crown under the Regent until 1907 (1914 before CR-040), and then a crown of its own, offered by the Cortes and confirmed by vote, equal with the Hawaiian crown whose people also vote. The Union's guano claims pass by treaty in 1867 and 1871. Micronesia and Tutuila become trusts of Ka Lōkahi, the council of the crowns.

**CR-030 integration:** [The railway behind the Siberian victory](CR-030.md), revision 2. The coalition's victory rests on one operational schedule: five railway sections from Urajio to the Ob, a Pacifican railway board that inherits the engines and cars Pacifican works built for Russia on Entente credit and pays and feeds the railwaymen and miners, depots filled behind the Ob the summer before the battle, the Amur summer route to Sretensk and a first Kara Sea convoy. The narrowest stretches lie far behind the front, on the Chinese line and the single-track branch into Transbaikalia. Capacities and costs are MODEL estimates.

**CR-031 integration:** [The Treaty of Harbin: the North, the victors' terms and China's articles](CR-031.md), revision 4. Siberia (its republic of this note became a federal land of Russia under CR-040 and CR-041) is a federation of graded autonomy: provinces, an autonomous Sakha land with a port at Okhotsk, and a North-East Territory from Ola Bay to Nikolayevsk with Chukotka. Kamchatka and the Koryak coast become a republic under Japanese protection; Transbaikalia is a protectorate under a Transbaikal Council and a civilian Resident after the Cossack Host turns Semenov out in 1919. China recovers Russia's privileges, Amur navigation to the sea and suzerainty over Mongolia. Pacifica takes the Commander Islands, Arctic leases and a charter, insists that Siberian ships pass the straits, and participates in the Kamchatka Company's development under the professional construction and paid-employment practices Japan and Pacifica have developed together (CR-031-R1). The fortification question is **[OPEN-031-11]**.

**CR-033 integration:** [Settlement, Native recovery and the shape of Pacifica's population](CR-033.md). The gold rush settles: about 1.2 million people by 1861 and about 37 million by 1932 (MODEL; CR-052). The Republic is the sole buyer of Native land; the 1851–52 treaties reserve homelands under a compact of protection and pre-emption, a Land Fund outside the London pledge pays annuities in goods and helps pay for assisted passage, and the register is the title. Native Californians suffer disease without mass killing and grow again from the later 1890s; land still leaks away through leases, debts and courts. Japanese immigration overtakes Chinese in the 1900s.

**CR-034 integration:** [From the secret find to a destination, and the Polytechnic's first decade](CR-034.md). Seven people know of the October 1847 find and the opening is managed (both PROPOSED, with CR-028); the rush becomes a settlement, and Elias and then Victoria make women's immigration a priority. The Polytechnic grows from the Carquinez assay room through the State Assay Office and Genth's school of 1852, with a royalty share, paid assistants, prize questions, the engineer-corps privilege and citizenship with a chair from 1854; 1858 is its prestige year.

**CR-035 integration:** [The Pacifica League](CR-035.md). The 1783 Mining Ordinances give the Republic its school without a schools law; the churches keep their schools and the state pays only for what it examines. Every college joins the League, women are admitted by examination from 1859–62, and the Methodists' medical college saves Victoria in 1866.

**CR-036 integration:** [Science: the scholars Europe would not promote](CR-036.md). Sylvester, Remak, Cohn, Koch, Ehrlich, Michelson and Morley work in Pacifica; Carquinez finds indium (1863) and californium (1873); Koch finds the cholera bacillus at Manila in 1882. The interior has schools and advances of its own. Talent comes from every faith and background, and Victoria's 1891 famine relief is her decisive test of diaspora loyalties.

**CR-037 integration:** [No cliques](CR-037.md). The examination state guards against capture by any group: anonymous scripts and avoidance from the late 1870s, published appointment tables and audits from 1888, the first great audit of 1890, and a county rule that allows cultural concentration but not cliques. The test is the examination, never a quota. The Japanese naval reservation stays until 1932.

**CR-038 integration:** [The machinery's own safeguards](CR-038.md). Retirement at sixty-eight for senators, judges, Commissioners and estate heads, except the Truce's named holders; Vallejo College at Monterey from 1889, where the Carquinez men first know one another; unannounced inspectors and figures hard to fake; bonded Realm scholarships and posted grants; official pay set against the champions'; county halls with a central minimum and local freedom; pride in measured results; Hawaii's abolition of penal labour contracts in 1856; and the trades congress of 1895 with its Guest Workers' Aid.

**CR-039 integration:** [Loose ends in the commitments](CR-039.md). The Protectorship is renewed in 1862, 1863 and 1864; the Philippine tobacco company's quarter belongs to the state, not a Crown that does not yet exist; the Truce's signing in 1887 and Governor Waite's part in the Bread March are approved; the Pacific Concord replaces the compact of 1887 when its renewal falls due in 1927, and its first executive term runs from 1927 to 1932; the Gulf Republic is founded in 1900, the year the canal opens, and its founding compact sets the tests for admission; Silas dies at Sacramento in the winter of 1912.

**CR-040 integration (Stages 1–2):** [The crown of 1907, the failed peace and the Russian war](CR-040.md). Kiyoshi's 1905 victory is over Russia's Petropavlovsk cruiser squadron, and Russia keeps its battle fleets. Silas renounces in December 1906; Kiyoshi's claim fails in March 1907; the Act of Settlement of 1907 joins the crowns, and Julian accedes to the Realm and Hawaii in November and swears in Manila in December 1907; Kahekili is born about 1916 after daughters. Julian tours Europe from 1908, helps settle Bosnia, Agadir and the Balkan wars, and fails in July 1914. Ka Lōkahi agrees in its 1927 review that Japan joins. The earlier integration notes describe their own CRs; where they gave 1913–1915 dates for the succession, the dates now point to CR-040. Stages 3–4 (the Great War changes, the Red Terror, the Russian war, the arrests and trials, Kronstadt, federal Russia at Harbin) are integrated together with CR-041 on 29 October 2026. Where the CR-030 and CR-031 notes spoke of a republic in Siberia, it is now Siberia, a federal land of Russia.

**CR-041 integration:** [The Russian war re-planned: one summer, one winter](CR-041.md), integrated with CR-040 Stages 3–4. The line is seized in one week from 26 May 1918 without a declaration of war; Kazan is held in September 1918; the tri-partite Siberian treaty (Komuch, Siberia, Alash) is signed on 4–6 August 1918 under Pacifican guarantee and ratified on 10 September; the Omsk coup of 18 November fails and the Directory survives; Perm falls in December; Vance holds the Volga bridge at Zelenodolsk in June 1919; Wrangel inherits the Directory's signature in October 1919 and asks for Siberia's own vote, which the Tomsk Duma gives in 1921 with Alash unasked. The 1920 sequence is CR-040's. Emba, Grozny, Maikop and a Baku share are Pacifica's; Kolchak is federal Russia's navy minister. Ukraine's form and coast, Bessarabia and the Turkish frontier are OPEN for a later CR.

**CR-042 integration:** [Huludao, a door of Pacifica's own at the end of the Chinese line](CR-042.md). In the settlement of 1898–99 Dalian and Port Arthur stay Chinese through Victoria's negotiation with the Empress Dowager; China pledges the Liaodong Gulf coast to no power and gives Pacifica first refusal on a harbour at Huludao. In 1909 Julian charters the Huludao Harbour Trust on the Keelung pattern under a Qing commission, which the Republic confirms; works begin in 1910 and the harbour opens in 1914. A Chinese line runs from Harbin to Mukden by 1912. A British and Pacifican syndicate, Britain the senior partner, builds the railway from Mukden to the harbour in 1908–1910. The Transbaikal ataman retires to Urajio. Japan's reaction to the trust and the scenes of Victoria's friendship with Cixi are left for later edits.

**CR-043 integration:** [The peace of 1919: special mediation at The Hague, Kaʻiulani and Bryan](CR-043.md). Kaʻiulani lives: Kocher operates on her goitre in 1897 and she survives the rheumatic fever of 1899. The peace is made by special mediation under Article 8 of the 1907 Hague Convention, Pacifica named by the Allies and the neutral Union by the Central Powers; Kaʻiulani and William Jennings Bryan are the mediators, with Dawes and House in Bryan's delegation. Salonika breaks in September 1918, Mudros is British alone, and the armistice of 11 November is signed on the line Germany holds. The conference opens in the Peace Palace on 18 January 1919 and the Treaty of The Hague is signed on 28 June 1919; the Union Senate refuses the naval limits and the court. Alsace-Lorraine becomes a neutral republic in union with Switzerland; Wilhelm II abdicates for his grandson under a regency (regent OPEN); Bohemia becomes a federal kingdom of the monarchy (detail PROPOSED); France pays the Czechoslovak Legion in francs from Pacifican credit; Victoria's objection stays private; the 1918 fire raid on London stands.

**CR-044 integration:** [Omsk, the hostages and the Russian war's loose ends](CR-044.md). The armoured train is *Pele*; Patton, Lenin's capture on 22 April 1920, the burial, the Tomsk consul, Alash's regiments and Dzerzhinsky on Kronstadt are approved, and the hostages' identities and most of the Omsk detail below are PROPOSED. Nagai Shōzō, a Japanese-Pacifican engineer, reports Petrograd's three days of flour in February 1917 and is among eleven advisers shot in Cromie's week; Vance's father, a railway surveyor, is kept as a hostage and comes up alive at Kronstadt. The Omsk night is written out: Knox's foreknowledge, Ward's battalion standing by, the railway's telegraph and trains, the Legion and the line guard, Vance's hour with Kolchak, and Volkov's papers as the first folder of the British file. Major George S. Patton commands *Pele* at Kotelnich, where Lenin is taken on 22 April 1920. Dzerzhinsky, not Stalin, is the last man on Kronstadt; Stalin goes to Georgia and is never tried. Victoria attends the Romanov burial; the Tomsk vote is seen through the British consul; Alash's regiments go to the Emba guard and a Turkestan cavalry division.

**CR-045 integration:** [Omsk in depth: Mikhailov, the paymaster and Maria](CR-045.md). Ivan Mikhailov, Siberia's finance minister, loses the Kazan gold to the Reserve Bank's paymaster at Omsk, Pavel Kashevarov, owes Pacifica his escape from the Czechs in September 1918 and sells it the coup of 18 November the evening before; Julian lets the coup begin so that it fails in public, and Mikhailov is dismissed to Harbin, London's man on the surface and Pacifica's underneath. He confronts Julian at Harbin in 1921 and is shot there by Volkov in January 1928. Maria Grishina-Almazova refuses him, keeps a Tokyo salon, reports to the Navy General Staff through Kiyoshi's son, marries the fleet-faction officer Kashiwagi, and warns of the May 1932 coup.

**CR-046 integration:** [The Petrograd account](CR-046.md). A British secret-service account at Petrograd paid for Rasputin's murder and the palace-coup plotters of 1916–17, was drawn on for the Omsk promise of credit in 1918, and in 1920 paid through the Copenhagen prisoner channel for the Cheka's British file to be burned. Kashevarov matches it in 1920; Dzerzhinsky kept the file; Victoria, who has read it, snubs the King's representative at the Romanov burial; Stalin tries to sell it to Pacifica and is killed in Tiflis in 1921; in June 1927 Kashevarov ends London's Baku review by showing Churchill one sheet, and the Chronicler holds the whole file.

**CR-047 integration:** [The three districts: Kars, Ardahan and Batum](CR-047.md). No Turkish–Armenian war in 1920; a Pacifican-supervised vote in 1921, district by district on the 1914 rolls, gives Kars and Kağızman to Armenia and Olti and Ardahan to Turkey; Ararat stays Armenian; Batum is a Georgian free port without a garrison; the Treaty of Kars, 13 October 1921.

**CR-048 integration:** [The rump Confederacy and the long emergency](CR-048.md). The rump of South Carolina, Georgia, Alabama, Mississippi and Florida governs from Montgomery, carries more than two-thirds of the Confederate debt under the Protocol of Richmond of 1898 and never signs the Gulf compact. Pacifica reads its 1871 guarantee narrowly and acts only through a thin layer of published money and the Office's honest reporting. The revolution never comes: Atlanta in 1906 and the Parchman raid of 1910 fail, and from 1916 Garvey's movement absorbs the anger. Virginia and North Carolina rejoin the Union on fundamental conditions the Union can neither enforce nor drop; the Parchman Act of 1911 sends scientists abroad, Ernest Everett Just among them to Moku o Loʻe. The *Chicago Tribune* voices the theory that Pacifica made it all. The Confederacy's presidents to 1934 follow the Union's. Convict leasing, Birmingham's wages, the Gulf mirror, life in the rump and the 1932 qualifier are PROPOSED; population figures are MODEL.

**CR-049 integration:** [Pacifica's scientific nomos and ethos, computing and the atom](CR-049.md). Elias writes *On the Public Arts* (1851–1854), the brief Genth, Zeuner and Sylvester are hired on: find ability, judge it by its works, never make anyone falsify an account. Travelling fellowships from 1854, the Calculation Office (1855), a Scheutz-type printing calculator (1856), the Laboratory of Light, Electricity and Matter (1859), Peirce's Boolean relay demonstrator (1863) and programmed calculation by 1879; examinations qualify, achievement ranks among the qualified; a contested civic usage of *mana*. Charles Sanders Peirce, Kate Layne (later Curran and Brandegee) and Samuel Kamakau carry it. The atom runs from uranium's activity (1861) through transformation (1870), the electron (1879), the nucleus (1883) and the neutron (1899) to fission (1908) and the chain (1909) in Julian's first years, on the sources of the Light and Matter Works, an atomic estate with a seat among the Five under Hillebrand. Pacifica takes all the uranium it can get: the Western Slope on a Ute lease, Joachimsthal through the Bosnian loan of 1909 and a concession at The Hague, Shinkolobwe three-quarters owned by the Works, Fergana alongside the Emba concession. A secret desert test about 1920 (moved to May 1924 by CR-050-R1); a uranium device destroys a line of old capital ships in open ocean in 1927, completing Britain's retreat; an atomic station lights Tokyo, with Nishina as co-lead. The Works' dates, the secrecy settlement (G1), the fission paper, the element names, the expeditions and the means of further mining (next CR) remain PROPOSED or OPEN; the figures are MODEL.

**CR-050 integration:** [The sealed envelope: secrecy, the walled college and the lead in atomic research](CR-050.md). The fission paper of November 1908 and the chain result of 1909 are sealed in the Thalassia archive, never falsified: Peirce raises the alarm at the challenge evening, Hillebrand holds his own paper, Julian sanctions the seal in 1909, Victoria accepts it only with a twenty-year term, and Julian and Vance open it at the demonstration of 1927. The work goes on behind a wall where the customs and the rivalry of Carquinez and Ralston College continue; silence costs no credit because a sealed roll names every result; recruits pass an outer college before a bonded inner circle. Pacifica publishes true science on everything but energy, its dangers first, so no craze for the barium-borne salt arises; it buys whole ores as a greedy trust and gives the salt as gifts of state. Europe answers Pacifica's data early (Haas's quantum atom, 1907); Meitner, Dempster and Nagaoka find and prove the light isotope (1909–1917); Langmuir, Tesla's student, builds the magnet halls (decision 1917, first hall 1919); the desert test moves to the Black Rock Desert in May 1924; Hahn rediscovers fission in 1920; Millikan stays and Nagaoka resigns; Langmuir succeeds Hillebrand, who dies in Honolulu in 1925; Nishina enters in 1922; the breeding line runs to an experimental breeder about 1930–1932; the Tokyo station is built in 1928–1931 and opened on 29 April 1932. The demonstration moves south of the great-circle route, with a closed zone.

**CR-050-R1 correction:** [Ralston College and the walled college at Hetch Hetchy](CR-050-R1.md). The college of 1866 is Ralston College, after William Ralston: Carquinez's gold and Ralston College's silver. The walled college stands in Hetch Hetchy, publicly the Tuolumne Station; the Tuolumne Me-Wuk lease its use and the Kootzaduka'a Paiute make their own compact; Ralston College enters under the Compact of the Tuolumne (1910); San Francisco takes Lake Eleanor and Cherry Valley (1913); Peirce names fission; the workbook is re-timed. The Native leaders' names remain OPEN.

**CR-051 integration:** [The moat: the two hard facts, the industrial lead and the long secret](CR-051.md). Pacifica's lead rests on what Europe cannot guess and cannot build, not on fission staying secret: that separated light uranium explodes in kilogram amounts, and that it can be separated at industrial scale. The Works throttles its neutron sources from 1909 instead of stopping them, and keeps publishing around uranium; Marie Curie's school takes the Nobel Prize of 1911 for elements beyond uranium, from which Hillebrand withdraws; Rutherford's satellite model of 1912 becomes Europe's prohibition; Hahn, with von Baeyer and then Paneth, finds barium in 1920 and Bohr reaches the rare isotope; Dempster publishes the light uranium line in 1921. Ralston College's thermal diffusion loses its contest in 1920 and becomes the halls' feed in 1921; the halls are built big on the war's nitrogen power and the Works' own dams, the district's largest load, published as such; Mint silver winds the first halls. William Luther Sibert, the canal's builder and the engineer of Tsingtao, is the Chief from 1917 under a Council of the Seal; O'Shaughnessy, Hoover (ore office 1920–1925) and Samuel Wilder King serve with him. The valley holds only the inner circle; the Columbia station at White Bluffs takes the piles under three compacts with a measured promise. The *Chicago Tribune*'s Sierra story of 1922 is contained; Lindemann and Tizard read the evidence two ways; the 1924 test is announced in one true sentence; Fowler's memo is declined in 1926. The record opens in two parts in 1927, the second sealed to 1947, with the re-bonding of 1927; Europe's heavy-water pile runs about 1930–1931 and no weapon route exists before 1932. Figures are MODEL; the Villa question, the dams' compacts and the bands' leaders remain OPEN.

**CR-052 integration:** [The energy workbook: Pacifica's power](CR-052.md). California's oilfields are found early and flooded early, so home oil reaches about 228 million barrels in 1913 and peaks in 1924; Realm coal at four times history and all the Gulf crude the trust controls carry the rest, and the 1932 Gulf compact is a price floor, not an output cut. California is the most motorized place on earth. The mountain divisions of the main lines go electric after the oil strain of 1914–1918, county grids carry electric cooking to nearly every home, Boulder Canyon is decided in 1920 and gives power in 1926, and the Northwest's mills burn the river's secondary power. Harris J. Ryan, Pacifican-raised, builds the halls' electrical supply and takes the power seat from Tesla in 1924. The Hilo smelter runs on a Hawaiian hydro board's windward plants. Figures are MODEL (energy workbook, twelfth build).

**CR-053 integration:** [Oil abroad: the concessions of the 1920s](CR-053.md). The oil trust takes minority positions abroad with money, partners and published terms: Tropical Oil at De Mares, Lago Petroleum at Maracaibo, a Peruvian company with a reciprocal Union fifth, Holmes's Bahrain and Kuwait options, and a share of the Mesopotamian company beside the Union's group. Company details and the 1927 settlement's two quiet terms remain PROPOSED; amounts are MODEL.

**CR-054 integration:** [The energy-intensive champions](CR-054.md). Aluminium at half the world's output by 1929 is mostly absorbed at home and in the Concord (the grid's conductor, the air arm, the Concord pool), on Arkansas bauxite through the Gulf compact; electrolytic ammonia on the Columbia serves the Valley and the Concord's own market, not Japan's; the Hayama annex, agreed at the first Council of the Two Crowns in 1928, divides the partners' industries; the Concord joins the nitrogen convention of 1930 and Pacifica stays outside the European aluminium cartel. Hilo is the showcase, not the largest plant, and Pacifica's 1931 is a champion's bad year, not a national one.

**CR-055 integration:** [The Columbia's reserve](CR-055.md). The Columbia is built early: Bonneville (1913), Rocky Reach (1919), Wells (1926), McNary (1927) and a laddered Chief Joseph (1931), each leased from the peoples of its reach, none where salmon cannot pass. Its power serves purposes no other country has: the Columbia–Sierra line of 1924, hydrogen for California's heavy residue, synthetic rubber against Britain's restriction scheme, electric pig iron for the carrier yards, and a decade-early electrochemistry. After 1927 the same river is the atom's second lead. The 1928 decision, the diffusion plant's site, the reactor metals and the Rjukan contract remain PROPOSED.

**CR-058 integration:** [North America: containing the Union, drawing Canada and Mexico in, who fuels the Union, and Villa](CR-058.md). Pacifica is the giant its neighbours need not fear. It backs Juárez, encourages British Columbia into Confederation and grants Canada the Alaskan rivers in the spring of 1871, while Grant's war of 1870 shows each neighbour a different face of the Union's plan. Canada learns its diplomacy on western waters, seals, salmon and halibut with a partner that ratifies its treaties; its brigade serves at Irkutsk; Great Bear Lake's pitchblende is found about 1912 and Ottawa keeps the Works' contract in 1927. Doheny is a Pacifican oilman, more successful and less criminal. The Union, with none of the Gulf Republic's oil, no California and no Sequoyah, imports four-fifths of its oil by 1929, most of it from the Gulf Republic; Standard survives as a refinery without crude, and Pacifica never embargoes. The Gulf's oil law separates the stages of the business under a 55% ceiling, and the Caribbean understanding of 1921–1923 gives the Union Venezuela and the Gulf and Pacifica Colombia, with no bases on the mainland. In 1915 Pacifica and the Gulf recognise Carranza; Villa raids Columbus; a consented ninety-day expedition under Funston follows, and Patton makes his raid at San Miguelito. Dates marked PROPOSED and amounts marked MODEL keep their local labels.

**CR-058-R1 integration:** [Doheny among the Five](CR-058-R1.md). Logan Turner reaches sixty-eight about 1907, and the champions' seat passes to Doheny after a decade in the Senate as the trust's critic; he gives up the management of his companies, enforces Article 27 on them in 1917 and holds the seat until 1924.

**CR-066 integration:** [Elias before California: Missouri, Taos, the Colonel and Kit Carson](CR-066.md). Elias is born at Franklin, Missouri, to Reuben Thorne (not Silas), a Santa Fe trader and mule trader, and an Anglo mother; from 1834 he winters at Taos and reads aloud to the illiterate Kit Carson. The Colonel, a Champ d'Asile exile and assayer at the Ortiz, teaches him French through Plutarch, Vauban, Jomini, strata and assaying, lends him the volume with Lycurgus and Numa, and is hanged for treason at Taos in April 1847. The pastor's Independence, the Arkansas drowning of 1837 and Carson at Gavilan Peak and San Pasqual follow; Part E (Taos, December 1862) goes in with CR-061.

**CR-015 integration:** The peace, interior line, continental association and transpacific sequence are integrated through 1900, with their consequences carried into the later generations. [CR-015](CR-015.md) preserves the decision record; the [Power Timeline](power_timeline_1865_1900.md) governs cadence and guardrails, with the later CR-015 decisions controlling its named corrections. **[PROPOSED]** identifies connective mechanics that the attachment still labels proposed; their inclusion here does not approve them. Numerical targets remain subject to the feasibility audit. CR-014 continues to govern origins and constitutional machinery. [CR-013 revision 5](../../outputs/CR-013/CR-013-consolidated.md) records which older questions this integration settles or narrows.

## The novel's larger movement

An unusually intelligent boy raised in the Rocky Mountain trading world learns to see terrain, finance, machinery, animals, and human groups as systems whose hidden constraints can be understood. In an unstable California he helps assemble an independent republic before the Mexican-American War, then uses public gold discoveries to finance industry. In 1856 that republic and the Hawaiian Kingdom establish the Confederation of Pacifica, whose emergency constitution later becomes the instrument of his extraordinary power. His accomplishments depend on friendships, the cooperation of Californios and Indigenous peoples, the Hawaiian royal house, Japanese reformers, merchants, technical specialists, and competent soldiers, not on solitary genius. California's sovereignty survives through political compromises and costly confrontations rather than inevitable success. His later interventions in the American Civil War help remake the continent. The bilingual republic offers enforceable rights, public employment and opportunities for technical enterprise. Its expanding workshops, professional services and infrastructure attract capable immigrants whose achievements increasingly depend on the state Elias administers. Emergency government and a later delegation of legislative authority make continued growth and personal power mutually reinforcing. His ambition makes partners dependent through credit, standards, naval protection and careers. Some become subjects; Japan retains sovereignty, China retains its territory, and Korea remains independent under guarantee. The choice to extinguish Hawaiian consent makes the betrayal of his oldest partnership especially stark.

The most intimate version of that political history is the story of Elias and Victoria Kamāmalu, sister of two Hawaiian kings. They begin in love and share an ambitious conception of Pacific cooperation. They marry and have a son. After Lot Kapuāiwa's death she becomes Queen of Hawaii. In 1874 she leases Puʻuloa to the Confederation, and Elias's government stretches the lease into effective rule over the kingdom; she refuses a royalist rising raised in her name and lets her own Supreme Court's judgment go unenforced. Their child subsequently dies in an accident that also leaves Victoria unable to bear children. Grief, responsibility, empire, and rival ambitions corrode but do not end the marriage. Elias has children with other women. Near the end of his life his Hawaiian-American physician Kaʻanoe Ward bears Julian in 1886. While Julian’s mother lives, Victoria adopts him and raises him as successor. After Elias’s death Noe dies in the contested typhoid outbreak of 1888; her registers become the foundation of Victoria’s measures. Victoria becomes Regent of Pacifica through a negotiated succession while remaining Queen of Hawaii, tries to keep the factions in balance, and hands power directly to Julian in 1907. Mother and son then struggle over the uses of the power she has preserved. Their conflict culminates in the concealment of the Altai atrocity and her renunciation, while the Pacific order itself survives. 

Across succeeding generations, commanders replace improvisation with military science, administrators replace personal loyalties with examinations and numerical identities, and the empire comes to mistake the perfection of its machinery for political wisdom. The Chronicler, once an admirer of the founding order, witnesses its successes and atrocities, encounters competing accounts, and tries to understand the human cost of an order that wins. He may not know everything that the independent viewpoint narratives disclose.

Japan’s mastery of Pacifica’s methods makes its strength a condition of survival and a source of tension. Imperial careers give Japanese elites a stake in institutions centered at Thalassia; compact renewals, Chiyo’s marriage and Julian’s bond with Hirohito lead to the Pacific Concord of 1927, given lawful consent through Victoria’s settlement in 1928. Julian wins the Russian war with Japanese, Chinese, Siberian and Russian partners, while Pacifica’s credit and supplies bring Europe to a brokered peace. The depression then permits association with the sovereign Union. By 1932 the Pacific order dominates and its institutions hold. The Altai record, the price industry exacts from the land, and continuing exclusions give that triumph its unresolved human cost.

---

# PART I — The apprenticeship of Elias Thorne and the rise of California (1824–1861)

## 1. Missouri, Taos and the pastor (1824–1837)

<!-- CR-066: Missouri, the Trail and Taos -->
**[APPROVED — CR-066]** Elias Thorne is born about 1824 in Missouri, at the end of the road to Santa Fe, to Reuben Thorne and his wife. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** He is born at Franklin, in the Boonslick, where the Santa Fe trade began three years earlier. His father is a Kentuckian who came to the Boonslick about 1819 and works the far end of the trade, trading, trapping and later driving mules between Missouri, Taos and Chihuahua. In August 1826 he takes the Santa Fe road in the caravan that sixteen-year-old Kit Carson joins when he runs away from his apprenticeship to a Franklin saddler. About 1828 the Missouri swallows Franklin and the family moves to Independence. In 1829 Reuben rides with Ewing Young and Carson to California and comes home with stories of San Gabriel and the Pacific. At nine Elias watches Jackson County's mobs drive the Mormons out and burn their houses. His mother understands how little security possession offers when debt and distant authority determine who may remain. The boy is vigorous, exceptionally observant, almost unable to remain indoors, and capable of remembering a route, the shape of a ridge or a passage read aloud with unnerving precision.

**[PROPOSED — CR-066]** When the beaver trade collapses, Reuben turns to mules and the Taos–Chihuahua road, and from 1834 the boy goes with him: out with the spring caravan, the winter at Taos, home in the spring. He learns his Spanish there among traders, guides, herders and Pueblo and Hispano households whose knowledge, interests and political boundaries cannot be replaced by a map. **[APPROVED — CR-066]** Kit Carson drifts through between northern seasons, small, quiet and unable to read, and the boy reads aloud to him and the other trappers by the fire.

<!-- CR-066: the Colonel -->
**[APPROVED — CR-066]** An older French émigré winters at Taos. People call him Colonel, although no one has quite established which army awarded his commission or whether he awarded the last promotion to himself. He served in the military world shaped by Napoleon, and in 1818 he was among the exiles of Champ d'Asile, the Bonapartist colony on the Trinity in Spanish Texas that Spain and the United States squeezed out within months. He drifted by Louisiana and St Louis to Taos, took Mexican citizenship and went back to the sacraments, and he works summers as an assayer at the Ortiz gold diggings south of Santa Fe. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** Reuben pays him in mules and favours to keep the boy out of the cantinas. **[APPROVED — CR-066]** Among his few valuable possessions are the writings of Vauban and Jomini, maps from European campaigns, practical texts on engineering and mechanics, and Dacier's French Plutarch, from which Elias learns the French he needs for everything else on the shelf. The Colonel teaches him surveying, the relation between roads and armies, earthworks, water supplies, slopes, siegecraft, metallurgy and the hidden politics of supply. He reads Taos Pueblo to him as Vauban would read a fortress and the Santa Fe Trail as Jomini would read a line of operations, with water the whole of war on the Cimarron. At the Ortiz he teaches him to recognise gold-bearing ground and assay it. On walks in the Sangre de Cristo foothills he teaches the boy to read rock strata and drainage basins, to set them beside the crinoid limestone of the Missouri bluffs, and to separate observation from a wish.

**[APPROVED — CR-066]** The colonel brings memories of imperial glory and imperial collapse, and of a frontier state that failed. Elias hears both the exhilaration of Napoleon's armies and the vulnerability of a system inseparable from one man's will. The old man offers no settled moral; the examples of his own life sometimes contradict his lessons. Elias reads Jomini for the limits imposed by ground, movement and material. **[OPEN]** The Colonel's name.

<!-- CR-066: the pastor at Independence -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-066]** Fossil-bearing rocks and the order of strata make Elias question how long the earth has existed. In the summer of 1836, at Independence, the argument becomes public. The Rev. Silas Merritt, who preaches to the Santa Fe traders, hears the boy say what the crinoids in the bluffs say about the age of the earth and strikes him first; Elias, twelve, retaliates and breaks the man's nose. The blow reveals frightening physical force and an unwillingness to yield even when restraint would serve him better. He respects serious belief while doubting creeds that require him to deny what he has examined.

Elias can quiet a difficult horse through patience and an acute reading of fear, yet hunts competently and does not shrink from killing. Long journeys through mountain country develop his stamina and memory of routes. A mistaken judgment that endangers an animal, a companion or himself teaches him the cost of error. Work as a youthful guide and trader develops these abilities without making him an accomplished veteran before adulthood.

<!-- CR-066: the river, the fire and the book -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-066]** In 1836 Reuben borrows from an Independence outfitter to buy the land he farms, now that the land office wants gold or silver, and to outfit a Chihuahua venture that will bring the silver home. Elias goes with him and spends his last winter at Taos. **[APPROVED — CR-066]** In the spring the Colonel lends him the first volume of his Plutarch for the road home, with Theseus and Romulus, who founded by force, and Lycurgus and Numa, who founded by law. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** He means to bring it back in the autumn. On the way home Reuben finds the Arkansas, the border river, in flood. His note is falling due. **[APPROVED — CR-066]** He chooses the place and forces the crossing, and drowns; his son, thirteen, is on the bank. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** The Panic of 1837 runs the debt from New York to St Louis to Independence and to the widow. The creditors take the holding under Missouri law; the house burns during the dispossession, and his mother's possessions are destroyed or carried away. Who set the fire is never established: the Chronicler records the creditor's men, an accident, and Mrs Thorne burning the house rather than hand it over. The loss deepens Elias's hostility to power exercised without personal accountability; the lender never stood on the riverbank. It does not create his ambition from nothing: he already imagines achievements larger than the world he knows.

**[PROPOSED — CR-066]** He never goes back to Taos and never returns the book. He reads Lycurgus and Numa until the binding fails, and two pictures stay with him: the lawgiver struck in the eye by a young man's stick, who takes the young man into his house; and the lawgiver who binds his city by oath to keep his laws until he comes back, and does not come back.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** The pastor whose nose Elias broke takes in the widow and her son. His daughter Abigail, two years older than the boy, keeps the house while Mrs Thorne sinks under the loss; she feeds him, mends for him, reads with him and scolds him. He finds in her the mother that grief has taken from him.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** Years later Abigail Merritt says her dying father told her to marry the boy he had taken in. Only she heard it. The story is persuasive, flattering and impossible for the Chronicler to verify; its source is her own papers, kept with the Thorne family Bible and the letters Elias's mother wrote to a son they never reached. Whether the pastor admired Elias, forgave him, or was remembered according to his daughter's needs remains uncertain.

## 2. Texas, Comanche diplomacy and the road to Mexico (1837–1842)

In his teens Elias leaves the pastor's household and makes his living along mountain, plains and river routes. He guides, hunts, transports goods, handles livestock and takes boat work where opportunity offers, learning the economics of risk from people whose lives depend on weather and imperfect information. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** In the end he takes the Texas Road south out of Missouri. By 1840, about sixteen, he reaches the Republic of Texas, where the words *republic*, *sovereignty* and *treaty* bear meanings more immediate and dangerous than those in his books.

In San Antonio he witnesses the Council House negotiations with Penateka Comanche representatives in March 1840. The Texans demand captives from men who cannot compel every autonomous Comanche band to comply. When the negotiators attempt to take the visitors hostage, peace talks become a slaughter. Elias has seen armed men break faith in the name of public authority; he also sees the bloody reprisals that follow. The episode lodges in his memory as a warning about the difference between political promises and the force necessary to honor them. He will eventually violate the principle himself, to terrible effect in Hawaii.

In 1841 Elias joins the Texan Santa Fe Expedition. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** Few of its men have seen the country it means to annex; he has wintered at Taos. The expedition's ambitions far exceed its preparations. Drought, hunger, uncertain routes and exhaustion defeat its men before a decisive engagement can save them. Caleb Vance is among the more experienced travelers. He first treats Elias as a capable but overconfident youngster, while Elias finds in Caleb the sort of practical toughness no book can confer. They begin by disagreeing over ground and survival, then become indispensable to one another when the party surrenders to New Mexican authorities.

Elias and Caleb are marched south with the captives toward Mexico City and endure imprisonment and dependence on strangers. Elias deepens the practical Spanish acquired through frontier trade, observes the Mexican army and government firsthand, and encounters guards, priests, civilians and officials who cannot be divided into one class of enemies. Some act cruelly; others show compassion, intellectual curiosity or professional pride. The men are released during 1842. Their shared vulnerability produces the friendship that will survive both military glory and imperial power.

<!-- CR-035: the guard tells of the Mining School and del Río -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-035]** On the march south a guard who had studied at the Mining School in Mexico City had talked with Elias about its aged professor of mineralogy, del Río, who found a new metal and was not believed. The school was founded on the miners' own fund under the Ordinances of 1783, and it admitted its pupils by examination. Elias will remember a state that paid for knowledge out of the ore.

After their release they move through the contested borderlands. Elias's familiarity with Spanish, Mexican politics, surveying and local commerce becomes as important as his skill with firearms. He can negotiate with men he once feared and recognize when another community's political purposes do not resemble his own. He and Caleb work their way west, crossing mountains and deserts as Elias's attention turns increasingly toward the long Pacific coast.

## 3. Californio politics and the birth of the republic (1844–1846)

Elias reaches Sutter's Fort with Caleb in autumn 1844, aged twenty, riding a horse and with little money. Sutter offers him a position among the foreign riflemen who guard his interests. Elias declines: he has no wish to become the instrument of a man whose fortunes depend upon the governor's patronage. He rides instead to Monterey and finds work surveying land, roads, harbors and approaches to the coast.

In November Juan Bautista Alvarado and José Castro challenge Governor Manuel Micheltorena. Elias brings them accurate reports about routes and defensive positions and offers advice in the Spanish he refined during captivity in Mexico. His judgment earns him a patron in Castro. At the Battle of Providencia on 20–21 February 1845, he helps persuade foreign riflemen serving Micheltorena not to fight for a government they are unlikely to save. The engagement ends after artillery demonstrations and limited bloodshed. Elias's first Californian achievement is to prevent needless killing by understanding what the men on the other side want.

Pío Pico governs from Los Angeles while Castro commands from Monterey; the northern and southern elites become increasingly suspicious of one another. Elias serves as Castro's surveyor and adviser on coastal defense. In mid-1845 he accepts Catholic baptism with Alvarado as godfather and becomes a naturalized Californian. He regards the voluntary rite chiefly as an act of allegiance to the community whose laws he now lives under, although even he is not entirely certain whether inward belief accompanies his practical choice. In the following year's republican settlement, naturalization rests on civil allegiance rather than religious conversion. The public gesture coexists with his private skepticism and his increasing respect for traditions that sustain a political people.

Before the year ends, Pico grants him a rancho on the Carquinez Strait. Elias selects the site with the attention of a surveyor: it commands maritime access to the Sacramento and San Joaquin watersheds. There he begins assembling workshops, boat-building facilities and commercial arrangements rather than a private kingdom. News arrives that John C. Frémont's surveying expedition has entered California. Elias recognizes in the American officer another intelligent and energetic man, but one whose loyalties are unlikely to align with the Californio coalition.

The coalition against Micheltorena develops into negotiations between Californio leaders and an organized English-speaking constituency of settlers, merchants and tradesmen. The latter demand an independent republic in which they can conduct business, seek justice and hold office in English. Californio partners seek enforceable protection for their property, language, religious institutions and local authority. Alvarado remembers the 1836 declaration of a free and sovereign California. Pico fears American annexation and considers British protection; Castro is unwilling to sacrifice military prerogatives to an assembly dominated by southern landholders. Vallejo, sympathetic to American connections, refuses to assume that permanent separation from the United States is in California's interest. Sem-Yeto, known as Solano, negotiates on behalf of Suisun interests that include recognition of territorial and political authority, not ceremonial admission to a settler republic. Elias helps bring these competing interests into conversation without erasing their differences.

## 4. Monterey and the decision not to invade (1846–1848)

Frémont fortifies Gavilan Peak in March 1846 after Castro orders him to leave. Elias examines the slopes and supply lines and advises Castro against a frontal assault. The Americans withdraw, but the near-confrontation demonstrates how easily a local dispute could become war between the new republic's neighbors. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** Through the glass Elias has known the small man on the parapet: Kit Carson is Frémont's guide. As the Americans come down the road, the two speak once.

During April and May the proposal for a general council at Santa Barbara becomes the central political issue. Pico and Castro remain rivals; Alvarado drafts institutional compromises, while Vallejo demands safeguards for commerce with the United States. Delegates bargain over land grants, customs, ports, the standing of immigrants, the rights of allied Indigenous communities, and the future relation with Mexico. The founding bargain joins Californio self-government to English-speaking residents' demands for representative institutions and recognizable civil liberties. Each constituency accepts independence partly to avoid becoming subordinate to the other or to a distant capital; neither speaks for all inhabitants. Elias represents the Carquinez district, one of two recently naturalized foreigners elected to attend. He writes much of the defense and customs scheme, drawing on the military lessons of his mentor and on the weak finances of the prospective state.

On **Pentecost Sunday, 31 May 1846**, the convention proclaims the *República de California*. The Christian feast of languages made mutually intelligible gives the ceremony an apt meaning in a region whose constituencies distrust one another. Pío Pico becomes provisional president; José Castro serves as commander-general. The declaration bears the names of Pío and Andrés Pico, Castro, Alvarado, a reluctant Vallejo, Pablo de la Guerra, William Hartnell and Elias Thorne, among others. Solano signs or marks a separate compact as an allied chief rather than acquiring a fictitious mandate to speak for every Indigenous people. The founding settlement gives English and Spanish constitutional standing in legislation, courts and public administration. Citizenship and eligibility for civil employment rest on defined civic qualifications rather than language ancestry or conversion; exact franchise and naturalization rules remain **[OPEN]**. Religious liberty protects public worship alongside Catholic institutions. A bill of rights guarantees representative government, jury trial, habeas corpus, due process and protection against arbitrary seizure. Existing laws continue where consistent with that settlement, including the prohibition of slavery, vested property rights and mining rules awaiting legislative adaptation. The republic must reconcile English-speaking expectations of justice with land grants, water uses and Indigenous political and territorial claims; declaring a right does not settle whose claim is valid.

The declaration immediately faces force. On 14 June American settlers seize Vallejo at Sonoma and raise the Bear Flag, now in rebellion against an already-proclaimed Californian state. Frémont supports their movement without yet obtaining a clear federal mandate for an occupation of the republic. At Olompali on 24 June, a mixed Californio and Indigenous force including Elias and Caleb defeats a force of Bear Flaggers at serious cost and drives Frémont's following back toward Sutter's Fort. The losses leave Elias painfully aware that winning loyalty by persuasion does not remove the consequences of taking up arms.

Commodore John D. Sloat reaches Monterey in July. His orders were issued in anticipation of a war against Mexico, and he finds a government asserting that its ports no longer belong to Mexico. The memory of Commodore Jones's mistaken seizure of Monterey in 1842 makes him cautious. Rather than simply repeating the historical occupation of 7 July, he maintains a threatening naval presence, questions the republic's legitimacy and refers the conflict to Washington. A reply may be months away. Admiral Seymour arrives aboard HMS *Collingwood* on 16 July; **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** Pico asks the admiral for protection. Seymour's only instruction on California is to observe the Americans, and London has ruled a protectorate "entirely out of the question"; he declines. Britain does not offer military protection, but British witnesses and merchants increase the risks of an unauthorized attack. The Republic learns that it will have no patron.

When Robert F. Stockton replaces Sloat, Elias helps negotiate a naval standstill. American vessels may provision in Californian ports for operations against Mexico but may not seize them. Domestic critics accuse Elias of furnishing an invading navy; officers on both sides argue over what neutrality requires. The agreement is tested when Stephen W. Kearny approaches overland in December. Andrés Pico's Californio lancers defeat his column at San Pasqual. It is a victory won by Pico and his men, not Elias, and it obliges Stockton to reconsider the practical cost of overturning the standstill. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** Kearny's guide from New Mexico, among the beaten, is Kit Carson, who had been riding east with the commodore's dispatches when Kearny turned him back. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** At Cahuenga on 13 January 1847, Frémont, cut off and acting on his own authority, signs terms with Andrés Pico to get his battalion out of the country. Kearny's paroled survivors embark at San Diego, prisoners are exchanged, and Bear Flag men who are American citizens receive an amnesty that Californios who lost men at Olompali do not forgive. The standstill is put in writing until Washington replies, and Stockton adheres for the squadron. Kearny never forgives Frémont for treating with a foreign government over his head. With the consul Thomas Larkin, Elias drafts a memorandum of what the Republic could offer Washington. It travels east with Kearny's report.

In 1847 Elias expands the powder works and boatyard at Carquinez and surveys the foothills with experienced local partners. The arrival of the Mormons in the Salt Lake basin complicates the territorial balance: there is a continental neighbor whose political objectives neither Washington nor California controls. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** The Mormon Battalion, arriving at San Diego after San Pasqual, is interned under the Cahuenga terms and then released to march to the Salt Lake; some of its men work a season at Carquinez first. Kearny has Frémont arrested, and a court-martial in Washington tries him for his conduct at Sonoma and Cahuenga. Benton defends him and threatens to prove that Frémont acted on instructions carried west by Lieutenant Gillespie, orders written for a Mexican province and carried out against a republic. On 31 January 1848 the court convicts Frémont. Polk remits the sentence, Frémont resigns, and his father-in-law breaks with the President. Frémont's future dealings with Elias are colored by a defeat neither man has forgotten.

<!-- CR-066: the Colonel's end at Taos -->
**[APPROVED — CR-066]** The Colonel does not live to see California recognised. On 19 January 1847 the Taos rising kills Governor Charles Bent in his house; the women and children escape through an adobe wall, Carson's wife among them. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** In April a court sits at Taos. One judge is Charles Beaubien, whose son the rising killed; the other is the dead governor's friend, and the governor's brother is foreman of the jury. It convicts the old Frenchman, a Mexican citizen under a flag some months old, of treason against the United States on a letter in French that Beaubien reads to the court, and he is hanged in the plaza on 9 April 1847 beside Hipólito Salazar. What he did in January is never established. **[APPROVED — CR-066]** The news reaches Elias in California that summer. It is the first of his grievances against the United States to wear its uniform. **[PROPOSED — CR-066]** Carson's household lost Bent to the rising; Elias lost the Colonel to the court, and Carson's friends sat on its jury.

**[PROPOSED — CR-028]** Kearny's report reaches Washington in April 1847, as Scott's army lands at Veracruz. The Secretary of War has no regiments to spare, and the Navy cannot hold a hostile coast under British eyes. Polk orders his commodore to observe the Cahuenga arrangements and sends Stevenson's New York regiment, already at sea for San Francisco, on to Mazatlán and La Paz. He calls it a suspension. His envoy Nicholas Trist leaves for Mexico with instructions that still claim Upper California, and Polk expects to win at the peace table what he will not fight for on the coast.

**[PROPOSED — CR-028]** California sends two missions. In Washington, Mariano Vallejo, Frémont's former prisoner and the most pro-American man to sign the declaration, leads the envoys with Pablo de la Guerra. Secretary of State James Buchanan had written in 1845 that an independent California would receive the kind offices due a sister republic; in September 1847 he initials with them a conditional text, insurance in case the peace leaves the Republic standing. In Mexico, Alvarado and Elias reach Querétaro after the fall of the capital and Santa Anna's resignation. They leave Monterey in haste: in October Elias has found gold on the Mokelumne, and he knows the Republic's bargains will hold only if they are made before anyone else does. Peña y Peña's government needs money and a way to call the peace something other than surrender. In the **Convention of Querétaro** of December 1847, Mexico recognizes California. In return California assumes approximately **£3 million of Mexican obligations to London bondholders**, secured against customs and prospective mining revenue. The bonds of Mexico's 1846 conversion carry no land rights, so California buys no territory from them, only a creditor with a reason to want it to survive. William Hartnell carries the convention to London for the bondholders' consent. The convention also guarantees Mexican citizens' property and the Church's, and pledges never to join the United States or cede any of its territory to a foreign power.<!-- CR-039: the pledge guards against annexation, not confederation --> Elias pledges royalties from a find that only seven people in the world know of, and he says nothing false: the mines, he tells the Mexican foreign minister, are California's hope. The precise valuation, creditor arrangements and amortization remain matters of financial modeling.

**[PROPOSED — CR-028]** Trist, recalled but staying on, finds the coast no longer Mexico's to give. The treaty he signs at Guadalupe Hidalgo on 2 February 1848 cedes New Mexico, the Great Basin and the Rio Grande line, and binds the United States to respect California's frontier as Mexico has recognized it. Polk is furious. But the House has just voted that the war was unconstitutionally begun, Benton is waiting with the Gillespie papers, the Whigs have a war hero for November, and to reject the treaty he would have to ask a hostile Congress for money to make war on a republic that declared its independence before it knew of the war. He sends the treaty to the Senate together with the **Buchanan–Vallejo Convention**, signed on 28 February. California pledges never to cede, lease or place under protection any part of its territory to a European power. It opens San Francisco, Monterey and San Diego to American warships, with a revocable naval storehouse at Sausalito. It keeps California's ports closed to the enemies of the United States in its wars. It promises to return deserters from American warships. It grants most-favored-nation commerce, whalers' privileges and civil guarantees for American residents, the commercial and naval articles for twelve years.<!-- CR-039: the naval articles share the twelve-year term --> It closes the claims of 1846 by amnesty and a mixed commission, and it renounces the Basin pending a boundary commission. Buchanan asks for an accession clause, and Vallejo refuses, citing Querétaro. Buchanan also offers the United States' guarantee of California's independence, as he had just given New Granada's, and Elias's instructions refuse it: a guarantor has a right to come in.

**[PROPOSED — CR-028]** The Senate ratifies both on 10 March. Calhoun's Southerners vote for a settlement that keeps a free California out of the Union, and the Whigs vote for peace and for the harbors' commerce. Benton calls it the Trist surrender. Polk confides his consolation to his diary: California will come to the United States of itself, as Texas did. Larkin brings the ratified convention to Monterey in June as the first American chargé d'affaires. On Elias's advice the Assembly ratifies at once. The spring's gold finds are already rumoured in the foothills, but the government has said nothing, and no minister tells Larkin anything false. It confirms the gold only in October, when its mining decree is in force. When the news reaches the East, the instruments have been in force for months. Britain, which had refused since 1844 to encourage California's separation, recognizes the Republic once Mexico has. It declares, as it had with France for Hawaii in 1843, that it will take no part of California under any form; France accedes in 1849. Palmerston's circular of 18 January 1848 has already told the world that Britain may take up the claims of its bondholders. California accepts a geographically limited claim whose boundaries will be settled more precisely in 1850. Debt gives British creditors a stake in the republic's survival and a dangerous lever over its finances.

## 5. The visible gold discoveries and a contentious economy (1847–1852)

Elias has already studied placer gold discovered in 1842 near Placerita Canyon and follows reports from Sonoran miners, Mexican ranchers and people familiar with the Sierra foothills. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** In October 1847, at the end of the dry season, he and Caleb travel through the Stanislaus and Mokelumne drainages with an experienced Sonoran placer miner and a Miwok guide, and find coarse gold in the Mokelumne gravels. Elias assays the samples at Carquinez with the metallurgy the colonel taught him. He tells only Pico, Castro and Alvarado, and within weeks he is on the road to Querétaro. The guide keeps the secret for a price: Elias's promise that the Republic will protect a homeland for his people when the miners come. The winter rains close the foothills. In the spring of 1848 the party returns and finds rich gravels and promising quartz-bearing geology. The guide's knowledge of ground, seasons and waterways and the miner's practical methods are essential; the find is not credited to Elias alone. Elias reports the discovery formally, in confidence, to President Pico and the Council of State and registers a small number of private claims. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** The government keeps an official silence for another six months. Miners and rumours reach the foothills, but no minister confirms anything until the treaties with Washington are exchanged and an emergency mining decree, an assay office and a royalty are in force. Before the exchange it says nothing false; afterwards it calls the reports promising gravels under survey. The silence protects the Republic, and it gives Elias's enemies at home a charge he can answer but never erase: that he registered his claims on state secrets.

The republic publicly administers claims under laws inherited from the Spanish and Mexican mining regime, including the 1783 Mining Ordinances, continued in force by the founding constitution. The legal survival of that regime has limits: a system developed for silver veins requires new rules for dispersed placer fields, while private land grants, Indigenous territories and overlapping claims remain bitterly contested. Gold attracts Californios, Sonorans, Oregonians, Hawaiians and Chileans; then large numbers of Americans arrive as foreigners subject to Californian law.

<!-- CR-034: the managed opening of 1848-1849 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The opening is managed. The decree of October 1848 opens the gravels to anyone who registers a claim and pays the royalty, and the first newcomers are already at work. In December President Polk tells Congress that there is gold in the Republic of California. By then San Francisco's harbor master is registering every ship and every passenger, and the more than hundred thousand who come in 1849 land with a paper naming the law they will live under. Hundreds of sailors desert the American squadron for the diggings. The Buchanan–Vallejo Convention obliges the Republic to return them, and its sheriffs find few; it is the first quarrel under the convention.

Elias is appointed Director of Mines in late 1848. He establishes an administrative framework for assays, claims, courts, mine safety, royalties and records. The **Mining Law of 1849** sets practicable sizes for placer claims and a **5 percent public royalty**, assessed at the mint and trade choke points. Foreign-miner licenses provoke political fights. American miners demand punitive treatment of Sonoran, Chilean and Chinese competitors. Elias resists their campaign and accepts a limited compromise: **$2 monthly licenses for residents and $4 for nonresident foreigners**. His principled insistence that useful labor matters more than ancestry wins partners and permanent enemies.

A mint opens in San Francisco in 1850 beside the State Assay Office. A new California gold coinage gives merchants a reliable means of settlement, and the state begins accumulating revenues from gold, customs, land and trade. Elias's private wealth from properly registered claims is considerable but separate from public gold, public debt and the nation's annual mineral production.

<!-- CR-034: the State Assay Office and Genth -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The assay office is older than the mint. It began in the secret winter as the room at Carquinez where Elias tested the Mokelumne samples alone, and it keeps the government's rule from those months: silence, but no lies. With the decree it moves to San Francisco as the State Assay Office, because a royalty is only as good as the assay behind it. From 1849 no one may sign an assay for the Republic without passing its examination, the first the state sets. In 1850 the office finds the ten-dollar pieces of the Miners' Bank of San Francisco underweight, coins the merchants have been taking at face value, and Elias publishes the result over their protests. **[APPROVED — CR-034]** Frederick Genth, back in Philadelphia that summer from a failed mine in North Carolina, analyses Californian ores on commission for the State Assay Office. In 1851 he accepts the Republic's offer: a laboratory, assistants, a salary that does not depend on clients, the right to publish and time for his own chemistry.

<!-- CR-049: On the Public Arts -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** That year Elias begins *On the Public Arts*, a memorandum he works on until 1854 and never publishes as a law. His own improbable education has taught him that ability is everywhere and opportunity almost nowhere, and Plutarch that a lawgiver works through habits rather than decrees; he has no foresight of what the school will find. Its central passage reads: "A people is as strong as the abilities it raises. Find ability early, train it hard, give it work and judge it by its works. Whoever holds an office holds it for its work, and no one may make him give a false account of that work: not a patron, not a minister, not I. A falsified account robs everyone who must build on it, and I will treat it as theft from the commonwealth. What we build must let those who come after us see further than we do, and beat us." On contest it says: "Give ability its trial and achievement its honour. Let the schools fight for able minds and the counties for the excellence of their works." It is the brief on which Zeuner and Sylvester are hired. Genth's contract is its first clause put into practice, the mining royalty pays for the rest, and its rules live in the school's contracts. The Republic's first computing is people: human computers, commercial arithmetic machines and checked tables for the assay office and the surveyors, with a register of readings, sources and corrections kept from the start.

The **Treaty of Monterey of 1850**, the work of the boundary commission created in 1848, settles the eastern frontier with the United States. From the 42nd parallel, California's boundary follows the 120th meridian south to latitude 39°, then runs southeast to the Colorado River at 35° north and along the Colorado to the Gila. A related agreement fixes the southern border toward the Pacific south of San Diego. California gains internationally recognized ports and access to Yuma while abandoning claims to the Great Basin it has never governed. The United States retains the inland routes and the Salt Lake country. In consequence, the Compromise of 1850 unfolds without an American state of California entering the Union, altering the arithmetic of sectional politics. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** The same arithmetic guards the Republic: Southern senators will not admit a free California, Northern Whigs and Free Soilers will not conquer one, and Washington's policy becomes waiting for the ripe fruit to fall. The Americans of the gold rush arrive as foreigners under Californian law, and the Republic's courts, licenses and careers make many of them Californians instead.

The gold rush brings growing English-speaking commerce and technical employment without extinguishing Spanish-language public rights. During 1849–1852, legislation establishes common-law courts and commercial practice while preserving vested rights and selected Mexican rules concerning land, water, marital property and mining. **[OPEN]** Exact statutes, transitional jurisdictions and claim procedures. English-speaking newcomers can enforce contracts, register enterprises and patents, enter technical education and seek public employment without changing their working language. The election of 1850 strengthens merchants and American-origin settlers in the assembly. Vallejo succeeds Pico as president, offering a compromise to constituencies that fear the older Californio oligarchy. In August squatters contest the land-grant system at Sacramento and blood is shed. In 1851 San Francisco's Committee of Vigilance erects its own machinery of justice and executes men while claiming to protect honest citizens. President Vallejo hesitates before confronting a powerful body of merchants and armed volunteers.

Elias argues that public government cannot coexist with private courts enforced by wealthy factions. A constitutional convention at Monterey in 1852 establishes a Council of State and the **Superintendency of Mines and Public Revenue**, combining management of the gold economy, currency, public credit and expenditure on works. Elias receives the superintendency, a seat on the council and unprecedented influence over industrial policy. The Vigilance Committee is dissolved through an amnesty underwritten by the credible presence of the militia. Elias has no presidency; his strength comes from controlling an increasingly indispensable treasury. He makes public promises credible by paying contractors, opening technical careers and financing works beyond the initial resources of private investors. Immigrants arrive because these opportunities and ordinary legal protections produce visible results. Exact incorporation and patent measures remain **[OPEN]**; recruitment begins with the assisted passages of 1852.

<!-- CR-033: Native policy, the Land Fund, the register and the compact of protection -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-033]** In 1851 volunteer companies want the state to pay for expeditions against the Native villages. On Elias's figures Vallejo's government refuses: every expedition would be paid from the Republic's own treasury, and a militia that makes its own war is a private army. **[APPROVED — CR-033]** Commissioners sign treaties with the Native nations in 1851–52 instead. **[PROPOSED]** Elias keeps his promise to the Miwok guide: his people sign first, and their homeland becomes the pattern. **[APPROVED — CR-033]** A homeland stays the nation's own; it can't be sold, and it can be leased only through the Republic, which keeps a fee. Beyond the homelands only the Republic may buy Native land. It pays in annuities of goods from a Land Fund kept outside the revenues pledged to London, and sells to settlers on instalments that carry the annuities, with the margin helping to pay for assisted passage. Each sale is entered in a public register, kept from 1852 by the Superintendency, and the entry is the title. Militiamen who serve under the Republic's command are paid in land warrants, good only on land the Republic has bought. **[PROPOSED]** Licence commissioners with mounted police keep order in the diggings and bring killers to trial. Squatters and volunteer captains call it robbery. Prices, annuity terms and boundaries remain **[OPEN]**.

The republic's mining revenue finances infrastructure, but its debt to London remains. British bondholders hold claims secured by gold royalties and San Francisco customs. Elias has replaced the foreclosure that ruined his family's home with an even larger act of borrowing in the name of a state. Whether he will retain the ability to pay becomes a question not only of wealth but of sovereignty.

<!-- CR-035: the 1852 convention gives the Republic its school -->
**[APPROVED — CR-035]** The convention of 1852 also gives the Republic its school, without a schools law. The Ordinances of 1783, still in force beside the Mining Law of 1849, charge the miners' fund first with the support of the mining tribunal and its college. Elias reads the article to the delegates and proposes the same arrangement for gold: a share of the royalty for a school of mines, paid after the bondholders' coupon, over their agent's protest. Vallejo and Alvarado, Hartnell's pupils and the men who took the missions from the friars, sponsor it. The Church keeps its parish schools and its colleges; the state pays only for what it examines.

## 6. A Hawaiian friendship becomes a constitutional partnership (1849–1857)

The Hawaiian princes Alexander Liholiho and Lot Kapuāiwa pass through San Francisco with Gerrit Judd in autumn 1849. Elias rides with them into the foothills, where Lot's enthusiasm for horses, exertion, hunting and rough country becomes the beginning of a lasting friendship. Alexander sees California as a serious political possibility: an independent Pacific republic, neither American nor European, that could help his small island kingdom preserve its autonomy. During his American travels he suffers a humiliating encounter with a train conductor who mistakes him for a servant, and the incident deepens his misgivings about the treatment of Hawaiians by the United States.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** In 1849 the Rev. Silas Merritt brings a Methodist mission to Sacramento, with his daughter Abigail, unmarried at twenty-seven. She seeks out the famous young man she once mothered; she alone calls him Eli. Her father dies in the cholera of 1850, telling her to marry him. They become lovers, but in 1851 Elias sails for Honolulu as the princes' guest, drawn by a larger world than Sacramento's settlers. Abigail finds that she is pregnant and does not tell him.
