Only Caleb knows. She marries Henry Pruett, a San Antonio man who shared the Mexican captivity, and goes home to Texas, where Silas is born late in 1851, legitimate in law as Pruett's son.

In 1851 Elias visits Honolulu as the princes' guest. He encounters the royal household and meets their sister, Victoria Kamāmalu, born in 1838 to Kīnaʻu and Kekūanāoʻa, a granddaughter of Kamehameha I and a pupil at the Chiefs' Children's School. She is about thirteen; theirs is a passing, entirely non-romantic introduction. Hawaii is already a complex constitutional monarchy with a commercial and diplomatic world of its own, and Elias learns that the islanders do not need a Californian to invent political institutions for them.

William Walker's filibusters seize territory in Baja California and Sonora during 1853–1854. The Republic of California, which cannot afford to be seen as a sponsor of private American conquest, cuts off their maritime supplies. The expedition fails, and captured adventurers face proceedings in California. The episode demonstrates the value of naval power and earns goodwill from Mexican authorities without making them subordinate to Elias.

At the same time the Hawaiian government hears rumors of further American adventurers and contemplates a treaty of annexation to the United States. Elias offers protection, naval assistance and access to Californian industry **without annexation**. Alexander and the anti-annexation chiefs regard the offer as a way to preserve the crown. Kamehameha III dies in December 1854 with the American annexation treaty unsigned, and Alexander succeeds as Kamehameha IV.

<!-- CR-033: the Hawaiian princes watch California's Native policy -->
**[APPROVED — CR-033]** Alexander and Lot, who rode with Elias into the foothills, have watched how the Republic treats California's own peoples. Its treaties weigh with chiefs who fear what American settlers would do to theirs.

Victoria comes to San Francisco in 1854 with a Hawaiian diplomatic mission. Now about sixteen, she meets Elias as an increasingly serious student of law and statecraft. Their affection begins with long conversations about the security of small nations, the proper limits of rulers and the opportunities of the Pacific trade. He admires her political intelligence and composure; she sees an astonishingly energetic builder whose ambition might preserve both their countries, though his certainty already makes her uneasy. Their courtship develops over the following years as she matures into a stateswoman.

In 1855 Kamehameha IV appoints Victoria **Kuhina Nui**, granting her defined Hawaiian co-executive duties, a place in government and standing to exercise a regency. The office establishes authority within the kingdom; confederal decisions proceed through the institutions authorized by the interstate compact. As in history, she takes the office in January 1855, at sixteen, and presides over the Privy Council; no Hawaiian public document is valid without her signature. The office is not a courtesy offered to Elias's prospective bride; it is an independent legal foundation for her authority. In the **Treaty of Honolulu**, negotiated in 1855, she agrees to customs reciprocity and a Californian coaling station at Honolulu while refusing Elias's wish for a permanent base at Pearl Harbor. He respects her refusal because he needs a willing partner more than another hostile port.

The relationship between the two states grows closer. Alexander remains the political ally who understands the cost of dependence; Lot remains Elias's personal friend and occasionally his most uncomfortable mirror. Victoria and Elias envisage a shared Pacific order with a common navy and trade system while preserving the governments at Honolulu and Monterey. Their negotiations will eventually produce a confederation that precedes their marriage.

Kamehameha IV marries Emma in June 1856. **[APPROVED — CR-027]** Victoria has been betrothed since childhood to her cousin William Lunalilo, a match her mother Kīnaʻu arranged and the Hawaiian people love. Her brothers block it: Lunalilo's rank, estates and following on Hawaiʻi island would give the couple's children precedence over the king's own line. In 1857 they press David Kalākaua on her instead, and she refuses. Her answer survives in two songs of her own, *Pua Hau ʻo Maleka* and *Aita ʻOe e Parau*, which defy her brothers and claim the right to love the man she chooses. All Honolulu knows the man is Elias. She turns down both suitors for him.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** The brothers come round for their own reasons. Elias's children would rank through her alone, below the king's line; he claims none of her lands, which make her one of the kingdom's great landholders; and an American who has defied Washington takes her out of the reach of the missionary party that Wyllie believed had plotted in 1854 to put her on the throne. In **November 1858**, two years after the Confederation came into force, Elias and Victoria marry at Kawaiahaʻo Church in Honolulu, the church whose choir she leads. The wedding seals a partnership already legally binding, not a bride given in exchange for it. Elias is about thirty-four and Victoria twenty. Each expects to influence the other; neither yet imagines how dangerous that expectation will become.

<!-- CR-034: Victoria's reception houses from 1859 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** Victoria takes up the matter Elias has only paid for. From 1859 she opens reception houses for women at San Francisco and Honolulu. She recruits through the missions and the British, German and eastern consulates, and she finds the women places as teachers, nurses, seamstresses and clerks. Her husband's subsidy brought women to the docks; her houses keep them in the country. She also begins to ask, in the Council and at her own table, why the women her houses train may be certified as teachers but may sit no other examination of the Republic.

## 7. The Japanese connection: rescue, reform and the treaty (1849–1860)

Elias meets Nakahama Manjirō in California around 1849–1850. Manjirō has worked in the gold fields and knows the American maritime world, its languages and ships; Elias is fascinated by his description of Japan's institutions. They become friends, and Elias helps him arrange or finance the voyage home through Honolulu. When Manjirō lands in Ryukyu in 1851 and is questioned in Satsuma, his account reaches Shimazu Nariakira, a reforming lord hungry for modern technology.

In 1851 the survivors of the Japanese *Eiriki Maru* reach San Francisco, among them the thirteen-year-old Hamada Hikozō. The boy finds shelter in Elias's household and begins forming bonds that will give him a life beyond the historical accident of his shipwreck. In 1852–1853 a Californian ship carries some of the castaways through Naha toward home. The mission sends letters and gifts by routes that Japanese authorities can accept without surrendering control of their ports.

Nariakira, already improving Satsuma's industrial workshops, sees a modest Pacific partner without missionary demands as an alternative to stronger outside powers. He opens deniable channels through Ryukyu, while Abe Masahiro and shogunal officials weigh foreign dangers, domain rivalries and the need for naval modernization. Matthew Perry's actual plans put pressure on every participant. California's diplomacy changes the course of the American expedition, but the details of Perry's altered mission remain **[OPEN]**: an empire-wide coaling embargo cannot by itself credibly explain his failure to reach Japan.

Private exchanges grow into a **Shimoda agreement in 1855**, following tentative technical arrangements made in 1854. Californian machinists visit Satsuma's Kagoshima works; Japanese shipwrights, naval students and artillery specialists train at California's yards. Coal, provisions, silk, copper, tools and mutually advantageous commercial terms flow through the developing relationship. The shogunate does not simply hand Nariakira power to sign national treaties on its behalf.

The political danger deepens when Abe Masahiro dies in 1857 and Nariakira dies in 1858. With Ii Naosuke consolidating authority and persecuting political opponents during the Ansei crisis, Californian friendship becomes entangled in dangerous Japanese factional politics. Elias has already recognized a severe imbalance between Japan's domestic gold–silver valuation and foreign bullion prices; he warns his partners and offers financial expertise instead of maximizing the profit from their vulnerability.

**In 1858, California and the Tokugawa government conclude the Treaty of Edo.** It formalizes the partnership in commerce, shipbuilding, coal, technical exchange and naval training under the new political leadership. Japanese ministers accept the arrangement because it strengthens their options against larger Western navies without openly ceding the country's sovereignty. A number of Japanese factions distrust the treaty; the agreement is therefore an achievement of diplomacy rather than an inevitable triumph of Californian charm.

In March 1860 the *Kanrin Maru* arrives in San Francisco with Manjirō serving as interpreter. The voyage is an important public demonstration of Japanese navigational ability and the practical fruits of the **1858 treaty**, not a second treaty-signing ceremony. Experienced officers, engineers and merchants meet their Californian counterparts, inspect facilities and renew commercial relationships. Japan supplies technical advisers, training, observers, markets, harbors and resources. Large Japanese expeditionary combat forces are not yet part of California's military establishment. This restraint preserves the independence of the two powers even as their fortunes become increasingly interconnected.

<!-- CR-034: the Kanrin Maru at Carquinez -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The *Kanrin Maru*'s engineers spend their days at Carquinez, among machines whose drawings they have studied, and leave asking that Japanese students be admitted to the school, not only the yards.

## 8. The constitutional test of public money and private force (1850–1861)

California's revenues invite domestic conflicts that Elias cannot solve with engineering alone. The Sacramento squatter crisis of 1850 and San Francisco's Committee of Vigilance in 1851 expose the republic's weak courts and the ability of wealthy or armed private associations to govern in place of elected institutions. President Vallejo hesitates to confront powerful merchants. Elias argues that the treasury and the law must be defended by public institutions rather than competing private armies.

In 1852 the assembly reorganizes government at Monterey, creating a Council of State and elevating Elias to **Superintendent of Mines and Public Revenue**. He controls the collection and investment of mineral royalties, the mint, public credit and important works contracts but does not become president. He administers a financial state in which taxes, railway subsidies, foreign loans and port revenues are contested by legislators who can still frustrate him.

A regular Californian army is organized in 1852, alongside an academy at Monterey. The state develops its Benicia arsenal, Mare Island naval facilities and the foundries on the Carquinez Strait. Skilled immigrants and officers from several military traditions join Californio lancers, Mexican and Indigenous scouts, seamen and engineers. Marcus Vance, a relative of Caleb who joins the republic in the aftermath of the rush, gradually develops a professional command and military training system. Elias continues to direct money more readily than he can command troops.

<!-- CR-034: from rush to settlement, 1851-1856 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The rush becomes a settlement in the years when it could have ended. Since the Australian strikes of 1851 many diggers have sailed home. Most of the others stay, because a claim, a town lot or a farm in California now comes with a registered title, and from 1852 the Superintendency pays the passage of single women with a contract of employment and of whole families. Married women keep their own property under the Mexican rules the Republic retained, and from 1854 a woman who passes the teachers' examination is certified like a man. The same year as the first assisted passages, twenty thousand Chinese land at San Francisco. By 1853 the easy gold is gone. Water companies turn hoses on the old river gravels, quartz companies sink shafts at Grass Valley, and the men who remain become wage-earners, mechanics and shareholders. **[APPROVED — CR-033]** Holders of the old grants who come forward get a surveyed patent within months and pay its cost in land, which many Californio grantees call a tithe on their own property; squatters buy their improved plots from the Land Fund on instalments.

<!-- CR-034: Elias's priority; the 1855 panic; population -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** Elias makes women's immigration his own priority: a country of single men is a camp, not a republic. In February 1855 the failure of Page, Bacon's parent house in St. Louis brings down San Francisco's leading bank and two hundred firms with it. The mint's bullion and the Superintendency's credit carry the solvent houses through, and the Republic's money keeps its value. Emigrant guides in New York, Hamburg and Canton begin to say so. California, about 350,000 people in 1850, counts nearly 900,000 with Hawaii at the end of 1856, the Confederation's first year **[MODEL]**.

<!-- CR-034: the Carquinez school, its loops and its first chairs -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** In 1852 Genth's laboratory moves to Carquinez and becomes a school beside the foundries, separate from the army's academy at Monterey. In 1853 Genth reports that the Superintendency's own quartz mill loses most of the gold locked in sulphides, and Elias prints the report. Gustav Zeuner fought on the Dresden barricades in 1849 and is barred from teaching in Saxony. He comes in 1854 on the word of his teacher Julius Weisbach to build the engineering course: mathematics first, then the testing floor and the works. The school is paid by a fixed share of the royalty, so it grows with the gold. Each chair carries two paid assistants whom the professor chooses, and Genth and Zeuner fill theirs from Philadelphia, Giessen and Freiberg. From 1854 a chair also brings citizenship, with no religious or national test, to men whom their own countries have dismissed or would never appoint. Zeuner measures the water the hydraulic companies buy by the miner's inch and the debris they wash into the rivers, and from 1855 he tests the engines built at the foundries. Andrew Hallidie, who came as a boy in 1852, brings his wire bridges and the first wire rope for the quartz mills to its testing machine.

<!-- CR-049: the first precedents and the travelling fellowships -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Elias makes Genth's mill report a precedent on purpose: an honest, unwelcome conclusion will always have a home. On Zeuner's testing floor anyone can watch the difference between a convincing account and dependable performance, and the workshop begins to mark an apprentice's first accepted piece in its book, with a meal that names the teacher. From 1854 the royalty also pays for travelling fellowships, which send gifted young people where instructions run out: to the mountains, the islands, the sea and Japan. A fellow states the undertaking before the people who pay for it, and on return the records, accounts and testimony come before any honour; failed ventures and lost companions are remembered by name. The qualifying examinations still decide who may compete, but among those who have passed them, a fellow who brings back something no examination could test outranks the examination's top scorer for honours and selection. **[OPEN]** The expedition episodes themselves, which must carry real danger.

Throughout the 1850s, California and then Pacifica accumulate a mutually reinforcing community of engineers, manufacturers, financiers and skilled workers. Marcus's prewar recruitment and procurement relationships connect workshops with foreign suppliers and technical talent; public contracts, private capital, apprenticeships and established businesses give the network continuity before war interrupts its overseas connections. John Ericsson forms a central naval-engineering connection within this community. Theodore Judah, Andrew Smith Hallidie, Peter Donahue, Collis P. Huntington, Charles Crocker, William Chapman Ralston, William Kelly, Alexander Lyman Holley and Yung Wing participate in its development through settlement, employment, investment or sustained exchange. Ericsson's path and mentorship and seven collaborators' complementary industrial roles are developed below. **[OPEN]** Exact recruitment, arrival, residence, citizenship, contracts and prominence as distinct characters; Judah and Yung Wing remain included in principle without separately settled undertakings. Participation does not imply that all work together in one place or share identical political commitments.

Their combined influence compounds over years. Better tools and foundry practice improve engines and repair capacity; more reliable machinery expands mining, shipping and transport; growing revenues and commercial confidence support further investment, experiments and training. Successful workshops produce skilled teams and new enterprises, while failed prototypes leave useful measurements, techniques and experienced workers. Logan Turner, a boy from a Kentucky holler who reaches California as an adolescent, belongs to a younger generation educated within this developing community. Early familiarity with farm repairs, mills and river transport gives way to Carquinez apprenticeship and technical instruction; his exact migration and training dates remain **[OPEN]**. His practical discoveries arise from work with other skilled people. Pacifica's stunning rise rests partly on this expanding ability to design, manufacture, maintain and reproduce complex systems. Technical progress remains uneven across industries: capital, materials, fuel, transport and labor constrain what even an exceptional group can accomplish. Private interests and overseas partners retain their own purposes. Manufacturers own their works and can enforce contracts, yet increasingly depend on state orders; railway investors rely on guarantees and rights of way; officers and engineers build careers in expanding public establishments. Their successes create a constituency for the continuity of Elias's program. Some oppose his politics, others accept stronger administration to protect their undertakings, and some correctly warn that public dependence is becoming personal dependence.

Ericsson comes to California around 1855, initially on a paid commercial inspection and experimental assignment arranged through Marcus's eastern purchasing relationships. Disappointment with his large caloric ship and the failure to obtain construction of his 1854 armored-warship proposal make a new patron attractive without erasing his American attachments. Elias offers an experimental workshop associated with existing foundry capacity, protected commercial rights and paid trials for mining machinery and marine propulsion. Ericsson retains eastern commercial relationships, including Delamater's. Their confidence develops through demanding technical discussion, measured results and funding that survives an expensive failure. **[OPEN]** Exact invitation, intermediary, visit dates, contracts and first-meeting scene.

The visit becomes a sustained commitment during the working 1856–1858 period. Ericsson improves engines, pumps, screw propulsion, testing and the training of engineers and machinists. Small caloric applications remain separate from dependable steam propulsion for warships. During 1859–1860 on the working calendar, an experimental harbor-defense programme tests compact machinery, turret rotation, gun handling, armor arrangements and ventilation. Imported iron, guns and selected machinery supplement developing domestic production. The first fighting turret ship remains unfinished when war begins. Washington can observe this activity and develop competing designs; the programme is neither magically secret nor an instant ocean-going armored fleet. **[OPEN]** Facilities, budgets, suppliers, armament, dimensions and construction schedule.

Logan's adolescent migration places him within this community during its formative years. Donahue's workshops provide a proposed apprenticeship route into Ericsson's programme. Ericsson teaches him precision and the integration of a whole vessel; Logan learns from crews and repair workers how machinery behaves with poor fuel, wear and imperfect operation. Their respect includes recurring disagreement over how much a design may assume correct manufacture and handling. Logan leaves with the small eastern technical mission in 1861 on the working chronology, before the Columbia vessel enters combat. Ericsson remains in Pacifica when war forces a choice among his established commitments, supporting its naval defense while resisting automatic conversion of every technical undertaking into Confederate aid. American ties and the terms of his Pacifican residence remain consequential. **[OPEN]** Logan's precise apprenticeship and departure, Ericsson's legal nationality and wartime contractual limits.

The industrial community's work develops through three connected undertakings. First, Donahue builds manufacturing and repair capacity while Huntington organizes purchases and investment, allowing Ericsson's experimental naval design to reach production. Procurement begins before war restricts eastern suppliers: machine tools, suitable iron, selected machinery and trained people must be acquired while commercial access remains possible. Donahue bargains over workshop capacity and delivery dates; Huntington weighs availability, price and competing orders. **[OPEN]** Specific companies, contracts, imports, workforce, recruitment dates and the allocation of scarce equipment. Their historical Californian careers supply foundations for a different development programme rather than representing a wholesale subtraction of eastern industry.

Kelly's metallurgical experiments begin a slower undertaking whose greatest effects emerge after the war. Sustained patronage allows imperfect trials to produce measurements, process knowledge and trained workers before dependable large-scale steel production is available. Holley contributes comparative engineering and assessment of foreign manufacturing and armor, then develops the means to turn process knowledge into reliable plant operation. Neither man's presence settles the chemistry, patents, raw materials, fuel, rolling capacity or economic viability of a Pacifican steel industry. **[OPEN]** Arrival, consulting arrangements, experimental works and sequence of technical successes.

Throughout the 1850s, California's sovereignty changes the destination and uses of its gold. Public royalties and reserves support its own credit, workshops and infrastructure; private bullion belongs to miners, merchants and investors who trade across borders. Pacifican banks and purchasing relationships increasingly connect mineral production with domestic industry and overseas suppliers rather than providing an assured stream into the American financial system. This develops before the Civil War and contributes to Pacifica's accumulated productive advantage. **[OPEN]** Annual flows, reserve policy, private capital movements, banking arrangements and the scale of the difference from historical eastern access.

The **Defense Law of 1855** establishes broad compulsory military service inspired by European, particularly Prussian, systems. Pacifist sects perform alternative service in an irrigation and engineering corps, carrying religious liberty into a demanding public obligation. Young men spend time with the colors and remain liable for reserve and territorial service. An emerging general staff plans movements by rail, river and telegraph. The new forces create a state considerably more capable than the California of 1846, although it remains dependent on foreign armor plate, coal, expensive heavy guns and credit.

<!-- CR-034: the engineer-corps privilege -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** Students and graduates of the Carquinez school serve their time in the engineer corps, building the roads, bridges and telegraph the general staff needs. Families who would never send a son to the ranks compete to send him to the school's examination.

As naval requirements grow, the two Pacific governments negotiate the next stage of their partnership. By late 1855 President Vallejo names Elias **First Minister**, head of the Californian Council of State, without transferring the presidency. Victoria, as Hawaiian Kuhina Nui, and Foreign Minister Robert Wyllie negotiate with him and other Californian envoys over customs, defense, naval command and the limits of shared authority. The controversy surrounding their increasingly public courtship makes every concession a question of both sovereignty and family.

In May and June 1856, while Elias is occupied by negotiations in Honolulu, San Francisco's **second Committee of Vigilance** challenges the republic's authority on a much larger scale than the 1851 movement. Its leaders portray the confederation as Elias's plan to place California under a combined personal dictatorship. William T. Sherman, then active in Californian banking and militia affairs, sides with constitutional government. Elias returns, rallies regular forces and loyal citizens, and breaks the vigilantes' claim to extralegal authority. He has now used organized force against Californians in defense of laws whose administration he increasingly dominates. Sherman remains in California after banking work ends around 1857, accepting an institutional military appointment under Vallejo and choosing Pacifican citizenship before the war. **[OPEN]** Exact appointment, naturalization and household decisions. He supports Victoria's restraint at Washoe in 1860. His relationship with Elias becomes one of professional respect and disagreement within Pacifica.

At Honolulu on **31 July 1856**, the anniversary of the restoration of Hawaiian sovereignty after the British seizure of 1843, the two governments declare the **Confederation of Pacifica**. Kamehameha IV, Kuhina Nui Victoria and Robert Wyllie sign for Hawaii; President Vallejo and First Minister Elias Thorne for California. The British consul witnesses, and a young Japanese observer attends through the developing Satsuma connection. The naming date is purposeful: the new polity presents itself as a protector of Pacific sovereignty, not a mechanism for erasing its members' autonomy.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** At the ceremony Victoria leads the Kawaiahaʻo choir. Late that night, among the court, Elias hears her chant a mele of her own composition. He has known her since she was thirteen; from that night her voice stays with him for the rest of his life.

California's assembly ratifies the Articles in September, narrowly; Hawaii's legislature ratifies in November, bringing the Confederation into force. California and the Hawaiian Kingdom retain domestic self-government and residual powers. The common institutions control defense, the navy, customs, currency and external relations. The equal member delegations of the Confederal Council elect the Chancellor, and Elias takes that office. The Confederal Assembly is elected by the property-holding men of the member electorates. California’s president and Hawaii’s king remain their states’ heads; there is no common head of state. The Council and its offices sit at Monterey, with a resident Hawaiian delegation; Honolulu remains the Hawaiian seat. Confederal acts are published in English, Spanish and Hawaiian. California retains English–Spanish guarantees, Hawaii its Hawaiian-language institutions. Japan remains a treaty partner.

<!-- CR-038: Hawaii abolishes penal labour contracts on joining the Confederation -->
**[APPROVED — CR-038]** Hawaii pays a price of its own for union. A republic that punishes no Californian for leaving his employer will not share a common market with plantations that jail Chinese labourers for quitting theirs. Over the planters' protests, the kingdom's legislature abolishes penal labour contracts in its ratifying session. From then on a plantation contract buys inspected housing, a protected wage and the passage home, and at the end of its term the worker may take his labour wherever he likes.

The Articles include an emergency **Article XIV**. During war or grave danger the Council may appoint a Protector by a two-thirds vote with assent formally recorded by both member governments through their authorized constitutional procedures. The one-year mandate brings joint military command, common customs administration and temporary emergency decrees, including specified restrictions on rights when necessary to restore public safety. The elected Assembly must be informed promptly and can revoke emergency measures; renewal requires both member legislatures. The initial mandate cannot amend constitutions or annex territory. Assent belongs to governments acting through institutions; no confederal article assigns the Kuhina Nui an exclusive personal countersignature. The limited mandate and member assent distinguish legal emergency authority from annexation or constitutional amendment.

A separate dissolution provision allows the Protector, acting with cabinet responsibility, to dissolve the elected Confederal Assembly and appeal to the electorate. An election must follow within sixty days, and dissolution may occur only once for the same cause. Member legislatures and the state Council remain in place. Dissolution does not revive a decree already revoked. Its defenders see an appeal to citizens against legislative deadlock; critics see a way to remove the body charged with checking emergency government. Cabinet responsibility does not give a single Hawaiian officer a personal confederal veto.

Constitutional change follows a distinct route: two-thirds approval in both confederal chambers and ratification by both founding states. Each provision has defenders who expect the others to contain it. Victoria and Wyllie secure Hawaiian representation and state assent, while Elias argues that a small confederation must act quickly enough to survive. None yet knows which interests future emergencies will mobilize.

They marry in November 1858, with the treaty two years in force. Their private alliance and the political Confederation begin under compatible ideals but contain different assumptions about who may say no when strategic necessity becomes urgent.

The external crises of the following years test those safeguards. During the **Utah War of 1857–1858**, federal troop movements near the border and the murders at Mountain Meadows expose Pacifica's inability to deploy a brigade quickly across the Sierra. **[PROPOSED]** Elias keeps a discreet channel through San Bernardino's Mormon leaders, preserving their land titles after Brigham Young's recall of the colony. He offers trade rather than military protection. The emigrants murdered at Mountain Meadows were bound for California; public hostility ensures that any later Mormon bargain will carry a political price. In 1858 the Fraser River gold rush draws workers north and Britain's establishment of British Columbia introduces a neighboring colonial administration whose interests do not always coincide with Californian shipping.

<!-- CR-034: the prestige year 1858 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** In 1858 the Carquinez school publishes two results that travel, in the first number of its *Mittheilungen*, sent in German and English to every technical library in Europe. Zeuner's method for slide-valve gear, worked out on Ericsson's engines, will set the valves of the harbor-defense engines. Genth, analysing quartz from Carson Hill, identifies a gold telluride that ordinary milling cannot touch and shows that roasting and chlorine recover it, and the Superintendency's mill that he shamed in 1853 now pays its royalty. Freiberg's journals carry both. The same number sets the school's first prize questions, with prizes in gold, and the answers come from men who have not yet decided to sail. Behind them come the engineers laid off in the panic of 1857, which the Republic's money has outlasted. The school now has about a dozen teachers and 120 students, and by 1862 twice as many **[MODEL]**. By 1860 it calls itself the Polytechnic, students apply from Europe, and engineers who have never seen the Pacific know where Carquinez is. It takes clever children at fourteen if they pass its examination, whatever their family; in 1861 one of them is Rafael Soberanes of Monterey. **[OPEN]** Contracts, salaries, buildings and the order of later appointments.

<!-- CR-036: scholars Europe will not promote: Sylvester, Remak, Cohn; Michelson as a boy -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** The offer of a chair with citizenship reaches men Europe will not let graduate or advance. James Joseph Sylvester came second in Cambridge's mathematics examination and could not take his degree, because he would not sign the Church of England's articles. In 1855, after ten years as an actuary, he is offered a professorship at Woolwich and a chair at Carquinez with citizenship, and he sails. Robert Remak showed that the embryo grows from three layers and that cells arise by dividing. He was the first Jew allowed to teach at Berlin, but never as its professor. In 1858 he takes the chair of physiology and pathology at the Methodists' new medical college in San Francisco. Ferdinand Cohn, kept waiting a year at Breslau before he might take the oath to teach, comes to the same college that year to study the smallest living things. At the gold camp at Murphys a Prussian Jewish merchant's son, Albert Michelson, is learning to read.

<!-- CR-049: the Calculation Office, the printing calculator and Peirce in Japan -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1855 Sylvester proposes the Calculation Office. Ten years as an actuary have shown him what a calculating staff can do for navigation, surveying, insurance and engineering, and he brings London's mathematics with him: Babbage's mechanical notation of 1826, and Menabrea's account of the Analytical Engine with Lovelace's notes of 1842–1843. In 1856 Carquinez commissions a Scheutz-type printing calculator from Stockholm, a year before the Dudley Observatory at Albany and the General Register Office in London buy theirs, and the race for the tables is on. Installing and adapting it takes 1858–1860; it computes particular tables well and nothing else, which is the Office's lesson about machines. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** In the spring of 1856 Sylvester offers Charles Sanders Peirce, the brilliant and difficult son of Harvard's mathematician, whose examination record is undistinguished, a place where achievement outranks rank, and the boy comes west at sixteen. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** He is the Office's youngest computer, and he reads Boole's *Laws of Thought*. In the autumn, after his seventeenth birthday, he sails with the Japanese partnership as the youngest fellow (1856–1857) to compare measures and calculation practice in the cooperating workshops. At the Dutch-run naval school at Nagasaki his hosts test him as hard as he tests them and keep the better of his methods, and he learns how much of a numerical account rests on assumptions nobody wrote down. He accounts for his work before he is honoured, and returning fellows become the school's admired examples. **[OPEN]** The journey's dangers.

<!-- CR-035: the Pacifica League: grants per examined graduate, the colleges' bargain, women from 1862 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-035]** From 1855 the Republic pays a grant for every graduate who passes its examinations in the fields it needs. The mint, the Superintendency and the licensed professions take only examined candidates, and the engineer corps only examined men. The Jesuits of Santa Clara are the first to bargain. They want the grant and the corps privilege for their Californio boys; the Republic wants surveyors and engineers who can work in Spanish. The price is the Carquinez examiners in their halls. The bishop calls it simony from the pulpit, and the rector signs. From 1859 women may sit the state examinations, and when in 1862 the grant also requires the colleges to admit the women who pass, the Jesuits hold out a year longer than anyone. The Methodists' college opens a laboratory medical school in San Francisco in 1858, and the Oakland college trains the Superintendency's statisticians. The Hawaiian kingdom pays Oahu College on the same terms for navigators and Pacific linguists for the common navy. Each college keeps its chapel and its Latin; none is paid for them. By 1860 the newspapers call them the League.

<!-- CR-049: the Laboratory of Light, Electricity and Matter; Kate Layne and uranium -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1859 Genth founds the Laboratory of Light, Electricity and Matter inside the school, with photographic recording, mineral analysis and electrical measurement for its practical work. The challenge evening begins there as a laboratory habit: a researcher sets out what is established, what is conjecture and what would settle it, and an appointed respondent and the room attack it, the people who built the apparatus and ran the calculation included. A conjecture earns a trial before it earns belief. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** In 1860 Kate Layne of Folsom, born in Tennessee, passes the Republic's examination at fifteen, one of the first women to do so, and joins the laboratory's recording staff. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Niépce de Saint-Victor has reported in the *Comptes rendus* that uranium salts darken photographic plates in the dark, and explained it as stored light; nobody has followed it up. The laboratory's cabinet holds Joachimsthal pitchblende and the uranium yellows the Austrian state's colour works there has made since 1853. Kate asks Genth to let her try. She tests the effect against darkness, black paper, months of storage and every uranium compound the school has, and adds a gold-leaf electroscope, which discharges near uranium at a rate that follows the uranium content. In 1861 Genth publishes that the activity belongs to uranium itself, under her name and his. By 1864 thorium is found active too, Peirce has fitted the electroscope's readings, and pitchblende proves several times more active than its uranium can explain. Something else is in the ore.

The Pike's Peak rush establishes new mining districts around Denver, often through men accustomed to California's mining courts. The discovery of the Comstock lode in Washoe in 1859 is especially provocative: the silver lies on the American side of the carefully negotiated 1850 border, but much of the credit, capital and workforce comes from San Francisco. One group of miners demands annexation to Pacifica; others prefer an American territorial government.

<!-- CR-034: Ott's first Washoe assay -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-034]** The first assay of the Washoe ore is made at Nevada City by James J. Ott, an assayer the Republic licensed, and San Francisco's money moves before Washington's officials arrive.

The decisive warning comes in **1860**. The killing of miners who had abducted Paiute women leads to retaliatory violence and the Pyramid Lake campaign. After Numaga's warriors defeat a volunteer column under William Ormsby, Elias recommends negotiations over the border and disputed claims but agrees to an armed relief force to retain support for the naval program. Jack Hays commands some five hundred Californian volunteers across the boundary and defeats Paiute forces. The immediate tactical result cannot erase that Pacifican troops have operated inside territory claimed by the United States, with serious consequences for Paiute communities and the legitimacy of California's stated principles.

Washington threatens retaliation, dispatching troops and naval forces; Britain refuses to support a war of Californian expansion, and the London bondholders warn that their credit was not extended to finance such adventures. The Council debates activating Article XIV. **Victoria organizes opposition in the Hawaiian government and delegation**, supported by Wyllie, legislators and military critics including Sherman. She argues that a confederation formed to preserve sovereignty cannot treat an unfavorable boundary as an invitation to war. Hawaii withholds the governmental assent needed for Article XIV, and the coalition defeats escalation through the existing institutions. Elias must accept his first unambiguous constitutional defeat; Victoria has won by persuasion and political organization.

At the **Carson Convention** in autumn 1860, Pacifica withdraws its troops and recognizes American sovereignty over Washoe in exchange for property guarantees and transit rights. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** The convention also renews, for a further term, the commercial articles of 1848, which had run out that year: the ports, the Sausalito storehouse, commerce and the rights of American residents. The crisis becomes a formative wound for Elias: his financial and military power does not suffice when his own constitution allows an organized coalition to block him. It also makes him respect, and increasingly resent, the wife whose judgment has frustrated his plans.

The government embarks on intensified preparations for future emergencies. By Fort Sumter in April 1861 Pacifica has a regular army and trained reserves, unfinished Sierra railway works, telegraph lines toward Carson, a Colorado River flotilla, imported heavy artillery and some armored warships. The country can mobilize far more effectively than in 1846 but cannot match the Union's peak military power. Its young army has limited experience of extended large-scale war, and its navy is dangerously dependent on foreign fuels, engines and plate. The precise force totals are recorded as scenario estimates in the historical and planning files rather than asserted here as established fact.

<!-- CR-033: the Confederation in 1861; Native villages; scouts -->
**[APPROVED — CR-033]** By 1861 the Confederation counts about 1.2 million people **[MODEL]**. Pacific shipping has brought Australians and Chileans as well as Americans moving west, and more miners have bought land and stayed. Measles, smallpox and malaria meanwhile sweep the Native villages of California. **[PROPOSED]** From 1861 men of the treaty nations scout and guard the Sierra routes and the telegraph.

# PART II — The struggle for North America (1861–1867)

## 9. The republic faces the American Civil War (1861)

Fort Sumter's bombardment in April 1861 transforms the Confederation's strategic position. California and Hawaii have survived as separate states within Pacifica, but their shared customs, fleet and communications are now exposed to a continental war. Washington remembers the Washoe incident as a violation of American territory; California remembers American threats to its independence. **[PROPOSED — CR-028]** Secession also removes the Southern bloc whose votes had made every proposal against California a sectional impossibility since 1848. For the first time, the men who call 1848 the Trist surrender sit in a Congress the South no longer balances. **[APPROVED — CR-028]** When war comes, the American naval storehouse at Sausalito is sealed and its stores interned. Southern agents solicit bullion, shipbuilding and recognition, while Northern representatives ask the confederal government to supply or finance federal armies. The republic's American-born inhabitants carry divided loyalties into its legislative and military institutions.

The loss of dependable access to Californian gold and Pacifican credit becomes a continuing weakness in the Union's war finances. Once Pacifica is an enemy, Washington cannot count on its government's reserves or assistance, and established commercial channels are disrupted. Gold can still reach Northern buyers through private and third-country transactions, including London; access costs more and depends on exchange, credit and confidence. Northern taxes, bond sales and paper currency continue to finance armies and industry, while specie obligations and overseas purchases keep demand for coin and foreign settlement alive. Under the working arrangement analogous to historical wartime finance, customs duties and public-debt interest remain payable in coin. **[OPEN]** Alternate financial legislation, bullion and exchange flows, borrowing terms, price effects and the quantitative burden.

The Confederate states' secession and Union mobilization do not create an effortless opportunity for Elias. The United States retains formidable armies, railways, factories, experienced officers and the capacity to raise forces far beyond anything Pacifica can sustain alone. In the Confederation, an army of around twenty thousand regulars with approximately ninety thousand trained reservists is a planning estimate, not an invincible host. Naval purchases and equipment still depend on foreign credit and supplies; the Sierra railway remains incomplete. The two American belligerents each have able commanders who learn from their enemy's methods.

A Union captain intercepts a Hawaiian packet outside American territorial waters, removes Hawaiian subjects and a suspected Confederate privateering agent, and sends the detainees to Vancouver. A Hawaiian sailor dies during the boarding. The intelligence has substance, but the officer exceeds his authority. Victoria initiates demands for protection and redress, requiring disclosure of Elias's preliminary Southern dealings. The Council invokes Article XIV with formal assent from both member governments and appoints Elias Protector for one year. Victoria helps win Hawaiian support and takes political responsibility for the authorization; Vallejo supports California's assent. A separate resolution authorizes limited coercive operations, with civilian protections, no forced allegiance, no annexation by emergency decree and renewed negotiations when adequate redress becomes possible. **[OPEN]** Exact participants, instruments and dates; late May is the working activation date.

Demands and attempted interim arrangements precede a July offensive on the working calendar. Washington eventually offers a route to release and investigation in exchange for withdrawal. Elias demands broader strategic guarantees and protection for collaborators; a limited crisis becomes a territorial war through choices rather than inevitability. Victoria's advocacy implicates her without eliminating political opposition or conditions on renewal. Sherman organizes readiness and reserves in the proposed office of Inspector-General, initially supporting protection while challenging the expanding objectives.

## 10. The Northwestern campaign and occupation (1861–1862)

Marcus Vance commands the expedition while Thorne directs its political aims. Sea transport and a limited overland advance from northern California support operations without a completed Sierra crossing. Federal defenses strengthened after Washoe are dispersed among the coast and interior. George Wright and Silas Casey preserve competent opposition. Pacifica takes Astoria, Portland and Vancouver on a working July–September calendar; Wright evacuates mobile troops and usable stores toward the upper Columbia. Olympia and the abandoned establishment at Steilacoom fall during autumn. Control beyond major settlements and routes remains uneven.

Casey defeats a Pacifican landing against Port Townsend. Marcus had reservations about support but approved the operation and accepts responsibility. Union troops preserve a northern anchorage and interior concentration, giving later reinforcements somewhere to arrive. Sherman inspects reconnaissance, command, naval cooperation and casualty recovery; he criticizes actual failures while rejecting political scapegoating of Marcus for government objectives. His findings give Victoria evidence for renewal conditions. **[OPEN]** Forces, losses, precise positions, alternate appointments and operational schedules require audit.

John Whiteaker negotiates civil protections without conceding a governor's right to transfer Oregon's allegiance. Unionists retain competing authorities; commercial accommodationists and Southern sympathizers have distinct interests. Washington Territory's federal administration withdraws to the surviving enclave. Indigenous nations make separate decisions about passage, supplies, land and protection; settler petitions cannot speak for them. The occupation develops costly policing, garrison and supply obligations. Recognition and later association require peace and constitutional agreements, not emergency annexation. **[OPEN]** Local officeholders, succession, compacts, boundaries and accession dates.

Washington sends a limited expedition to hold its foothold and support recovery. Candidate changed assignments are the 11th and 13th Iowa, followed as organization permits by the 15th and 16th, transported through Panama to the Sound. Lancaster and a Pacific consort protect communications; Pensacola is the candidate additional seagoing diversion from the historical Gulf assignment. **[OPEN]** These are planned allocations, not audited strengths or sailing schedules. Retained Pacific units are not counted again as newly diverted Eastern forces. The surviving federal enclave ties down Pacifican troops during winter and spring 1862.

The first operational Ericsson turret ship helps secure the lower Columbia during a federal recovery attempt in late spring or summer 1862 on the working timetable. Its difficult machinery and armor sections are manufactured or procured through California's industrial network, then shipped aboard conventional transports for assembly at a protected lower-river yard after the 1861 conquest. Donahue organizes difficult manufacturing, installation and repair arrangements, drawing on workshop teams; Huntington helps secure the equipment and materials on which the programme depends. Imported specialists and local shipwrights share the work. This avoids depending on a low-freeboard prototype making a hazardous coastal passage and bar crossing under its own power. **[OPEN]** Yard, hull construction, cranes, skilled labor, transport, material supply, exact completion date and vessel name; the assembly scheme requires a capacity audit.

Inside sheltered estuarine waters, the armored battery contests a passage and landing operation that Pacifican wooden vessels cannot safely hold alone. Its resistance to ordinary gunfire and heavy turret guns disrupt federal naval support and damage a leading vessel. Mechanical trouble or battle damage limits its own action, making supporting vessels, pilots, crews and Marcus's troops essential. The federal commander withdraws vulnerable transports rather than persist in an inadequately protected landing. Pacifica retains its lower-river supply route and gains time to consolidate. Union forces preserve a surviving fleet, northern foothold and capacity to adapt through shore batteries, obstructions and combined operations. The ironclad cannot patrol the entire coast, occupy territory or reverse the earlier Townsend defeat. **[OPEN]** Commanders, ships, location, tactical sequence, malfunction, losses and relationship to the audited reinforcement schedule.

The second undertaking sustains the gains through finance and transport. Ralston's commercial relationships help bridge the gap between future public revenues and immediate payments to suppliers, workers and carriers. Crocker develops construction teams able to repair track, build wharves and establish short connecting or portage routes; he cannot promise a completed mountain railway wherever the army advances. Hallidie's wire rope, lifting equipment and bridge experience improve material handling and vulnerable supply connections. **[OPEN]** Particular advances, security, interest, payment crises, routes, crossings and dates; Ralston's historical 1864 Bank of California must not be silently placed in the early war. An emergency advance, restored crossing or critical equipment shipment remains a possible incident rather than a newly fixed battle outcome. Skilled workers, including Chinese construction crews, retain agency over labor conditions and participation.

Southwestern and interior expansion follows the Union's wartime distraction and Pacifica's supply routes. Deseret accedes as a confederate state in late 1862–1863, extending the religious freedom promised to all. **[PROPOSED]** The Morrill Act supplies the breaking point, and the accession protects member-state marriage and church-property law while placing its militia within the Defense Law. Brigham Young surrenders John D. Lee in 1863; Pacifica tries and hangs him for Mountain Meadows. Young obtains protection at a price he resents. Confederal law forbids member states from banning marriage on grounds of race or origin; it does not impose one marriage system on every state.

**[PROPOSED]** A Pacifican California column establishes control of Arizona and New Mexico in 1862; Richmond renounces its Arizona claim in return for credit and the future railway connection. **[PROPOSED — CR-022]** Before the renunciation the column drives Baylor's Texans from Mesilla in a brief clash both governments prefer to forget. Among the Texan dead is Captain Henry Pruett of San Antonio. Idaho's mining districts turn toward the Northwest in 1863, western Montana's camps toward Salt Lake and Walla Walla in 1863–1864. Washoe is retaken in 1863–1864. Washington still admits Nevada in October 1864 to claim its electoral votes, then loses it in the peace. Exact forces, routes, accession instruments and the new members' representation remain **[OPEN]**; civilian and Native authority require their own agreements.

Bear River becomes a Pacifican atrocity in January 1863 under a local commander. In Navajo country the government chooses a different course: a treaty recognizes a homeland and avoids the Long Walk. **[PROPOSED]** Barboncito and Manuelito negotiate that settlement; an Apache peace in 1865 combines homeland reservations, paid scouts, honest agents and prosecution of settler massacres, followed by a Mexican convention ending scalp bounties. Pacifica abolishes New Mexico's debt peonage and Native captive trade gradually with compensation by 1866, facing a short Hispano revolt. Religious toleration and civic protection demand actions that cost the new state support among its own settlers.

## 11. The three-sided war and its terrible costs (1862–1865)

At Hampton Roads in March 1862, the absence of a timely Union turret ship turns Virginia's attack into a more substantial Confederate victory. Cumberland and Congress are destroyed; the loss of the grounded Minnesota, by enemy action or evacuation and burning, is the working additional outcome. Federal wooden ships withdraw from exposed positions, and transport protection and the Peninsula campaign become more difficult. Virginia gains a period of local advantage while remaining constrained by machinery, deep draft and dependence on her base. Union pressure against Norfolk can eventually make her position untenable. **[OPEN]** The exact March sequence, Minnesota's fate, duration of Confederate advantage, Norfolk operations and Virginia's disposal; the historical May destruction is an anchor rather than a fixed alternate date.

Union shipbuilding responds through other designers, yards and types. Galena and New Ironsides provide alternatives to Ericsson's design, while Pook and Eads sustain the distinct western river-ironclad programme. The Union loses the historical Monitor's timely intervention and the same rapid sequence of combat validation and Ericsson-derived production. It retains an industrial economy capable of replacement designs and learning. The difficulty lies in delivering suitable armored ships to the right theatre in time. The western fleet remains capable of supporting Grant and Porter's eventual capture of Vicksburg. **[OPEN]** Alternate orders, construction dates, technical adaptations and allocations; future turret ships are not excluded indefinitely.

Confederate commanders attack Grant at Shiloh on 5 April 1862 on the working timetable, gaining a day through earlier movement rather than an unexplained long delay to Buell. Diverted reinforcements affect the balance. Grant preserves his river position with artillery, difficult terrain, gunboats and eventual reinforcements but cannot mount the historical second-day recovery. Johnston dies during the first day in the working outcome; Beauregard accepts a costly operational respite and withdraws as Buell approaches. **[OPEN]** Specific Union command functions must be reassigned because Sherman is Pacifican; force calculations, losses and the duration of the Corinth delay require reconciliation.

Farragut attempts New Orleans in spring 1862 and suffers an initial failure. His wooden fleet remains formidable; its historical breakthrough did not depend on Monitor. Pacifican credit, procurement and a small technical mission help Confederate officers and workers maintain and coordinate defenses. Logan brings testing, machinery and gun-handling lessons learned with Ericsson. Better placement and usable gun mountings for the unfinished Louisiana, coordinated with a maintained obstruction and defensive fire, form the working mechanism: the passage delays and disorders the Union column, concentrated fire damages leading ships, and insufficient force gets through to exploit a partial penetration. Louisiana does not acquire magically perfected propulsion. Confederate officers authorize and execute the defense; Logan's practical work and accurate reports earn Thorne's attention. Northwestern diversion and Atlantic alarm constrain available support and subsequent reinforcement, without removing ships already with Farragut by assertion. Farragut preserves a dangerous surviving squadron; the Gulf blockade continues. New Orleans remains Confederate. The Union occupies the evacuated Pensacola navy yard in May 1862; Pensacola, Ship Island and Key West sustain the Gulf blockade. Pacifican supplies and credit travel around Cape Horn to neutral Matamoros and overland into Texas, under the working supply plan. Without a Union New Orleans base there is no Red River campaign in 1864. **[OPEN]** Exact obstruction, ship placement, local repairs, command coordination, partial passage, losses, aid authority, allocations and detailed losses. No further Union attempt on New Orleans occurs before 1870.

Sherman refuses an assignment sustaining Confederate armies. After stabilizing the Northwest and transferring command to a deputy, Marcus accepts and leaves late in 1862 with a small staff. He meets Logan in a New Orleans repair yard in early 1863. At Vicksburg they disagree over a concentrated productive workshop versus distributed, movable capacity and agree to imperfect contingency preparations. Their mission has limited authority and resources; Confederate commanders retain command. **[OPEN]** Alliance terms, deputy, staff, travel and formal responsibilities.

Grant and Porter bypass the Vicksburg defenses, cross downstream and threaten inland communications. Richmond authorizes preservation of the mobile army before encirclement. Pemberton begins a costly withdrawal while routes remain available. Marcus assists Confederate staff with engineering and transport; Union pursuit closes an intended route. Logan destroys much of his workshop and preserves only portable tools, knowledge and some components. Workers decide independently whether to accompany the retreat. Grant captures the city, matériel and prisoners; much of the mobile Confederate army survives. Port Hudson and New Orleans preserve a contested lower-river connection, subject to Union naval disruption. **[OPEN]** Dates, routes, commands, numbers and subsequent campaigns; the historical sequence is not repeated unchanged before adding an escape.

Marcus and Logan rebuild capacity in Confederate-held Louisiana. Marcus returns to Pacifica in late 1863–early 1864; Logan stays to lead the reduced technical mission and gains independent procurement responsibility and direct access to Thorne. His relationships create commercial potential, not a predetermined fortune. Sherman confronts Marcus with depleted reserves and occupation costs; Marcus argues from preparations that prevented greater losses. Both can oppose an unprepared operation while disagreeing about the peace Pacifica should seek.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** Early in 1863 Elias goes to Texas in person to settle the Arizona renunciation and the Matamoros supply terms. Caleb insists that they carry Pruett's watch to his widow together. Abigail opens the door; behind her stands an eleven-year-old with Elias's build. Elias learns he has a son in the week the boy learns who his father is, from the men whose soldiers killed the father who raised him. She will not come to California and takes money only for the boy. She asks for the name, and Elias refuses it.

<!-- CR-012: Confederate characterization and primary-source framework. -->

Jefferson Davis defends an independent government's authority to bind agreements, while Judah Benjamin weighs what that government can still protect when its armies and households require relief. Davis understands that accepting dependence can preserve an administration while extinguishing the sovereignty it exists to defend. Benjamin understands that refusing concessions can preserve a claim while destroying the institutions and people that give it substance. Both serve a state committed to slavery, and their language of protection and consent excludes people whose subjection sustains that state.

Major Julian St. Clair carries an understanding of honor in which a pledge survives the immediate advantage that produced it. Payment alone cannot discharge every political obligation. His claim on others exposes him to the same test: what he will sacrifice for an inconvenient promise or a person entrusted to him. Among Confederate fighters, concern for home, confidence in a commander, attachment to comrades and allegiance to the government pull in different directions. A withdrawal can preserve an army while abandoning its soldiers' own households. These competing loyalties give Elias leverage without making resistance irrational. **[OPEN]** St. Clair's early affiliation, the particular undertaking and his injury chronology remain to be reconciled with the campaigns.

The consequences of the altered armored fleet continue along the Atlantic and Gulf coasts. A Drewry's Bluff expedition and the 1863 Charleston assault require different timing or composition; the historical failures do not become additional Confederate victories merely because the same attacks are not mounted. The rapid Union capture of Atlanta at Wassaw Sound cannot be assumed without an equivalent to the historical Weehawken. **[OPEN]** Whether these operations occur, alternative ships, outcomes and the wider blockade effects.

At Mobile, Eads's river monitors remain trapped above Confederate Port Hudson. The Union's Atlantic yards can build broadside alternatives, but the fleet cannot spare sufficient seagoing armor before the election. Farragut waits at Pensacola; the historical August 1864 attack is delayed. **[PROPOSED]** Logan's reduced mission helps repair Tennessee's exposed steering and jamming gunports while leaving her slow engines as a real limit. Mobile's rail connections help sustain Johnston at Atlanta.

In December 1864–January 1865 the Union assembles a seaborne force of broadside ironclads of the New Ironsides type and troops at Pensacola. The February attack fails. A lead armored ship strikes Logan's denser mine field and sinks; the column breaks under Fort Morgan's fire, and Buchanan's Tennessee engages. Farragut is killed. His surviving squadron withdraws with heavy losses and re-embarks the Dauphin Island troops. The order to ignore mines becomes the symbol of a lost fleet. Farragut's martyrdom later serves Grant's revanchism, while Logan carries the knowledge of whose death his work enabled.

The paired failures at Mobile and Petersburg close Lincoln's last winter offensive. **[PROPOSED]** Johnston, retained instead of replaced by Hood, has held Atlanta past the election; the western Union armies advance without Sherman. Exact Eastern assignments, campaign calculations and casualty totals remain **[OPEN]**. Pacifica's influence comes through a second front, finance, specialist work and decisions by competent opponents, rather than a Pacifican field army overrunning the East.

Victoria is deeply involved in the political consequences. She must defend the shared state before people who question her royal position, its use of foreign soldiers, and the legal status of non-Christian subjects. Elias insists that service and ability should matter more than inherited race or religion; critics object to the forced integration accompanying his expansion. His principle of broad civic incorporation increasingly coexists with ruthless military punishment.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** In 1863 Victoria founds the Kaʻahumanu Society, named for the queen whose name she bears as Kuhina Nui, to nurse the victims of smallpox and care for the sick and elderly. She signs her acts as Kaʻahumanu IV, leads the Kawaiahaʻo choir and composes chants in the old style, among them laments for her nephew, the Prince of Hawaii, who dies in 1862.

<!-- CR-036: indium at Carquinez, 1863 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** In 1863 Genth's spectroscope finds a bright blue line in zinc ore from a California mine that belongs to no known element. Carquinez names the metal indium for its color. Freiberg, testing its own ores, finds it second.

<!-- CR-049: the founder keeps the people; Peirce's relay demonstrator -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-049]** The navy wants the laboratory's instrument makers and the Calculation Office's computers for its gunnery tables. Elias gives it the tables and keeps the people: the ability to work out unfamiliar problems is a strategic asset, and the royalty that pays for it buys one fewer gunboat a year while the war lasts. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1863 Peirce and the Office's workshop demonstrate a relay arithmetic apparatus. The idea is Peirce's: telegraph relays wired in series and in parallel behave as Boole's "and" and "or", so a circuit can be designed on paper as algebra before it is built. The demonstrator is slow and temperamental, and it takes years to make it useful. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** Since his late teens Peirce has suffered a facial neuralgia that brings on his tempers, and success makes him overconfident in elegant procedures.

## 12. Electoral peace, the Western line and the Providentorship (1864–1867)

<!-- CR-039: the Protectorship is renewed for a third time in 1864 -->
The war places the legal machinery of the 1856 Confederation under steadily increasing strain. In 1862, 1863 and 1864 both member legislatures renew Elias's one-year Protectorship as difficult campaigns produce fresh dangers. Renewals that were intended to be exceptional begin to resemble annual recognition of a permanent fact. He remains the commander whose wartime prestige, control of finance and intimate authority over the common institutions make his removal politically hazardous.

When Alexander dies on 30 November 1863, Victoria, as Kuhina Nui, holds the powers of the crown for a day and proclaims her brother king. Lot succeeds as Kamehameha V. He admires decisive government and seeks stronger royal authority while retaining the Kuhina Nui. Victoria continues to hold the office. The historical Lot abolished it in 1864; here he judges its abolition politically costly and unnecessary, and his 1864 constitution names his sister his heir. Their disagreements concern the kingdom's policy, the scope of the crown and the terms of cooperation with Elias. The monarchy and co-executive remain visible institutions through which Hawaiian interests can be argued.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** Victoria keeps a kahuna as well as her pew at Kawaiahaʻo. In May 1865 he dies, promising to rise on the third day, and does not. In February 1866 she falls gravely ill; physicians of the Methodists' medical college at San Francisco, brought to Honolulu by fast steamer, save her where, in our history, she died that May. The kahuna who failed and the doctors who succeeded turn her toward one test for any power, Hawaiian or haole: whether it can show results. She keeps the chants, the language and her faith.

<!-- CR-035: Victoria at the college that saved her; the counting begins -->
**[APPROVED — CR-035]** That summer she sails to San Francisco to see the college that saved her. Its wards keep a table of every case, who came in, what was done and who lived. Its nurses are women, and two of its students are women who passed the Republic's examination. She asks for the tables and takes copies home. The Kaʻahumanu Society begins to count its own sick and its own dead.

Wartime decrees keep transport, procurement, credit and military administration moving. Each successful intervention creates commitments whose interruption could endanger firms, jobs, soldiers and public borrowing. Manufacturers and professional officers disagree with individual measures while increasingly favoring continuity of executive government. Critics describe the cumulative danger accurately, but supporters trust legislative review, the next election, temporary mandates or Elias's record of competent action.

In the 1864–1865 consolidation struggle, an Assembly majority threatens to revoke emergency measures and restrict the government's program. Elias uses the dissolution provision before the threatened revocation is enacted, calling an election within the prescribed interval. He campaigns on security, paid contracts, unfinished works and the costs of interruption. The administration's economic constituency helps him secure a more favorable Assembly. **[OPEN]** Exact dissolution and election dates, issues, campaign pressures and results; the state Council and member governments still require separate negotiation.

Financial strain compounds the military disappointments. Costly access to bullion and overseas credit burdens a Union still able to tax, borrow, issue currency and keep large armies in the field. The war's political decision comes when Lincoln loses the election of 1864. The Northern republic chooses peace through exhaustion and democratic change while preserving its constitution, army and industrial economy.

**[PROPOSED]** McClellan wins a civilian swing across the Lower North after Atlanta and Mobile fail to provide Lincoln reassuring victories. McClellan remains committed to Union, so the result does not itself authorize Southern independence. Lincoln pursues his last winter operations; the lame-duck House fails to pass the Thirteenth Amendment, and Hampton Roads fails over separation. After taking office in March 1865, McClellan signs executive armistice conventions with Richmond and Pacifica during March–June. The initial war ends, while the Union's unratified claim to the South survives to fuel a second conflict. Sherman's brother John in the Senate provides a possible negotiating channel.

The Western line is agreed in the 1865 peace and ratified by the Union Senate in 1867, alongside Pacifica's Alaska purchase. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** The peace declares the conventions of 1848 and 1860 ended by the war. The Western line replaces the renunciation of the Basin and the recognition of Washoe; American owners in Nevada keep the property guarantees Carson gave them. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** A Pacific article goes with it. The Union renounces its claims under the Guano Islands Act of 1856 to a schedule of named islands: Midway, Baker, Howland, Jarvis, Johnston and Kingman, with a quitclaim to Palmyra, which Hawaii annexed in 1862. The New York guano companies keep their workings until the deposits are exhausted and land their cargoes duty-free at Hawaiian ports, where their labourers were recruited. A nominal sum disappears into the vendor credit. Senators who are selling half the West can give up a few guano rocks under their own statute, which never obliged the United States to keep an island once its guano was gone. Pacifica keeps the Northwest, Great Basin, Arizona, Utah, Nevada and Idaho; western Montana includes the Missouri-headwaters gold camps, while Fort Benton remains Union. Southwest Wyoming includes South Pass, Fort Bridger, the Green River basin and Rock Springs coal. Colorado's Western Slope, North and Middle Park and the San Luis Valley are Pacifican; Denver, the Front Range, Pikes Peak and Leadville stay Union. New Mexico runs east to 103°W. The detailed boundary follows the mountain front, divide crests and Sangre de Cristo exceptions recorded in the territorial plan; surveys remain **[PROPOSED]**. Federal cession does not settle Native title or consent.

**[PROPOSED]** The surviving Puget Sound enclave is evacuated under the treaty, leaving the Union without a Pacific port. Pacifica pays a $40 million gold vendor credit in instalments through 1872 and offers a separate Treasury loan. Eastern financiers become participants in the settlement; Elias, once dispossessed by creditors, becomes creditor to the United States. The price, payment schedule and loan capacity require financial testing. Senate ratification of the paid Western cession is separated from executive recognition of Richmond, which the Senate never ratifies. The Plains remain a buffer, and the Union's grievance points chiefly toward the South. **[PROPOSED]** War debt and demobilization limit immediate capacity; Democratic government and financiers profiting from transit and loans reduce the will for a second Western war, while the unresolved South, Plains conflicts and British quarrels compete for attention. Restraint rests on incentives and geography, not an incapable or permanently pacified Union.

The Union holds Tennessee and the Mississippi corridor from Memphis to Vicksburg. The Confederacy holds Port Hudson, New Orleans, Mobile and its lower bay, Galveston and the Texas coast; Pensacola, Key West and Ship Island remain Union. Indian Territory goes to the Confederacy. **[PROPOSED]** The 1865 armistice initially freezes its division along the Arkansas River; the 1871 border convention transfers the northern Cherokee and Creek lands, leaving the Public Land Strip Union. Tribal self-government, the rival Watie and Ross parties and the claims of enslaved people remain active political forces.

A Pacifica–Confederate Treaty of Amity and Commerce in spring 1865 supplies Pacifica's formal recognition. Britain and France also recognize the Confederacy. **[PROPOSED]** McClellan's reception of an envoy provides Union executive recognition without surrendering the Senate's legal claim. Wartime alliance clauses and exact treaty drafting remain **[OPEN]** within this selected postwar structure.

Marcus seeks defensible gains and can accept lawfully established durable executive authority. Sherman presses for fewer commitments and insists that the army serves lawful government rather than a separate personal oath to Elias. Neither officer has an individual constitutional veto. Their disagreement informs consolidation without giving either a personal right to prevent a constitutionally adopted settlement.

In 1865 Elias secures the Enabling Law through constitutional amendment: two-thirds of both confederal chambers and ratification by both founding states. The cabinet may legislate around specified provisions, but the Hawaiian crown, Kuhina Nui and local institutions are protected. The ordinary chambers and member governments remain. Elias becomes Providentor for life and presides over the cabinet; the chancellorship lapses. This is a legal concentration of power obtained through votes and bargaining, not a decree amending its own limits.

Lot supports the bargain in return for assurances protecting Hawaiian kingship, the Kuhina Nui, local institutions and agreed revenues. Victoria negotiates these protections and helps secure the settlement while retaining reservations about its reach. Constitutional critics warn that institutional survival may offer little protection against an executive legislating around ordinary controls. Supporters believe that reconstruction, occupation, debt and industrial commitments require a government able to act continuously. The guarantees become protected provisions of the Enabling Law, whose later breach cannot be justified by the authority it grants.

Elias controls ministerial appointment and dismissal, sets the cabinet's legislative agenda and appoints senior administrators. Cabinet legislative power consequently concentrates in his person, although ministers retain expertise, constituencies and opportunities to resist. Courts continue to enforce ordinary contracts and property claims; the same productive society that attracts newcomers accommodates growing restrictions in matters the government defines as security or reconstruction. Dependence on grants, appointments and contracts makes the concentration harder to reverse. From 1865 he deals directly with county boards over state legislatures’ heads through water, railway and land grants, legally hollowing out the states.

The Providentorship lasts for Elias's life. Its enabling delegation lapses when the regency begins in 1887; neither a later regent nor a county inherits his power. The constitutional machinery remains visible even as executive patronage drains the member states' practical independence.

Pacifica negotiates infrastructure, trade and financial relationships while administering its agreed gains. The Northern and Confederate governments retain sovereignty and substantial institutions; association deepens through contested commercial, defense and customs agreements. No Confederate state is a Pacifican member by 1895. Membership becomes constitutionally possible after 1887, with admission dependent on published social measures rather than an immediate transfer of millions of voters to the founding electorate. Elias's victories depend on the courage and skill of others, foreign credit and diplomacy, superior preparation in particular theatres and willingness to pay terrible costs; the resulting state is never an uncontested inheritance.

Colonel Armistead's question is what continuity of service preserves for the men and households to whom he remains responsible. Defeat does not end those obligations, but employment by the victorious power can change their meaning. The profession that offers survival also demands obedience in new campaigns against people defending homes of their own. **[OPEN]** His original enlistment motive, transfer into the Atlantic Auxiliary, the terms offered to his men and his later itinerary remain to be designed; no particular bargain or act of refusal is settled.

# PART III — Ocean partners, continental dependence and industrial limits (1865–1887)

This part follows the construction of the Pacific network and interleaves with the Hawaiian and family story in Part IV. Its major commitments are separated by consolidation. Pacifica seeks local naval superiority, paying and supplying partners who fight on land.
