Acquisitions must earn their cost within about ten years; there is one operation at a time, no populated mainland annexation outside North America, no Pacifican land war against Russia, and no war with Britain before 1900.

## 13. Winning the peace and binding the continental partner (1865–1875)

The prewar technical community turns wartime experience into expanding capacity. Donahue's workshops and Huntington's procurement, Ralston's credit and Crocker's construction teams, and Kelly's experiments, Holley's plant engineering and Hallidie's material handling contribute different parts of a cumulative industrial system. Outside processes, licensing, fuel, workers and capital still determine what can be built. Logan's Louisiana repair relationships offer an engineering base and commercial opportunity; no private fortune or monopoly follows automatically.

**[PROPOSED]** Demobilization brings land offers and paid contracts for engineers, naval officers and shipwrights from both American armies. Sierra railway crews remain employed on interior links. As France withdraws, a Baja purchase in 1867, paid over five years, a Guaymas railway concession and free port secure the southern flank. Mexico retains Sonora. Prices, garrisons and firms await audit.

<!-- CR-058: Juárez, Querétaro and the Sonora credit -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** As France withdraws, Pacifica recognises Juárez's government and lends to it on the Sonora customs, and rifles for the Republic's army go south through Paso del Norte from New Mexico. Maximilian is shot at Querétaro on 19 June 1867, in the city where Elias and Alvarado signed the convention of 1847, and Mexico's liberals learn that Pacifica, not Washington, ended the French adventure. Monterey restrains its Confederate partner, whose exiles had courted Maximilian and settled in his empire. Juárez's words of 15 July 1867, that among individuals, as among nations, respect for the rights of others is peace, become the common language of the continent's neighbours.

The Confederate economy needs credit, markets and protection. A railway treaty in 1866–1867 gives Huntington's line a route across Texas to New Orleans. Johnston succeeds Davis in February 1868 after his 1867 election. Richmond's Mississippi tolls and fugitive raids into Union Tennessee give Grant a popular cause; elected in 1868, he attacks in July 1870 while Europe is occupied by the Franco-Prussian War. Pacifica sends a Gulf squadron, turret ships, mines under Logan, rifles and credit, and closes its Pacific ports to Union shipping. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Hawaiian schooners raise the Confederation's flag over the remaining American guano bonds in the Phoenix group, at Kiritimati and on Swains, without firing a shot. It sends no field army and undertakes no Asian military commitment during this war.

Union attacks fail at New Orleans in autumn 1870 and stall against entrenched lines in Virginia. Among the Texan volunteers at New Orleans is Silas Pruett, nineteen, who sees Pacifican mines and credit at work. After the association treaties he takes a commission in the Atlantic Auxiliary. In spring 1871 Sherman negotiates with Grant, whose friendship now crosses national allegiances. Richmond restores free Mississippi navigation; Pacifica guarantees Confederate independence. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The Union quitclaims the Pacific islands Pacifica occupied during the war. The Union Senate keeps its claim to the South. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** Association treaties in 1871–1872 refinance Confederate debt on customs security, install Pacifican customs co-administration and secure the lower Mobile Bay naval station, Galveston commercial privileges, Atlantic Auxiliary recruitment and Birmingham concessions. Pacifica replaces the surrendered toll revenue. Pensacola remains a nearby Union base; protection and exposure coexist.

<!-- CR-058: the neighbours in Grant's war -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Grant's war is part of a plan. Having lost the West, the Union means to make up for it in the Caribbean, on the Mississippi and in the north, and each neighbour meets a different part of the plan. Canada meets it in Sumner's speech of 13 April 1869, which puts the Alabama damages at about $2.1 billion, swollen by the raiders that helped the South win, a sum read on both sides of the Atlantic as payable only in Canada; in the Fenian raids of May 1870, which Washington tolerates; and in the refusal that month to let the steamer *Chicora* through the Sault canal with troops and supplies for the Red River expedition, which shows Ottawa that the Union can cut the Dominion in two at the Soo. St Albans and the Confederate agents of Montreal are an account the Union means to settle, and Britain is taking its garrisons home. Montreal also earns from Richmond's tolls, which drive Midwest grain onto the St Lawrence canals. Mexico meets the plan in Grant's treaty to annex Santo Domingo, which the Senate rejects on 30 June 1870, and Juárez would rather have a weak Confederacy on the Rio Grande than a reunited giant. Pacifica coordinates nothing; it shows each capital the whole map. When Grant attacks, the Canadian militia still under arms after the May raids stays on the border and ties down Union troops, and Mexico closes its Gulf ports to Union coaling.

<!-- CR-058: the spring of two treaties; British Columbia and the Canadian Pacific -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** In the spring of 1871 two treaties are made. At Washington on 8 May Britain trades Canada's fisheries away for peace with the Union. In the same weeks Pacifica grants Canada, for nothing but the asking, the navigation of the Stikine, the Yukon and the Porcupine through Alaska, free forever. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** The grant is a convention signed at Monterey with Britain, with a British Columbian delegate in the British party. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** British Columbia enters Confederation on 20 July. Pacifica has encouraged it, because a strong Canadian buffer is worth more to it than a province that would provoke Britain, and since 1866 it has let London know that a Union move on Rupert's Land or the coast would concern the Pacific. Its money goes quietly, through London houses and never into the charter, into the finance of the Canadian Pacific, whose western traffic and, from 1887, trans-Pacific steamers feed Pacifica's own shipping. The scandal of 1873 over the foreign money behind Allan's syndicate shows what visible money would cost. The river grant matters at once: the Cassiar rush of 1873–1874 goes up the Stikine through Wrangell.

The Union turns to an anti-slavery cold war. Greeley defeats Grant in 1872, then dies before the electors meet. After a disputed count, Brown takes office in 1873 on condition that he sign the Freedom Act: refugees are free on arrival and citizens after a year; discriminatory duties and European campaigning penalize slave-grown cotton. The Panic of 1873 weakens cotton and slave prices while Pacifica recruits laid-off engineers and workers. A disputed Hayes election and the railway strike of 1877 deepen the Union's crisis without ending its anti-slavery policy.

Gordon succeeds Johnston in 1874 and works with Pacifican railways, coal and credit. Benjamin helps carry a free-womb amendment in 1874–1875 as refinancing pressure mounts. **[PROPOSED]** Benjamin first serves as Louisiana senator from 1868, then Gordon's Secretary of State from 1874. A convention establishes freedom for children born after 1 January 1875, while allowing masters their labor to twenty-one or state compensation. The constitutional route requires state consent; its economic effect and finance await audit. Black Union regulars and freedmen's homesteads along Tennessee sustain escape networks. Flight, free-labor markets and campaigning make the Confederacy poorer and more dependent on Pacifican credit.

Pacifica enforces a fugitive agreement with Richmond from 1871–1872 until abolition in 1888–1889, preventing enslaved people from escaping to Pacifican territory. **[PROPOSED]** Travel-paper controls refuse entry at Texas, the railway and Gulf ports; when habeas corpus frees a returned fugitive, the cabinet overrides the court under the enabling law. Victoria objects to the policy, Sherman to that use of delegated constitutional power. Pacifica's own slavery prohibition remains, but its border assists a slaveholding partner. Abolitionists and Chinese and Hawaiian citizens recognize the contradiction.

## 14. Alaska, Japan, China and the ocean road (1867–1880)

Alaska is purchased in 1867 for the Aleutian route to Japan and to exclude a rival northern foothold. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Midway passes to Pacifica in the 1867 ratification and becomes part of the Hawaiian Crown lands, with its naval ground leased to the Confederation on the terms later used at Puʻuloa. **[PROPOSED]** A subsidized San Francisco–Honolulu–Yokohama–Hong Kong mail line, coaling also at Hakodate, preserves Pacific traffic after Suez opens. Northern gold income belongs to the later Juneau and Klondike developments; the northern administration must initially pay for harbors and transport without a ready gold windfall.

**[PROPOSED]** Pacifica's fleet guarantees the negotiated transition at Edo in 1868 through Katsu Kaishū and Saigō Takamori. The Kōtetsu reaches the Meiji government through Pacifica, and the Hakodate regime falls in 1869. Kuroda Kiyotaka's Hokkaido development brings Pacifican advisers north; in 1886 Kiyoshi enters his house by adoption and marriage, and later founds his own branch of it. France's defeat creates an opening for a Pacifican army mission, followed by naval training and Japanese construction to Pacifican designs.

The 1870–1871 Japanese alliance is equal: full Japanese tariff autonomy and no consular courts, dockyard rights at Yokosuka, a coaling lease at Hakodate, Takashima coal and foreign credit. No sovereign Pacifican base is imposed on Japan. Training and standards make cooperation valuable and eventual independence from the system expensive. The Iwakura Mission's San Francisco landfall in 1872 puts Japan's officials inside this industrial world.

<!-- CR-031-R1: reciprocal Japanese–Pacifican innovation and nineteenth-century construction productivity. -->
Over the following decades Japanese and Pacifican engineers learn from one another and compete to improve the work. Dockyards, railways and factories bring their methods into regular comparison: how materials arrive, how parts fit, how crews are trained and how long a completed job takes. Japanese contractors hire and train their own paid crews, organise repeated tasks in planned sequences and coordinate machinery with deliveries and skilled work. By the later nineteenth century Japan has a construction industry whose speed and efficient use of labour become an example to its partner. Contractors win by completing more work with the same resources, retaining skills and cutting delays; handing recruitment and control to labour syndicates and local gang bosses would surrender both their margins and their command of the job. Japanese firms carry this organisation into their overseas undertakings.

China receives technology and education in Chinese-owned undertakings, protection for Chinese Pacificans and a policy of no missionaries. **[PROPOSED]** An 1868 treaty preserves ordinary foreign treaty rights while including a jointly licensed family-emigration article. Yung Wing's machinery orders go to Carquinez; the Educational Mission studies in San Francisco with naval-academy places. Chinese steamships and Kaiping mines draw on Pacifican engineering and credit. California wheat and Chinese Pacifican committees support North China famine relief in 1876–1879. A proposed prosecution and execution of the Los Angeles rioters after the 1871 massacre makes protection costly at home. The partners judge Pacifica by enforcement, not declarations.

Korea signs its first Western treaty with Pacifica around 1878–1880 through Li Hongzhang's mediation under the **[PROPOSED]** diplomatic plan. Advisers support Korean neutrality in 1882–1887 but knowingly stand aside from the 1884 Gapsin coup. Their restraint preserves a relationship while abandoning reformers who trusted them.

## 15. The Spanish window and the settlement of families (1869–1885)

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** Elias first tries to buy. In 1869–70 a confidential offer for the Philippines goes to Prim, who had refused Napoleon's Mexican empire at Veracruz and seen the American war at first hand. Prim declines: no rising in the islands justifies a sale, and his name rests on avenging insults to the flag. He keeps the channel open. Assassins in the Calle del Turco close it at the end of 1870.

Spain's seizure of a Pacifican schooner off Jolo in the spring of 1873 gives Elias his pretext. The Republic refuses compensation and a public offer to buy; a republic cannot be seen to sell territory. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** In August 1873, with the cantonal revolt dividing Spain's navy, a Pacifican squadron fights a one-day action against the Cavite station in Manila Bay. It does not land. Governor-General Alaminos signs a local convention: an indemnity, the crew's release, and an anchorage for a Pacifican squadron to protect its trade. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Japan, negotiating treaty revision with every power that holds a treaty, Spain among them, sells the damaged ships coal for the nearest home port and refuses them a dock. The nearest home port is Honolulu.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** That autumn the *Virginius* brings the Union and Spain close to war over Cuba; Pacifica's good offices in Madrid help settle it, and Madrid remembers. In March 1874 Serrano's Republic gives the Bank of Spain its monopoly of notes to pay for the war at home. For the war overseas it borrows Pacifican gold, secured on the Philippine customs and tobacco revenues. Pacifican agents sit in the customs houses, and the Banco Español-Filipino services the loan in Manila. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** The price includes the pardon of the Filipino exiles on Guam. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The Restoration of December 1874 inherits a debt it cannot pay. In 1876, with the Carlists beaten and Martínez Campos's army growing toward a hundred thousand men in Cuba, Cánovas, the historian of Spain's decline, asks the Cortes for a law of sale. He calls it the honourable liquidation of a republican debt that saves Cuba. Pacifica's price is to recognize no Cuban insurgents and to close its ports to their filibusters; Union abolitionists say Pacifican gold pays for the army that keeps Cuba slave. The sale includes the Philippines, Marianas, Carolines and Palau. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The islands pay for themselves. A Philippine Redemption Loan, guaranteed by the Confederation and serviced from the Philippine customs, raises the price in London and San Francisco, and a covenant limits its service to the gain the islands make over their last Spanish years, published every year. Filipinos still say they paid for their own sale. The government answers that they never paid more than Pacifica added.

**[PROPOSED — CR-029]** Until 1887 the Philippines is a dependency of the Confederation, not a member state, with no members in the confederal chambers. Its Organic Act vests authority in the Providentor's office, not his person. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Romualdo Pacheco, schooled in Honolulu as a boy and now forty-four, goes from Sacramento to Manila as Governor-General for a long term. He governs with a Philippine Council, and the Audiencia keeps Spanish law. His contempt for the Chinese is the Sacramento faction's; Manila's Chinese merchants and Elias's protection keep him on a short leash. Forced labour, forced crop sales and the tobacco monopoly end, and offices open by examination. The monopoly's factories and leaf are sold to a chartered tobacco company in which the state keeps a quarter.<!-- CR-039: the state, not the Crown, before 1914 --> The Marianas, Carolines and Palau are separated from Manila under a naval administration at Apra Harbour. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The sale follows Malcampo's capture of Jolo in February 1876, so Pacifica inherits a Spanish garrison and a war. In 1878 a compact with the Sultan of Sulu, and separate compacts with the datus who hold the real power, settle the south. Their Tausug text is authoritative, and the Sultan alone can consent to undo them. The cholera of 1882 comes in through Jolo and Zamboanga and reaches Manila in August, killing thousands in the city and tens of thousands in the islands. Polytechnic physicians read it as a matter of water. In 1883 a Municipal Ordinance gives every town an elected tribunal, its own funds and no parish priest over its budget, and binds the towns to piped water, quarantine of ships, latrines kept away from wells, and burial away from rivers. Health is taught through the cabezas and the parish priests, with no cordons or burned quarters. The Office of Public Measures publishes deaths by town; Elias deals with the towns over the heads of Manila and the friars, as he dealt with the county boards. In 1878 the Sultan's lease of North Borneo goes to a Pacifican Borneo company rather than to the British. Friar-estate purchases, financed by Philippine land bonds under the same guarantee, resell land to tenants in installments to a land board. Reforms do not erase coercion, and the land board becomes a landlord too.

<!-- CR-036: Koch and the cholera at Manila, 1882 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** The physicians who read the cholera as a matter of water are led by Robert Koch, who came to Pacifica in 1872 as an unknown country doctor from Posen and works in a Polytechnic laboratory at Carquinez. There he traced the life of anthrax in 1876, and in the spring of 1882 he found the bacillus of tuberculosis. That autumn in Manila his team finds the comma-shaped bacillus of cholera in the water and shows how it travels. The tables of deaths by town, before and after the pipes, go round the world. **[PROPOSED]** He had answered the Republic's call for district physicians in the mining country, and Cohn, seeing his microscope work, had the Polytechnic give him the laboratory.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** The Manila station, a squadron kept in Asian waters to secure the convention and then the loan, sharpens the admirals' demand for unconditional control of Pearl Harbor and carries the strategic conflict into Hawaii's 1874 crisis. **[PROPOSED]** Pacifica supplies Japan's Formosa expedition that year and mediates Ryukyu in 1879: Japan keeps the islands, China receives trade guarantees. In the Satsuma Rebellion of 1877 Pacifica backs the Meiji government against Saigō and its oldest Japanese friends. Friendship remains a strategy whose beneficiaries can change.

Assisted immigration of women and families extends into the new interior territories, with China, Japan and the Philippines as partners. **[PROPOSED]** Mercer-style parties, larger family land grants with wives owning their shares, irrigation districts and railways support settlement; Mennonite alternative-service teams carry Mormon irrigation practice into new districts. A marriage-floor case in the 1870s brings a Mormon–Shoshone or Mormon–Chinese union before the confederal court; the pairing and exact judgment remain **[OPEN]** within the approved test-case direction.

**[PROPOSED]** Hakka families lead early Chinese family migration, jointly licensed brokers can also become traffickers, and an earlier Japanese emigration scheme sustains the Wakamatsu colony and arranged marriages. Filipino reformers and families begin arriving after 1876, especially in Catholic, Spanish-speaking Arizona and New Mexico. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** From the 1880s Filipino families also come to Hawaii as kindred settlers under Victoria's programme of restoring the Hawaiian people. Victoria's international programme combines passage, reception, training and scholarships with household, employer, philanthropic and public funding. Its workbook schedule remains a scenario, not a new population guarantee.

The republic's mixed Californio and Hawaiian inheritance gains Chinese and Japanese family traditions. First-generation mixed children come of age around 1895; Hapa unions become commonplace symbols over the following generations. The ideal faces nativist politics and the fugitive agreement rather than erasing them. Rock Springs in 1885 and Hells Canyon in 1887 occur on Pacifican soil and receive prompt prosecution. No census target or legal proclamation proves that equal civic standing has become daily experience.

<!-- CR-036: the interior's own schools: Santa Fe, Deseret, the Sutro School; the 1878 eclipse -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** Since the war the interior has built schools of its own. The Colegio de Santa Fe, founded in 1864, trains the surveyors of the treaty lines and the engineers of the acequias. **[PROPOSED]** It keeps an observatory in the high, clear air. When the total eclipse of 29 July 1878 crosses the mountains, its spectroscopists set up Pacifica's station at a railway siding in the Great Divide Basin, a few miles from the Union's astronomers across the frontier. **[APPROVED — CR-036]** Deseret's university, revived after accession, measures how much water an acre needs and teaches the arid world to build canals. At Virginia City the Sutro School, founded with the tunnel in 1869, studies the heat of the deep Comstock and the cold that lets men work in it.

## 16. Vladivostok, the Customs Inspectorate and the Korean guarantee (1877–1887)

While Russia fights Turkey, Pacifica and Japan concentrate one maritime action at Vladivostok in February 1878. Marcus Vance I dies there in winter fighting; ice, machinery and burning fuel give his last action its physical terror. The exact operation remains **[PROPOSED]**, with no Pacifican army marching through Siberia. Southern Sakhalin is restored to Japan under the proposed settlement, folded into the Congress of Berlin; Britain recognizes the Philippine purchase. Russia remains powerful and seeks a railway connection to reinforce its Far Eastern position. Japan later takes the eastern railway and Vladivostok before Russia can complete its own through connection. The line will reach the Pacific under Japanese control, through Chinese-owned Manchuria.

Elias loses the commander whose restraint and organizational skill made distant commitments possible. **[PROPOSED]** Rafael Soberanes, a Californio officer unrelated to the Vances, serves as Marcus’s chief of staff, completes the operation after his death and brings the squadron home. Born in Monterey in 1847 and examined into the Polytechnic at fourteen, he earns the authority later used to lead it through service rather than descent. His name and birth are settled by the later constitutional record; the unprovided details of his early life and exact service remain qualified. Caleb remains a living alternative to professional calculation.

In 1884–1885 France's war with China opens the Keelung window. **[PROPOSED]** A Pacifican squadron protects the coal trade after Ferry's fall, preferring demonstration to battle. China grants a coal concession to a Chinese–Pacifican company, retains sovereignty and receives railway construction and fleet-rebuilding credit.

Soberanes commands the Keelung squadron in 1884–1885 without a battle, with Kiyoshi captaining its Japanese-crewed ships.

The Customs Inspectorate passes to Pacifican leadership in 1885 by China's appointment. Edmund Fong Kai-ming, born in San Francisco in 1859 to a Taishan merchant family and a customs officer since 1878, becomes the named official through whom trade, revenue and divided loyalty acquire a personal face. **[PROPOSED]** Hart takes the British ministership, Li backs Fong, multinational staff remain, Chinese staff rise and accounts are published. The small agency fee and China’s retained appointment power distinguish administration from territorial seizure.

Private Pacifican merchants can still carry opium; no general state ban is adopted. **[PROPOSED]** Strict collection of duty and inland tax raises Chinese revenue and makes British supply dearer, without claiming moral purity. Pacifican credit secured on customs makes friendship increasingly consequential to China's independence.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** Papal arbitration in 1885 confirms Pacifica's Carolines on condition of freedom for the Catholic missions, while granting Germany trade and coaling rights; Germany keeps the Marshalls, and the settlement records Pacifica's claim to Wake, taken five years earlier for a future cable. Leo XIII's price and Pacifica's are one bargain: the Philippine parishes pass to Filipino secular priests, Filipino bishops are named with the right to succeed, and the friar estates are paid for partly into an endowment that stays in the islands. The cause for which Gómez, Burgos and Zamora were garrotted in 1872 is won by treaty. Port Hamilton in 1885–1887 becomes a near-war Britain loses diplomatically. British withdrawal rests on a proposed China–Russia–Pacifica guarantee of Korean territory. The decision gives Pacifica a duty to protect Korea without owning it. Foreign commitments pause around the 1887 succession; the network must survive its founder before it can expand again. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The pause has a price in Samoa. Hawaii had signed the 1878 treaty for Pago Pago on the Confederation's behalf, and in 1886–87 Kalākaua carries a Polynesian compact to Malietoa Laupepa. In August 1887 Germany's consul deports Malietoa and installs Tamasese, and Pacifica, burying its founder, does not answer.

---

# PART IV — Hawaii, the ruined marriage and the founder's death (1870–1887)

## 17. The island alliance unravels after Lot's death (1872–1876)

During their early voyages and negotiations, Lot Kapuāiwa and Elias recognized much of themselves in one another. As king from November 1863, Lot retains the Kuhina Nui and bargains with its incumbent, Victoria, over Hawaiian policy. The 1865 enabling settlement preserves crown, co-executive and local institutions, yet gives the confederal cabinet increasing scope to determine the conditions under which those institutions operate. Victoria's office still carries authority within Hawaii; her ability to restrain the common executive depends on government support, representatives, finance and enforceable commitments.

Lot and Elias continue to disagree over the nature of their alliance. Elias's commanders increasingly regard Hawaii as the center of an integrated naval system whose facilities cannot be placed beyond confederal control. Lot sees a kingdom whose cooperation was freely promised and whose crown cannot survive if every customs and military decision becomes a Californian administrative order. They remain bound by friendship and necessity, making the disagreements harder to reconcile.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** Lot dies in December 1872 without a direct heir. Under Article 22 of his constitution the crown passes to his sister, and Victoria becomes Queen of Hawaii without an election. She appoints her half-sister Ruth Keʻelikōlani Kuhina Nui: a great landholder and governor of Hawaiʻi island who speaks no English and keeps the old ways, a reassurance to the chiefs. Lunalilo, the man her brothers forbade her, becomes the face of everyone who fears a queen married to the Providentor. Queen Emma, Alexander's Anglican widow, leads those who look to London.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** In September 1873 the Queen's Guard mutinies. Its drillmaster, Captain József Jajczay, is a Hungarian veteran of the hussars who escorted Maximilian to Mexico, hired by Lot to train a guard loyal to the crown alone and owing nothing to Monterey's academy. The adjutant general is Colonel Charles Judd, a missionary's son and Victoria's schoolmate at the Royal School. When Jajczay jails four men for deserting the treasury post, the soldiers beat him and Judd, seize the barracks and load two cannon. The admirals at Honolulu offer marines; Victoria refuses them, because Hawaiians will settle this themselves. On the first day she crosses Palace Walk to the barracks with her women and no guard and speaks to the men in Hawaiian. She dismisses Jajczay and Judd, as the men demand, and the men lay down their arms before her. She does not disband the Guard: she gives its command to Major William Moehonua and takes its oath again herself. Jajczay leaves the islands and dies in Costa Rica the next year. Judd, whom she has sacrificed, attaches himself to Kalākaua. She suspects Kalākaua's hand in the mutiny, says nothing, and makes him answerable for the Guard's loyalty.

**[APPROVED — CR-027; amended CR-029]** For the admirals of the Manila squadron, who brought their damaged ships into Honolulu that September because neutral Japan would sell them coal but not a dock, a kingdom with a guard loyal only to its Queen is the decisive argument: Pearl Harbor lies beside a force Hawaii can use. They insist that the harbour can no longer depend on Hawaiian consent. Lunalilo dies on 3 February 1874. Days later his followers rise in Honolulu in the Queen's name against the confederal demand. Confederal marines land to restore order; there are deaths in the rioting, and each side blames the other. Victoria keeps the Guard in its barracks. She will not let a rising in her name become a war against her husband.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** In March 1874 she signs the Puʻuloa Convention: a fifty-year lease of Pearl Harbor and its shores to the Confederation and a treaty of common defence. It is a lease, not a cession; no Hawaiian land is alienated. Ruth countersigns as Kuhina Nui, and the legislature ratifies narrowly, carried by the nobles Kalākaua brings to the Queen's side. The reversal of her 1855 refusal is her own act, made to keep the crown rather than lose it to London through Emma or to the planters. Elias wants the harbour for cold reasons: the Manila station, the transpacific fleet and a kingdom whose legislature an outside power could sway.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** The illegality comes after. Between 1874 and 1876 the confederal cabinet stretches the Convention by ordinance under the Enabling Law: harbour police, then the Honolulu port administration, then the defence districts, and in 1876 an appointed Territorial Council of planters and confederal officials that takes over the legislature's business. The ordinances breach the Enabling Law's protected provisions and Article XIV's bar on annexation. Ruth refuses to recognize the Council and resigns as Kuhina Nui; the Queen leaves the office vacant. Hawaii's Supreme Court declares the ordinances void. No one can enforce the judgment against confederal marines, and Victoria will not ask her people to bleed for it. She lets the ruling stand on paper and the Council stand in fact, as she once let the constitution's form go to see her brother proclaimed. No missing signature or later claim of necessity supplies consent. The judgment is never reversed, leaving sovereigntists a legal claim even after the Act of Settlement of 1907.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** Emma becomes the loyalists' counter-queen and remains so until her death in 1885. In 1883 she builds a country house on the Puʻuloa shore outside the leased ground and plants banyans and two thousand coconuts at the gate of the fleet.

Afterward Victoria and Elias remain married and politically associated, but their trust has been damaged beyond easy repair. Elias insists that divided strategic authority threatened the security of the Pacific; she answers that no security can be called common if one partner has lost the ability to withhold consent. Both have contributed to the institutions whose consequences they now confront. The marriage survives through affection that neither will admit has vanished, mutual dependency, and Victoria's ambitions for what the empire might still become.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** The Territorial Council's English Schools Ordinance of 1876 removes Hawaiian from government schools. Many Hawaiian parents have long wanted English for their children; the planters on the Council make it the only language. Elias, who needs the Council for the harbour works and the cable, does not overrule it: he will not spend his capital on what he thinks minor, and the 1887 constitution's entrenchment of Hawaiian comes eleven years too late for a generation of children. Victoria fights the ordinance and loses, then pays for Hawaiian-language schools through the Kaʻahumanu Society for the rest of her life. This cultural wrong becomes part of the price of the order.

In 1875 Elias chooses Kāneʻohe Bay for a new capital. Honolulu carries the memory of February 1874 and San Francisco the bankers' and vigilantes' power; the Koʻolau separates the proposed Crown precinct from Kiyoshi's future fleet base at Pearl Harbor. Victoria leases the ground from the Hawaiian Crown lands and insists that it remain the throne's land, with Mōkapu's burial dunes unbuilt and Heʻeia fishpond kept working. Pearl Harbor is dredged in 1875–1885; the Pali railway tunnel and bay channel are built in 1876–1882. The San Francisco–Oʻahu cable opens in 1879 before the government moves. The Seat of Government Act transfers the offices from Monterey in 1885, into temporary halls. The capital's beauty rests on Crown land leased by a Queen governing under the disputed regime, local labor and expensive infrastructure.

## 18. Kalanimoku, the accident and a court divided (1870s)

Kalanimoku, Elias and Victoria’s legitimate son, born after the 1858 wedding and heir to his mother's Hawaiian crown, grows up amid Hawaiian traditions, frontier stories, technical education, sea voyages and the expectations of several political constituencies. He has fears, friendships and ambitions of his own. His name is settled; his birth year, age, personality and particular childhood experiences remain **[OPEN]**.

An accident in the late 1870s kills Kalanimoku and injures Victoria, leaving her unable to bear more children. Her grief and physical suffering create a division between what the state demands of her and what she can privately endure. Elias throws himself into travel, technical work and the organization of campaigns. Their attempts to comfort each other increasingly misfire. The accident’s circumstances, exact date and medical consequences remain **[OPEN]**.

**[PROPOSED — CR-027]** After the accident Victoria stops singing. Elias keeps a phonograph cylinder of her voice, made shortly before; on his deathbed in 1887 he asks for it.

Elias’s other relationships precede Kalanimoku’s death. Silas, his eldest son, born in 1851 before the marriage to Abigail Merritt, and Kiyoshi, his second son by a Satsuma mother, grow into the leaders of distinct constituencies. **[PROPOSED]** Kiyoshi is born in Kagoshima in 1859 to Uehara Hisa, a glass master's daughter and interpreter. **[APPROVED — CR-027]** He is conceived in the spring of 1858, during the Edo negotiations and months before Elias's wedding. When Victoria learns of him remains **[OPEN]**. Elias withholds his name from both. Their mothers have households, loyalties and political stakes of their own. Victoria retains political ambition and helps devise a viable inheritance for the empire.

## 19. The Golden Afternoon and the examination state (late 1870s–1880s)

The empire appears to reach a stable maturity. Cities, universities, arts and public works expand; a sophisticated culture of industry and imperial syncretism takes shape across the Pacific. Victoria encourages artistic and educational institutions and helps shape the state's memory of its founder. Elias's attention moves restlessly from trade to engineering and military construction even as infirmity makes activity more difficult.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** Kalākaua, the suitor she refused, becomes the Queen's minister of the image and her envoy. He revives the hula, prints the genealogical chants and retells Hawaii's legends for a foreign audience as the equals of the Iliad and the romances of chivalry. His tale of the Iron Knife, Japanese castaways on Maui whose descendants were plentiful, gives the Japanese partnership a Hawaiian prehistory. In 1881 he travels round the world in her name with Judd as his chamberlain, meets the Meiji Emperor and proposes an Oceanian union sealed by marrying his niece, the young Kaʻiulani, to Prince Komatsu; the Emperor politely declines. His motto, *Hoʻoulu Lāhui*, increase the nation, stands over the Queen's mortality figures. In his book he mourns Kekuaokalani, who died defending the old gods against the first Kaʻahumanu's canoes, and he publishes it under the patronage of the fourth. She never lets him near the purse. He dies in San Francisco in 1891, and his project of the image passes to the press seat of the Five.

<!-- CR-043: Kaʻiulani lives --> **[APPROVED — CR-043]** Kalākaua's niece does not die young. Kaʻiulani is schooled in England from 1889. In 1896 her physicians find an exophthalmic goitre, and in 1897 she goes to Theodor Kocher at Bern, who ties off its arteries and then removes part of the gland, leaving her enough of it. In January 1899, at home on Hawaiʻi, she rides into the mountain rain and comes down with rheumatic fever. Her heart, no longer driven by the goitre, holds. Months in bed and the salicylate of the day bring her through, with a damaged valve she carries for the rest of her life. **[PROPOSED — CR-043]** From 1908 she travels with Julian's missions to the European capitals, where her school friends have married into the houses he needs. When she dies is **[OPEN]**.

The decorated Pacific-war commander who leads the Polytechnic, Rafael Soberanes, and other academy-trained officials substitute measurements, numbered personnel, examinations and statutory procedures for the personal loyalties of the old armies. The method promises fairness across races and provinces but also reduces human beings to entries in large systems. Financial syndicates, officers and hereditary court factions compete to direct the machinery in anticipation of Elias's death.

<!-- CR-037: anonymous scripts and avoidance -->
**[APPROVED — CR-037]** Soberanes guards the machinery against patrons as well as factions. From the late 1870s candidates are known to their examiners only by number, and clerks recopy every script so that no hand is recognised and send it to examiners from another state. No examiner judges a candidate from their own state or college, officials take their first postings away from home, and every board that fills a chair includes outsiders. A patron, a family or a guild can still recommend a candidate; it cannot pass one.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** The examinations reach the officer corps in mid-career. Many of the old army's fighting officers fail them. Silas fails the mathematics and is capped below general rank; Elias refuses to intervene. The American officers' grievance against the examination state has his face.

The Polytechnic becomes the world's leading technical university system from the 1870s, with state-funded chairs, laboratories and technical schools drawing talent from Europe and Asia. Appointment to a chair has brought citizenship without a religious or national test since the 1850s. Tesla arrives in San Francisco in 1884; Steinmetz and Baekeland join in 1889. A fast patent office and the licensing of Polytechnic inventions turn the examination state's learning into firms, equipment and careers.

<!-- CR-034: the first chairs stay; Zeuner at Freiberg from 1871 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-034]** The Polytechnic's first chairs stay for what they have built. Zeuner keeps his students, his testing floor and his programme until 1871, when he goes to direct the Mining Academy at Freiberg, and from Freiberg he sends Pacifica engineers. By 1875 Carquinez alone has about ninety teachers and 1,400 students, as many as Vienna's polytechnic **[MODEL]**. **[PROPOSED]** Saxony's amnesty of 1862 would have let Zeuner teach at home; he stayed.

<!-- CR-036: californium, the college of 1866, Michelson and Morley, Ehrlich, Kitasato; CR-050-R1: the college named Ralston College -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** Its learning is not only applied. In 1873 Carquinez finds the metal Mendeleev had predicted beneath aluminium, in zinc blende the Sutro School sends down from the deep Comstock, and calls it californium, and the periodic table is proved right on a Pacifican specimen. Ralston College, founded in 1866 by San Francisco's merchant and banking houses of every faith, draws Sylvester from Carquinez and Cohn from the Methodists' college. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** It bears the name of William Ralston, its chief founder, whose fortune is the Comstock's silver, so that Carquinez, built on the gold royalty, and Ralston College become Pacifica's colleges of gold and silver. **[APPROVED — CR-036]** In 1868 it brings the chemist Edward Morley from New Jersey. In 1878 the Murphys boy Michelson begins there to measure the speed of light. Paul Ehrlich, who read Koch's anthrax paper as a student in Silesia, comes to Koch's laboratory that year and stains the cells no one could see. In 1887 Michelson and Morley's interferometer finds no ether wind. **[PROPOSED]** From 1885 Kitasato Shibasaburō works in Koch's laboratory, and Japanese medicine begins to learn from Pacifica.

<!-- CR-049: rival schools, a method that fails, the programmed machine -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Ralston College's founding in 1866 is the first great contest for talent. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** Both colleges claim the Calculation Office, and it becomes a shared service of the League with its machines at Carquinez, where Peirce stays. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Kate Layne marries Hugh Curran in 1866 and keeps her post. In 1867 Kate Curran finds that a checking rule Peirce devised has been discarding recurring observations as instrument faults, some of them hers. The register has kept everything, so she reconstructs the discarded readings, and they are real. Peirce has to acknowledge in public that the checking rule he was proudest of was wrong, and Genth puts the next computing money into independent checking and holds back the machines. She is credited only as an assistant, and she does not let it go. The colleges agree terms for recording omitted observations and contrary results, and the case is the first that the journals' corrections columns teach: the original claim, the correction and the name of whoever found the difficulty. The custom it founds will one day give credit back; this time it does not. Relay arithmetic enters service in 1868–1870, and limited preset sequences follow by 1872; the staff learn not to mistake a calculation that runs for an account that is true. Zeuner's leave-taking in 1871 becomes the model for every master's: it names his successors and his unfinished questions, and his instruments and notebooks pass on.

<!-- CR-049: the customs and the civic usage of mana -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Public provings of works, county showings, contests with declared terms and the welcomes and leave-takings of masters make skill visible, and fellows coming home from Japan, Hawaiʻi, the mountains and the islands shape these customs rather than merely undergo them. In Victoria's educational circle and Kalākaua's cultural programme, Hawaiian participants give the ethos a sacred register: achievement and remembered obligation acquire *mana* for some people, in the master's tools, the returning fellow, the works that stand. The historian Samuel Kamakau, whose series on the old ways runs in the Honolulu press from 1866, argues in print until his death in 1876 over whether the masters of the crafts can carry *mana* and how that sits with genealogy and the gods; the argument goes on after him, in the newspapers and from the pulpit. The founder who wrote "not I" ignores the kingdom's own judges in 1874–1876, and the ethos he founded has no answer for him.

<!-- CR-049: the atomic branch, 1864–1877 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Carquinez buys the Joachimsthal works' uranium residues, which nobody else wants, and separates them fraction by fraction, assaying each with the electroscope instead of the balance. In 1868 an active substance follows bismuth through every separation, a new element. In 1869 a far more active one follows barium and shows new lines in Genth's spectroscope. **[OPEN]** Their names; californium is the precedent for a Pacifican name that sticks. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The readings Peirce's rule threw away in 1867 were electroscopes near thorium preparations, drifting with the draughts because thorium gives off an active gas. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The gas is inert; its activity dies away at a fixed rate and leaves activity on whatever it touches; and a thorium product separated by chemistry loses its activity while the thorium regains it, both at rates Peirce's curves describe exactly. In 1870 Genth and Peirce conclude that one element is turning into another. The paper names them and not Kate Curran, whose readings found the gas, and her grievance becomes a quarrel the whole League hears. Morley confirms the result independently at Ralston College in 1872, and that year the journal's corrections column adds her name to the discovery, the first time the custom gives credit back. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** From 1872 the separation outgrows the laboratory, and the Superintendency's mill and the school set up a works at Carquinez that treats tonnes of residue a year. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** By 1876 Carquinez holds weighable salts of the barium-borne element and short-lived preparations of the bismuth-borne one, the first intense sources anywhere. In 1877 Morley's calorimeter shows the salt keeping itself warmer than its surroundings with no sign of slowing: gram for gram, more heat in a year than any chemical reaction gives once. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** From 1876 the works lends and sells sources to foreign laboratories under licence, each licence entered in a register, and for two decades Pacifica decides which laboratories abroad can do this physics at all.

<!-- CR-049: the atomic branch, 1876–1887, and the Light and Matter Works -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1876 Eugen Goldstein, a Jewish physicist from Gleiwitz who has named cathode rays in Berlin, comes at twenty-six to an assistant's post under Genth, with citizenship and the laboratory's discharge tubes. With the Sprengel pump the vacuum is good enough for an electric field to bend the rays as well as a magnet, and their ratio of charge to mass is the same for every gas and cathode. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The part of the barium-borne element's radiation that a magnet bends gives the same ratio for its slowest particles and a smaller one the faster they move, an anomaly no one in Pacifica can explain until Europe's relativity of 1905. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1879 Goldstein and Michelson announce a common negative constituent of matter. William Francis Hillebrand, born in Honolulu in 1853, has come home in 1878 from Oahu College, Carquinez and Bunsen's laboratory at Heidelberg. In 1879 he reports a gas driven out of pitchblende as nitrogen; in 1881, with a better spectroscope, he finds in it and in the gas collecting over the barium-borne salt the yellow line of the solar element, helium, and prints the correction to his own claim. That year Hillebrand and Goldstein bend the heavy, positive radiation slightly with strong magnets and electric plates, and it proves to be helium that has lost both its charges. Goldstein takes his doctorate in 1881 and becomes master of the laboratory. In 1881 Tom Talbert finds yellow uranium-vanadium ore on Roc Creek near the Dolores River, on the Western Slope that the 1865 line made Pacifican. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** It lies in Uncompahgre Ute country, which cannot be sold; the Republic leases the mining ground through its own office and pays the lease fee to the Ute, so the claims open and the Ute stay. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Hillebrand assays the ore in 1881–1882, and small mines work it for the barium-borne element's salt, with the uranium stockpiled.

**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Screens of Sidot's blende show each alpha particle as a single flash. In the counting room of 1882–1883 Kate Curran's observers count the flashes by eye in the dark as particles from the bismuth-borne source strike beaten gold leaf, and a few bounce back. Peirce computes what spread-out and concentrated charge would each do, and only a concentrated charge explains the rebounds. In 1883 Goldstein proposes that almost all of an atom's mass sits in a tiny positive core. Counting also gives the number of atoms that decay each second, so the heat of 1877 can now be stated per atom. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** In 1883, the year of the nucleus, Elias charters the works, the Western Slope mines and the source trade as one state champion with the state holding the shares, and makes Hillebrand, twenty-nine, its director. The laboratories stay in the Polytechnic, and the estate pays for them. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** It is the Light and Matter Works, named after the laboratory of 1859. By 1886 an element can have atoms of different weights: Hillebrand finds pairs of active substances no chemistry separates and lead from uranium ores lighter than common lead, and Goldstein's positive rays sort atoms by mass. In 1884–1885 Morley settles the ratio of oxygen to hydrogen to a few parts in ten thousand, and in 1886 his atomic weights show helium lighter than four hydrogens by about seven parts in a thousand. Peirce and Morley conjecture that the missing mass and the barium-borne salt's heat are the same thing, and say plainly that they cannot turn one into the other; the reason will come from Europe in 1905. In 1885–1887 Michelson's laboratory builds a counter in which a single particle sets off a small discharge in gas, read on a photographed electrometer, with the steady high voltage from Tesla's works. In 1887 the Japanese partnership sends Nagaoka Hantarō, newly graduated in physics at Tokyo, to Carquinez.

<!-- CR-049: the programmed machine -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** A programmed electromechanical calculator enters service in 1876–1879, its sequences written in a common procedural notation worked out in 1878–1882 from Babbage's notation and the operation tables of Lovelace's notes. It runs because Peirce's circuits were designed on paper first, and dependable operation becomes an achievement the Office is honoured for.

From 1880 a Development Fund directs seventy percent of each budget surplus, before debt repayment, into research, power, ports, rail and industrial credit. Development bonds and reserves above a crisis buffer supply further capital. A state savings and development bank channels household savings toward the champions. The state keeps a standing stock of public debt to support investment and the Reserve Bank's operations; the model's five-percent-of-GDP floor and three-percent reserve buffer are planning estimates. Productive investment leaves less cash idle and makes borrowing in a crisis part of the bargain.

The champions grow first on their own profits, and the savings bank lends them the nation's deposits. The state's shares, taken in exchange for charters and patents, make it an owner without building anything.<!-- CR-039: the state's shares, not the Crown's, from the 1870s --> Abroad the champions build with partners: Japanese and Chinese merchant houses and Gulf and Mexican investors take about a quarter of each venture. Before 1914 the champions sell bonds in London, Paris and Amsterdam, and in the 1920s on Wall Street and in Tokyo. Like the Union before 1914, Pacifica becomes a creditor through its Treasury and partly a debtor through its companies, and foreigners draw a growing income from its success. **[MODEL — CR-025]** By 1929 foreign holders own about $3.8bn of claims on the champions and draw about $280m a year from them.

The Panama Canal draws imperial labor and capital into dangerous tropical works. Engineers encounter disease, unstable ground, tropical rainfall, strikes and political resistance from neighboring states. Pacifica secures Colombian transit and neutrality rights, first refusal on the concession and control of the Panama Railroad in 1880. France begins the main excavation and bears its early disease and design failures. The regency later purchases the failed enterprise in 1889 and completes a lock-and-lake canal in 1900. Early Pacifican commitments concern the railway, treaty and technical participation, with the main construction burden falling after the purchase.

## 20. Kaʻanoe Ward and Julian's birth (1881–1887)

Kaʻanoe Ward, known as Noe, is born about 1860 in Lahaina to a settled New England ship's surgeon and a woman of a minor chiefly family tracing its Maui descent to Kahekili. During Honolulu's 1881 smallpox epidemic she nurses at the Queen's Hospital. **[APPROVED — CR-027]** The hospital is Queen Emma's foundation, and Noe's first patrons are loyalists. She passes the Polytechnic's medical examination in San Francisco in 1884, the first Hawaiian and one of the first women to qualify, and becomes physician to the failing founder in 1885.

Clinical, unsentimental and angry at waste, she tells Elias the truth about the body behind his legend. Her gallows humor pleases him, and her figures about the dying Hawaiian population command the attention that ministers struggle to obtain. Their relationship is real and clear-eyed. In 1886 his decree establishes the Hawaiian Board of Public Health, with money, quarantine powers and Noe as medical director. She builds district birth-and-death registers and begins vaccination in Honolulu and Maui; the unfinished work is her achievement, not a miraculous reversal of mortality in three years.

Julian is born in 1886. Noe agrees to his hānai adoption by Victoria, with Elias's written consent. Noe bargains for the health board, a place in the boy's life and Hawaiian upbringing; Victoria requires that he be raised as her son and successor. The two women respect and fear each other. One controls access to Elias's body, the other to the state. Noe hopes the Hawaiian people will stop dying and her son will sit on a restored Hawaiian throne; she lives to see neither.


## 21. The Korean settlement, the 124 counties and the death of the founder (1887)

The Port Hamilton settlement and the Chinese Customs appointment display an empire increasingly powerful through guarantees, finance and standards. The founder's final years also carry approved state programmes for fuel oil from 1876 and steam turbines from about 1885. Early oil finds, generator trials and later naval delivery dates are engineering targets to test. Tesla's 1884 arrival and the electrical institute established in 1886 connect Sierra hydropower, one AC standard, mining, irrigation, cities and future Asian equipment sales. Soberanes develops the professional administration and communications while foreign adventures pause around the succession. **[PROPOSED]** During Elias’s decline in 1883–1887 Victoria increasingly governs in practice; Keelung, the Customs appointment and Port Hamilton establish her record before she becomes regent.

The Constitution of the Realm of 1887 abolishes the states and draws 124 counties across their old boundaries, dividing Deseret among several of them. California and Hawaii remain historic lands with entrenched language and tenure guarantees, not sovereign members with vetoes. Thalassia is the capital, the Court of the Realm sits at Monterey and the Polytechnic is chartered at Carquinez. A regency article names Victoria, Queen of Hawaii, Regent of the Realm until an Act of Settlement establishes the Crown, and Julian Heir of the Realm. Elias's life office ends with him.

The constitution follows amendment procedure in form, with two-thirds of both chambers and founding-state ratification. Grants buy interior legislators; Hawaii's assent comes from the appointed Territorial Council created by the illegal ordinances of 1874–1876. Legal form does not restore the consent that made the original partnership. Hawaiian loyalists reject it.

Residual powers belong to the Realm; counties administer enumerated local work. The center controls credit, borrowing, immigration, armed force, networks and the general criminal and commercial code. County boards retain land taxes, roads, sanitation, primary schools and ordinary policing, with appointed judges and central standards. A Commissioner from the examined service executes the Realm's law and commands the reserve. He never serves in his home county, rotates at least every five years and may suspend a county act for thirty days for the Court's review. No county may borrow abroad; domestic borrowing needs Treasury consent.

<!-- CR-038: senators held to the age of sixty-eight -->
County boards choose one senator each. Appointments held to the age of sixty-eight form up to a quarter of the Senate, with twelve Polytechnic seats and eight aliʻi seats. The elected lower house becomes the Chamber of Deputies. The Court's constitutional chamber hears disputes; the Polytechnic's examinations, audit, General Staff and telegraph form a chartered estate with its own continuity. Its chief is chosen by the ruler from three graduate-council nominees and removed only with Senate consent. The Office of Public Measures, established in 1888, supplies the other independent estate, with twelve-year heads reporting to the Senate. The ruler appoints and dismisses ministers; they answer to the ruler, not the Chamber.

In August 1887 Elias dies. The news passes through the telegraph system he built. A son from his marriage to Victoria would have presented a widely recognized dynastic possibility, but that son is dead. Other claimants possess blood ties, military supporters, or control over parts of the state, yet no uncontested legitimacy. Victoria is both the widow of the founder and the reigning Queen of Hawaii. Julian is already Victoria’s adopted son and the intended successor, while his biological mother is still living. The negotiated pact takes effect; the empire consolidates without a succession war.

---

# PART V — Regency, the Japanese partnership and Julian’s succession (1887–1907)

## 22. The managed succession and the Donner Pass crisis (1886–1887)

Elias’s decline brings three adult power centres into negotiation. Silas, Elias's eldest son, leads the American military aristocrats, drawing on Confederate association, Auxiliary careers and Southern works. Kiyoshi, his second son by a Satsuma mother, leads the Japanese naval party. A decorated commander unrelated to the Vances leads the Polytechnic officers and communications establishment; Rafael Soberanes leads this estate from Carquinez. Each faction can block the others, but none can obtain power without an unaffordable civil war. Victoria’s adopted infant son is their acceptable successor, and she becomes regent without a war of inheritance.

**[APPROVED — CR-039]** Negotiations take place in Hawaii during 1886–1887 and the Truce of Thalassia is signed in spring 1887 while Elias lives. **[PROPOSED]** Victoria’s standing combines the founder’s widowhood, her reigning Hawaiian crown, Hawaiian aliʻi authority, control of harbours and her recent diplomatic record. The 1887 regency article supplies her authority despite the continuing legal wrong of 1874. Soberanes chooses institutions over a strongman; Sherman supports constitutional military allegiance rather than personal rule. His proposed June 1884 refusal of political power and February 1891 death remain working uses of his historical chronology.

The Truce is extra-legal before its terms become organic law. Silas's inland command sits at Sacramento, Kiyoshi's naval party at Pearl Harbor, Soberanes's Polytechnic at Carquinez; Victoria holds Thalassia and Honolulu. The named holders keep their offices for life, after which the Regent appoints with Senate consent. San Francisco holds mint, reserve, syndicates and champion headquarters but no government; Monterey holds the Court and Californio legitimacy. No single city can capture all the state’s functions, and the factions meet through the Crown. Los Angeles becomes a Crown city in 1910 for oil and film; Portland, Seattle and Salt Lake remain ordinary counties, with the Church steering a coherent Deseret Senate bloc.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** One armed standoff at Donner Pass in October 1887 tests the pact. The Polytechnic corps comes up to take over the Sierra telegraph under its charter; Silas's veterans hold the summit as the inland command's line. Caleb brokers a parley in the telegraph hut inside the snowshed. Before it, Abigail has told her son that Soberanes must not come down from that pass. When Soberanes says that Elias's last instruction to him was that the wire must not be divided, Silas draws and fires at him. Caleb steps across and takes the bullet. Dying in Silas's arms, he says: "Don't give it." Silas's officers wait for the order to advance, and he does not give it. Soberanes dictates the first dispatch: shot from outside the shed. The inquest finds a person unknown. Every man in the room is bound by the lie, and Silas is praised for the restraint of a man who fired. There is no campaign, general mutiny or civil war. Caleb's sacrifice remains the working implementation of the managed-succession direction.

## 23. Noe's death, the registers and the heir (1887–1889)

After Elias dies in August 1887, Noe retains a household, a mother's claim and her health work. As natural guardian she could claim a regency; Hawaiian loyalists who reject the ordinances begin to court her. **[APPROVED — CR-027]** With Emma dead since 1885 they also look to her cousin and heir Albert Kūnuiākea, by common knowledge a natural son of Kamehameha III, never acknowledged and the squanderer of his inheritance. Her rivalry with Victoria concerns the boy and Hawaii's future, not a simple contest for the dying man's affection.

She dies in the autumn of 1888 during a typhoid outbreak she is fighting. That is the official account, certified by Victoria's physician. Her servants swear she was poisoned. The registers disappear for a month and return in the new Office of Public Measures. Loyalists call it murder, and Silas's people spread the story as part of "the Regent's year": Caleb, the Donner telegraphist burned in his hut that January, and now Noe. Silas suspects his own mother; his mother suspects him. Neither asks. Victoria, the factional rivals and those who need a martyr have motives; the Chronicler can prove none guilty.

Victoria builds public legitimacy on Noe's honest figures. She completes vaccination across the islands in 1888–1892, brings Hawaiian-speaking nurses and clean water to plantation districts in the 1890s and reforms Kalaupapa. By about 1895 the population curve turns upward by Noe's method. Infant mortality becomes the state's sacred number, later carried into county league tables and the Gulf admission test. Care, rivalry with a dead woman and possible guilt remain inseparable to the observer.

Julian grows up speaking Hawaiian with Victoria, English with tutors, Spanish among Soberanes's circle and Japanese with the naval party. He later finds Noe's registers in the Office's archive. His two mothers have given the same state its measurements and its political uses; his later falsification in 1921 corrupts an inheritance more intimate than an administrative rule.


## 24. The Truce, the Japanese compact and the regency council (1887–1889)

The Truce supplies a governing balance through the life offices and Regency Council. The restored Chancellor is appointed by the Regent with Senate consent. Elias's enabling power lapses; Article XIV remains the emergency route. Victoria governs as Regent of a Realm whose crown has yet to be established, rather than as guardian obliged to surrender office on the boy's majority.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** The regency's working government sits in the chartered estates. The Constitution gives the Polytechnic its examinations, audit, General Staff and telegraph and the Office of Public Measures the Realm's numbers; the Crown holds its shares in the champions, and the Development Fund has its claim on every surplus. Five people at the head of the Polytechnic and the chartered estates run the technocratic state. In 1887 they are Soberanes for the Polytechnic and General Staff, Logan Turner for naval construction and heavy industry, Tesla for power, **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** William Francis Hillebrand for the Light and Matter Works, the atomic estate, **[PROPOSED — CR-027]** and William Randolph Hearst, San Francisco-born, who takes over the *Examiner* that year and makes the six-language weekly the Realm's press; from 1895 W. K. L. Dickson, recruited from Edison, gives it moving pictures. Seats pass to successors such as Baekeland, Doheny, Giannini and Montgomery, and after 1915 to Marcus Vance III; there are always five. San Francisco calls them the Five, Honolulu nā Kahuna, the priests and masters of their crafts. <!-- CR-052: Ryan in the power seat -->**[APPROVED — CR-052]** In 1924, when Tesla reaches sixty-eight, the power seat passes to Harris J. Ryan, raised in Pacifica and examined at the Polytechnic, the line-builder of the Realm's long transmission. <!-- CR-058-R1: Doheny in Turner's seat -->**[APPROVED — CR-058-R1]** About 1907, when Logan Turner reaches sixty-eight, the champions' seat passes to Edward L. Doheny, the oilman who has spent a decade in the Senate attacking the trust; he holds it until he reaches sixty-eight in 1924.

**[APPROVED — CR-027]** Their arrangement with the Crown is never written down. The Crown has no army beyond the Queen's Guard and no purse of its own; force, money, communications and the numbers sit in the estates. The Five have power but no legitimacy; Japan, the creditors, the Senate and the Hawaiian people accept them because the Crown sanctions them. The Crown does not touch their charters, appointments or the Development Fund; the estates never take sides against the Crown and never let a faction capture them. Victoria is the one person all five trust, because she shields their charters from the Senate and gives them prestige. Hearst wants spectacle and she wants honest numbers with the weak spots shown; he may dramatize the Union's failures, but he prints the Office's tables unaltered.

<!-- CR-049: the atomic estate among the Five; Hollerith at the Office -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** The atomic estate sits among the Five because the atom's great results come decades before anyone can use them, and with Hillebrand Honolulu's *nā Kahuna* has a native son among them. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** Herman Hollerith's punched cards tabulate Noe's registers from 1888 at the Office of Public Measures, whose heads report to the Senate and which has no seat among the Five. Peirce, nearly fifty, wants the registers' work for his relays and loses it to the younger man's cards, and the Calculation Office ends up borrowing the method that beat it.

<!-- CR-035: the Polytechnic examines its rivals -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-035]** The arrangement is tested in 1888, when Soberanes's examiners fail half of Santa Clara's engineers and a third of Oakland's. The rectors appeal to Victoria against the pass lists. She will not touch the estate's examinations, and they go back to Carquinez to bargain with the power that taught the Republic to examine the churches' colleges and is now their rival.

Kiyoshi has entered Kuroda Kiyotaka’s house in 1886 as an adopted son-in-law, marrying into it and taking the name Kuroda Kiyoshi. Japan gives him the name Elias withheld. His family position binds the naval party to a recognized Japanese house. Lady Chiyo belongs to another branch of that house; **[APPROVED — CR-026]** Kiyotaka's own son Kiyonaka remains heir of the main house. Kiyoshi's bride is Kiyotaka's adopted daughter Hyaku. After the victory of 1905 Kiyoshi founds his own branch house and is made Count Kuroda in 1907; his son will inherit it.

**[PROPOSED]** A compact with Tokyo is signed alongside the Truce. Japan backs the regency and holds its officers to the agreement; Pacifica guarantees Japan against Russia and supports its treaty revision with Europe. The western squadron’s command is reserved to officers of the Japanese naval party. Both states ratify renewals every ten years, in 1897, 1907 and 1917. Japan’s crown, domestic government and army remain Japanese. Examination, service and imperial careers gradually give Japanese-origin officers and financiers a stake in institutions seated in Pacifica. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** The compact runs until its fourth renewal falls due in 1927, when the Pacific Concord takes its place.

Victoria governs through bargaining. Elias’s enabling-law delegation lapses at the beginning of the regency, reassuring the factions while limiting her instruments. **[APPROVED — CR-022]** The 1888 restriction is presented as Silas's price, but it is what Victoria wanted, and she holds the Donner report. He takes the blame. In the Bread March of 1894 his border command detains the marchers, and he insists that they be fed and tried, not shot. Pacifica consolidates through 1887–1889, with foreign military adventures paused around the succession. The compact begins a relationship whose later Concord will depend on Japanese consent and on different understandings of who has gained command.

## 25. The regency's measures, continental settlement and Asian guarantees (1887–1902)

Victoria and Soberanes consolidate through examinations, finance and published comparisons. The 1888 Franchise Law gives each adult citizen one ordinary vote, a second for the civil examination and a third for a degree, commission or Polytechnic diploma. Each certified language beyond English on the Crown’s list adds a tongue vote. Tongue votes are uncapped. Referendums remain restricted to examined citizens. The Office of Public Measures builds on Noe's registers, and its county league tables reach the public through the Five's press: the six-language weekly, images, film and study tours. Victoria insists on honest numbers and Pacifica's weak spots appearing alongside its gains.

County autonomy is earned. Five consecutive years at the Office's four standards—infant mortality, examination passes, revenue per head and order—bring chartered status; three years below or declared disorder permit receivership. A board can be suspended for up to five years, renewable by the Senate. Measures grants follow results. Every Realm programme lapses after ten years unless the Office certifies it. County powers, boundaries and grades are organic law, amendable by both chambers without local consent. Victoria usually obtains obedience through prestige and comparison; Julian later uses commissions and ordinances more freely, giving county oligarchies reason to fight through their senators.

<!-- CR-037: appointment tables and the 1890 audit -->
**[APPROVED — CR-037]** The Office also counts who gets the posts. From 1888 it publishes, for every chartered body, college and licensed profession, how appointments and promotions compare with the examination lists, and a gap that lasts brings in the Office's auditors. The first great audit, in 1890, falls on the board of the savings and development bank in San Francisco. Its seats have passed for a generation among a few intermarried families, Yankee, Californio and Irish, and the examined clerks below them never rise. The auditors void three appointments and the families appeal to the Regent. Victoria will not touch the audit, and she refuses the newspapers' demand for quotas: the measure is the examination, not the mix. From then on officials declare their relatives and former students.

<!-- CR-037: the county rule and its limits -->
**[APPROVED — CR-037]** From 1890 each county's appointments are measured against its own pass lists, so a New Mexican county may be run mostly by Spanish speakers and a Hawaiian one by Hawaiians. A county loses marks for order when any group of examined candidates is appointed at under two-thirds the rate of the best-placed group, or when a single family, marriage network or congregation holds more than a third of its senior posts. Every county's treasurer, auditor, examiner and chief of police is posted from outside by the Office, in rotation, as the Commissioner is, even in chartered counties. The rules catch blood and faith better than school ties, and whoever calls an audit holds a weapon.

<!-- CR-038: retirement at 68, Vallejo College and its two sides -->
**[APPROVED — CR-038]** Victoria and Soberanes make the machinery guard itself. The regency extends the Constitution's age of sixty-eight for appointed senators to every senator, judge, Commissioner, estate head and senior officer, except the Truce's named holders. From 1889 the government offices left empty at Monterey when the seat moved to Thalassia house Vallejo College, beside the Court. Rising officers of the Polytechnic, the Office, the navy, the champions and the counties, women and men, spend a year there. Their rooms are assigned by lot across origins; their evenings are not. The engineers and managers take over Monterey's old Presidio barracks with their rowing and drinking clubs, and many of the women of the hospitals, schools and Office gather in the house they name after Victoria's Kaʻahumanu Society. A woman surveyor rows with the barracks crew; Polytechnic men sit at the House's lectures on sanitation. The barracks crowd of the 1890s will later be called the Carquinez men.

<!-- CR-038: inspectors, hard numbers, scholarships, official pay, county halls, results-based pride -->
**[APPROVED — CR-038]** The Office's inspectors arrive in a county without warning and check its returns against what is hard to fake: freight tonnage, coal and power, savings deposits and burials. They publish what they find. From 1888 a Realm scholarship binds its holder to five years' work in Pacifica, and a posted grant to five years in the county surgery, survey camp or island service it was made for. Each year the Senate prints senior officials' pay beside what the champions pay their managers. In the 1890s every county opens a hall for all its residents, with sick and burial funds, interpreters, night classes, notices of work and the county's tables on the wall beside the Realm's. An Office inspector holds it to the Office's minimum of services; above that it keeps its own language, festivals and character. In 1897 Fresno's hall rings its bell for a week when the county first meets all four standards. Pacificans learn to be proud of what the tables show.

<!-- CR-049: things work in this country -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Families honour schools, physicians, reliable works and successful fellows without knowing any of the science behind them. Things work in this country, and most people's honour rests on making them work. By 1900 the founders' circle has become several generations; Peirce is past sixty and Kate, Brandegee now by her second marriage, past fifty-five, the living memory of the careful account, the worthy opponent, the difficult journey and the person who makes the work dependable.

English is the Realm's language of command, the civil examination and business with the center. Spanish and Hawaiian are entrenched in their historic lands; further county languages are grants of organic law. Spanish acquires new commercial reach through the canal, Philippines and Pacific Latin America. Hawaiian remains protected on paper while schools operate in English, until 1914 restores Hawaiian-medium education and the Crown's Hawaiian oath and proclamation. No county has Chinese language status by 1932, though San Francisco courts use Cantonese interpreters. Japanese gains Realm standing in 1927; Hawaiian counties with large Japanese populations become trilingual in 1928.

Skilled entrants face no language test at entry: technical examinations can be taken in several languages, followed by a compulsory English bridging year. Naturalization and the civil examination require English. Guest contracts and wage disputes use interpreters, without requiring English; lack of English blocks the examination route to political power. Community language schools remain and gain subsidies from 1927. Additional interpreter certificates reward reach in Spanish and Hawaiian from 1888, Japanese and Chinese in the 1890s, and Russian from 1921.

In 1888 selective skilled and sponsored-family immigration replaces the open door. The Asian family programme continues, while unskilled Union arrivals are refused. The policy narrows the earlier Chinese labor promise and makes the bureau's favorable figures partly a result of selection. Confederate abolition in 1888–1889 ends the fugitive agreement; the Hapa ideal is proclaimed in 1889. Douglass and other critics see a door closed just as enslaved people become free. Whether Gulf planters quietly secured that result remains **[OPEN]**. Later mixed families inherit both the ideal and its exclusions.

The champions' demand for workers later broadens the routes into Pacifica. Skilled entry brings families and relatives; higher wages divert some European migrants who would have gone to the Union. When Washington begins to retain its own talent and compete for Europe's, Pacifica recruits through Japanese and Chinese universities and among skilled Koreans, Filipinos and Indians. Returning graduates also carry knowledge into the partners' own economies.

<!-- CR-036: talent from every faith and background -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** The examined come from every exile and every ambition. Forty-Eighters and Polish engineers of 1863 came early. German Catholic scholars and Jesuits come out of Bismarck's struggle with the Church, and Jews shut out of Russia's universities by the quota of 1887. Armenians come after the massacres of 1894–96 and Chinese reformers after 1898. Scandinavian, Scottish, Japanese and American graduates come simply for better laboratories and pay. They sit the same examinations, and the Realm takes them one by one, never as communities. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Not every offer is taken. In the 1890s Carquinez offers a chair to Fritz Haber, a Jewish convert at Karlsruhe still waiting for a professorship, and he stays in Germany, a German first. His synthesis of ammonia will give Germany the nitrates for its war.

<!-- CR-036: Victoria's decisive test of diaspora loyalties, 1891 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-036]** Victoria's decisive test of the diasporas' loyalties comes in 1891. Famine spreads along the Volga in the year Moscow expels its Jews. San Francisco's bankers ask her to send the Tsar nothing, and the nativist papers dare her to prove that the bankers do not govern. She sends grain on Pacifica's terms. Pacifican agents distribute it to the hungry of every faith, and the weekly photographs every station. The bankers get no say and the nativists no gesture. Both complain, and neither leaves.

From 1890 a guest-worker programme fills jobs left by resident growth and skilled migration. Sector quotas and an employer levy protect resident wages; three-year contracts provide inspected housing, wage protection and paid passage home. Workers may change employer after a contract and seek residence after six years and a trade test. The statistics bureau handles permits as routine administration. These routes sustain the industrial economy while leaving a class of workers outside citizenship.

<!-- CR-038: the guest contract's origins -->
