**[APPROVED — CR-038]** The programme's contract is the one Hawaii's plantations have used since 1856 and the railway and interior works since the 1860s, now made the Realm's law.

Texas abolishes slavery in 1884; Upper-South states follow, and an 1885 law frees those over sixty. Final Confederate abolition in 1888–1889 is financed by long compensation bonds secured on customs co-administered by Pacifica. Emancipation leaves sharecropping, coercion and contested citizenship. When Richmond misses coupons in 1893–1895, Pacifica's guarantee pays them, deepening dependence. No Confederate state is a Pacifican member in 1895.

The bureau's first great campaign follows Wounded Knee on Union soil in 1890. Photographs and reservation measures set it beside the Navajo treaty and Apache peace. Union critics answer with Bear River, the Chinese massacres, the Calamba evictions and the fugitive agreement. Prosecution and better treaties can support a comparison; they cannot make those wounds disappear. The depression, Coxey's Army and Pullman in 1893–1894 give the weekly further material.

<!-- CR-033: Native Californian recovery and the leakage of land -->
**[APPROVED — CR-033]** Protected in their homelands and paid for their work, Native Californians, reduced by disease to about two-thirds of their number at the gold discoveries, begin to grow again in the later 1890s. The Office's county tables show them poorer and sicker than their neighbours, beside its comparisons with Wounded Knee. The treaties hold, but long leases at low rents, annuity claims signed over to merchants for debts, and court rulings on boundaries and water keep moving land out of Native hands, and the Realm earns a fee on every lease. By 1932 they number about 130,000 **[MODEL]**.

In the Bread March of 1894 armed industrial marchers gather in Union Colorado and try to force the passes. A border post is attacked and men die. Pacifica detains hundreds and tries the armed men fairly, on film. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** Governor Waite's acquiescence helps the march gather. In 1897 the prisoners return under Whitney's commerce and arbitration treaty. **[OPEN]** The sentences and any detention settlement remain to be specified. **[PROPOSED]** The affair damages silver populism and revanchism while exposing the exclusions of Victoria's settlement.

<!-- CR-038: the 1895 trades congress and the Guest Workers' Aid -->
**[APPROVED — CR-038]** Victoria means Pacifica's own workers never to need a march. In 1895 a trades congress is chartered by law. It keeps sick and burial funds and the register of tradesmen who have passed the Polytechnic's trade tests, and with the employers it publishes a reference table of wages that contracts may cite. The contractors still hire, train and pay. Guest workers do not join the congress. Its Guest Workers' Aid, paid partly from the employer levy, finds them interpreters and lawyers in a dispute, and shelter and the passage home when an employer fails.

Pacifica rescues the Union's gold reserve in 1895. **[PROPOSED]** The price includes canal recognition, Asian-war neutrality and tariff concessions. Whitney wins the 1896 presidency with a supportive House; the Senate follows the 1898 state elections and recovery. He seeks competition through investment rather than reconquest, and keeps the Union neutral during the Primorye war. Revanchists call him bought and go underground. The Northern republic remains the continent's largest industrial power, with a navy capable of revival.

In October–November 1894 Victoria supports Japan's case for Korean independence with coal, equipment, credit and diplomatic cover, while keeping Pacifica out of combat. After the victories at Pyongyang and the Yalu she brokers an armistice: Korea independent and neutral under Chinese, Japanese and Pacifican guarantee, Chinese suzerainty ended, Japanese economic predominance, a moderate indemnity and commercial rights, with no mainland cession. There is no Liaodong cession, Port Arthur massacre or Triple Intervention. Queen Min survives and Gojong does not flee to the Russian legation. Japan's army resents the shortened victory; the Qing retain solvency and an ally in the regent.

The Formosan Revenue Convention of 1895 leaves the island Chinese. A Pacifican Director-General nominated by the Regent holds a Qing commission; the Republic later continues it. The administration controls the camphor monopoly, Keelung mines, Formosan customs and Keelung harbour trust, including pilots, lights, works and coaling rights. China keeps the governor, courts, police, land and taxes outside the specified revenues. Receipts pay the indemnity, Japan, Pacifica's fee and then the provincial treasury. Fong's Inspectorate collects revenue while his government backs Japan, deepening his divided duty. **[PROPOSED]** A smaller indemnity facility and Pacifican gold loan support Japan's gold transition in 1897.

**[PROPOSED]** Credit offered before Li Hongzhang's 1896 tour supports a Chinese-owned Manchurian railway and the Hankow–Canton trunk.

When Russia demands Port Arthur or a Korean harbor after Germany’s Kiaochow seizure, the guarantees are tested. Japan fights on land in 1898–1899; Pacifica holds the sea and lends, while China remains benevolently neutral. Japan gains the Ussuri region, Vladivostok, the Razdolnaya valley and all Sakhalin; China recovers the Amur’s north bank. <!-- CR-039: Japanese place names after 1899 -->Japan names its new province Enkaishū, the port Urajio and the island Karafuto. France remains neutral while its lending sustains Russia. Kamchatka, the Okhotsk coast and the Amur mouth at Nikolayevsk remain Russian until 1921. <!-- CR-042: Dalian stays Chinese; the Liaodong Gulf pledge and the Huludao option --> **[APPROVED — CR-042]** The Liaodong peninsula stays Chinese. Russia does not get Port Arthur, and Japan, which has fought to keep Russia out of it, does not take Port Arthur or Dalian for itself. Victoria negotiates the terms with the Empress Dowager and holds Tokyo to the armistice of 1894, which gave Japan no mainland territory. **[PROPOSED — CR-042]** Cixi's return to her friend is small and written down. China pledges that no part of the coast of the Liaodong Gulf will be leased or ceded to any power, and gives Pacifica first refusal on a harbour at Huludao, a sheltered bay on the gulf's western shore below Jinzhou, if a harbour is ever built there with foreign capital. In the year Germany takes Kiaochow, Pacifica takes nothing, and Victoria keeps the paper.

Russia loses Vladivostok before joining its trunk railway to the Pacific. Japan completes the eastern link and holds the terminal; the Manchurian section belongs to China, with Japanese running rights and a Pacifican railway board in wartime. Russia retains its western line into Transbaikalia and the capacity to threaten Manchuria. **[PROPOSED]** Japan retains the inherited Russian gauge, enabling through traffic before 1914 and the coalition’s later westward advance. Kiyoshi commands at sea and enters Vladivostok on the proposed service chronology.

The Confederacy fractures after emancipation and the debt crisis. **[PROPOSED dates]** A 1891 Deep South presidency and deadlocked 1897 election lead Virginia and North Carolina back to the Union in 1898–1899. Tennessee has been Union since 1865. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** In 1900, the year the canal opens, Texas, Louisiana and Arkansas form the Federal Republic of the Gulf, with Indian Territory as Sequoyah. At its founding the Republic signs the Gulf compact with Pacifica, which sets the tests any admission must pass. **[PROPOSED dates]** Austin is capital, New Orleans the economic center. A mutual defense pact and trade treaty bind it to Pacifica; citizenship stays separate. South Carolina, Georgia, Alabama, Mississippi and Florida remain the rump Confederacy.

<!-- CR-048: Spindletop, 1897 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-048]** Oil is worth more here than in the Union's history: Pacifica's oil programme since 1876 and its oil-fired navy since the 1890s keep prospectors drilling where they would otherwise have given up. **[PROPOSED — CR-048]** In January 1897 Pattillo Higgins's Gladys City company, drilling the salt dome at Spindletop near Beaumont with a Pacifican crew and a rotary rig, brings in the gusher four years before it came in our history. Texas has a fortune of its own in the year the Confederacy's election deadlocks.

The Gulf Republic adopts a strong executive, merit civil service, independent courts, equal civil rights and an examination franchise for specified referendums. Its official language is English, with French in the Louisiana parishes and Spanish in south Texas. The Defense Law test may be sat in English or Spanish, measuring competence rather than command of English alone. Pacifica considers admission only if the Republic adds usable power and freedom of action without adding risk or a veto. Five measures—infant mortality, literacy, real wages, homicide and typhoid—must meet the bar set by Pacifica's weakest former-territory state. The sixth test has two parts: Black and freedmen twenty-year-olds must pass the Defense Law's joint medical and literacy examination at no less than ninety-five percent of the white rate, and their infant mortality must be no more than 1.05 times the white rate. The inegalitarian examination state accepts differences between individuals, but refuses a member that wastes a large part of its people or leaves an underclass a rival could arm.

The compact also requires registers covering at least ninety percent of births, deaths, land titles and taxpayers; revenue sufficient for the common navy and debt quota; and a limit on debt held by Union creditors. Gold, standard-gauge rail, the AC standard, Crown-share concession law and ports open to the common navy must accompany examinations for office and franchise, recognition of the continental settlement and a common foreign policy. Every measure must hold for twenty-five consecutive years before Pacifica will consider admission, and Pacifica may demand a second generation. The Republic's leaders doubt they can meet the challenge; Pacifica is in no hurry to inherit problems they cannot solve.

<!-- CR-058: the oil law and its ceiling -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** The compact also writes Texas's antitrust law into the Republic's founding. Under its law of 1889 Texas had expelled Standard's hidden affiliate Waters-Pierce in 1900 for secret combination and price agreements, not for its size, and it expels the company again in 1907. The Republic bans secret affiliates, rebates and price agreements and separates the stages of the business: no refiner may own more than a minority of producing wells, trunk pipelines are common carriers at published tariffs, and no refiner may refine more than fifty-five percent of the Republic's crude. The ceiling is the compact's, so the Pacifican trust's share is a treaty limit, not an accident. The trust owns refineries, tankers and canal terminals and minority stakes in fields, and buys crude at posted prices; it earns its share as the best buyer, because it has the canal and the Asian markets. Standard's method, fields and pipelines owned through hidden affiliates, is what the law forbids, so Standard buys Gulf crude at the border like anyone else. Gulf populists, Huey Long among them, call the ceiling a foreign quota.

Oil royalties from Spindletop and later Sequoyah finance the Gulf's own schools, health services and statistics. Registration initially makes its reported conditions look worse. Compulsory schooling, clean water, mosquito and hookworm control, licensed midwives, Black teacher training and county clinics attack the gaps. Laws against debt peonage, the end of convict leasing, written sharecropping contracts, farm credit and a professional constabulary challenge planter power. The 1916 Sequoyah settlement secures equal citizenship and land for freedmen. Pacifica supplies engineers, credit and statistical standards while the Gulf chooses its means.

The five general measures first pass together in 1925. They hold narrowly through the Mississippi flood of 1927, when Hoover runs relief and the Gulf prosecutes planters holding Black refugees in levee camps. Its published numbers remain honest. Planters oppose both the cost of Black health and schooling and the examination franchise; Huey Long fights that franchise in the name of the common white voter and loses. Black Gulf leaders divide over a bargain that brings protection and opportunity while treating their people as a resource. The 1931 oil glut breaks the general-measures streak, and both parity tests remain far from passing in 1932. Admission is outside the novel: even a late-1930s first success would put the earliest conceivable entry in the mid-1960s. The Gulf ends as an ally, oil partner and protégé with a course for the next century.

**[PROPOSED]** Sequoyah surveys further define the oil partnership; Mobile's lease and Birmingham works remain treaty rights inside the rump, with Galveston the fallback. Galveston's seawall is built in the 1890s; the 1900 storm strikes a protected city, with the residual damage unchosen.

<!-- CR-048: the rump Confederacy and the long emergency -->
**[APPROVED — CR-048]** The rump that remains governs from Montgomery. **[MODEL]** In 1900 just under half its seven million people are Black, far more in the Delta, the Black Belt and the Lowcountry, and the share falls every decade as people take the road north: by 1932 the rump has some nine million, fewer than three in ten of them Black.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** The Protocol of Richmond of 1898 divides the Confederate debt by rules Montgomery's own delegates cannot fault. The war debt follows the customs that secured it, and the compensation bonds and the coupons Pacifica's guarantee paid follow the owners who were compensated, who lived in the Deep South because Texas and the Upper South had abolished by their own laws. Virginia and North Carolina pay their small share in cash, borrowed in New York, and Pacifica's customs officers leave Norfolk and Wilmington. The Gulf turns its share into a loan of its own on Spindletop's royalties at its founding, and Pacifica's customs officers leave Galveston and New Orleans. The rump keeps more than two-thirds of the whole. Pacifican customs officers still sit in Charleston, Savannah, Jacksonville and Mobile, and the coupons are paid before the schools. Pacifica offers Montgomery the Gulf compact in 1900, unchanged. Montgomery never signs, because the sixth test would oblige it to register, school, examine and keep alive the people it rules, and the offer is reprinted every year. Cotton carries the state until the weevil crosses the river in 1907; after it, the modern places in the rump are foreign: Pacifica's Birmingham works and Mobile station, Union money in Florida. **[PROPOSED — CR-048]** Convicts are still leased to the mines, but not to Pacifica's, which pay the Realm's wage-board rates and hire more Black miners than any employer in the South.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** Pacifica reads its 1871 guarantee narrowly: it protects the Confederate States from foreign invasion, not from their own people. It refuses the planters' calls for help, sells arms to no one and offers a mediation that neither side accepts. The guarantee, which passes to the rump in 1898, still keeps Union regulars out, so the trouble can never be ended by war. Pacifica's part is a thin layer of money and a loud honesty. From 1901 the statistics bureau funds Du Bois's Atlanta Studies on the bureau's counting rules; the Office of Public Measures buys pictures from Black photographers at its standard rates; Carquinez offers fellowships to Black scholars the Union will not appoint. Every payment is in the Realm's published accounts. Each year the Office prints the Montgomery table, the compact's measures for the rump beside the Gulf's and, on the same yardstick, the Black districts of Union cities. The charter forbids a false figure. Where the camera points is a choice. **[PROPOSED — CR-048]** Du Bois, who takes Pacifica's grant, says in print that the same Pacifican customs officers collect the coupons that come before the rump's schools.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** The revolution never comes. In 1906 Black families in Atlanta's Brownsville district arm against a mob and a pogrom nearly becomes a rising. On 16 October 1910, the anniversary of Harpers Ferry, Josiah Haynes, a Delta-born veteran of the Union's Black cavalry, crosses from Memphis with forty men, opens the camps at the Parchman prison farm and calls the Delta to rise. It does not rise. The militia and the posses take or kill his men within four days, the reprisals fall on families who never heard of him, and Mississippi hangs him in December while half the North calls him a martyr. The emergency becomes chronic, and the movement it feeds does not stop at the border.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** Virginia and North Carolina came back into the Union on fundamental conditions: every resident a citizen, no citizen's vote taken away except for crime, no Confederate debt and no compensation for the Union to pay, and a general amnesty that keeps the Confederacy's cabinet, congressmen and generals out of federal office for ten years. Wilmington's white supremacists overthrow its elected government in November 1898 while North Carolina's act is before Congress, and Congress adds federal election supervisors there for ten years. The white machines accept the conditions on paper and then write poll taxes and tests equal on their face, Virginia in 1902 and North Carolina in 1910 once the supervisors go. The Union's Supreme Court will not strike them down; Congress can neither enforce the conditions nor drop them. George Henry White holds eastern North Carolina's seat until 1910, and the new Southern Democrats give Wilson his majority in 1912. Washington must answer Springfield in 1908, East St. Louis in 1917 and Chicago in 1919, segregate its service under Wilson and half undo it, alternately appeasing and striking at an assertive Black minority it can no longer ignore. Each concession raises Black expectations, and each backlash radicalises the next demand.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** The cost falls on Roosevelt's programme. Garrisons and refugee relief draw on the money meant for laboratories, and when letters on Haynes's men name the Northern professors who paid for his raid, Congress passes the Parchman Act of 1911, attaching loyalty and neutrality oaths to the programme's grants, so the instrument built to keep talent at home helps send it abroad. Ernest Everett Just, born in Charleston and refused a research post in the Union, signs the Parchman oath at Howard, and after Chicago's riot takes a Carquinez fellowship in 1920 to survey Kāneʻohe Bay; he is on the reef when the Crown Prince dives it. Conspiracy-minded Union writers, most of them revanchists and loudest in McCormick's *Chicago Tribune*, say Pacifica made the whole crisis. They cite the Atlanta grant, the raid backer who once held a Carquinez fellowship, the Office's purchases, Carnegie's Tuskegee gift and *The Crisis* carried as freight on Pacifican lines. All of it is public and lawful; none of it proves design. The Office learns the raid backer's name from the Union's newspapers. Pacifica's attention is on London. **[OPEN — CR-048]** Who suffers the Act's celebrated dismissal, the case every European scholar reads, is undecided.

**[APPROVED — CR-048]** After 1916 the movement's mass belongs to Marcus Garvey. His divisions spread through the Delta and the Black Belt, and the generation that watched Parchman fail joins him instead of raiding: pride, uniforms and the road out take the place of a rising. Montgomery tolerates a man who preaches separation, and in Atlanta in 1922 he meets the Klan's Edward Young Clarke. Emigration becomes the one programme the rump's populists and the Black nationalists share, and the rump cannot pay for it; its revenue is pledged to the coupons. Garvey makes the Pacific order his proof of what non-white peoples can build, a Hawaiian queen at its origin and a brown crown at its head, though in Colón in 1911 he had seen West Indians paid well on Pacifica's canal and offered no road to its citizenship. The *Tribune* calls his praise Pacifica's payroll. Pacifica answers by refusing him in public. Its yards sell the Black Star Line no ships, its finances having failed the published credit test, and Honolulu declines his lecture. Garvey is a British subject whom London hates, and Julian will not let London think Pacifica turns Garvey's race against its empire in the years before the Concord. In 1927 the Union deports Garvey to Jamaica in concert with the British, and Thalassia, which watches the two services work together for the first time, refuses him entry.

**[PROPOSED — CR-048]** The rump's civil religion is the Lost Cause. The second Klan is refounded on Stone Mountain in 1915 and travels north; the carving on the mountain begins in 1923. Mill villages live with pellagra and hookworm, and populists who once courted Black farmers win office by hating them. Black life gathers around Tuskegee, Atlanta's colleges and the churches, with the road north always in view. Across the river the Gulf is the mirror. In 1921 its Republic Constabulary stops the mob at Tulsa. In 1927, while the Gulf prosecutes planters holding refugees in its levee camps, Greenville on the rump's bank holds them at gunpoint, and Montgomery takes Hoover's relief only through its own committees and refuses Pacifican inspectors. The Office films both banks.

Cuba becomes independent after the Maine crisis of 1898; Puerto Rico goes to the Union. Pacifica stays neutral while its forces support the Primorye war. Tomás Estrada Palma, based with the exile junta in New Orleans, becomes Cuba's first president. Pacifica, the Gulf Republic and the Union jointly guarantee Cuban independence, allowing intervention only by agreement of all three and no foreign bases. **[PROPOSED]** Pacifica buys discounted colonial debt secured on Cuban customs, funds Finlay's yellow-fever work and acquires exclusive Nicaraguan canal rights. Victoria suppresses her engineers' suspicion that the Maine suffered a bunker fire; that suspicion remains evidence, not a proven cause.

The French Panama enterprise is bought in 1889. A lock-and-lake design and mosquito control guide the regency's work. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** The canal opens in 1900. **[PROPOSED]** A Galápagos station protects the approach; Colombia's war, from 1899, threatens the transit. The canal makes earlier Gulf access less indispensable without dissolving the partnerships built around it.

**[PROPOSED]** Cheap network moves fill the intervals: Baring-crisis gold loans, Chilean nitrate and diplomacy, Tutuila through the Samoan settlements of 1889 and 1899, Pearl Harbor works, a cable through Midway and Guam to Manila, electrical equipment for Asia and wireless.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** In Samoa the hurricane of March 1889 wrecks the German warships at Apia while a Pacifican cruiser steams out to sea; the Berlin Act of June makes Germany, Britain and Pacifica the protecting powers, and the partition of 1899 gives Pacifica Tutuila. In the Philippines the land board's evictions at Calamba in 1890–91 turn José Rizal from a physician into a critic. In 1892, when he founds the Liga Filipina, the Regent answers with elected provincial juntas. The Philippine Office of Public Measures grades each province: Pampanga, Bulacan, Batangas, Ilocos Norte, Cebu, Iloilo and chartered Manila first, the Cordillera and Moroland administered. The evicted tenants are resettled at New Calamba on the Pacifican Borneo company's land at Marudu Bay as a self-governing colony. When cholera returns in 1888, the quarantine at Mariveles and the ordinance towns hold it to a few hundred deaths, where Spain's islands had buried twenty thousand. The Office's tables say so in every province. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** In 1886 the peso goes onto a gold-exchange basis, ending the silver chaos Spain had left. Its reserve is built from the profit on coining a token peso and held in San Francisco, and the Banco Español-Filipino remains the bank of issue. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Manila hemp makes the islands indispensable: every navy's rope and every wheat farmer's binder twine is spun from it, and a Pacifican cordage champion spins most of it.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** In August 1896 the excluded rise: artisans and clerks of Tondo and Cavite, perhaps eight thousand, in a secret society with no ilustrados in it, over rice prices, estate installments and debt. The chartered towns refuse to join, Rizal declines to endorse them, and gunboats seal Manila Bay and the Cavite shore. The rising lasts weeks. A German cruiser division arrives to protect German nationals; it finds Pacifican ships in strength and leaves for China. A young advocate, Felipe Calderón, defends the accused, and the Regent commutes the sentences. The Union's illustrated weeklies still call her Pacifica's Weyler. In the spring of 1897 a steamer bought in Japan by Filipino exiles, a pan-Asian patron and a Chinese revolutionary, with a vice-minister looking the other way, is followed from Nagasaki and stopped in the Balintang Channel. Pacifica protests in private, because Japan's war with Russia is near and Tokyo needs the fleet. The vice-minister goes, the ship goes back, and its rifles find their way to the Chinese revolutionaries. The exiles learn that Japan will not take their side against Pacifica, and the southward school in Tokyo learns what acting without the cabinet costs.

The approved Klondike policy holds the Alaska boundary firmly while offering Canada fair transit; a proposed Skagway–Whitehorse railway, assay and banking capture route income from Canadian gold. **[PROPOSED]** Honolulu and San Francisco shelter Chinese reformers and revolutionaries after 1898; a limited Boxer relief contingent promptly withdraws and turns its indemnity into scholarships. A settlement with Britain during the Boer War fixes canal, Alaska, Samoa and Pacific recognition before the next stage of rivalry. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Pacifica recognizes British Fanning as the station of the Pacific Cable; Britain recognizes Pacifican sovereignty over the equatorial islands from Palmyra to Baker.

<!-- CR-058: seals, salmon and the western waters -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Canada learns to conduct its own diplomacy with Pacifica, on the business London knows least: seals, salmon and rivers. Pacifica owns the Pribilofs and makes no claim that the Bering Sea is closed; **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** in the 1890s **[APPROVED — CR-058]** it proposes a conservation regime and seats Canadian delegates at the table, and the four-power convention bans pelagic sealing and gives Canada fifteen percent of the Pribilof catch, fifteen percent of the Commander Islands' and ten percent of Robben Island's. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** About 1906, before the Union and Canada make their own treaty, **[APPROVED — CR-058]** Pacifica and Britain, for Canada, with Canadians doing the negotiating, sign a treaty on the western waters: the Columbia, the Kootenay, the Okanagan, the Similkameen, the Skagit, the Pend Oreille, the Fraser's salmon and the Alaskan rivers of 1871. Its joint commission is a commission of equals with powers to regulate as well as to investigate, the strong commission Canada wanted, and Pacifica, downstream on the Columbia, rejects the doctrine that an upstream state owes its neighbour nothing. When Canada and the Union make their treaty in 1909, Canada asks the Union for what Pacifica has already granted and gets less; its quarrels with the Union over the Chicago diversion, the St Mary and the Milk and Niagara go on in the east. Canadians learn that a treaty with Pacifica is a treaty and a treaty with the Union a draft for its Senate. <!-- CR-058: the 1911 election -->In 1911 nobody in Canada fears annexation by a Union that has lost two wars, but Laurier still loses narrowly to Borden on 21 September: the Canadian Pacific's Van Horne sets out to bust reciprocity, the Toronto Eighteen and the Montreal banks defend the east–west economy in which Pacifican capital and the Vancouver–Asia trade now sit, Bourassa splits Liberal Québec over the naval law of 1910, and the West's wheat can leave through Vancouver and the canal. Canadians also remember that the Union cancelled reciprocity in 1866 and the fisheries articles in 1885. Pacifica does nothing visible.

Gold also comes from Alaska's Juneau, Nome and Fairbanks, Nevada's Tonopah and Goldfield, and the Unsan concession granted by Korea in 1897. Foreign concession gold reaches San Francisco's reserve through sale to the Reserve Bank against credit. It is neither Pacifican mineral ownership nor domestic GDP. These sources widen the financial base without making every discovery an automatic public windfall.

### Industry beneath the measures

Coal from Washington, Utah, Rock Springs and New Mexico and partner contracts in Japan, Keelung and Kaiping supply the earlier fleet's colliers and stockpiles. The oil programme of 1876 and turbine programme of about 1885 lead to oil-fired torpedo boats, protected cruisers, turbine destroyers in 1893–1895 and turbine cruisers by 1898. Submarines follow from 1900 and all-big-gun turbine capital ships from 1903. Britain's Dreadnought of 1906 answers Pacifica's ships; its arrival causes no Pacifican technical or fiscal shock. Aircraft carriers follow from 1922. The design dates are approved narrative targets whose engineering and procurement capacity still require evidence.

National champions grow outward from Polytechnic patents, infrastructure and Asian markets. The Crown receives twenty-five percent in chartered and concession industries—oil, rail and ports, telegraph and cable, power, and shipping on the canal route. Competitive steel, motor, chemical, electrical, film, aluminium and aircraft firms license patents in exchange for eight percent and a golden share blocking foreign sale. Banking has no Crown equity stake and remains under Reserve Bank regulation. Private firms retain management and competition.

The oil trust refines Gulf crude from Spindletop onward, sells lamp oil across Asia and supplies bunkers to Pacific shipping. Steel draws on western coal, hydropower, Philippine and Chilean ore to serve Pacific rails, ships and ports. In 1901 a San Francisco syndicate buys Carnegie Steel and combines it with the Pacifican mills as Pacifica Steel, the world's first billion-dollar company. The syndicate pays the $480m price in paper, Pacifica Steel gold bonds and preference shares, and Carnegie becomes the largest creditor of a Pacifican company. The Union's great apostle of peace lives on Pacifican coupons, paid twice a year in gold. The sale succeeds and produces lasting Union anger at Pacifica and at Wall Street's foreign ties.

A Pacifican engineer and firm open the moving motor assembly line in 1911. Chemicals develop from licensed dynamite and naval powder to electrochemistry, plastics and drugs. The AC standard spreads with Pacifican equipment and credit through the Pacific, East Asia, Latin America and the Persian Gulf oil coast. British India and most of Africa remain beyond its general reach. Cables, radio, rail concessions, shipping and underwriting connect these industries; Los Angeles film and sound, Hawaiian aluminium and aircraft add new products. Patent and corporate control make the network profitable without making every trading country Pacifican territory.

<!-- CR-052: the oilfields found early -->
**[APPROVED — CR-052]** The state survey of the 1876 programme maps California's anticlines from the start, and the fleet's and the railways' demand for fuel oil drives the drilling, so the limit on the oil is the depth a well can reach, not knowledge. **[MODEL — CR-052]** The shallow fields of the Los Angeles basin, Los Angeles City and the Puente and Brea hills, come in during the early 1880s; Kern River, Coalinga, McKittrick and Midway–Sunset follow in the San Joaquin by the 1890s, and Huntington Beach about 1895, at the deepest reach of cable tools. Rotary drilling from Spindletop carries the wells to Signal Hill, Santa Fe Springs and Elk Hills in 1902–1910, and the Gulf's Hughes bit of 1909 takes them to Wilmington and the deeper sands; Kettleman Hills, which needs a seven-thousand-foot well, waits until 1923. **[APPROVED — CR-052]** From the 1910s the state survey's reservoir engineers keep the fields' pressure up with gas and water, fifteen years before the Union learns how. **[MODEL — CR-052]** Home output is about 74 million barrels in 1900 and 228 million in 1913, peaks at about 304 million in 1924 and is 255 million in 1929. **[APPROVED — CR-052]** Haste brings the oil forward; it does not make more of it. In the later 1920s the old fields decline, and the trust ships home through the canal all the Gulf crude it controls beyond its Asian contracts. Coal from Utah's Carbon County, Rock Springs, New Mexico and Washington, raised to about four times its historical output, carries the railways and mills that cannot get oil, and the Realm imports no coal.

<!-- CR-058: Doheny, the railway fuel and Mexican oil -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Edward L. Doheny, born at Fond du Lac in 1856, leaves school at sixteen for the West, drives mules for a boundary survey and then works for the state survey, which from 1876 maps California's anticlines, and its geologists teach him to read rock. In the 1880s he mines at Kingston, New Mexico, where he marries Carrie Louella Wilkins in 1883 and makes a friendship with Albert Fall that never becomes a transaction. He reaches Los Angeles about 1890, after the survey has found the city field, and wildcats on its fringes; his gift is selling. He wins the railways' fuel contracts, the Santa Fe's among them, and becomes the largest independent beside the trust. In 1900 the president of the Mexican Central, A. A. Robinson, invites him south because his locomotives need fuel oil; the Mexican Petroleum Company's wells at El Ébano come in in 1901, the year after the canal opens, so Tampico crude can also go west. Its Huasteca company brings in Cerro Azul No. 4 in February 1916, the greatest gusher the world has seen, and his holding company, Pan American, builds a tanker fleet for the canal. In Mexico his companies contend with Pearson's El Águila and with the Union's Standard. He pays the Crown its quarter, resents it, and from about 1896 sits in the Senate for Los Angeles as the voice of the independents against the trust. <!-- CR-058-R1: the critic brought inside -->**[APPROVED — CR-058-R1]** About 1907, when Turner reaches sixty-eight, the Five bring the critic inside: Doheny leaves the Senate for Turner's seat, the seat of naval construction, heavy industry and the champions, where the Realm's fuel policy now lives, and holds it until he reaches sixty-eight in 1924. A rival who keeps winning is safer at the table than outside it. Under the rules against capture he keeps the ownership of his companies and gives up their management, to Canfield until Ned is old enough. When Mexico's constitution of 1917 gives the subsoil to the nation, Doheny the oilman wants intervention, and Doheny of the Five must enforce the Realm's refusal on his own company. He does it, furious, and his companies take concession terms, because Pacifica's own model is state-owned subsoil, concessions and a Crown share. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** Pan American, under the golden share, is never sold abroad, and there is no scandal to force a sale. In 1919–1921 his money for the Irish republic is a private embarrassment to Thalassia, all the sharper because it comes from one of the Five while Britain is inside the coalition in Russia; Thalassia leaves it private, it costs him with Victoria, and London remembers. His son Ned takes over the business in the 1930s.

<!-- CR-055: the Columbia built early, and its electrochemistry -->
**[APPROVED — CR-055]** The Columbia is built early, because Pacifica has uses for its power that no other country has. **[MODEL — CR-055]** The power estate's hydro companies decide Bonneville, on the lower river at the Cascades, in 1908, and it gives first power in 1913; Rocky Reach above Wenatchee follows, decided in 1914 and running in 1919, then Wells on the Methow reach (1921, power 1926), McNary above the Umatilla (1922, power 1927) and Chief Joseph (1926, power 1931). **[APPROVED — CR-055]** Chief Joseph is built lower than the site allows so that salmon can climb it. Every dam is laddered, and each is leased from the peoples of its reach under a compact that pays a royalty on every kilowatt-hour, sells their villages electricity at cost, gives replacement fishing sites and publishes the fish counts before their observers. None is built where salmon cannot pass: there is no dam at The Dalles over Celilo Falls, none on the lower Snake, and none at the Grand Coulee over Kettle Falls. The Bonneville compact of 1908, with the Cascade and Wasco bands and the Yakama, Warm Springs and Umatilla fishing peoples, sets its own terms a year before the Me-Wuk lease. **[OPEN]** The peoples' leaders and the councils' names. **[APPROVED — CR-055]** The river's electrochemistry comes a decade before the Union's: electrolytic copper refining at Great Salt Lake from 1905, electrolytic zinc at Spokane on the Coeur d'Alene ores from 1908, magnesium from Great Salt Lake brine from 1909 and from seawater at Tacoma from 1924, ammonia on the Columbia's electrolytic hydrogen from 1915 under a BASF licence of 1913, and furnace phosphorus on the upper Snake from 1915. **[PROPOSED — CR-055]** The cells' residue is rich in heavy hydrogen. The Works recognises it in 1916–1917, beside the proof of the light uranium isotope; the isotope itself appears in Dempster's published survey, but the Works keeps to itself that the Columbia's cells concentrate it by the ton.

<!-- CR-049: the Works' leverage, the valve and the neutron -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-049]** From 1888 to 1907 the counters, the neutron and the hunt for new activities run on the Light and Matter Works' sources. Ralston College, a private college, depends on them like every laboratory in Europe. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1891 the Ames plant near Telluride sends alternating current to the Western Slope mills. Joachimsthal remembers the cheap years: from about 1890 Vienna raises its prices for residues and begins extracting the barium-borne element itself. The current that crosses the vacuum from a hot lamp filament is explained at once by the electron of 1879, and Tesla's wireless work for the navy needs faint signals strengthened; by about 1895 his estate and the Works build valve amplifiers, and with them the counter records single particles reliably. <!-- CR-050: the subscription trade and Langmuir -->**[APPROVED — CR-050]** Because the bismuth-borne preparations lose half their strength in a few months, the Works sells them by subscription, renewed several times a year. The valves of the 1890s are soft and erratic. In 1903 Irving Langmuir, a Brooklyn-born graduate of Columbia's School of Mines, comes to Tesla's electrical institute instead of Göttingen and becomes Tesla's student; about 1908 he makes the first hard-vacuum valves. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The bismuth-borne source, put against beryllium, then gives off something that no magnet bends, that goes through lead, and that knocks hydrogen nuclei out of paraffin with more energy than any ray could give them. Goldstein reads it as an extremely hard ray and refuses Nagaoka's request to measure the recoils. Ralston College gives Nagaoka Michelson's valve-amplified counter, and Peirce works the recoils of hydrogen and nitrogen through the conservation of energy and momentum to a neutral particle of about hydrogen's mass. Ralston College announces in 1899. Carquinez confirms it in 1901, and Goldstein accepts it then; he keeps his chair and the credit for the nucleus, but the next generation's best students go to Ralston College. The neutron is public. From 1902 to 1907 laboratories on both sides of the world slow neutrons in paraffin and water, fire them into every element, uranium among them, and report heavier elements that are not there.

<!-- CR-050: Europe answers Pacifica's data -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** The nucleus of 1883 proves an atom that classical physics says should collapse, and Balmer's formula of 1885 for hydrogen's lines makes the puzzle sharper. Kelvin, Lorentz, Larmor, FitzGerald and Poincaré build models that fail. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** At Carquinez in the 1890s Nagaoka proposes a ring of electrons around the core, the brave Pacifican model that fails. In the late 1880s Peirce's fits to the scattering from foil after foil give each core a charge near half its atomic weight and in the order of the periodic table, so charge, not weight, orders the elements while Mendeleev is alive to see his table's anomalies explained. Morley's heat of 1877 has already ended Kelvin's objection to Darwin about the age of the Earth. Vienna founds an institute for the barium-borne element in the 1890s on the salt it learned to keep after the cheap years. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Planck reaches the quantum in Berlin in December 1900 by his own road, and Einstein's light quanta and relativity follow in 1905. Physics has needed something like the quantum since 1883, and Pacifica has measured the effect of light on metals precisely since the 1890s, so the quantum is accepted almost at once. In 1907 Arthur Erich Haas in Vienna joins Planck's quantum to the nucleus of 1883 and Balmer's formula and derives hydrogen's spectral constant: Europe answers Pacifica's question, as Einstein answered Morley's deficit. Between 1906 and 1910 a European astronomer, reading Morley's helium deficit with Einstein's relation, suggests that the sun shines by turning hydrogen into helium. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** Bohr extends the quantum atom to heavier atoms in 1911–1913, and quantum mechanics comes in 1922–1924. The Kaiser Wilhelm Society (1911) is set up explicitly to compete with Pacifica.

<!-- CR-050: the dangers published -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** Pacifica publishes the dangers, and no craze for the barium-borne salt ever takes hold. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** In the 1880s the Works' physicians record burns and blood changes among the people who handle its sources. From 1888 the exposure registers of miners, mill hands and laboratory staff are tabulated on Hollerith's cards at the Office of Public Measures, and Kate Curran's counting room is the first group studied. In the 1890s the link between the mines' active gas and the miners' cancers is proposed, and ventilation rules follow on the Western Slope and in the Ute lease's mines; treatment of tumours with the sources begins at Carquinez and Honolulu, and the Works' own protection rules become a public standard about 1900. About 1910 Pacifica convenes an international conference on protection from the barium-borne salt and from the rays of the discharge tubes, honest science and diplomacy at once, which also gives the Works' slow licensing of strong neutron sources abroad a public reason. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The public learns to think of activity as medicine and danger, not power. The popular idea of atomic energy is thirty years older than Europe's, a theme of romances and of Hearst's press, but so is the belief that the rate of release is fixed by nature and can never be tapped.

The pre-1900 strategy seeks local superiority in the western Pacific; later combined-fleet parity emerges through the alliance and negotiated limits. Peacetime military and overseas spending has a planning ceiling of four percent of North American GDP, interest fifteen percent of public revenue and public debt thirty percent of that GDP. The audit keeps period, perimeter and crisis exceptions explicit.

## 26. Japan’s victory, the hard renewal and the Act of Settlement (1900–1907)

Julian grows up amid the public memory of Elias and the private history of Victoria’s marriage. Officers, servants, teachers and his adoptive mother give him incompatible accounts of the founder. The factions court the intended heir while the conditions attached to his future power become visible. Victoria offers tenderness and a frightening example of political authority. His education, friendships and early public life remain **[OPEN]** and must form an independent trajectory.

**[PROPOSED]** During the Boxer rising in 1900 Russia enters Manchuria from Transbaikalia and occupies China’s railway toward Harbin. It seeks to regain a southern outlet lost in 1899. In 1904–1905 the completion of Russia’s Circum-Baikal section strengthens that advance without giving Moscow control of the Japanese terminal or Chinese eastern route. Japan’s army answers from Enkaishū and north through Korea, supplied by sea. The decisive land battle centers on Harbin and the Sungari; Russia is driven toward the Transbaikal border. <!-- CR-040: the Petropavlovsk squadron replaces the Baltic Fleet --> **[APPROVED — CR-040]** At sea Russia has no Pacific port left to fight from. Between 1900 and 1903 it builds a coaling and repair depot at Petropavlovsk in Avacha Bay, and in 1903, under ordinary peacetime orders, two armoured cruisers and a protected cruiser come out to it. They cannot blockade Japan, but through 1904 they raid the transports carrying its army to Enkaishū and Korea, and escorts, diversions and the hunt tie down half of Japan's cruisers. In May 1905, as the squadron returns from its spring cruise, Kuroda Kiyoshi gathers merchant sightings, port reports and wireless signals and concentrates a stronger cruiser division on it east of Hokkaido. He disables and takes one cruiser with most of its crew. The other two escape damaged to a harbour that cannot repair them, and he does not chase them into the fog. The squadron was the only threat to the army's sea road, and he ends it; at the New Year of 1907 Japan makes him a count for it. Russia's battle fleets never came east and survive, so Russia ends the war with its navy and its trained men intact, and it turns its money and railways west. **[PROPOSED]** Takahashi Korekiyo raises the war loans in San Francisco.

Japan wins the war it fought. Victoria advises it to refuse Theodore Roosevelt’s mediation, and Russia negotiates directly. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Russia's defeat is regional, not a catastrophe, and its revolution has causes of its own. The zemstvo congress demands an elected assembly in November 1904; strikes follow, and Bloody Sunday in January 1905; in October a railway strike becomes a general strike while peasants seize land, and the Tsar concedes a Duma. **[PROPOSED]** A government that needs a peace to govern at home, and Pacifican pressure on French lending, bring Witte to terms: withdrawal from Manchuria, an indemnity, recognition of Japan’s southern gains and renunciation of claims in Korea. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** Witte comes to Urajio, the port Russia called Vladivostok until 1899, to sign away Russia's Pacific there.

<!-- CR-039: Korea's post-1905 status is canon -->
Russia retains its northern coast until the later Siberian settlement. Korea remains an independent guaranteed state with Japanese economic predominance and agreed military and transit rights; coordinated foreign policy does not make it a protectorate. It is not annexed in 1910. China retains the Manchurian line with Japanese running rights. **[PROPOSED]** Victoria restrains Japan at the Transbaikal border, leaving the army’s remaining territorial ambition pointed north.

Russian indemnity payments help keep Japan's postwar debt manageable. The working model assigns about $300 million paid in 1906–1910; that amount remains a MODEL estimate rather than an independently fixed treaty clause.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** Silas's nativists raise a school-segregation crisis in 1906. His former adjutant Harlan Tate now runs Sacramento; his board segregates Japanese pupils and revokes the licence of the mission school Silas's mother endowed. Silas, declining into drink, lends the crisis his name and is privately ashamed of it. Roosevelt courts Tokyo and sends the Great White Fleet around Cape Horn to Yokohama, while his immigration restrictions make the courtship unreliable. A more confident Japan bargains hard in the 1907 compact renewal, securing a joint war council, family immigration and civic equality for Japanese Pacificans.

<!-- CR-033: Japanese predominance among Asian immigrants -->
**[APPROVED — CR-033]** Japanese emigrants have been choosing Pacifica in growing numbers, and in the 1900s their arrivals overtake the Chinese. **[PROPOSED]** With Japanese families admitted under the compact, the lead widens, and by 1932 Japanese-origin Pacificans outnumber Chinese-origin ones, about 5.7 against 4.9 million of some 37 million **[MODEL — CR-052]**. Filipino migration stays small until the Philippine crown joins Julian's, and grows after it.

The Carnegie purchase gives Theodore Roosevelt a cause for a broader response from 1903. That the richest man in the Union now draws his income from a Pacifican company makes the grievance personal. The Union funds laboratories, technical chairs and universities, develops a central bank after the 1895 gold humiliation, and keeps industrial trusts as national champions. Scholarship bonds and taxation of citizens abroad discourage skilled departure; compulsory licensing and tariffs constrain Pacifican patents and firms. The response is in full force by 1910 and continues under Taft and Wilson. Pacifica answers by recruiting more skilled Asians. The Union emulates at home and contains abroad through 1932, retaining the capacity and ambition to challenge the order. From 1919 Union investors buy champion bonds and stakes in Pacifican oil terminals and power companies, and containment begins to collide with their portfolios: on Wall Street the "Pacifican interest" argues for peace. **[APPROVED — CR-048]** From 1911 the Parchman Act's oaths turn the programme's own grants against it.

<!-- CR-036: the Nobel decade -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-036]** Between 1905 and 1909 the Nobel committees confirm what Roosevelt's programme fears. Koch wins in 1905, Michelson in 1907 and Ehrlich in 1908, all three as Pacificans. In 1909 Ehrlich and Hata Sahachirō find the arsenical that cures syphilis. The Union's new laboratories are measured against Pacifica's colleges.

Pacifica's capital ships already embody the new naval technology when Dreadnought appears. Oil firing reduces fuel handling and crew demands rather than forcing an emergency rebuilding programme. The MODEL fleet plan reaches fourteen Pacifican capital ships by 1918 and ten Japanese ships, the combined twenty-four that set the 1919 British parity.

Japan’s demographic and industrial weight makes the partnership increasingly unequal. Pacifica supplies oil, wheat, finance, the canal and ocean communications; Japan supplies manpower, officers, yards and a land army. Japanese-origin families rise through the examination state, shipping and imperial contracts. Japanese merchant houses take about a quarter of the champions' ventures abroad, in oil terminals, power companies and mines, so Japanese money owns part of the order long before a treaty says so. They need Japanese credentials and domestic allies as well as common careers. The Hapa generation carries these connections through schools, services and households while racial hierarchy and selective entry remain visible.

In 1905–1906 Julian spends a year in Tokyo attached to the legation and navy, meeting Saionji as he becomes prime minister. Victoria's language education gives the nineteen-year-old access beyond interpreters and formal audiences. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** He also meets Chiyo, of another branch of the Kuroda house.

<!-- CR-040: Silas's renunciation of 1906 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-040]** He comes home to the school crisis, and in 1907 he comes of age. Victoria has delayed the Act of Settlement while the founder's eldest son lives, and Tokyo's terms for renewing the compact in 1907 now include a guarantee that the man whose name stands on Tate's campaign will never stand in the line. In the autumn Lieutenant Tanabe Makoto of Kiyoshi's staff asks Silas for his younger daughter, and she wants to be married in her grandmother's chapel, which Tate's board has closed. Victoria offers Silas a bargain: renounce, and the renewed compact reopens the mission school; refuse, and Tokyo refuses the renewal and the Regent says publicly why. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** In December 1906 Silas renounces, for himself and his descendants, any claim to the crown the Act will create. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Victoria says nothing of Donner: it would expose her own silence and the bargain on which the regency was founded, and she does not need it. She weighs what the renunciation opens, that the founder's second son becomes his eldest claimant, and judges that Tokyo will never back him. Silas does not know she knows.

The Polytechnic of Formosa opens at Taipei in 1906 after the Qing examination system ends, with engineering at Keelung and Boxer-indemnity scholarships. Chinese and English teaching and examinations in mathematics, engineering, law and administration train officials for Chinese ministries, railways and Pacifican firms. Its graduates carry both useful standards and the nationalism that may turn against foreign administration.

<!-- CR-040: Kiyoshi's claim and the Act of Settlement, 1907 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-040]** With Silas out of the line, Kuroda Kiyoshi claims the crown the Act is to create. Early in 1907, Count Kuroda and commander of the western squadron, he petitions the Regent as the founder's eldest son who has not renounced, and asks that the crown follow the founder's blood. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** He offers to reign only for his lifetime, with Julian after him; Victoria sees that an eldest-son rule would carry the crown to his Kuroda son. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** He needs to deny Julian two-thirds; outlasting a Regent of sixty-eight is no plan. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** He lets it be known that if the Act passes over him, no officer of the naval party will accept the western squadron's command. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** The squadron anchors off Kāneʻohe Bay on a courtesy visit, in sight of Thalassia, and he gives no order.

His coalition comes apart piece by piece. The ʻAha Aliʻi will proclaim only Julian, and Thalassia stands on the Hawaiian throne's land. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Saionji's government will not let a count of Japan take a crown equal to the Emperor's. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** The crown would cost Kiyoshi the house he founded and the name Japan gave him. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** Victoria publishes the memorial; Tate will not be seen with the Japanese pretender and votes against the Act alone. The examined young officers of the naval party will not strike; **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Lieutenant Tanabe, who is to marry Silas's daughter, tells the count so to his face. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** Julian goes to Pearl Harbor without an escort and asks his brother, in Japanese, for a wife from his house. Kiyoshi accepts and renounces the crown for himself and his descendants. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** He renounces in March 1907. Chiyo and her branch give their own assent, and the compact renewed later that year makes Kiyoshi Japanese Fleet-General: the price of the crown he gave up is the command of the common fleet.

**[APPROVED — CR-040]** The 1907 Act of Settlement establishes the Crown of Pacifica in the House of Thorne-Kamehameha and joins to it the Hawaiian crown Victoria has worn since 1872. The Act dissolves the Territorial Council and restores Hawaii's legislature. **[APPROVED — CR-029, redated by CR-040]** Victoria will not join her crown without asking her people, as she did not ask them in 1874. The restored legislature and the ʻAha Aliʻi put the union to a plebiscite of Hawaiian subjects, and the loyalists campaign against it. The ʻAha Aliʻi proclaims Julian heir to the Hawaiian crown, to reign as Kamehameha VI; Senate and Chamber confer the Realm's sovereignty by two-thirds. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** In November 1907 Victoria lays down both the regency and her crown, and Julian, about twenty-one, accedes to both. Her long government has prepared the ruler who will make its institutions his own. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Hawaiian is restored as the Crown's language and in its schools. The ʻAha Aliʻi keeps custody of Hawaiian crown and kuleana lands; Thalassia's ground is confirmed as land of the Hawaiian throne. The Polytechnic retains its charter.

**[APPROVED — CR-029]** In the same months the Philippine Cortes offers the Philippine crown, and a plebiscite confirms the offer. Its terms are signed before the vote, because Filipinos remember what was promised at Biak-na-Bato and never written down. Calderón's organic law gives the kingdom a single-chamber Cortes with a permanent commission, its own budget and army, and provinces under Gobernadores who are never posted to their home province. Neither crown is subordinate. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** The Realm takes over what remains of the Redemption Loan at its 1907 balance **[MODEL]**. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** The Crown of Pacifica becomes a crown made of three sources: Hawaiian rank, a Filipino people's offer and the Senate's law. Each crown keeps its own legislature, language of the Crown and coronation, and the alternat names each first in its own texts. A Kuhina Nui in Honolulu and a Lugarteniente General in Manila govern day to day in the Sovereign's name, on his instructions and without the old veto. Ka Lōkahi, the council of the crowns, gives the Realm, Hawaii and the Philippines one vote each over the succession, the agreed matters renewed every ten years and disputes between them. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Its first review falls in 1917. **[APPROVED — CR-029]** Hawaii votes for the union narrowly; the Philippines, broadly. **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** Julian's style becomes Sovereign of Pacifica, Mōʻī of Hawaiʻi, Rey de Filipinas, and each land promulgates it with its own crown first. **[APPROVED — CR-029, redated by CR-040]** In December 1907 he swears before the Cortes in Manila, as Spanish kings swore, and is proclaimed under the raised standard. No bishop crowns him. At the Te Deum in the Cathedral, sung by the first Filipino archbishop, Victoria, Kīnaʻu's daughter, sits in a pew and sings the one hymn her church shares with Rome.

Succession passes to the eldest son, then eldest daughter, otherwise to an heir adopted with Senate consent. Noe's Maui chiefly descent strengthens Julian's claim alongside his adoption by a Kamehameha queen. Loyalists dispute whether a hānai son may continue the Kamehameha line. The Act heals much politically, but does not reverse the Supreme Court's ignored judgment of 1876. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** The Act recites Kiyoshi's renunciation and names him "Kuroda Kiyoshi, son of Elias Thorne", the only public document that ever does. He never uses the name.

In November 1907 Julian marries Lady Chiyo, a Japanese woman of the kazoku and another branch of the Kuroda house. Kiyoshi arranges the match, binding the naval party to his half-brother’s throne. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** The match is the price of his renunciation: his house supplies the queen, and the heir after Julian is of Kuroda blood. Chiyo’s Japanese standing protects the imperial class from appearing to have abandoned Japan. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Their first children are daughters, and for eight years the Kuroda bargain waits on a son. He is born about 1916. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** He is named Kahekili for Noe’s Maui line, with the Japanese name 雷, Ikazuchi, given by meaning through Chiyo’s family. The heir will reign in Hawaii as Kamehameha VII; he is distinct from Elias and Victoria’s lost Kalanimoku. Japan’s succession law excludes him, so the shared dynasty does not merge the crowns.

**[PROPOSED — CR-022]** At his accession the regency's sealed papers pass to Julian. Looking for the truth of his mother's death, he finds instead the truth of Donner, kept by the woman who raised him. He tells no one that he knows. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Silas keeps the inland command until 1912 and never learns that his sovereign has read the file.

The growing dynasty gives each public a different account of the partnership. Japanese supporters can see a half-Japanese heir to come and Japanese admirals at its summit; Pacificans can see Japan entering institutions their state created. The Austro-Hungarian constitutional template will become useful only when the later financial and political crises make a formal Concord acceptable.
