# PART VI — Personal rule, the failed peace and the Russian war (1907–1922)

## 27. The tour, the peace of the capitals and China’s revolution (1908–1913)

<!-- CR-040: the tour and the peace of the capitals -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-040]** In the spring of 1908 Julian sails for Europe with ministers, bankers, engineers and interpreters, leaving Chiyo in the Pacific. On this and later visits he sees Asquith, Grey and Haldane in London; Bülow and then Bethmann Hollweg in Berlin, with Tirpitz and Ballin; Clemenceau and then Briand in Paris; Franz Joseph, Franz Ferdinand and Aehrenthal in Vienna; and Nicholas II, Stolypin and Kokovtsov in St Petersburg. He wants a Europe that pays its debts and buys Pacifica's oil, and in 1908 he believes war can be made too expensive to start. Britain and Germany each undertake, separately, to give no state-backed money, bases, intelligence or arms to a Union attempt to overturn the North American settlement. Ballin becomes a friend.

**[PROPOSED — CR-040]** When Austria annexes Bosnia that October, the crisis turns on a bargain Aehrenthal and Izvolsky made at Buchlau and remember differently. Julian, within a year of reading the Donner file, knows what a buried bargain does to the people who keep it, and he insists on putting terms in writing. The settlement of 1909 gives Habsburg sovereignty with a Bosnian Diet, Serbian trade through Ottoman Salonica with the Porte's consent, and a standing commission that can order an inquiry and a standstill before either side uses force. Russia is given a public part instead of a humiliation. At Agadir in 1911 Germany takes shipping and engineering contracts and a public result in place of most of the territory it demanded. In 1912–1913 the same methods help close the two Balkan wars. Each crisis ends in an agreement that works, and by 1913 Julian believes the method works.

<!-- CR-049: fission and the chain in Julian's first years -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Hillebrand's group finds that one of uranium's new activities behaves in every test like barium. Remembering helium, he trusts the chemistry. In 1908 the uranium core is understood to split. The fragments make ionisation pulses many times larger than any alpha particle's, a direct measurement of the energy, and Einstein's relation of 1905, applied to Morley's deficits, explains it: Europe's theory accounts for Pacifica's measurement. <!-- CR-050: the challenge evening, the hold and the seal -->**[APPROVED — CR-050]** In November 1908, by custom, the result goes to a challenge evening before it goes to the journal. The appointed respondent is Charles Peirce, sixty-nine. He works out on the spot the energy of one split times the atoms in a block of uranium, and asks whether the split also releases neutrons: if it does, the prudent course is to assume a chain is possible and act on that before it is proved, a conjecture earning its trial before its belief. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** He gives the split its name, fission, borrowing Cohn's word for the way bacteria divide, his *Spaltpilze* of the 1870s. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Hillebrand withholds his own paper for verification; six or eight people know. In December he tells Victoria, his patron since the charter, and Soberanes, whose estate holds the General Staff. Victoria says to publish once it is verified. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1909 counting shows that each fission releases more than one new neutron, so a self-sustaining reaction is possible in principle. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The matter is now too serious for the scientists to hold alone, and Hillebrand and Soberanes take it to Julian, home from Europe with the Bosnian crisis behind him. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** What follows from it is Julian's to decide, and it is where he and Victoria begin to drift apart. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** She is no longer Regent and keeps her influence through Hillebrand and the Works. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Both argue from Elias's memorandum. Victoria says that a withheld account robs those who must build on it as surely as a falsified one, and that the thing could never be built without turning the country into one factory. Julian says that nothing false will be printed, that the account is kept and will be opened, and that a small chance of Europe building it first is not small if it ends the commonwealth. Hillebrand's compromise settles it. The fission paper of November 1908 and the chain result of 1909 are written in their authors' hands, dated, witnessed by Goldstein, master of the laboratory, and by the Polytechnic chief, and sealed in the Thalassia archive, with the Works' director as its custodian. Opening needs two keys, the sovereign's and the Polytechnic chief's: Soberanes's, then from 1915 Vance's. Victoria accepts the seal on one condition written into the deposit: it is opened no later than twenty years after the first paper was written, by November 1928. <!-- CR-051: throttling, not a cut-off; no hole in the literature -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** A sudden stop would be a signal, so from 1909, on Hillebrand's order as director, the Works throttles the strong neutron sources it sells abroad: its waiting lists lengthen, its prices rise and the new safety licensing applies to every buyer, at home and abroad, so that renewing a subscription becomes slow and dear. Within two or three years Europe's laboratories make their own sources from the barium-borne element and beryllium, and complain of Pacifican greed. Hillebrand's group goes on publishing true work on everything around uranium that does not touch the split, the decay series and their branchings, the first isomer, tracer chemistry and radiation medicine, so the field's leaders never vanish from the journals. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Pacifica prints nothing false and denies nothing, and it leaves Europe's own errors uncorrected, which is the price the people behind the wall feel. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Julian never approves a false paper. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** He builds the industry in peacetime because he fears what Europe will make of the same physics, because his peace needs teeth, and because the industry can pay its way.

<!-- CR-049: Joachimsthal in the Bosnian settlement -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1909 Austria pays the Porte for its lost rights in Bosnia with money lent by Pacifican banks, and a finance rider to the loan gives the Light and Matter Works a thirty-year contract for all the uranium compounds and residues left after extraction at the Joachimsthal state works, with first refusal on new Bohemian uranium ore. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Austria keeps the barium-borne element it extracts, because Vienna's institute is a matter of national pride, but the rider is greedy in a way any banker can read: the Works takes the exclusive marketing of the Austrian salt outside the monarchy and a share of its sales, and the uranium clause sits among those terms like an afterthought. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Pacifica pays generously for the uranium, buys it as the world uses it, for glazes, glass and alloy steel, and says nothing false about it. In Vienna Stefan Meyer asks why Pacifica wants uranium, and Europe's catch-up begins with his question. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** It is easy to dismiss, because the obvious answer is money.

<!-- CR-049: as much uranium as Pacifica can get; the research installation; the people -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Julian treats the moment as an opportunity that comes once: before the world learns what uranium is worth, Pacifica means to mine and buy as much of it as it can get its hands on. From 1909 the Roc Creek and Paradox Valley deposits are mined for uranium itself. The rule is to buy what nobody values: Pacifica pays well for uranium, which the world treats as waste, and never states anything false. Before 1908 every partner keeps the barium-borne element it prizes. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** From 1908 the rule changes: the Works buys whole ores wherever it can, the salt with the uranium, and is seen as a ruthless trust in the salt. Because Pacifica publishes its dangers, the salt's value rests on hospitals, which want it for treatment, and navies, which want it for luminous instruments. Grams of it become gifts of state, presented under the Works' name to foreign institutes, universities and hospitals with ceremony and newsreel and timed to concessions over ore, mining ground or transit. What Pacifica takes in exchange is always the uranium side. **[OPEN]** How Pacifica gets hold of uranium beyond these four sources, the subject of the next change request. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** A research installation needs tens of tonnes of uranium as metal and oxide and hundreds of tonnes of very pure graphite, and Hillebrand's group makes both in electric furnaces on Tesla's hydroelectric power. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** In 1910–1911 the counting team finds that some neutrons from a split arrive late, and the first test assemblies absorb too many: the graphite is impure. It is remade from California petroleum coke, every batch is tested in a neutron beam, and the uranium is purified in Hillebrand's laboratory. The first research installation runs in 1913, late in the year, mostly on pressed oxide with some metal, and the late neutrons make it controllable. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** People follow the instruments. Ernest Rutherford comes about 1909–1910 and becomes the face of New Zealand's drift towards Pacifica, and Lise Meitner comes from Berlin about 1910.

<!-- CR-058: Great Bear Lake -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** The Works reads every survey report in the world for its signature, and in the Geological Survey of Canada's report of 1900 on Great Bear Lake, Bell and Camsell note cobalt bloom and copper near Echo Bay: cobalt in a silver and cobalt country, the Joachimsthal signature. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** About 1912 **[APPROVED — CR-058]** a canoe party goes down the Athabasca, the Slave, the Mackenzie and the Great Bear River with Charles Camsell, born at Fort Liard, and finds the pitchblende. The deal follows the pattern of the salt trust. A Canadian-chartered company works the deposit on Crown land, so Canada's sovereignty is never in question; the Works finances it and takes all its ore under an exclusive contract, as a radium play, and Canada's hospitals receive a free allowance of the salt, the charity on show; the uranium rides along as an afterthought. A treaty with the Sahtú Dene comes with the find, and the Works' code governs the ore: the men who carry the sacks are paid, shielded and examined, and Délı̨nę does not become a village of widows.

<!-- CR-050: the walled college -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** From 1909 the work moves behind a wall. Its rules are written into the Works' charter and the Polytechnic estate's statutes, which already bind their officers and examined staff; no ordinance goes before the Senate and no law is made. Inside, the customs go on: challenge evenings, contests with declared terms, an internal journal with its corrections column, and a sealed roll of honour that dates and names every result, so that silence costs no one credit. The work is better inside than anywhere else, and a dissenter who leaves cannot continue it without the Works' sources. Recruits enter an outer college that publishes. The inner circle that works on the split admits only on the sponsorship of two masters already inside and a real need for the newcomer's work, and each new member signs a bond, an extension of the bonds that already tie the Realm's scholars to service, that binds him to the seal for its term under the Polytechnic estate's discipline; every chair carries citizenship, so foreigners sign as Pacificans. The inner circle's letters pass through the Works' own post and are read. Victoria's term gives the idealists inside a written end to wait for. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** Some Hawaiian participants speak of the wall as *kapu* knowledge held by those trained to hold it, a civic usage Kamakau's heirs contest in print. <!-- CR-050-R1: Hetch Hetchy and the Compact of the Tuolumne -->**[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** In 1909 Hillebrand and Soberanes choose Hetch Hetchy, a granite valley of the Tuolumne in the Sierra Nevada, secluded, near the Sierra's power and beautiful enough to hold the people they need. The state has been surveying the Tuolumne for San Francisco's water since 1908, and its survey railway from the Sierra Railway to the camp at Hog Ranch carries the Works' spur and the tunnel at the valley's mouth without remark. <!-- CR-051: O'Shaughnessy; who lives in the valley -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Michael O'Shaughnessy, a Limerick-born engineer who has driven irrigation tunnels for Hawaiian sugar, builds the line, the spur and the tunnel for the Works, and he knows the Sierra's rock and water better than anyone. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** Until the valley opens in 1911 the inner circle works in a closed wing at Carquinez. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** In 1910 Ralston College goes behind the wall under protest. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** The Compact of the Tuolumne gives it its own compound at the valley's east end, its own director and a budget fixed for five years at a time. The college nominates its own people, one of their two sponsors may be its own, and Carquinez cannot refuse them except on the bond. It has equal access to the sources, the instruments, the installation's schedule and the internal journal, names its own respondent at every challenge evening, and keeps its independence of method, so it may run rival routes. Contests between the schools are judged by one judge from each and a chair named by the sovereign, not by the Polytechnic's chief. The sealed roll credits results by school; nothing in the sealed work is private property, and the state endows chairs at the college for the people it seconds. Its president may appeal to the sovereign in person once in each dispute, and the college may leave on a year's notice, its people still bound for the term. Its public life is unchanged. The Works pays for a champion of the college's choosing, and it chooses Robert Millikan, recruited from Chicago that year, who measures the elementary charge in the open with his oil drops in 1910–1913. The trustees sign under a written protest for openness, deposited with the seal. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Rutherford refuses to go behind the wall. He stays the public face of Pacifican physics, works in the open, knows there is a wall and never asks what lies behind it. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** The valley holds only the inner circle and the staff it needs; the outer college works on the Carquinez and Ralston College campuses, and a recruit admitted to the inner circle moves to the valley. The valley's challenge evenings are for the inner circle alone, and the instrument makers and machinists who build to inner drawings sign the inner bond, not the staff bond. **[MODEL — CR-051]** By 1927 about 150 inner scientists and engineers live there, some 1,000–1,200 people with their families, the staff, the school and the clinic; growth beyond that goes to the campuses or the halls' district, never into the valley.

<!-- CR-050-R1: the Tuolumne Station, its neighbours and San Francisco's water -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** The valley is publicly and truthfully the Tuolumne Station of the Light and Matter Works, a high-Sierra research station whose annual report and journals carry its open work: radiation medicine and the registers' clinic, the ionisation of the air at altitude, the inner members' open papers. Anyone may learn that there are buildings, lights, scientists and a railway in the valley; all of it is in the report. The Works buys the floor from its private claimants and leases the Me-Wuk's rights of use, and the floor is closed as a working station on the Tuolumne watershed, which the state's survey protects. The spur from the camp at Hog Ranch ends in a tunnel through the granite at the valley's mouth, and the tunnel's mouth is the gate, with the guardhouse, the siding, the workshops and the post office where the inner circle's letters are read. Carquinez's laboratories stand on the south terrace near the gate, and the challenge-evening hall in the meadow under the oaks, its north window framing Wapama Falls. Ralston College's compound and its row of houses are at the east end, and beyond them the research installation, in a hall cut into the granite. The families live on the sunny north bench across a footbridge, behind an earthwork against rockfall, with the school and the clinic. A small electric tram on the valley floor carries the rivalry across the meadow every morning; Hog Ranch is under an hour away and San Francisco most of a day.

**[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** No one lives in the valley year-round; the Tuolumne Me-Wuk use it in season, for the autumn acorns, summer camps and the burning that keeps the meadows open. In 1909–1910, as their rancheria is settled, the band's council leases the valley's use to the Works for the seal's term, renewably, for a fee paid every year. In exchange the band receives title to Poopenaut Valley below the gorge and protected groves outside the fence, more acorn ground than the valley held, and keeps an announced autumn fortnight in the north groves, camping at the west end, far from the laboratories. Me-Wuk men are hired at the Works' rates as the valley's wardens, patrolling the rims of their own country and burning the meadows, and they sign the staff bond that binds every employee inside the fence; gatherers who come only for the harvest sign nothing and are shown nothing. The clinic serves the band's families. The Kootzaduka'a Paiute of Mono Lake, whose routes come down the Tuolumne canyon from the east, make a compact of their own: summer rights in the high country above the rims, a marked rim trail around the valley's east end for their trade with the Me-Wuk, a share of the fee, and wardens for the east rim. No one from either people enters a laboratory. What they see is what the station publishes. The wardens guard the rims as their own country, on their council's choice, and they are the first to meet a stranger asking questions; a leak would cost the band the lease and the wages, not the land, which is theirs. **[OPEN]** The leaders' names, for archival research.

**[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** In 1913 the state chooses Lake Eleanor and Cherry Valley, side valleys of the same Tuolumne, for San Francisco's water, and buys out the Spring Valley Water Company as a bridge; the waterworks' railway and camps carry the Works' traffic without remark. John Muir has fought the station's fence since 1910 and lost; he counts the valley's escape from the reservoir his last victory and dies in December 1914 without knowing what the fence is for.

<!-- CR-050: the light isotope, 1909–1912 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** From 1909 the inner circle's measurements show natural uranium behaving as if only a small part of it splits easily, while thorium behaves differently. Meitner is admitted to the inner circle in 1911 on the sponsorship of Hillebrand and Goldstein, and in 1911–1912 she proposes that the part that splits easily is a rare, lighter isotope hiding in the common one. Goldstein, past sixty, attacks the conjecture at a challenge evening; it is his last stand, and he loses.

<!-- CR-051: Europe's road, 1908–1914: Hahn alone, the prize of 1911, Rutherford's satellite model -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** In Berlin Hahn and Meitner spend 1908–1910 on beta spectra and the thorium series, and the transuranic chains of 1902–1907 look settled on both sides of the world. When Meitner leaves for Carquinez, Hahn loses his questioner. Otto von Baeyer, the careful physicist with whom they measured beta spectra, works with him from 1910, but von Baeyer, like Hahn, asks what happens rather than why. The physicists who ask why are drawn to atomic spectra after Haas, and women of Meitner's kind are drawn to Pacifica, whose examination has been open to them since 1859. The Works' salt trust and its throttling raise the price of the barium-borne salt, and with it Europe's demand for Hahn's mesothorium, which the Knöfler company sells as the cheaper substitute: the best radiochemist in Germany spends his years in commercial chemistry because Pacifica charges what it likes. In 1911 the Nobel Prize in chemistry goes to Marie Curie, for her school's work on the elements beyond uranium that neutrons produce. When the committee sounds out Carquinez, whose group co-published the work of 1902–1907, Hillebrand withdraws it from consideration pending verification, and the prize goes to Paris alone. The laboratory most likely to break the error becomes the one most committed to defending it. About 1911 Rutherford, with Pacifica's strong thorium sources, finds that fast alpha particles bounce off uranium in the normal way while uranium emits slower ones, and in 1912 Rutherford, outside the wall, publishes his satellite model of the nucleus: an indivisible core with neutral satellites that break up only once they have left it. It explains everything then known, and coming from the most famous experimentalist in the world it becomes Europe's prohibition and enters the textbooks within a few years. Friedrich Paneth teaches it in his lectures at the Vienna institute. The inner circle knows it is wrong and publishes nothing about it either way; Meitner, newly admitted, asks at a challenge evening to warn him privately, and Hillebrand refuses. In 1912 Hahn moves into the new Kaiser Wilhelm Institute for Chemistry, and, by his own account no theorist, defers to physics, which now says a nucleus cannot split.

**[APPROVED — CR-022]** Meanwhile, in 1909, Silas's younger daughter marries Lieutenant Commander Tanabe Makoto of Kiyoshi's staff in the chapel of her grandmother's mission school; the Fleet-General comes to Sacramento, and the two sons denied their father's name meet for the only time. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Both have now given up his crown. **[APPROVED — CR-022]** In the autumn of 1912 Silas comes to Thalassia and confesses Donner to Victoria, who has known for twenty-five years; he dies at Sacramento that winter. In 1913 an examined officer receives the inland command.

<!-- CR-040: the Senate quarrel moves to 1910–1911 -->
In a Senate dispute of 1910–1911 Julian humiliates the representatives and manipulates the voting machinery. County boards resent Commissioners and receivership, supplying the senators’ material resistance. **[PROPOSED]** The immediate business concerns an Australian petition whose external jurisdiction remains qualified. Victoria recognizes the form of power she struggled to contain in Elias. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** She has seen it already in the wall around the atom, whose rules live in the estates' charter and statutes where the Senate cannot reach them. Her bond with her adopted son now carries political disagreement as well as memory, love and suspicion.

<!-- CR-042: the Huludao Harbour Trust -->
**[APPROVED — CR-042]** In 1909 Julian takes up his mother's option. **[PROPOSED — CR-042]** Xu Shichang, Viceroy of the Three Eastern Provinces, wants a Manchurian port that no foreign power holds, and Peking has known for ten years who will pay for it. The Huludao Harbour Trust is chartered under a Qing commission on the Keelung pattern: a Pacifican director, Pacifican engineers, bonds placed in San Francisco, the harbour dues pledged to the bondholders until the bonds are paid, and Chinese sovereignty, customs and police. It is offered as commerce, an outlet for Manchurian soya beans and coal, and it is commerce. Works begin in 1910 and go on through the revolution, and the Republic confirms the commission as it continues the Formosan one. **[APPROVED — CR-042]** By 1912 a Chinese line runs from Harbin through Changchun to Mukden and joins the line to Peking, and Huludao is a day's run from Mukden. **[PROPOSED — CR-042]** The harbour opens to shipping in 1914. **[APPROVED — CR-042]** The railway to the harbour, from Mukden past Jinzhou to the gulf, is built in 1908–1910 by a British and Pacifican syndicate with Britain the senior partner, an idea first discussed in London on Julian's visit of 1908. **[PROPOSED — CR-042]** The British and Chinese Corporation, which financed the line to Peking, leads the loan and San Francisco takes the junior share. The bondholders hold the line's receipts as their security, so the last stretch of Pacifica's door runs on a British lien.

The Qing falls in 1911–1912. Julian, four years on the throne, supports Sun Yat-sen’s movement; Pacifica backs the Honolulu-educated revolutionary. **[PROPOSED]** Sun tours Pacifica’s Chinese communities after the Wuchang rising, becomes provisional president, yields to Yuan Shikai’s superior army and loses the Second Revolution of 1913. His exile is in Honolulu. Pacifica’s chosen partner begins defeated, and backing him requires patience rather than an instant Chinese client state.

## 28. July 1914, the Pacific war and Soberanes’s retirement (1914–1915)

<!-- CR-040: the failure of July 1914 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-040]** On 28 June 1914 Franz Ferdinand, the man in Vienna most opposed to war with Serbia, is killed at Sarajevo with his wife. Thalassia hears it the same day, half a day behind Europe's clock, and Julian's ministers invoke the commission of 1909. Belgrade accepts an inquiry; Vienna does not answer. Vienna has the commission's own reports of 1912 and 1913, which named Unification or Death and officers of Serbian military intelligence. Pašić's government gave assurances it could not enforce against its own army, and Julian accepted them rather than embarrass the settlement he was proud of. Conrad and Berchtold argue that Pacifica's procedures gave the conspirators five years. In Berlin the staff calculates that a Russia which lost its Pacific in 1899, kept its fleets in 1905 and has put its railways and new army into Europe can still be beaten in 1914 and not in 1917.

**[APPROVED — CR-040]** Pacifica supplies the fuel oil Germany's new ships burn, under contracts Ballin brokered. Victoria and the Five urge Julian to say in Berlin that deliveries stop if Vienna refuses the inquiry. He refuses for a reason that is his virtue: Pacifica's credit rests on never breaking a contract for politics. On 5 July, with Jagow away, his envoy is received at the Foreign Office by the under-secretary, Arthur Zimmermann, the same day as Count Hoyos. Zimmermann hears him out and gives him nothing; that afternoon the Emperor gives Vienna Germany's unconditional support. At the Vienna council two days later Tisza argues alone for demands Serbia could accept, using Pacifica's terms, and loses; he gives way on the 14th. Serbia's answer to the ultimatum accepts almost everything except the participation of Austrian officials in an inquiry on its soil. Julian offers the commission for that one point and Pacifica's guarantee of Serbian compliance, and only now says the oil will stop. The cables reach men already mobilising. The tankers have kept sailing all month, and Germany goes to war with its tanks full. Austria declares war on 28 July, and within a week Russia, Germany, France and Britain are at war.

He made every earlier crisis cheaper to settle than to fight. In July the men who decided had concluded that war now was cheaper than war later. He had let the conspirators' names sit in a report, and he broke the contract only when breaking it could no longer change anything. He concludes that offering men something to lose does not stop them, and only taking something away does. Victoria concludes that he built a peace on one man and a procedure, and kept faith with his contracts longer than with his judgment.

Europe’s war begins in August 1914. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Julian could keep Pacifica out of it; Japan will not stay out. Its navy wants Tsingtao and the German islands, and Tokyo will take them with Thalassia or without it. If Japan goes to war alone, the joint war council of 1907 means nothing and the partnership splits on its first test. Julian declares war on Germany on 23 August, the day Japan does, so that the two fleets act under one plan, and the war council's staff starts the campaign from plans it drew up in peacetime. The contracts Ballin brokered become enemy contracts, and Ballin a friend on the other side. **[PROPOSED]** Pacifica and Japan take Germany’s Pacific islands and Tsingtao with their partners, including Australia and New Zealand. <!-- CR-051: Sibert at Tsingtao -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Tsingtao falls in November to a methodical siege in the autumn rains. William Luther Sibert, an Alabama-born engineer officer of the Polytechnic who built the canal's Atlantic locks and its great dam, is in China that summer on a survey for a Chinese-owned conservancy of the Huai River. As the senior Pacifican engineer within reach he commands the Pacifican engineers in the combined siege beside Japanese officers, rebuilds the Shantung railway behind the lines, and puts the heavy guns and their ammunition forward across flooded ground on time. From 1915 to 1917 he runs the anchorage and works Pacifica holds at Tsingtao under occupation. **[PROPOSED]** Pacifica’s war is maritime and Pacific; its influence in Europe rests on supplies and finance. Britain remains a partner whose dependence can be negotiated through debt. The imperial strategy avoids a British war through the 1932 ending.

**[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Sent to Tokyo as Julian’s envoy, Victoria blocks the worst of Japan’s Twenty-One Demands on China. Tokyo retreats and concludes that access to Thalassia’s institutions offers more than direct defiance. **[PROPOSED — CR-026]** Kiyoshi backs her, the first service he renders the settled crown. The episode tests the limits of a partnership in which Japan has learned Pacifica’s methods and grown strong enough to demand a share in their direction.

Soberanes retires in 1915. His name, Monterey birth and Carquinez role are settled; unprovided early service details remain qualified. From California he watches graduates use his examinations, audits and communications to govern a scale of affairs he can no longer restrain. **[PROPOSED — OPEN-018-18]** Marcus Vance III, a descendant of Marcus I through a private railway-surveyor father, has been educated under his patronage. Julian has Victoria’s throne; Vance is Soberanes’s heir to the system.

## 29. Julian, Chiyo and the court at Thalassia (1907–1917)

Thalassia stands on Kāneʻohe Bay on windward Oʻahu, beside old Hawaiian fishponds that become its canals. It houses the Crown, ministries, gardens and the Senate’s glass-domed chamber; arsenals belong at Pearl Harbor and Carquinez. Heavy industry is directed to the leeward districts. Julian studies phonographic recordings of Elias and learns his cadence. The cultivated imitation supports a desire for personal rule that grows through decisions, success and conflict with the people who trained him.

The precinct rises on the Heʻeia shore in dark basalt terraces, white Californio lime walls and arcades, and broad timber roofs with deep eaves and green copper. Dredge spoil supplies lime and fill; living reef is not quarried for blocks, and protected koa is not the city's timber reserve. Temporary halls give way to the dome in the 1900s. The city stays lean while San Francisco, Carquinez and Sacramento retain much of the administration. The chambers' Season runs October to March; outside it Julian governs by ordinance, laid before the next session. Distance gives him freedom and makes the ministry's faith in cables and figures dangerous.

Chiyo builds patronage for Japanese-origin officers, financiers and ministers in imperial service. She possesses aims and connections of her own, and gives this class credible standing in Japan. Japanese prime ministers govern through the Meiji and Taishō courts, their cabinets and Diet; they cannot simply order the Pacific institutions into war. The shared system remains seated in Pacifica even as Japanese-origin people reach its highest offices.

Yuan Shikai attempts a monarchy in 1915–1916 and dies after abandoning it. Julian leaves him to his fate, conserving influence over Tokyo for Sun’s movement and Manchuria. **[PROPOSED]** Pacifica funds Sun in Canton while working with Zhang Zuolin in Manchuria for Siberian security. The contradiction becomes a criticism of Julian’s strategic bargains.

## 30. Credit, oil and the brokered peace (1914–1919)

The Union stays outside Europe’s Great War. **[PROPOSED]** Its Canadian frontier, rivalry with Pacifica and German- and Irish-descended constituencies make neutrality attractive, while Britain’s blockade provokes disputes. Pacifica fights at sea and in the Pacific, financing the Entente without a continental army. California and the Gulf Republic supply oil; Galveston and New Orleans carry Atlantic supplies. <!-- CR-052: the main lines go electric -->**[APPROVED — CR-052]** California's fields produce more every year, but the war's oil, the Entente's and the fleet's, strains even them, and the power estate turns the first electrified mountain crossings into a programme: the Sierra, Cascade and desert divisions of the main lines go on wire. **[MODEL — CR-052]** About a seventh of the Realm's rail traffic is electric by 1920 and half by 1929. <!-- CR-051: Hoover at war -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** The neutral Union feeds occupied Belgium. Herbert Hoover, raised in Oregon and trained as a geologist at Carquinez, a mining engineer on four continents and a financier in London, is Pacifica's food administrator in the war. **[APPROVED — CR-020]** The later moratorium belongs to Thalassia too. His exact relief-to-treasury appointments remain **[OPEN-019-04]**.

<!-- CR-058: who fuels the Union -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** The Union holds none of the Gulf Republic's oil, no California and no Sequoyah. Its own fields, in Appalachia, Illinois, Kansas and Wyoming and Montana east of the divide, yield about sixty million barrels in 1913; Illinois's boom tops out in 1908. It stays a civilisation of coal, and from about 1910 it imports its oil. Most of the Gulf crude the trust does not refine, mostly but not only, goes north to the Union by barge, by common-carrier pipeline from Sequoyah into Kansas and Illinois and by sea, so the Union's largest supplier is Pacifica's ally. Standard survives as a giant refinery without crude: its lamp-oil export trade lost to the trust in Asia and to Russian and Gulf oil in Europe, its fields running down, its wells barred from the Gulf. By 1910 it is no monopoly, and Roosevelt keeps it whole as the Union's oil company abroad, with Navy contracts and the State Department behind it. It owns Imperial Oil in Ontario and, through Imperial, International Petroleum in Peru. Its refinery at Whiting brings in thermal cracking in 1913, to stretch crude it must buy. Mellon's oil company is J. M. Guffey Petroleum of Pittsburgh, built on Kansas and Illinois. The Union Navy turns to oil only about 1916, keeps its reserves at Teapot Dome and Salt Creek, and its fleet stays an Atlantic and Caribbean fleet. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** From 1916 Huasteca's heavy Tampico crude goes to the Union's Atlantic fuel-oil market and its navy on long contracts. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** The contracts carry no political termination clause. **[MODEL — CR-058]** By 1929 the Union produces about 110 million barrels and imports about four-fifths of what it uses, which coal holds to about 530 million: some 270 million from the Gulf, some 110 million from Venezuela, about 25 million from Mexico and the rest from Peru and Colombia.

**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Thalassia's doctrine is never to pull that lever. A great power cut off from oil does not give way; it fights. Pacifica never embargoes the Union's oil to win a dispute in peacetime, and holds the Gulf to the same line; its contracts cannot be cancelled for politics; it never corners the Union's sources, so the Union always has oil that neither Pacifica nor the Gulf controls. A Union with secure oil has importers and Wall Street lobbying for peace. The dependence is a lever because it is never pulled; in a war the doctrine ends.

<!-- CR-058: Mexico, 1910–1915 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Through Mexico's revolution Pacifica protects its nationals and property and backs no faction. It refuses to recognise Huerta after Madero's murder in February 1913. Its natural partners are the Sonorans next door, Obregón, Calles and Adolfo de la Huerta, whose state runs on Pacifican copper, the Guaymas railway and the Sonora customs. On 27 June 1915 Pacifican agents arrest Huerta at Newman, New Mexico, on his way to a counter-revolution paid for with German money; Orozco escapes and is killed by a posse in Culberson County, in the Gulf Republic, on 30 August. Felix A. Sommerfeld, Villa's arms buyer north of the border, works for the German naval attaché, and Pacifica's wartime intelligence knows it. The Plan de San Diego of 6 January 1915 calls on Mexicans, Black and Native Americans and Japanese to rise in the Southwest, and its raids of July to September fall on south Texas. The Gulf answers with courts: it prosecutes vigilantes, and its homicide figures stay honest. Thalassia reads the Plan's appeal to the Japanese as a German provocation aimed at the partnership, and Japanese Pacificans ignore it. The raids slow sharply when Carranza is recognised, though they revive in 1916. After Obregón breaks Villa at Celaya in April 1915, Pacifica and the Gulf recognise Carranza together in October. They send no arms. General Benjamín Hill's troops cross from Piedras Negras at Eagle Pass and ride Gulf and Pacifican rails to Douglas to reinforce Calles at Agua Prieta, where Villa's attack fails on 1–3 November. Villa believes the searchlights that blinded his night assault were Pacifican, and Agua Prieta's current comes from Douglas; nobody ever settles it.

<!-- CR-058: Santa Isabel, Columbus and the ninety-day expedition -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Villa's revenge falls on Pacifica, not the Gulf. El Paso was his commercial capital, his family lived there and the Gulf's Tejanos are his public, while Pacifica is Carranza's paymaster and the power that took California and bought Baja. His Naco manifesto of November 1915 says Carranza has sold Mexico to the foreigner who paid for his victory and that the Thornes now want Sonora, and many Mexicans believe it. On 10 January 1916 at Santa Isabel, Pablo López's men take the staff of a reopening Pacifican mine at Cusihuiriachi off a train and shoot them, Carquinez graduates among them. On 9 March Villa raids Columbus, New Mexico, a lightly held border town where he believed a merchant had cheated him. The garrison is a squadron of the 1st Border Cavalry, the regular regiment raised after the Magonista rising on the Baja border in 1911, about 350 men on the roll and half of them out on patrol that night; many of its troopers are Hispanos from New Mexico and Arizona, so Villa's men fight Spanish-speakers on both sides of the line. Its officer pursues into Mexico the same day. Thalassia reads the raid as a German trap to tie Pacifica down in Chihuahua and does the opposite of an occupation. Carranza, who depends on Pacifican credit and fears a Union-style occupation, consents formally under the pursuit clause of the Apache convention; Obregón's War Ministry attaches liaison officers, so the columns never fight Carranza's troops; and the expedition has ninety days and keeps to them. Frederick Funston, who commands the southern border from Tucson with Fort Huachuca as his field base, leads it in person from the railhead at Columbus, supplied by rail through El Paso. **[MODEL — CR-058]** It is about six thousand men: a Pacifican column of about four thousand, with a cavalry brigade of two regiments, a battalion of truck-borne infantry on the supply line, an aero squadron of about a dozen machines and some 150 trucks with Holt tractors, and a Gulf brigade of about two thousand, **[APPROVED — CR-058]** the first use of the defence pact of 1900. One of the Gulf's two cavalry regiments is raised in south Texas from Tejanos who sat the Defense Law test in Spanish, so the men the Plan and Villa called on to rise ride against Villa as citizens of the Gulf Republic. The aircraft scout where the mountains defeat horsemen. On 14 May at the San Miguelito ranch near Rubio, Major Patton, Funston's aide, with three Pacifican-built touring cars, about ten soldiers and civilian guides and drivers, kills Julio Cárdenas, commander of Villa's Dorados, and two other men and straps the bodies to the bonnets: the first motorised raid in his service's history, and the lesson of speed, engines and nerve he takes to the armoured trains. Candelario Cervantes, one of the Columbus raid's leaders, is killed on 25 May. Villa is not caught; his Columbus bands are broken, and the expedition leaves on the ninetieth day. Mexico learns that Pacifica keeps its word, and the Sonorans remember it. The Sierra Madre is hard on Funston's heart. He dies at the Santa Rita Hotel in Tucson on 19 February 1917, lies in state at San Francisco's City Hall and is buried at the Presidio, the city he held together after the earthquake. Villa surrenders at Sabinas on 28 July 1920 under Adolfo de la Huerta's amnesty and is murdered at Parral on 20 July 1923; Pacifica stays out. The ballads sing of his raid on the *californios*, and in Mexico's popular memory Pacifica stays the neighbour that took California. When Germany's offer reaches Washington in January 1917, it is Berlin's second attempt to use America against Pacifica.

The war cuts Asia and the Entente off from German dyes and drugs. Pacifica seizes or licenses the patents, supplies munitions and expands into a major chemical power. The champions' wartime gains rest on factories, trained people, transport and credit as well as intellectual property.

<!-- CR-025: wartime credit, Japanese industrial expansion and the later bank crisis; mechanism approved, quantities MODEL. -->
San Francisco syndicates gather the Treasury's money, Pacifican savings and foreign subscriptions into the Entente loans. Investors in the neutral Union buy through Pacifican correspondents even while Washington competes with Thalassia; they keep their own claims and their own risk. Washington discourages loans to belligerents at first, so in 1914 Pacifica's own savers carry the loans alone. When the Union's banks grow wary of unsecured Entente paper at the end of 1916, loans secured on the Allies' requisitioned foreign securities, many of them Pacifican bonds, help carry the last two years of the war, and Pacifican savers take up more than before. Much of the money comes back as orders, and Japanese shipyards, mills and trading houses take a large share of them. **[PROPOSED — CR-030]** Russia's share comes back as locomotives and freight cars. Pacifican works build Russian-gauge engines on Entente credit and ship them through Urajio, and most of them go to work on the Siberian and Manchurian lines. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** From 1915 Pacifican erection and maintenance crews come with the engines, and by 1916 a Pacifican railway mission advises the Ministry of Ways of Communication in Petrograd. In the winter of 1916–17 it watches the capital's bread run short because the trains cannot bring the grain, and it cannot mend the lines in time. Its reports go to Thalassia.

The war also brings Pacifica's own bonds home. British and French holders sell or pledge them, and San Francisco buys them back cheaply. A country that borrowed in Europe for forty years ends the war owning itself and lending to the Entente.

<!-- CR-049: Shinkolobwe and Joachimsthal in the war -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1915 Robert Sharp, prospecting for Tanganyika Concessions, finds the uranium of Shinkolobwe in Katanga for Union Minière du Haut-Katanga, the company of the Comité Spécial du Katanga, the Société Générale and Tanganyika Concessions. Cut off from occupied Brussels and short of capital, Union Minière gives the Light and Matter Works an exclusive option on all the deposit's ore in 1915–1916, in return for financing the mine. In 1917–1919 the Works buys Tanganyika Concessions' shares in Union Minière through London houses. Shipments from Joachimsthal stop under the blockade, and Pacifican-owned uranium piles up at the mines.

<!-- CR-050: the companion, the proof and the bred elements, 1914–1917 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** In 1914 a sealed conjecture at the walled college holds that the installation breeds new elements from uranium. That year Arthur Jeffrey Dempster, a Toronto-born physicist leaving Germany at the outbreak of war, is recruited to Carquinez, where his instrument descends from Goldstein's positive rays. About 1915, on a long exposure, he sees a faint lighter companion beside the uranium line. He is admitted to the inner circle when his survey of isotopes, element by element, reaches the heavy elements, and he agrees to leave uranium out of what he publishes; the survey itself is published and honoured. The heaviest elements are the hardest to resolve and come last, so the gap looks like slow progress, not silence. In 1915–1917 Hillebrand's radiochemists identify two elements heavier than uranium in samples from the installation, while the war's demands pull them away from the other work. In 1916–1917 a few grains separated in Dempster's instrument are tested in a neutron beam, and the light isotope is the one that splits. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** Nagaoka leads the test. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Both results are sealed. In 1917, on the proof, Julian and Vance authorise the magnet halls. <!-- CR-051: two phases; the Chief; the Council of the Seal -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Until then the programme has been a sealed laboratory led by scientists, Hillebrand under the two keys, its military side a guardhouse, a post office and the bonds. With the halls it becomes an industry, and Julian and Vance call Sibert home from Tsingtao to be its Chief, a stranger in uniform among people who have kept a secret for eight years without him. No one in Pacifica has built anything larger. He is quiet, methodical and honest to the last bar of silver, a builder who has never failed to finish, and he keeps the margins. The Chief and the Works' director are equals under a Council of the Seal that meets monthly from 1917: the director in the chair, the Chief, a General Staff officer for the Polytechnic's key, an officer of the naval constructor's estate, the Treasury's sealed comptroller and, on matters of the Compact, Ralston College's director. The director decides what to research and whom to admit; the Chief decides what is built, where and when; production against science goes to the Council by majority, and secrecy and publication to the keys. The cost is speed, and the gain is that no one man holds the whole of it. The money comes by routes that are all true: the Works' own earnings, the state's dividend on its shares left in the Works, and Development Fund credit for electrochemical expansion, which is what the halls are. The Treasury's sealed comptroller is the one man in the Treasury who sees the whole ledger. **[OPEN]** Whether the Senate's budget committee is told anything.

Export orders tempt Japanese banks to finance factories and ships that will take years to pay for themselves. Japanese savers deposit their wartime earnings, and San Francisco's correspondents lend shorter money against trade and the expectation that the orders will continue; London, itself borrowing, lends nothing new. From 1916 Japan, flush with export earnings, lends to Russia, which buys its rifles and shells, and from 1917 to Britain and France. The Entente can go on buying Japanese goods only if someone lends it the money, and the loans give Tokyo a creditor's voice. Its exporters and the Bank of Japan keep their dollar earnings in San Francisco, and Japan ends the war a creditor nation, owed more abroad than it owes.

**[MODEL — CR-025]** The advances total $8.5bn: $1.7bn from the Treasury, $2.85bn from Pacifican savers and $3.95bn from abroad, about half of it from Union investors and the rest from collateral loans, Japan, the neutrals, and the Gulf and Latin America. About $1.66bn becomes Japanese orders. Japanese firms keep about $124m of their profits for a $1.33bn expansion and borrow the other $1.2bn from their banks.

Victoria wants peace then; he lets the spring offensive exhaust itself, accepting further mass death to secure a balance. **[APPROVED — CR-043]** The disagreement stays private: the public sees the peace made, and only her cables to Kaʻiulani at The Hague record what she wanted first. **[PROPOSED]** Without a Union army the front reaches deadlock. **[APPROVED — CR-043]** A German incendiary raid burns London in 1918, making the city receptive to a broker. In September 1918 the Salonika front breaks and Bulgaria asks for an armistice. On 30 October the Ottoman Empire signs one with Britain at Mudros, on a British battleship, before the broker has a seat. Germany is exhausted and hungry but not beaten in the field, and it still stands in France and Belgium.

Pacifica brokers the peace. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Both sides believe the broker because Julian spent 1908 to 1914 in their capitals, and this time he uses the lever he would not use in July 1914. **[PROPOSED]** In autumn 1918 Julian stops the Treasury's lending and the San Francisco syndicates' new issues for the Entente, and offers food to the starving Central Powers for an armistice. **[APPROVED — CR-043]** It is signed on 11 November, on the line Germany then holds. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** Ballin does not live to see it; he dies by his own hand on 9 November. <!-- CR-043: special mediation --> **[APPROVED — CR-043]** The peace is made by special mediation under Article 8 of the Hague Convention of 1907, which charges the two mediating powers, once war has come, with "the joint task of taking advantage of any opportunity to restore peace". The Allied Powers name Pacifica and the Central Powers name the neutral Union, and the two mediating powers negotiate the treaty between them. Pacifica's mediator is Princess Kaʻiulani; the Union's is William Jennings Bryan. **[PROPOSED — CR-043]** Bryan, who as Wilson's Secretary of State called loans to belligerents the worst of all contraband and resigned rather than lean on Berlin, is the American the Central Powers trust. He speaks for a Union whose stake in the peace is its bankers' German and Austrian loans. **[APPROVED — CR-043]** Dawes sits in his delegation for the lenders, and House comes as Wilson's own eyes. Kaʻiulani's commission, from Julian under all three crowns, makes her Mediator for the Allied Powers and First Plenipotentiary of the Pacific Crowns. Her instructions fix the outcome in outline and leave her the detail: no indemnity and no clause of war guilt; Germany's crown and Reich kept; Austria-Hungary federalised, not partitioned; the eastern states recognised; the naval limits, the court and Japan's racial-equality clause; Shandong returned to China; and the debts funded in gold dollars. Julian keeps the credit and the food lever, which Hoover administers, and chooses the moment. The conference opens on 18 January 1919 with the Dutch crown as host. **[PROPOSED]** The delegates meet in the Peace Palace that Carnegie paid for, partly from Pacifica Steel's coupons; he dies that August. **[APPROVED — CR-043]** Six commissions do the work: western, eastern, Danubian, naval, financial and the court. Kaʻiulani chairs the Danubian commission herself. **[PROPOSED — CR-043]** She presides seated, and the conference knows why. **[APPROVED — CR-043]** The Treaty of The Hague is signed in the Peace Palace on 28 June 1919, five years to the day after Sarajevo. Bryan signs for the mediating Union, and the Union Senate then refuses the naval limits and the court.

**[PROPOSED]** Germany evacuates Belgium and northern France, pays no indemnity and loses its colonies. <!-- CR-043: Alsace-Lorraine and the crown --> **[APPROVED — CR-043]** It gives up Alsace-Lorraine, though not to France: the Reichsland becomes a neutral republic in union with Switzerland. The Republic proclaimed at Strasbourg in November 1918 survives, because the armistice line keeps the French army out, and a vote of its people confirms it. It enters a customs and currency union with Switzerland on the franc; Strasbourg is a free Rhine port; the signatories guarantee its neutrality. Bern offers annexation, and the Swiss, voting by people and cantons, refuse it and approve the union. Wilhelm II abdicates as part of the peace in favour of his grandson Wilhelm, born in 1906, who reigns under a regency, and a parliamentary monarchy survives. Who is regent is **[OPEN]**. **[PROPOSED]** Germany’s clients in Poland, the Baltic region, Ukraine and Finland are recognized. Austria-Hungary survives as a fragile federation. <!-- CR-043: the Bohemian settlement --> **[APPROVED — CR-043]** The peace binds Emperor Karl to his federal manifesto of October 1918, and Bohemia is its test. Bryan will not hear of partition, which would ruin the Union's loans to Vienna, and Kaʻiulani wins what she can against him. **[PROPOSED — CR-043]** The lands of the Bohemian crown, Bohemia, Moravia and Silesia, become a federal kingdom of the monarchy with their own diet, government, courts and language: the arrangement Vienna promised in 1871 and withdrew. Karl is to be crowned in Prague. The legionnaires come home under an amnesty the treaty guarantees and form the core of the kingdom's guard. The three million German Bohemians have their rights under the court. Slovakia stays in the Hungarian half with an autonomy Budapest promises and postpones. Masaryk accepts and becomes the kingdom's first minister-president; Beneš refuses and stays in Paris. France, which recognised their National Council in 1918, has to withdraw it, and Pacifica offers land and citizenship to legionnaires who will not go home. **[PROPOSED]** Britain and France keep their Ottoman and African conquests. France’s grievance remains, and Germany dominates central European land power while lacking colonies and depending on imported oil.

<!-- CR-049: Ruanda-Urundi, Shinkolobwe and the Joachimsthal concession at The Hague -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** With no German indemnity there are no Belgian reparations. Belgium receives Ruanda-Urundi, which its troops took in 1916, from Germany's lost colonies, and Pacifican reconstruction credit. The Comité Spécial transfers the Shinkolobwe concession out of Union Minière into a new Belgian-registered company three-quarters owned by the Light and Matter Works, for ninety-nine years, with all its output going to Pacifica. Belgium keeps sovereignty, royalties, seats on the board and a free allowance of the barium-borne element for its hospitals; Union Minière gets long copper contracts; the miners are paid wages and none is forced. Britain loses Sharp's find, the Union's extraction firms are shut out, and Belgium understands what it signed in 1927. Bohemia, now a kingdom of the federal monarchy, takes Pacifican reconstruction credit, and the Joachimsthal contract becomes a fifty-year concession: the Works runs the uranium side, the mines stay the property of the Bohemian crown with royalties paid to Prague, and renewals pass through the Bohemian Diet. <!-- CR-051: the food lever is the ore lever -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** At The Hague the man who decides where Pacifican food and credit go is Hoover, and starving Vienna and Bohemia and ruined Belgium negotiate with him. Told only that the Works values the uranium side of the two concessions and that Julian wants it secured, he secures it without asking why.

Pacifica and Japan retain Germany’s Pacific islands. Pacifica keeps the Tsingtao anchorage on lease, returning the rest of Shandong to China and denying Japan the prize. **[PROPOSED]** The settlement creates a permanent arbitration court and carries Japan’s racial equality clause. Naval limits count the combined Pacifica–Japan fleets at parity with Britain, Germany below them and France and Italy below Germany. The Union stays outside these limits until 1932. The 1919 arrangement regulates allied fleets; formal common ministries are established in 1927.

The fleet limits require Britain and Germany to dispose of their older twelve-inch ships by 1922, through scrapping, training hulks or targets. Britain's Treasury welcomes the saving alongside its gold-dollar obligations. The MODEL plan reduces Britain from forty-two capital ships to twenty-four and Germany from twenty-four to sixteen; Pacifica's fourteen and Japan's ten count together. Carrier tonnage is also limited, with the Pacific partners holding the largest allowance. The Union remains outside the agreement, with eighteen capital ships by 1925 in the model, and builds two carriers after 1927. Its extra three modern ships, begun in response to the 1927 Concord, bring the modelled total to twenty-one in 1929. The crash halts further construction; the Union joins the limits in 1932.

The peace leaves two financial systems: Union loans in Berlin, Vienna and Germany’s eastern clients; Pacifican claims in London, Paris and Asia. **[PROPOSED]** Britain funds its gold-dollar debt over sixty-two years in 1923 at reduced interest, with base rights at Singapore and Hong Kong and central-Pacific islands as possible payment in kind **[OPEN-019-05]**. Sterling depreciation leaves the gold-dollar obligation intact. Britain retains fleet and empire while becoming the senior junior partner of an order Pacifica leads through debt and sea routes.

Peace ends the orders before the Japanese loans fall due. The banks renew loans to troubled borrowers rather than close employers and trading houses whose wartime success has made them powerful. Some loans are repaid; others survive only by renewal. Their foreign money stays short while the factories and ships need years. In the 1920s London's acceptance houses and the Union's banks come back, and earthquake reconstruction draws in more short money. Meanwhile the wartime balances in San Francisco are spent: on imports, on holding the yen, and after 1923 on rebuilding. Japan prospers and its Treasury runs surpluses; the danger sits in the private banks.

## 31. The Russian war: the line, the Volga, the treaty and the road to Moscow (1918–1920)

<!-- CR-040: Stage 3, the revolution, the Red Terror and the arrests; CR-041: the campaign re-planned, the tri-partite treaty and the signatory sequence -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-040]** The revolutions of 1917 come on their historical timetable. Since 1908 Julian's envoys, and since 1916 his railwaymen, have reported how brittle the Russian state is, and he is not surprised. <!-- CR-044: the railway mission --> **[PROPOSED — CR-044]** In February 1917 one of them, a Japanese-Pacifican engineer of the Polytechnic named Nagai Shōzō, cables from Petrograd that the city has three days of flour, because the trains cannot bring it. An old railway surveyor named Vance, the father of the officer who will command the corps, came out with the mission in 1916 and stays. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** In March the monarchy falls; the mutinies at Kronstadt and Helsingfors are larger than in our history, because the fleet that survived 1905 is larger, and in October its sailors are the shock troops of the Bolshevik rising. The Provisional Government fails; the Bolsheviks take power, disperse the Constituent Assembly in January 1918 and sign the peace of Brest-Litovsk in March. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** The railway mission stays in Petrograd and Moscow beside the Allied missions, to keep its engines running and to arrange the Czechoslovak Legion's passage east.

**[APPROVED — CR-041]** The war council's staff study of December 1917 becomes an operation order on 3 February 1918, the day the Bolsheviks repudiate the Russian state's debts, among them the Entente credit Pacifican savers put into the locomotives. The Treasury freezes Russian state accounts in San Francisco and sequesters the Russian gold held against arms contracts as security for the savers. The Siberian Regional Duma, dispersed at Tomsk in January, re-forms in exile at Harbin under Pacifican protection. In March the Senate votes the credit as protection of the savers' collateral and the Entente's war, and Tokyo and Peking are brought in. **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** Through the spring Julian does what the Entente does, and more of it: while London and Paris still hope to turn Trotsky against Germany, Pacifica offers the Soviets railway help, which Trotsky takes because the engines are the only thing moving grain, the Allied missions are offering the same, and the alternative is famine in Moscow, and under that cover more crews go in and depot stock is laid down along the line "for the transit". After the Penza agreement of 26 March the railway mission arranges the Legion's passage, and a Pacifican liaison officer rides with every echelon carrying sealed orders. Japanese divisions concentrate at Harbin through the thaw; a Chinese agreement opens the Khingan; the Pacifican corps ships across the Pacific. By May the Legion is strung from Penza to Urajio in some sixty echelons, and that dispersion is the weapon.

<!-- CR-040: the May 1918 decision, framed and triggered as approved on 29 October 2026; CR-041: no declaration of war -->
**[APPROVED — CR-040]** Brest-Litovsk makes Moscow part of the German danger. The treaty frees Germany's eastern divisions for France and opens Ukraine's grain, the Donbas coal and the road to Caspian oil to a Germany the blockade was starving, and the Entente feels it on the Western Front. On 25 May Trotsky orders the Czechoslovak Legion disarmed on its way east, and the Legion seizes the railway. Julian moves the same week. To London and Paris he states the Entente's own objectives: to bring the Legion out, to keep the Siberian railway, its grain and Pacifica's engines out of German reach, and to put Japan's army to work in the one theatre of the war it can reach. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** Nobody declares war on Russia. The Entente has not recognised the Council of People's Commissars, Russia is at peace with Germany, and the coalition acts, as Britain does at Murmansk, to protect stores, nationals and the Legion's transit at the request of local Russian authorities. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** With London burning, the Entente would rather have Pacifica's credit than its regiments, and the credit keeps flowing; the German offensive that opens on the Aisne on 27 May makes the week the right one. Julian's private reason is the one he had in August 1914: Japan's army will go into Siberia in any case, and he would rather it went under a common plan.

<!-- CR-041: the Week, the order of battle and the line -->
**[APPROVED — CR-041]** The line is taken by seizure, not by advance. Between 26 May and 10 June 1918 the Legion takes the stations from Chelyabinsk to Samara on its own timetable; the railway brigade, whose crews already sit in the cabs, takes the workshops at Omsk, Krasnoyarsk, Chita and Irkutsk; the Japanese 12th Division takes Irkutsk and the thirty-nine Circum-Baikal tunnels before the retreating Reds can bring their explosives train to Port Baikal; the Transbaikal Cossacks, paid, open the line to Chita; one Pacifican division lands at Urajio and the second comes up through Harbin. By the second week of June the line is in coalition and Legion hands from the Pacific to the Volga, except the Yenisei–Baikal section, which is cleared in July. No action is larger than the Legion's fight for Chelyabinsk. The coal fields at Cheremkhovo, Anzhero-Sudzhensk, the Kuzbass and Suchan are listed before the stations are. **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** The order of battle in May 1918 is the Legion, about 42,000 men; a Pacifican Expeditionary Corps of 50,000 under Marcus Vance III, two infantry divisions, a railway brigade of 8,000, a river-flotilla detachment and two air squadrons, the only force from outside Russia, the Legion apart, that ever goes west of the Yenisei; three Japanese divisions, five by September and seven by 1919, whose field divisions hold the line east of the Yenisei and the Transbaikal and go no further west, because Tokyo will not and Julian does not ask, while Japanese line-guard battalions relieve the Legion section by section as far as Omsk and the Altai branch; a Chinese brigade on the Chinese Eastern Railway, since Peking's price is that Chinese soldiers guard it; the Siberian Army of Pepelyaev and Grishin-Almazov, 5,000 in June and 40,000 by October; Komuch's People's Army under Kappel on the Volga; Alash cavalry on the steppe; and the Cossack hosts, bought, not commanded. The Red forces east of the Volga are detachments of a few hundred to a few thousand. The line passes twelve pairs of trains a day on the single track east of Omsk and thirty on the doubled track west of it; one supply train a day holds a division in the line and two feed one in battle; half the trains are the line's own. The corps can fight at the Urals with its two divisions and feed a Russian army there on Siberian grain bought with Pacifican gold **[MODEL]**.

The Russian war gives Julian the decisive military test he seeks. Pacifica commands a coalition of Japan, China, Siberians and, from August 1918, Alash, with Britain inside the coalition. Japan provides the mass of the army in the east; China provides manpower and the Manchurian railway; Siberians defend a country of their own. Pacifica contributes command, finance, railway organization, artillery, armored trains, aircraft, river flotillas and the expeditionary corps. **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** Command is divided as the money is: Fleet-General Kuroda Kiyoshi is the coalition's commander-in-chief at Irkutsk; the Japanese field commander has the east of the Yenisei, the Russian commanders the field west of the Urals, Vance the line and the depots, and Julian the credit; the man who feeds and pays commands. Julian sits at Irkutsk with the war council through the summer of 1918 and commands no army; from the autumn he governs from Thalassia and the war from its cables.

<!-- CR-058: the Canadians at Irkutsk -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Canada sends a brigade of 4,192 men under Major-General James H. Elmsley, carried from Vancouver in the Canadian Pacific's liners. It lands at Urajio and goes up the line to Irkutsk, where its headquarters stand beside the coalition's command, with a squadron of the Royal North-West Mounted Police to police the railway zone. The Canadian Economic Commission (Siberia), created in October 1918 under Dana Wilgress, goes with it; the Royal Bank opens a branch at Urajio and keeps it open after the war for Canadian trade through the Concord's port; and the Canadian Locomotive Company of Kingston, which built decapods for Russia in 1915–1918, builds for the railway board. Borden keeps a volunteer brigade under the coalition's command through the campaign of 1919, its costs paid by Pacifica, for Siberian trade, the orders and Canada's own standing at the peace. French Canadians volunteer gladly: Pacifica, with its county languages, its religious freedom, its refusal to send missionaries and its distance from London's wars, is popular in Québec. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** The brigade's duties at Irkutsk are the depots and the command, the railway zone and the board. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** The veterans come home in 1920 as the Irkutsk men, a network friendly to Pacifica in the militia and among the bush pilots of the 1920s.

<!-- CR-043: the Legion's pay --> **[APPROVED — CR-043]** France pays the Czechoslovak Legion, part of the Czechoslovak army it recognised in December 1917, in francs from the Entente credit Pacifica raised. Pacifica feeds it, carries it and pays a supplement on the line. **[PROPOSED — CR-043]** The Legion takes its orders from Paris and its bread from the railway board, and it fights for a country of its own. **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** The Legion sails from Urajio in 1919, a year earlier than it could have, once Siberian and Japanese troops take over its stretch of the line and the Volga is held; its last homebound echelons cross the Volga bridge in June 1919, and the wagons it has lived in go back to carrying freight. The coalition moves west from Urajio through Chinese-owned Manchuria under a single command. Its local partner is the Siberian Regionalist government, initially under Pyotr Vologodsky, while Zhang Zuolin supplies the Chinese cooperation. A Pacifican railway board runs the line west of Chita. **[PROPOSED — CR-030]** Trained before the war, the board takes over the engines Pacifican works built for Russia and pays the railwaymen and the coal miners on time in sound money. It feeds their families from its own stores so that nobody leaves the line to look for bread. New loops, larger water mains and strengthened bridges on the single-track branch from Manchuria double its trains within a year. Three paths in ten stay with Siberian grain, coal and passengers: the railway half of the land-and-grain guarantee. **[PROPOSED — CR-031]** The board's worst enemy in 1919 is not the Red Army but the ataman at the junction, who holds its trains for payment and shoots whoever he calls a Bolshevik. Tokyo stops his subsidy, and that summer the Transbaikal Cossack circle meets at Chita with a Japanese diplomat in the hall. A frontline officer with his own St George's Cross moves that the Host recognise the Siberian government and put its regiments under the coalition. The ataman's officers walk out, the vote carries, and he leaves for Urajio on a Japanese pension. The Cossacks of the Onon and the Argun ride west to the Urals. Chinese ownership, Japanese running rights and common wartime administration keep the supply route usable without giving its territory to Japan.

<!-- CR-041: the Volga summer, Kazan and the gold -->
**[APPROVED — CR-041]** Kappel and the Legion go up the Volga: Simbirsk on 22 July, Kazan on 7 August 1918, and with Kazan the Imperial gold reserve, some 650 million rubles. The Pacifican corps does not follow them. With the Legion it takes Yekaterinburg on 25 July, relieves the Legion at Chelyabinsk, and builds the Ural gate; it sends west a river flotilla of eight motor gunboats shipped by rail and a brigade. The arms workers of Izhevsk and Votkinsk rise on 8 August, about fifteen thousand armed men, and the corps' Perm wing reaches them from Yekaterinburg in September, before the Red 2nd Army can; the works change hands. In September the Red 5th Army and the flotilla Trotsky has brought through the canals attack Kazan: Kappel's 4,000, the Czechs' 3,000, a Pacifican brigade of 6,000 and the gunboats hold it. The turbine gunboats outgun the canal destroyers in the shallows at the Kama mouth and the Red landing above the town is shot off the bank. Kazan is held, and the Volga is the coalition's line through the winter. The gold has already gone east to a Reserve Bank escrow at Chelyabinsk.

<!-- CR-041: the tri-partite Siberian treaty of August 1918 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-041]** Vologodsky's Provisional Siberian Government declared Siberia independent on 4 July 1918, and the Committee of Members of the Constituent Assembly at Samara, Komuch, has recognised Alash autonomy pending the Assembly. At Omsk on 4 August 1918, the day after the Siberian government's commission under Sapozhnikov closes its sittings with Bukeikhanov, Yermekov and Tokhtamyshev, and at Samara two days later, the two governments and the Alash Orda sign the Siberian treaty under Pacifica's guarantee. It takes the commission's draft of 29 July–3 August: mixed institutions in Akmolinsk, Semipalatinsk and Turgai under commissars appointed jointly, Alash courts, the kibitka tax and local councils, a Kazakh army raised by Alash on the Siberian army's plan under common command, and the land of the three oblasts frozen as it stands. Pacifica adds what the parties could not give each other: the mutual recognition Alash had withdrawn at Omsk is restored; Komuch's suspensive veto over Alash is replaced by the guarantor's arbitration; Siberia and Alash are recognised as two autonomies in union, with "a federal power of autonomous Siberia and autonomous Alash, or an all-Russian federal power created with their participation" as the agreed horizon, in the draft's own words; and the Constituent Assembly's land law, which Komuch had let the landlords evade, is a condition of credit, enforced by the Reserve Bank's paymaster at Samara, who withholds the second tranche until it is. The Western Alash Orda at Dzhambeity, whose ground is the Uralsk oblast, adheres to the treaty and signs an Emba oil concession to Pacifica with it. The Ministry of Agriculture and Colonisation's dissent, that the political relation between Siberia, Alash and Russia is left undefined and will become acute and dangerous, is recorded in the protocol. At Victoria's insistence the Office prints the treaty and deposits it in Peking and Tokyo before a rifle reaches any Russian general. The Kazan gold is the escrow against it. <!-- CR-045: the paymaster and the finance minister --> **[APPROVED — CR-045]** The escrow answers to the Reserve Bank's chief paymaster in Siberia, Pavel Kashevarov, a Creole from Sitka, at Omsk. **[PROPOSED — CR-045]** His great-uncle charted the Alaskan coast for the Russian-American Company and died a Russian general. **[APPROVED — CR-045]** Siberia's finance minister, Ivan Mikhailov, is twenty-six, the quickest mind in Omsk and the man his enemies call Vanka-Kain, after the thief who turned police informer. He spends August trying to bring the gold under his ministry's seal, and fails. Afterwards each of them knows what the other is.

**[APPROVED — CR-040 R9]** On the night of 16–17 July 1918, as the coalition and the Legion close on the Urals, the Ural Soviet's men shoot Nicholas, Alexandra, their five children, their doctor and three servants in the cellar of the Ipatiev House at Yekaterinburg, so that no one can free them. The next night the Romanovs held at Alapayevsk are thrown alive down a mineshaft. The Legion and the corps take Yekaterinburg on 25 July. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Pacifican investigators come in with them: Polytechnic chemists and surveyors, with officers of the Office of Public Measures. The bodies were moved from the first mine and buried in a pit on the Koptyaki road when a lorry stuck. By questioning the villagers, following the lorry's tracks and surveying the soil, the investigators find the grave within weeks.

**[PROPOSED — CR-040]** On 30 August 1918 Uritsky, head of the Petrograd Cheka, is assassinated, and Lenin is shot and wounded in Moscow. The next day Cheka men force their way into the British embassy in Petrograd and kill the naval attaché, Captain Cromie, on its staircase. On 5 September the Council of People's Commissars decrees the Red Terror: class enemies are to be isolated in camps, anyone connected with White organisations, conspiracies or risings is to be shot, and the names of the shot are to be published. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R6]** Pacifica's railway advisers are taken hostage in Petrograd and Moscow with the Japanese residents, and some of them are shot. <!-- CR-044: the hostages --> **[APPROVED — CR-044]** They are shot in the week Cromie dies on the embassy staircase. **[PROPOSED — CR-044]** There are eleven, and the engineer who counted Petrograd's flour is one of them. The Japanese residents die with them. The oldest of the advisers, the surveyor Vance, is kept alive, and is still a hostage two years later. **[APPROVED — CR-044]** When Petrograd falls the Pacifican and British dead are buried together. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** Thalassia now names the enemy: the Council of People's Commissars is declared a conspiracy in arms against the Russian people and its lawful government, and the coalition will treat it as such. It is the only declaration the war ever has, and it is not a declaration of war on Russia. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R6]** In the autumn the Office of Public Measures translates the Cheka's own lists and publishes them beside the decree that ordered them, **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** with the cellar at Yekaterinburg and the grave on the Koptyaki road. Nothing needs inventing. The regime's own words do the work, and the names left off and the lists that end in a number say the rest. Victoria's rule that seeing is believing now works against Moscow.

<!-- CR-040: the promise to Siberia follows the Romanov discovery (author, 29 October 2026); CR-041: it is the public ratification of the July treaty -->
**[APPROVED — CR-040 R10]** Julian goes into Siberia to build a country, not to hold a line. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** On 10 September 1918, after the cellar at Yekaterinburg and the decree of the Terror, he publicly ratifies the Siberian treaty of August and promises a Siberia on the Pacifican model, with the coalition's guarantee of its frontier: Siberians will not be handed back to the men who did this. Siberian soldiers enlist believing they have a country of their own. Two days earlier a State Conference has opened at Ufa to found one government for all of anti-Bolshevik Russia, and two days later Bukeikhanov tells it, for the native peoples, that Russia is federated, democratic and unified, and that they will go forward with the Russian people to build it. With Julian's word and a deposited treaty behind him, Vologodsky does not give up Siberia's independence to the conference. **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** The same week the Red flotilla fails before Kazan. When the conference forms its Directory on 23 September, with Avksentiev at its head and General Boldyrev commanding, the Directory inherits the treaty as a condition of Pacifican credit, and it never issues a decree abolishing the regional governments. A peasant land-and-grain guarantee supplies a political basis for holding the railway country. **[PROPOSED — CR-031]** The guarantee travels down the Lena. Yakutia keeps its own committees under Vasily Nikiforov, who founded the Union of Yakuts in 1906 and went to prison for it, and elects an assembly in 1919, and when the Directory's officials are overthrown on the Okhotsk coast, at Anadyr and in Kamchatka that winter, relief ships and an amnesty answer them instead of a punitive column. Pacifica freezes the debt books of its own San Francisco traders in Chukotka until a commission can write them down. German clients in the west and Chinese cooperation prevent Moscow from concentrating its full strength in the east.

**[APPROVED — CR-045]** Mikhailov's first debt is September's. On the night of 20–21 September 1918 Colonel Volkov, commanding the Omsk garrison, arrests the ministers the Regional Duma has sent to remake the Siberian government, and two days later his escort shoots the one who would not sign his resignation, Novoselov, in the Zagorodnaya grove outside the city. Mikhailov decided the arrests with Volkov and was nowhere near the grove. On 24 September the Duma asks the Legion to arrest him, and Czech counter-intelligence looks for him while he hides in Annenkov's Cossack barracks. Then the Pacifican liaison officers persuade the Legion's command to call the search off: Kashevarov will not have the treasury's signatory taken in the week the Directory is born. The Directory's investigator finds nothing that reaches Mikhailov, and Volkov swears on his honour as an officer that he had no accomplices. Mikhailov owes them both.

Colonel Ward's battalion of the Middlesex Regiment stands to in its barracks and does nothing, which is all Volkov needs from it.
