The Legion's Czechs at the station, whose National Council wants the Directory and who ride with Pacifican liaison officers, will not answer Volkov's call. The Japanese line-guard battalion on the Irtysh bridge answers to the railway board. The French mission, waiting for Janin and thinking of France's Russian bonds, gives the Legion no order and sends no one. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** The Reserve Bank's paymaster announces that no Siberian ministry is funded until the Directory sits, and Boldyrev comes back from Ufa on a Pacifican train. **[PROPOSED — CR-044]** Before the Council of Ministers meets, the corps commander, Marcus Vance, has an hour with Kolchak. He does not threaten him; he shows him the timetable: no train for a Supreme Ruler east of Omsk or west of it, and a ship for an admiral wherever he likes. Kolchak reads it twice and asks which ship. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** When the Council offers Admiral Kolchak, the Directory's war minister, the title of Supreme Ruler, he declines it in front of them. Volkov's officers are disarmed. Ivan Mikhailov, the finance minister behind them, is dismissed and leaves for Harbin. Omsk never falls, to anyone. **[PROPOSED — CR-044]** In Volkov's papers the brigade finds what the British knew and when, and the Office files them without a word: the first folder of Pacifica's British file. London protests through its ambassador at Thalassia the next week and is answered with the ledger of who pays the Siberian army. At the War Office from January, Churchill reads Knox's account of the night. Ward's book of 1920 tells the British public that Pacifica bought Siberia out from under them. **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** Kolchak is offered the Baltic under Kiyoshi in 1919, refuses to serve under a Japanese admiral, and spends 1919 and 1920 in Japan.

<!-- CR-045: the sale of the night -->
**[APPROVED — CR-045]** The railway was ready because it had been told. After the Armistice of 11 November Mikhailov can see that the war in the west is over and that the money in the east is Pacifica's. On the evening of 17 November, while the banquet is still singing, he walks down the line to Kashevarov's car and sells the night: the hour, the names, what Knox knows, and his own copy of what the British mission has promised. <!-- CR-046: the Omsk promise --> **[APPROVED — CR-046]** It is a promise of credit to whatever government sits in Omsk on 19 November, drawn on a British account; two copies were made, the mission's and the finance minister's. **[APPROVED — CR-045]** His price is his ministry under whatever government sits in the morning. Kashevarov wires Thalassia. Julian lets the arrests begin, so that the coup fails in public and is owned by the men who made it. The Directory's ministers spend the night in Cossack hands, **[PROPOSED — CR-045]** and some are marched toward the grove where Novoselov died before a brigade patrol meets them on the road. **[APPROVED — CR-045]** What the brigade finds in Volkov's papers is Mikhailov's gift, and the Office files it as found. Mikhailov is not where the guns are. He comes late to the morning Council, after the paymaster has spoken, and is dismissed, not given his ministry. At the court that tries the night's officers Volkov swears again that he had no accomplices, and this time he believes it. He is cashiered.

<!-- CR-045: Harbin and Maria -->
**[APPROVED — CR-045]** At Harbin Mikhailov runs the Chinese Eastern Railway's economic bureau and becomes London's man in exile. British money pays for his newspaper, and the White officers who meet in his house tell him what they tell the British. Kashevarov pays him, reads his reports before London does, and holds the night of 17 November over him. Mikhailov keeps a ledger of his own. In Omsk his mistress, Maria Grishina-Almazova, once a café singer in Khabarovsk and now the hostess of the city's monarchist salon, waits for her husband. General Grishin-Almazov, the Siberian Army's first commander, lost his ministry in September 1918 and went south to Denikin. On 5 May 1919, crossing the Caspian toward Omsk with Denikin's letters, he is taken at sea by a Red armed steamer out of Astrakhan and shoots himself. That winter she comes to Harbin on her way to Japan. Mikhailov asks her to marry him, and she refuses. She sails from Dalian.

<!-- CR-041: the gate, Perm and the winter of 1918–19 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-041]** The coalition's line settles on the Ural gate, Perm–Kungur–Krasnoufimsk–Ufa, with Dutov's Cossacks at Orenburg on the steppe flank and the Volga held in front of it. Pepelyaev's corps, the bulk of the Siberian Army and now some 45,000 men, with Pacifican heavy artillery and the armoured train *Pele* on the Kungur line, takes Perm across the frozen Kama on 24 December 1918 from a collapsing Red 3rd Army that loses 18,000 men and the Motovilikha gun works. In January 1919 the Pacifican 1st Division and Siberian cavalry go down the Perm–Vyatka line to Glazov and a cavalry raid cuts the Vologda–Vyatka line at the Kotelnich bridge and withdraws; Vyatka town stays Red, the 3rd Army's last base. Through the autumn, while the barges still run, and the winter after it, Vance fills the depots, and the Directory, with Boldyrev commanding and Pacifican instructors in every regiment, builds a Russian army at Chelyabinsk and Omsk on the Kazan gold, about 120,000 men at the front by April **[MODEL]**. Moscow never sees the Siberian harvest of 1918.

**[PROPOSED — CR-040]** In the autumn of 1918, the months in which he squeezes the Entente toward peace, Julian decides that the Russian war cannot stop at the Urals. No frontier with a state that shoots hostages by class will ever be quiet; Siberians will not accept it, and refugees will cross it every winter. One campaign that ends the regime costs less than a front held against it for ever **[MODEL]**. Since December 1917 Moscow has addressed its revolution to the peoples of the East, and the workers of Japan, Korea and China are next. And the partner exists. After the armistice his envoys reach Kyiv, where Alexander Krivoshein, Stolypin's minister of agriculture, whom Julian met in 1908, sits among the Russian conservatives under the Hetman's protection, and through him a cavalry general of the Volunteer Army, Pyotr Wrangel. Both offer a Russia that will recognise the settlement and pay its debts. Julian does not decide alone: Tokyo must commit more than the Siberian plan, Peking must keep the Manchurian line open, and the Senate must vote the credit. Victoria agrees that the regime must end, and reminds him that he has given the Siberians his word.

<!-- CR-041: the Volga battle of 1919 and Vance on the bridge (CR-040 K1 knock-on, relocated) -->
**[APPROVED — CR-041]** The Red Army of 1919 is formidable on paper and starving in fact. Between 25 May and 15 June 1919 its Eastern Front, some 120,000 men under Kamenev and Frunze, attacks the Volga at spring high water. The Directory's Volga Army of 50,000 under Boldyrev holds it with the Pacifican corps artillery and aircraft and the flotilla, which owns the river; Kappel's corps strikes the southern group's flank at Alatyr. **[APPROVED — CR-041, CR-040 K1 knock-on]** Marcus Vance III holds the Volga bridge at Zelenodolsk, then Zelyony Dol, while the trains clear it: the Russian wounded, the railway families, and the last homebound transport of the Czechoslovak Legion cross behind him, Vance having come forward from the depots to clear his own trains. **[PROPOSED — CR-030]** The battle is prepared the autumn before. While the line is free and the rivers are open, the depots behind the Urals are filled, barges come up the Amur and the Shilka to Sretensk, and the first Kara Sea convoy carries stores up the Ob and the Yenisei. In the battle the trains bring only what the depots cannot hold. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Czech and Slovak representatives honour Vance for the Legion's men. Nothing changes hands, which is the victory: the Red spring offensive is spent.

**[PROPOSED — CR-041]** In 1918 the Urals were to have been the line, with the Volga in front of it; after the bargain with Wrangel the coalition's line is wherever the Russian armies are. **[PROPOSED — CR-030]** The narrowest stretches of the supply line lie far behind the front: the Chinese line over the Khingan and the single-track branch into Transbaikalia. The alliance depends on Chinese goodwill as much as on its guns. **[PROPOSED — CR-042]** Pacifica's own door is Huludao. From 1918 part of the corps' stores comes in through the Chinese harbour and up the Chinese line through Mukden to Harbin, so that nothing the corps needs depends on one port alone.

<!-- CR-040: Stage 4, Wrangel, Moscow, the arrests and Kronstadt; CR-041: the bargain's mechanism -->
**[APPROVED — CR-040 R10]** The bargain that wins the war costs the promise. Moscow must fall, and only Russian armies can take it. **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** In October 1919, as Denikin's drive on Moscow stalls at Orel, a Russian military council convened by the Directory on Boldyrev's proposal, with Pacifica's paymaster in the room, gives the main campaign to Wrangel; Denikin keeps the Volunteer Army's name and the Caucasus front. Shatilov is his chief of staff, Kutepov leads the southern striking force, Kappel commands the eastern army, and Yudenich presses on Petrograd. Wrangel takes his guns from the Directory's hand, at Novorossiysk, from Pacifican convoys that unload only when the paymaster certifies a result, and he inherits the Directory's signature with them. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R10]** Wrangel's price is the unity of Russia, Siberia included, and Julian pays it. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** He cannot pay it by tearing up a treaty deposited in Peking and Tokyo, and he does not. Wrangel asks for something that looks smaller: that the coalition not stand in the way when Siberia's own assembly chooses the federation, and that Pacifica's credit, the Office's films and the railway board's payrolls lean the way Omsk already leans. Julian gives his word on that, which is the trade. He trades Siberian independence for Moscow, and for a Russia that will owe him its survival. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Pacifica's money reaches Wrangel only against results: Julian has learned in July 1914 that offering men something to lose does not move them. Victoria reads the bargain as the Union's old design on California, done this time by her son, and says so to him once, in private.

The Perm wing comes down the frozen Vyatka behind the armoured train. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Kutepov takes Tula in February 1920, **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** Wrangel's cavalry having cut the line behind the town at Uzlovaya and the Tula arms workers having stood aside. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** The Oka line breaks at the end of the month. **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** On 25 February Trotsky takes his command train south to hold the Oka at Serpukhov, where the Moscow–Kursk bridge crosses and Kutepov's advance guard is already on the south bank. That night Wrangel's cavalry cuts the line behind him at Lopasnya and blows the culvert. On the 26th the Volunteer armoured train *Ofitser* comes up the south embankment and the two trains fight across the river at two kilometres for an hour, until a shell disables Trotsky's lead locomotive; his escort train, backing north, finds the culvert gone and the cavalry on the embankment. Kutepov's infantry crosses the ice above and below the bridge in the night, and at first light on the 27th, after a six-hour siege, the train surrenders. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Trotsky is taken alive by Kutepov's troops. **[APPROVED — CR-040]** Moscow falls in March, through defeat, evacuation and local surrender. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Wrangel's terms protect the ordinary soldiers and the officials who keep the city's services running; the Cheka is left to the courts, not to the officers.

**[PROPOSED — CR-046]** With the city the Office's investigators take the records of the banks the Bolsheviks nationalised in December 1917, which nobody in Moscow read for what they were. They go east to the Reserve Bank for audit. In the winter of 1916–17 it paid men in Petrograd, and in December 1916 it paid for something its clerk called "the December affair".

It runs west from Glazov through Vyatka's suburbs under fire to the Kotelnich bridge, where the river is a kilometre wide, retakes it from its guard in the days before Lenin's train is due, and waits on the far side with two squadrons of Russian cavalry on the frozen banks; the engineers lift a rail behind the last siding west of the bridge and replace it with one cut through. The Pacifican commander is Major George S. Patton, a Californian cavalryman seconded to the railway brigade's trains. **[PROPOSED — CR-041]** He declines to come aboard: the government is to be handed to Russians, not treated with, and the man in the other train understands it. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** The coalition hands him to the Russian authorities for trial. The man who came home in a sealed German train in 1917 is taken on a railway the Pacificans run.

**[APPROVED — CR-040 K1]** In this war Siberia is the rear and the decision is in European Russia. In June 1919 he directed the recapture of the Krasnaya Gorka fort from the sea and told Lenin that he deplored the naval science that called it impossible. **[APPROVED — CR-044]** But when Moscow falls he slips away south and is next heard of in Tiflis, and it is Dzerzhinsky, who founded the Cheka, who goes north to hold the fort Stalin retook and make Petrograd and the Kronstadt fleet the regime's last base. **[OPEN]** Stalin's road south. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Yudenich takes Petrograd by land in March and April 1920, the squadron's aircraft spotting for his guns against Krasnaya Gorka, whose garrison surrenders with the city.

<!-- CR-040 K1: Kronstadt, approved verbatim; CR-044 B6: the commissar on Kronstadt is Dzerzhinsky -->

**[APPROVED — CR-040 K1, amended by CR-044]** Kronstadt is the last Red fortress. When Petrograd falls by land in the spring of 1920, Dzerzhinsky crosses the ice to the island with the Petrograd Cheka and the fleet's commissars. He makes the battleship *Petropavlovsk* his headquarters and keeps the hostages aboard her: the families of officers who went over to the Whites, and the last of the Pacifican railway advisers taken in 1918. When the ice goes out at the end of April and the coalition's Baltic squadron comes up the channel, he sends word that the hostages will be shot if a single ship enters the roads.

**[APPROVED — CR-040 K1]** Kuroda Kiyoshi does not enter the roads. The sailors' families live in Petrograd, which the coalition now holds, and he lets their letters cross under a white flag every morning. The commissars read every one, and cannot stop them without losing the crew. In the second week a reply comes back in clumsy Japanese characters. It is from a senior boatswain's mate of the *Petropavlovsk*, one of the men Kiyoshi took off the captured cruiser in 1905, who learned a little Japanese from the interpreters in the camp at Matsuyama. He writes where the hostages are kept, how many Chekists there are, and what the crew thinks of them.

**[APPROVED — CR-040 K1, amended by CR-044]** Dzerzhinsky demands that the admiral come aboard alone to bargain. Kiyoshi accepts, in full dress with his sword, and asks for only one thing: an interpreter who speaks Japanese, so that nothing is lost. Dzerzhinsky trusts no coalition interpreter and will not let one aboard. He sends for the only man in the ship who speaks Japanese. To make the admiral see who holds the ship, he packs the wardroom with his Chekists, and while they stand behind him the boatswain's mates quietly take over the guard below.

**[APPROVED — CR-040 K1, amended by CR-044]** Kiyoshi makes the offer plainly and has it translated in full: a neutral ship to any port Dzerzhinsky names, for him alone, in exchange for the hostages. Dzerzhinsky refuses with contempt, for his men to hear. Kiyoshi bows and turns to them. Since their commissar will not take the terms for himself, he says, he offers them instead: any man who lays down his rifle goes ashore to his family in Petrograd tonight. Dzerzhinsky sees his guards look at one another, and then at him; a sailor appears in the doorway to say the hostage deck is in the crew's hands. He understands that the admiral never came to bargain with him, and he loses his head. He shoots the interpreter, then fires twice into Kiyoshi.

**[APPROVED — CR-040 K1, amended by CR-044]** Kiyoshi was raised in Satsuma, whose school of the sword teaches that the blade stays sheathed until it is needed and that there is no second stroke. He draws and cuts in one motion, and Dzerzhinsky is dead before he falls. The guards throw down their rifles; the crew holds the doors; the shots are the signal for the cutters waiting in the dark. Kiyoshi lives long enough to see the hostages brought up alive and the red flag hauled down, and dies on the quarterdeck in the morning light. The fleet surrenders without a gun fired into the town. The ensign that goes up is the St Andrew's flag of the navy he fought in 1905, and the boatswain's mate who brought him aboard is buried with the honours of both navies. **[PROPOSED — CR-044]** The first of the Pacifican advisers brought up from the hostage deck was the corps commander's father, the old surveyor Vance. <!-- CR-046: Dzerzhinsky's dispatch case --> **[PROPOSED — CR-046]** In Dzerzhinsky's cabin the boarding party finds a dispatch case: the Cheka's British file, which London had paid to have burned. **[APPROVED — CR-046]** In February, at Copenhagen, Britain and Moscow agreed to exchange their prisoners, and with the prisoners went gold for the papers. Dzerzhinsky took the gold, let the British go and kept the file. It names whom the Petrograd account paid. Some of the gold paid his Chekists on Kronstadt in their last weeks, while the coalition's hostages waited below. Nobody in London meant it for Japan. Julian never tells Hirohito.

**[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Japan enshrines Kiyoshi as a war god. A son of Elias Thorne is now sacred in Japan. Julian has the sword brought home. **[OPEN]** Where it rests, and whether Kiyoshi leaves a death poem.

**[PROPOSED — OPEN-018-18]** His death gives Marcus Vance III a command he never wanted. **[PROPOSED — CR-030]** Vance's timetable decides how fast the coalition can move west: a division goes forward only when the line can feed it. A cautious railway and logistics officer, Vance has designed the line and its fallback; he fears failing the dead whose name he carries. His competence helps win the war while exposing him to a decision that damages the partnership he serves.

## 32. The trials, the Altai, the Treaty of Harbin and Victoria’s renunciation (1920–1922)

<!-- CR-040: the Romanov burial and the trials -->

**[APPROVED — CR-040 R9]** When Petrograd falls, Wrangel's government buries the Romanovs in the Peter and Paul Cathedral. <!-- CR-044: the burial --> **[APPROVED — CR-044]** Victoria comes from Thalassia. In the cathedral she meets the British royal party: the Dowager Empress is Queen Alexandra's sister, and the asylum George V withdrew in 1917 is a thing both houses remember and neither mentions. <!-- CR-046: Victoria in the cathedral --> **[APPROVED — CR-046]** By the burial Victoria has read the Petrograd account and the file the boarding party brought out of Kronstadt. **[PROPOSED — CR-046]** In the cathedral she takes Queen Alexandra's hands and turns from the King's representative without a word. London's papers ask whether it is the asylum, Hawaii's old grievances or her age, and members ask in the Commons. **[APPROVED — CR-046]** The few in London who know what was paid in Petrograd in 1916 wonder whether she knows it, and from that day London is looking. She keeps the ledger shut to protect the settlement, as she will keep the Altai a year later. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** The Dowager Empress and Grand Duke Kirill give the monarchists a figurehead, and whether federal Russia ends as a monarchy or a republic is **[OPEN]**.

**[APPROVED — CR-040 R8]** Lenin and Trotsky are tried together before a Russian court in Moscow for state crimes of which they are guilty, on evidence that exists. Lenin's Council of People's Commissars decreed the Red Terror on 5 September 1918, and on 11 August he had telegraphed the Penza communists to hang no fewer than a hundred kulaks publicly, as an example. Trotsky's order of 30 September 1918 had the families of officers who went over to the Whites arrested as hostages. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R9]** The murder of the Romanov family is charged against them both. The Ural Soviet carried it out; who decided is the question the court must answer. Trotsky answers it from the dock: Sverdlov told him that they had decided it in Moscow, and Lenin did not want to leave the Whites a live banner to rally around. He names Lenin to save himself on that count, and it does not save him. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R8]** Both are executed in late 1920. Stalin is never tried: **[APPROVED — CR-044]** he is in Georgia, whose Menshevik government will not hand a Georgian to a Russian court. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** The Office publishes the record of the trial as it published the lists. Julian has backed a public reckoning in Moscow.

<!-- CR-046: Stalin's offer and death -->
**[APPROVED — CR-046]** Georgia's refusal to hand Stalin over suits London. He knows from the Politburo what was bought at Copenhagen, and London knows he knows; a Bolshevik in a Russian dock says what Trotsky said. After the executions he tries to sell what he knows to Pacifica. He sends Kamo, the bank robber of 1907 and the only man he trusts, with nothing on paper, overland by Kars to Pacifica's envoy at Ankara, the one capital near him with no British official in it, to offer it for passage abroad. The offer reaches Kashevarov, and Thalassia hesitates over a Bolshevik commissar months after the executions. The British secret service keeps a man on the Pacifican envoy's door, and Kamo's face has been in Europe's newspapers since 1907. London never learns what he said, and does not need to. In the autumn of 1921 Stalin is shot in Tiflis by Georgians with old reasons of their own, while Thalassia's answer is still on its way. **[PROPOSED — CR-046]** Kamo dies the next summer, when a lorry runs down his bicycle in Tiflis.

The decisive gas massacre takes place in the Altai in 1921, in the partisan war behind the coalition’s line. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R10]** The partisans are a mixed force: Red remnants, and Siberian peasants and regionalists who refuse the federal compact. **[PROPOSED — OPEN-018-18]** A partisan army threatens the Altai branch of the railway while holding prisoners from a Japanese line-guard battalion. Vance uses gas knowing the prisoners are present; Japanese soldiers die with their captors. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R10]** Some of the dead are Siberians Julian promised a country. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** The crime betrays Vance's own act on the Volga bridge and Kiyoshi's at Kronstadt: Kiyoshi died to bring hostages out alive, Vance's own father among them, and Vance gasses prisoners. **[PROPOSED — OPEN-018-18]** A war marked by terror and mass killing gives this crime particular force because it sacrifices the partner’s own men to secure a victory.

**[PROPOSED — OPEN-018-18]** Julian approves a falsified report to protect the approaching treaty, months after he backed the trials in Moscow. Vance signs it, then tells Victoria the truth. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R10]** She confronts her adopted son over the gas, the lie and Siberia. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Behind the charge stands the seal she was refused in 1920, a withheld account set beside a falsified one. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** Beside both she sets the true sentences Pacifica leaves standing to mislead Europe. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R10]** Her charge is one chain: the promise of 1918, the trade of 1919, the gas and the lie. Pacifica exists because California refused to be handed back to a continental empire, and he has done to Siberia what the Union tried to do to California. It cuts at her own guilt too: she let Hawaii's sovereignty be stretched after 1874 and let the 1876 judgment go unenforced, and she cannot forgive her son for repeating her failure. To her the federal compact is a cage with a constitution. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** She adds the count the treaty itself supplies: Siberia was asked and Alash was not; he put the question to one people and had the other's answer written for it. Julian answers that the federation is the only Siberia that survives, and that Siberia's own regionalists were federalists from the start, which Sapozhnikov had said for them at Ufa; she answers that a federation imposed by a victor is not consent, and that a vote nobody forced and nobody freed is not either. The storyline gives no verdict. **[PROPOSED — OPEN-018-18]** She renounces her remaining executive role in 1921 at about eighty-three, keeping the private standing she uses in 1924 and 1928. **[PROPOSED — CR-022]** He knows that she has kept the Donner lie since 1887. <!-- CR-046: the ledger she kept shut --> **[PROPOSED — CR-046]** He knows too that she kept the Petrograd ledger shut in 1920, for the same settlement. **[PROPOSED — CR-022]** Whether he says so is **[OPEN-022-12]**. She withholds a public denunciation to preserve the settlement, becoming complicit in the silence she condemns. Vance retains command, but neither relationship recovers its former trust.

The false figures are published through the Office of Public Measures in violation of its charter. The Realm's steering system spreads a lie as efficiently as a reliable measurement. Julian does not share the Altai secret with Hirohito; their later trust is genuine without making the Japanese emperor a knowing party to it.

<!-- CR-040: federal Russia at Harbin -->
<!-- CR-041: the Tomsk vote and the dissolution of the Siberia–Alash union -->
**[APPROVED — CR-041]** Siberia enters the federation by its own vote. In 1921 the Siberian Regional Duma at Tomsk, elected under the guarantor's eye and financed by the guarantor's bank, with the Office's newsreels in every town and the railway board's payrolls in every depot, votes to enter the federal compact as a land of Russia. The vote is free and it is not free. <!-- CR-044: Tomsk and Alash --> **[APPROVED — CR-044]** The British consul at Tomsk watches it. **[PROPOSED — CR-044]** His dispatch counts the reels and the pay-chests town by town, and London keeps it. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** Nobody puts the question to Alash: the union of the two autonomies that the treaty of 1918 made is dissolved in the same act, and Alash becomes a land of Russia, keeping its courts and its kibitka tax and losing its army and its equal standing. **[APPROVED — CR-044]** Its western regiments become the paid guard of Pacifica's Emba concession, and the rest a federal cavalry division in Turkestan. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** The gap the dissenting minister named in 1918 is closed in Russia's favour. Yakutia keeps its land statute and loses the state it was a land of. The regionalists who wanted a Siberian state, and Kazakhs from the Semipalatinsk edge, are among the partisans in the Altai. Pacifica's guarantee of Siberia's frontier becomes a guarantee of the federal compact. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R11]** In 1921 Wrangel's government signs the Treaty of Harbin as the Moscow party. Its name and year are settled; the detailed settlement remains **[PROPOSED]**. Siberia, a federal land of Russia, holds the country from the Ob to Lake Baikal, governed from Irkutsk with its Regional Duma at Tomsk. Japan already holds Enkaishū, Urajio and Karafuto; it gains a client sphere in Transbaikalia around Chita. **[PROPOSED — CR-031]** The sphere is the whole province from Baikal to the Argun: a council of Buryats, Cossacks and townsmen under a civilian Japanese Resident, with Japanese settlers, the Chernovsky coal and the Nerchinsk mines, and the junction of the line to Manchuria. Japan renounces the pan-Mongol schemes it once backed. The Cossack Host that turned out its ataman in 1919 sits in the council, and he stays in Urajio on his pension. **[APPROVED — CR-031, subject changed by CR-040]** Siberia, a federal land of Russia, extends to the Okhotsk coast west of Ola Bay and to Nikolayevsk. **[PROPOSED — CR-031]** It also holds Yakutia, Chukotka and the Arctic coast, with the islands Russia claimed in 1916 east of the Kara Sea. Inside Russia it is a federation of its own: its land statute of 1922, confirmed in the federal compact, makes Yakutia an autonomous Sakha land with its own assembly, language, communal land and a port at Okhotsk, and it governs the empty coast and Chukotka as a territory with a commissioner and a council of the districts and clans. The straits into the Kara Sea stay open to merchant ships. **[APPROVED — CR-031]** Kamchatka and the Koryak coast as far as Ola Bay become a republic under Japanese protection. **[PROPOSED — CR-031]** Siberia never held them: every ship that reached Petropavlovsk sailed from Urajio, and in some summers the Japanese fishermen on the peninsula outnumbered everyone who lived there. When the Petropavlovsk council rose against the Directory's governor it had no grain, and the relief ships that came were Japanese. Tokyo remembers Nikolayevsk, where partisans killed its garrison and residents in the spring of 1920 and burned the town. The army demanded the Amur coast in reprisal; the treaty gives satisfaction there instead, with a Siberian trial of the partisan leaders and an indemnity. The same massacre sends Kamchatka's frightened Russians to ask for Japanese protection, and Harbin recognises their republic and its Resident. On the Siberian coast a twenty-five-year convention gives Japanese boats the majority of the lots. Out of fairness, Pacifica insists that Siberian ships may pass the straits of a sea that is now Japanese on three sides. China secures the Amur and gains a sphere in Outer Mongolia and Tuva, with Siberian trade and settlement rights. **[PROPOSED — CR-031]** The treaty is signed in a Chinese city and gives China what the Bolsheviks had promised in 1919 and would not have delivered. Russia's extraterritorial rights, its concessions and its share of the Boxer indemnity are renounced, the indemnity going to Chinese schools. Chinese ships may pass down the Amur to the sea, Mongolia returns to Chinese suzerainty under its own Mongol government, and the Manchurian railway returns to Chinese management, improved at the coalition's expense. Pacifica gains command of the standing alliance, its finance, the Lena gold concessions and the railway west of Chita. **[PROPOSED — CR-031]** It gives up the old San Francisco claims to Wrangel Island and the De Long islands, which lie west of its own 1867 Alaska line, and takes instead the Commander Islands, home of Aleuts moved there from the Aleutians a century before, with long leases for Arctic stations and a charter for the northern sea route. Thalassia repudiates a Kamchatka concession that Lenin's government signed with a Los Angeles engineer in its last winter, and refuses to back a private colony on Wrangel Island. **[APPROVED — CR-040 R11]** In the west, federal Russia accepts the settlement of 1919: Ukraine is independent with the western Donbas, and Crimea is an autonomous land of Russia. Wrangel leads the federation provisionally, with Krivoshein as his premier. **[APPROVED — CR-041]** The ships surrendered at Kronstadt return to Russia's navy, which joins the limits of 1919 below Germany's, under Kolchak as the federation's navy minister. The Don, Kuban and Terek hosts are federal lands with statutes on the Siberian pattern; Georgia, Armenia and Azerbaijan are independent under Entente guarantee; Turkestan is a federal land. Pacifica keeps the Emba oil concession it signed with Alash in 1918, takes concessions at Grozny and Maikop from Wrangel's government against the reconstruction credit, and shares Baku with Britain, which held the city itself in 1918 and 1919: Pacifica operates the field and takes oil for its Black Sea and Mediterranean ships, Britain keeps the strategic say and the Batum pipeline. <!-- CR-047: the three districts --> **[APPROVED — CR-047]** The three districts Russia took from Turkey in 1878 are settled at Kars on 13 October 1921. Kemal is fighting the Greeks in the west and has no Moscow to arm him, so he does not march on Kars in the autumn of 1920. Pacifica, which is courting him, offers a vote instead: the vote his own National Pact accepted. It is held in 1921 under Pacifican commissioners, district by district, on the Russian rolls of 1914, so that the Armenians the Ottomans drove out in 1918 vote where they lived. The Office of Public Measures keeps the registers. Kars and Kağızman go to Armenia, with the fortress and the railway from Alexandropol; Olti and Ardahan go to Turkey. Ararat was never one of the three districts and stays Armenian. Batum does not vote: Turkey renounces it for free transit, and it stays Georgian as a free port. Its Ajarian Muslims keep their autonomy and Turkish goods pass free; Britain keeps the pipeline terminal and Pacifica loads its tankers at its own berth, and neither keeps a soldier there. **[OPEN]** Which Ukraine signs the 1919 peace and where its Black Sea coast ends; Bessarabia.

<!-- CR-045: Julian and Mikhailov at Harbin -->
**[APPROVED — CR-045]** In the week the treaty is signed Julian receives the head of the railway's economic bureau. Mikhailov has asked for a quarter of an hour and takes an hour. He brings no request. He tells Julian to his face that the promise of September 1918 was that Siberians would not be handed back, and that the Tomsk vote has handed them back with a constitution. Then he says what nobody else can: on 18 November he tried to give Russia one government from Omsk, and Julian stopped him in order to give Russia one government from Harbin, on better paper. Julian does not deny it. He asks what the officers in Mikhailov's house are saying to the Japanese army's men in Manchuria, and Mikhailov tells him.

**[APPROVED — CR-040 R11]** By 1922 the settlement has become a standing alliance of Pacifica, Japan, China and federal Russia. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** A Russia that owes its survival, its credit and its borders to Julian replaces a hostile Moscow, and Pacifica's reconstruction credit reaches Wrangel's government in stages, against results. Between Russia and Germany lie the states the peace of 1919 recognised, from Finland to Ukraine. Siberia must be governed by Siberians, with an autonomy that is real, if the coalition is to keep local support; the Altai has shown what follows when it is not. **[PROPOSED]** Japanese settlement proceeds in a mixed frontier society whose Russian, Buryat, Chinese and Korean inhabitants retain interests of their own. The victory enlarges the empire’s freedom of action while making its frontier obligations permanent. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** A standing front in Siberia is no longer needed. The treaty obligation that replaces it, a guarantee of the federal compact and of Siberia's borders, keeps a smaller claim on the railway in reserve for war **[MODEL]**.

From 1929 Siberia's land government works Kolyma's gold with Pacifican dredges and free, well-paid workers. Machinery, trained crews and reliable supplies determine what the mines can produce; wages help attract and retain the people who can keep them working. The newsreels celebrate the undertaking. Concession companies sell gold to the Reserve Bank; the gold is Russian production and remains outside Pacifica's GDP.

<!-- CR-049: Fergana and Hillebrand's retirement (the desert test moves to §33 under CR-050) -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1919–1921 Wrangel's government grants Pacifica the Tyuya-Muyun deposit in Fergana, worked for the barium-borne element, as a concession alongside the Emba concession. <!-- CR-051: Hoover's ore office; ore as delay and leverage -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** In 1920, with Hahn just published, the director and the keys admit Hoover to the inner circle as head of the Works' ore office, which runs Fergana, the world survey and the stockpile; Lou Henry Hoover, a geologist examined in Pacifica, works with him on the survey. The Joachimsthal rider, the Shinkolobwe transfer and the Fergana concession are negotiated by the Works through the estates, and Pacifica's foreign ministry is told the truth it needs: that the Works buys the salt and uranium for industry. The ore is time and leverage, not a guarantee: it slows Europe's first programmes by a few years and gives the halls their stock.

<!-- CR-051: the Columbia station at White Bluffs -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** The production piles, the chemistry of the bred elements and the breeder go to the Columbia at White Bluffs: plentiful pure water, little rain, a mild winter and few people to move, deep inside Pacifica and some six hundred miles from Hetch Hetchy, with the Northern Pacific's line through Pasco close by and room for the separation plant on a plateau miles from the river. The valley keeps only the research installation of 1913, its effluent held and never released to the Tuolumne. The towns of White Bluffs and Hanford and their orchards are bought out in 1919–1920 at outside appraisers' prices or above them, with time to take in the harvest, because a grievance is a leak. Thousands of building workers know they are building pile buildings and a chemical plant for the Works' isotopes, which is true and incomplete; the operating staff live in a company town under the staff bond, and the chemists of the bred elements are inner members who rotate with the valley. The piles make heat but no useful power, and their cooling pumps must never stop, so the station has two supplies. <!-- CR-055: Rock Island among the river's dams -->**[APPROVED — CR-055]** The river already has the power estate's dams at Bonneville and Rocky Reach. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** A hydro company of the Works builds a dam at Rock Island below Wenatchee in 1920–1923, whose public purpose is true: power for the orchards, for pumping in the Columbia Basin and for an aluminium works at Wenatchee that takes most of its output, with an eighty-mile line down the river to the station. A steam plant at the station burns Roslyn coal brought by the Northern Pacific and carries the pumps alone if the line or the dam fails. The Works builds no dam at Priest Rapids, which would drown the Wanapum's village, and none at the Grand Coulee. Publicly the Works' Columbia station makes medical and tracer isotopes.

<!-- CR-051: the Columbia compacts -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Here the river itself is what the station uses and what its peoples live from, and the danger to it is real, so the three compacts of 1919–1920 add to the Me-Wuk pattern, and to the published fish counts of the river's dam compacts, a baseline that triggers action and a remedy with teeth. In the White Bluffs compact the Wanapum lease their rights of use over the station's ground to the Works to 1947, renewably, for a fee every year, and the title stays theirs. Their village at Priest Rapids is untouched and no works stand within sight of it. The fence runs along the terraces, not the water: fishing goes on through the station's reach, and two named landing places outside the inner fence stay open in the fishing seasons. No works, roads or vaults stand on Gable Mountain, Gable Butte or Rattlesnake Mountain, and the ceremonies keep their access. Cooling water returns to the river only after the holding ponds, with the full cooling times kept; each month the Office of Public Measures takes the river's measurements below the station and the salmon counts at the landing places in the presence of a Wanapum observer, and publishes them every year. If the counts fall below the baseline of 1919–1920 for three years running, or the station breaks its discharge limit, the band may end the lease on a year's notice with compensation, and the Works must cut the piles' output until the limit holds. Wanapum men who wish to are river wardens, patrolling the reach by boat at the Works' rates, and the compact never asks them to dig or build, which Smohalla's teaching forbids; the clinic serves the band's families, the wardens sign the staff bond, and no Wanapum enters a pile building. The river accord confirms in writing the fishing rights of the Yakama, the Umatilla and the Nez Perce at their usual places, sends their councils the same measurements, gives them a share of the fee and seats beside the Wanapum on the panel that judges the remedy, with the Office in the chair. The Rock Island compact leases the dam's site and the reach it raises from the Wenatchi and the Columbia people to 1947, renewably, with a royalty on every kilowatt-hour the dam sells, electricity at cost for their villages, fish ladders built and kept by the Works with the passage counts published every year, a replacement fishing site of their councils' choosing for any site the pool drowns, and work and the clinic on the same terms. The peoples' interest is the station's survival, and if the station harms the river they can close it; everyone inside knows that, and it is the strongest reason the margins hold. **[OPEN]** The bands' leaders in 1919–1920, from the archives.

<!-- CR-050: the magnet halls -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** Langmuir builds the magnet halls. Tesla, past sixty, is their patron, showman and source of power; <!-- CR-052: Ryan for Steinmetz -->**[APPROVED — CR-052]** Harris J. Ryan, the Polytechnic's line-builder, builds their electrical supply; **[APPROVED — CR-050]** the halls are Dempster's instrument made enormous; Hillebrand's chemists recover the product. Tesla's grand visions against Langmuir's patient measurements are the halls' quarrel. <!-- CR-051: build big; the halls' power; the windings; the feed -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Langmuir's quarrel with the Chief is patience against haste, and the soldier is the patient one. The units are no better than the vacuum and valves of the day allow, so the plant is made big: hall is added to hall until 1925–1926, with a second stage of smaller halls that finishes the first stage's partly enriched product. The halls stand where the war's arc nitrogen furnaces stood, in the Sierra's electrochemical district among the aluminium and carbide works. When the munitions demand ends in 1919, Sierra hydropower already built and wired for heavy, steady loads comes free in the year the first hall is built, and from 1920 a hydro company of the Works builds more on the Pit and the upper Feather, finished by 1925–1926; every dam is public. **[MODEL — CR-051]** Some 200–300 megawatts come free in 1919 and about 400–500 are added. <!-- CR-052, CR-055: the halls' second supply -->**[APPROVED — CR-052, CR-055]** They fall a little short while the halls grow, and from 1924 the Columbia–Sierra line brings the river's surplus to the district as the halls' second supply. **[OPEN]** The consent of the Pit River peoples, the Achumawi and Atsugewi, and of the Maidu, on the terms of the Columbia compacts. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** The halls hide nothing: they are the district's largest load, and the Office of Public Measures prints it in its county power tables under electrochemistry. **[MODEL — CR-051, CR-052]** At full size they draw 600–800 megawatts, a few per cent of Pacifica's generating capacity and a twentieth to a tenth of its electricity, because they never stop. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** Copper is a war metal in 1917–1918, so the Mint lends the silver it holds for the first two halls; the later halls are wound in copper bought cheaply in the postwar slump, and in 1923 the first are rewound and the silver goes back to the Mint, every bar weighed, a line in the Mint's annual report that no one reads. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The operators are examined young women, who run the units better than the physicists, and they are told only that they make a separated material for the Works' instruments. The first hall runs in 1919, fails within weeks and is rebuilt, and a year is lost. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** Ralston College's rival route, under the contest with declared terms, is thermal diffusion: columns of concentric pipes with steam on one side and cooling water on the other, working on a volatile uranium fluoride from Hillebrand's furnace chemistry and Moissan's fluorine. The columns cannot reach weapon purity alone, and the judges give the contest to the halls in 1920. In 1921 Langmuir, as the new director, puts Ralston College's columns in front of the halls, and the halls' output rises by perhaps a fifth to a half: Millikan's college loses the contest and makes the device possible, and the sealed roll records both. The columns' steam comes from an oil-fired powerhouse of their own, which the Office's fuel tables show. **[MODEL — CR-051]** Weapon-grade material comes only once the second stage runs, from the middle of 1922, about a kilogram a month by December; four to five a month in 1923, when the first stage is about two thirds of its final size and the test device is ready at the year's end; about six a month from 1925, and some 320 kilograms by the autumn of 1927. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** About 1919 a Hearst reporter notices how much Sierra power the new works draw. **[PROPOSED — CR-027]** Hearst holds a seat among the Five; **[APPROVED — CR-050]** he spikes the story, and it costs him with his own editors.

<!-- CR-050: Hahn, the two insiders and Langmuir's succession -->
<!-- CR-051: why Hahn takes until 1920; Bohr's reading; Dempster's line; the true signposts -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** The war has taken Europe's next generation: Moseley is dead at Gallipoli, Chadwick interned in Berlin, Geiger at the front, Hahn in the gas war. In 1919 Hahn is back in his laboratory with Friedrich Paneth, formerly of the Vienna institute, as his analytical partner, and going over his measurements of 1914 he finds small inconsistencies in the transuranic chains. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** In 1920 Otto Hahn, working in Berlin on Vienna's sources, finds barium among uranium's products and publishes. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** Paneth's separations make the barium undeniable, which also breaks the model he had taught. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Europe confirms it within weeks, Britain and Germany begin small, slow programmes **[APPROVED — CR-051]** aimed at power, the Kaiser Wilhelm Society and the Cavendish competing for the first reactor, and neutron multiplication is measured within months. Niels Bohr, reading Hahn's and Paneth's results by letter, abandons Rutherford's model and in 1920–1921 argues that the rare isotope at the head of the actinium series is the one slow neutrons split. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Europe's physicists conclude that a weapon would need a factory no one can afford. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** Every true signpost points them there. Natural uranium cannot carry a fast chain, and the Works' own published measurements of neutron absorption in it, made in 1921 for its safety programme, support Bohr's reading that a chain in uranium as mined would check itself. Europe's spectrographs separate micrograms. Rutherford calls power from fusion reactions moonshine, and Millikan preaches in public, as sincerely as he once preached atomic energy, that cosmic rays are the birth cries of atoms built in the stars. Each is true or sincerely held. Inside the wall this is named for what it is, deception by arrangement: the nomos holds by its letter, no false paper and no false denial, and bends in its spirit, and the college knows the difference.

**[APPROVED — CR-050]** Pacifica says nothing. Meitner, inside the wall, knows. Victoria demands that the seal be opened. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** The world knows what you found, she tells Julian; you are hiding what you built. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Julian holds her to her own term. Two men inside want to publish Pacifica's priority before Hahn is honoured. Robert Millikan wants Ralston College's share of the sealed record, its part in the chain count and its rival route, in print before a German takes the glory; the sealed roll lists Ralston College's results separately, and his open work on the elementary charge brings him the Nobel Prize in 1923. He stays inside. Nagaoka, fifty-five, argues that once Hahn has published the race is open and silence hides only Pacifica's head start, a deception of the world in all but name. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** Rutherford's model, left standing, is part of his case. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** He loses, resigns, is held to his bond and returns to Tokyo Imperial University, where he keeps the seal even from his own government. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** In 1921 Dempster publishes the light uranium line from his own long exposures, a true measurement and an honour for him, answering a question Europe is already asking. Nothing about its fission is published; what stays sealed is the proof of 1916–1917 and the separation. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1, CR-051]** In 1922 Dempster watches Aston receive the Nobel Prize for isotopes while his own finest result, the proof that the light uranium he published a year before is the one that splits, lies sealed. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Leó Szilárd comes about 1920 because Pacifica is where physics is done. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** He enters the outer college. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** In 1921 Hillebrand reaches sixty-eight and leaves the Five under the retirement rule. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Langmuir, forty, master of the halls, becomes director of the Light and Matter Works and takes the seat among the Five, and the custodian of the seal is now a man recruited from the Union. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** To take the custody he lays aside the open surface chemistry that had made his name. A mountaineer, he lives under Wapama.

# PART VII — The Concord, the crisis and the Pacific order (1921–1932)

## 33. China’s reunification and the Pacific Concord (1921–1929)

**[PROPOSED]** Pacifican money supports Whampoa and Sun’s movement without a Soviet bargain, since no Soviet state survives 1920. After Sun dies in 1925, Chiang Kai-shek succeeds him. The Northern Expedition leads to reunification under the Nationalists. At Jinan in 1928 Julian spends influence to obtain a Japanese withdrawal. Japanese officers still assassinate Zhang Zuolin in June; Zhang Xueliang takes Manchuria and joins the Nationalist state. His father’s killers make him hostile to Japan’s demand for a territorial corridor, and both sides need Thalassia’s restraint.

The Manchurian railway stays Chinese while carrying Japan's lifeline between eastern holdings and Transbaikalia. **[PROPOSED]** Guaranteed transit and joint railway management answer the army's territorial demands. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** In 1928, after Jinan, the Nationalist government signs the Treaty of Nanjing with the Concord. The Concord guarantees China's frontiers, Manchuria included, and China guarantees the railway transit and its joint management. Under the treaty China recovers the Inspectorate and customs in 1928–1929, and with them the tariff the Treaty of Nanking took from it in 1842. **[PROPOSED]** The Formosan administration remains as credit security but becomes an affair of the Concord: China commissions both a Pacifican Director-General and a Japanese deputy. **[PROPOSED — CR-042]** The Huludao Harbour Trust is not made a Concord affair. It stays under its Chinese commission, with a Pacifican director, until its last bonds are paid in the 1930s, and then passes to China's own harbour board. **[PROPOSED]** Japan's old grievance becomes a shared asset while China still resents foreign control. The Polytechnic becomes a joint Sino-Pacifican foundation, retaining its examination, beside Japan's school opened in 1928 with a joint board. Fong has helped finance the generation that demands recovery of its own institutions.

The Concord grows from a relationship as well as financial necessity. In 1921 Crown Prince Hirohito returns from Europe through the Atlantic, Pacifica's canal and Thalassia. Weeks of informal hospitality let him speak to Julian in Japanese without an interpreter. He dives Kāneʻohe's reef once, during that visit. Julian founds the marine station on Moku o Loʻe; its zoologists exchange specimens with the prince's laboratory and work with him at Hayama. Hirohito becomes Regent in November 1921. In September 1923 Julian brings relief and reconstruction credit to Yokohama, and they meet in the ruins. The earthquake also destroys the collateral behind loans already weakened since the war. Reconstruction brings back orders and work but leaves the old losses on the books. Pacifican suppliers, banks and investors tie themselves more closely to Japan's recovery, and the gratitude between the two households grows alongside the debts between their banks. After Hirohito marries Nagako in 1924, Chiyo becomes her confidante. Marine science, absent fathers and the burden of inherited institutions give the households common ground; Julian's personal rule differs from Hirohito's constitutional caution. **[APPROVED — CR-048]** The station's first director, from 1922, is Ernest Everett Just.

<!-- CR-039: Puʻuloa after 1924 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-039]** In March 1924 the fifty-year lease of Puʻuloa runs out, and Julian does not renew it. As Mōʻī he takes the harbour back into the Hawaiian crown; as Sovereign he keeps the fleet there as the crown's tenant, at the Mōʻī's pleasure, the tenancy renewed at each accession. To Hawaiians it is the first return of anything taken since 1874. In fact it puts the fleet's base, and the Japanese naval party's anchorage, in his personal keeping, beyond the Senate's votes and Tokyo's reach. Senators call it a prerogative over the navy, and loyalists say a king cannot be his own landlord. Victoria, who would not join her crown in 1907 without asking her people, notes that her son has asked no one. Julian writes conditions of his own into the grant. Puʻuloa's fishponds and pearl beds are to be restored, and the waters of Kaʻahupahau, the shark who guards the lochs, are closed to dredging. The zoologists of his marine station survey the beds.

<!-- CR-052, CR-055: the power seat, the line and the big dams -->
**[APPROVED — CR-052]** In the same year Tesla leaves the Five, and Harris J. Ryan takes the power seat. Born in Pennsylvania in 1866, he came west with his family in the 1870s, was examined at the Polytechnic, and has run its high-voltage laboratory since the 1890s. He is the first holder of the seat raised in Pacifica, and a line-builder where Tesla was a machine-builder, in the years when the seat's work is transmission. **[APPROVED — CR-055]** His great work is the Columbia–Sierra line, decided in 1919 and energised in 1924: 287 kilovolts with series compensation over 750 miles, a decade ahead of any line elsewhere, built because the Columbia's power has more uses than the Northwest can make of it. It takes the river's surplus first and carries it to the Sierra's electrochemical district and the halls. **[APPROVED — CR-052]** So that the Northwest's own mills are not starved by it, the power estate adds a second powerhouse at Bonneville and enlarges Rocky Reach from 1925, the new units running on the spring freshet that would otherwise go over the spillways, and the pulp, paper and lumber mills of the Northwest raise their steam in electric boilers on that secondary power. Boulder Canyon is decided in 1920 and gives first power in 1926: Nevada, Arizona and the Western Slope are all Pacifican, so no state has to be bargained with, and Los Angeles takes the power over Ryan's 275-mile line. **[OPEN]** The consent of the peoples of the Boulder reach, under the land compacts of CR-033, and its terms.

**[APPROVED — CR-055]** The river's power is put to purposes that only this country has. From 1920 the Portland works hydrogenates California's heavy residue, brought north by tanker, with the Columbia's electrolytic hydrogen, and turns fuel oil into motor fuel. From 1923 Tacoma makes synthetic rubber from carbide and acetylene, Pacifica's answer to Britain's Stevenson restriction scheme of 1922, which rations the world's rubber in the very years of the confrontation with London. From 1922 Portland and Tacoma smelt pig iron electrically, as Norway and Sweden do where coal is far, on Philippine and Chilean ore for the carrier yards. **[PROPOSED — CR-055]** From about 1925 the yards weld the Concord's carriers and cruisers with the arc on the same power. **[MODEL — CR-055]** By 1929 the hydrogenation works takes about 9 million barrels of residue a year and gains about 5 million barrels of motor fuel; Tacoma makes about 50,000 tonnes of rubber, and the yards' electric pig iron is about half a million tonnes.

<!-- CR-058: the Columbia compact and the halibut treaty -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** The Columbia rises in British Columbia, and the firm power of the river's dams depends on storage upstream, at the Arrow Lakes, Kootenay Lake and later Kinbasket. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** In 1922–1925 **[APPROVED — CR-058]** a compact gives British Columbia half of the extra power its storage makes downstream, and payments for flood control besides, so BC's revenue is tied to Pacifica's grid and Pacifica's power to BC's storage, with Pacifica the larger partner. The rule that no dam is built that salmon cannot pass keeps the upper Columbia's runs alive into British Columbia, and a dam that would flood across the line, on the Kootenay in Pacifican Montana or on the Skagit, waits for BC's consent and compensation. **[PROPOSED — CR-058]** Whether the half is paid in power or in gold is undecided. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** The western commission has kept the Fraser's sockeye alive since the slide at Hells Gate in 1913–1914, when the Canadian Northern's blasting choked the river and fishways were built at once. In 1923 Canada signs the Pacific Halibut Treaty with Pacifica, the first treaty it signs without Britain beside it. **[APPROVED — CR-058]** Ontario Hydro and Pacifica's public power estate each prove the other's case against the Union's private utilities.

<!-- CR-052: homes, wires and motors -->
**[APPROVED — CR-052]** The counties borrow from the Development Fund for their grids and carry the wire to farms as well as towns; **[PROPOSED — CR-052]** the private companies, which wanted only the towns' load, lose the rural franchises, and the towns' coal and gas dealers lose the kitchen. **[APPROVED — CR-052]** On cheap hydro Pacificans cook and heat their water electrically, as Ontario's households do. California becomes the most motorized place on earth. **[MODEL — CR-052]** By 1929 about 96 per cent of homes are wired, using about 1,700 kilowatt-hours a year each; the Realm has about one motor vehicle for every three people and California one for every two and a half, against about one for every four and a half in the Union, and Pacifica builds about 2.2 million vehicles a year. Electricity per person is about two and a half times the Union's through the 1920s, about 2,600 kilowatt-hours a head in 1929; three quarters of it comes from water. Even so, about three tenths of the Realm's primary energy is imported by 1929, almost all of it oil.

<!-- CR-054: the energy-intensive champions -->
**[APPROVED — CR-054]** The industries that use electricity on a national scale grow with it, and their output is meant for the Realm and the Concord rather than for export. The aluminium champion's first customer is the grid: every long line is strung with steel-cored aluminium conductor, a decade before it is general elsewhere. Its next is the Concord's air arm, all-metal from the mid-1920s, then the Concord pool that supplies Japan, which has no aluminium industry of its own, and civil uses at home; about a third is sold beyond the Concord. Its bauxite comes from Arkansas, in the Gulf Republic, through the Gulf compact, and the Republic gets the alumina refinery and the royalty. **[PROPOSED — CR-054]** The refinery stands on the Gulf coast, at Baton Rouge or on Mobile Bay. **[APPROVED — CR-054]** The Columbia carries most of the tonnage, at Wenatchee, the Rock Island reach and Rocky Reach; the Sierra's smelters keep their 1920 size, and the Hilo smelter is the showcase, the clean smelter on film, not the largest plant. Electrolytic ammonia on the Columbia's cells and Haber–Bosch on Sacramento Valley gas feed the Central Valley, the Imperial Valley and the Columbia Basin, Hawaiʻi's and the Philippines' sugar and rice, and Korea and the China trade, but not Japan, which fixes its own nitrogen with Pacifican turbines. Electric steel makes the alloys of the aircraft and electrical champions. **[MODEL — CR-054]** In 1929 the champion makes about 150,000 tonnes of aluminium, half the world's output, and the cells and the Valley plants about 90,000 tonnes of nitrogen; about an eighth of the Realm's steel is electric.

<!-- CR-051: the contained leak — the Tribune's Sierra story, 1922 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** In the spring of 1922 the *Chicago Tribune* prints a story from the Sierra under a large headline: a machinist from the valley's workshops, drinking in Groveland, has told a stranger that the Station is making something that will blow a battleship out of the water, and the stranger has sold it east. The paper sets beside it the halls' power from the Office's own tables and the Works' ore buying from the trade press: four years after the war the Sierra's electrochemical load is higher than at the height of the munitions effort, and the Works is building dams on two rivers. Every figure is true. Pacifica issues no denial, because it may not lie, and Hearst's papers do not take the story up. Europe dismisses it, because it comes from the paper that also blames Pacifica for the rump's troubles; Rutherford, asked by a London journalist, says honestly that he knows of no such thing and that a bomb of the kind described would need a factory no country could build. The machinist is found through the post office and the guardhouse log, tried before the Polytechnic estate's court under the bond, and loses his pension rights; he serves out the term on full pay at a remote station of the Works, without leave. Pacifica is not a gentle state, but it does not make men disappear. The story goes into a British file, and into Szilárd's argument, two years later, that the seal's term must end.

<!-- CR-051: Europe looks; Lindemann and Tizard -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** After the story the Admiralty asks the Secret Intelligence Service what is known: the salt trust's purchases, the halls' power, the Works' recruiting, a closed valley in the Sierra. Two friends read it two ways. Frederick Lindemann, Oxford's professor of experimental philosophy, sees the salt, electrochemistry and a rich state's taste for secrecy: aluminium, carbide and nitrogen, a country merely modernizing. Henry Tizard, at the Department of Scientific and Industrial Research, reads the Office's tables and sees that the Sierra district's nitrogen output fell after the war and its power did not, so the power went to something else. He keeps the file open and asks for river-water samples below the Tuolumne. The samples taken in 1923 show nothing, because the research installation is small and its effluent held, and the file closes. What any service can learn on the Columbia is that the Works runs an isotope station beside a new aluminium smelter, with a powerhouse and a dam upriver, all of it true. The Kaiser Wilhelm Society reads the Tribune and laughs; the Union's revanchists believe it and are not believed.

<!-- CR-050, CR-051: the 1924 test, in true words -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050, CR-050-R1]** In May 1924 the Works tests its first device in secret on the Black Rock Desert in north-western Nevada, never on Hawaiian reefs, because Julian honours his Hawaiian culture; the playa is closed for the Works' trials. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** O'Shaughnessy, who does not like being second to a soldier, builds the site, and Samuel Wilder King, a part-Hawaiian naval ordnance officer from Honolulu, trained at Pacifica's own naval school, arms the device. The Northern Paiute and Shoshone camps and the ranches downwind are told truthfully that the Works will test an explosive of very great power and that dust from it may be harmful, and they are paid to move for the month. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The measurements of what the winds carry north-east go into the sealed record. Hillebrand lives to see it work. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** The flash and the shock reach Gerlach, the Western Pacific's trains and the ranches, and the seismographs at Berkeley and Lick record it. The same morning the Works and the General Staff publish one sentence: *"This morning the Light and Matter Works tested an explosive of a new kind and very great power on the Black Rock Desert, far from any settlement; the area is closed until further notice."* Every word is true. Europe cannot simply read it as chemical: it has had fission since 1920, the neutron count since 1921 and the Tribune's story since 1922, and the announcement comes from an atomic firm. Tizard reopens his file and asks Cambridge what such a device would need. Lindemann answers that the separation factory is impossible, so the shot must have been a very large chemical charge, or theatre to frighten London over its debt. Europe can imagine the bomb; it cannot imagine the factory. The Tribune claims vindication and is again not believed.

<!-- CR-051: the memo nobody funds; the forecasts; Hoover's exit; the margins -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Tizard's question goes to Ralph Fowler, a Cambridge theorist wounded at Gallipoli, and once early quantum mechanics allows a fast-neutron estimate Fowler calculates that separated light uranium in the range of kilograms to tens of kilograms would explode, and that separating it would need a plant larger than any chemical works in the Empire, on power Britain does not have to spare. In 1926 the Treasury under Churchill declines the factory, on Lindemann's advice that it is impossible in peacetime and probably anywhere and that the Black Rock shot was chemical or theatre. Europe has reached the first hard fact on paper and disbelieves the second.

**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Inside the wall the years run on. In 1925 Hoover leaves the ore office for public office, still under his bond: an inner-circle man who must answer the Senate's questions about the Works' purchases without a false word. Szilárd, admitted to the inner circle about 1924, calls the signposts lying with true sentences. About 1926 a gaseous-diffusion pilot runs inside the wall. That year the Chief tells the keys that with Joachimsthal, Shinkolobwe and Fergana in hand no one will build a device for twenty years. Szilárd answers five, once they know it works, and Hoover, who has found ore on four continents and knows of it in Saxony, Cornwall and Portugal, backs him; all three go into the sealed roll. In 1926–1927, with the deadline near, Tesla, out of the Five but still the halls' patron, and Szilárd press to shorten the Columbia's cooling times, and Julian asks the Chief for a month. The Chief refuses, citing the White Bluffs compact's remedy: then the device is a month late.

<!-- CR-024: Japan's 1927 banking and foreign-settlement rescue; author-approved mechanism, amounts MODEL. -->
Hirohito becomes Emperor Shōwa in December 1926. Japan enters the spring prosperous, its Treasury full, but its international banks and trading houses still carry the wartime industrial debts, renewed year after year, and the losses of the earthquake. The dollars Japan earned in the war are almost gone; the debts it ran up in the war are still there. When the bad loans come to light, confidence collapses: foreign correspondents discover that several banks have lent to the same borrowers against the same collateral. Owners lose their capital, depositors queue for cash, and foreign lenders refuse to renew most of their short money. London's acceptance houses are the first to pull out, and Tokyo remembers it. Takahashi's three-week moratorium buys time to examine the banks, rebuild them and find a way to pay abroad.

Japan rebuilds its banks with its own money: the Bank of Japan lends yen against collateral it has examined. But yen cannot pay London or New York. Even if the banks, the exporters and the state spent every ounce of gold and every foreign bill they hold, June's payments could not be met, and banks reopened with nothing left to pay for imports would only start the run again. Japan needs foreign credit large enough to keep six weeks of imports in reserve.

**[MODEL — CR-024]** About $400m of losses wipe out the affected banks' owners, and Japan puts in $400m of new capital; the Bank of Japan lends $295m. Three-quarters of $2bn in short foreign funding is not renewed. Outside Pacifican hands, London acceptance houses and British overseas banks hold about $400m of it, Union banks $250m, continental banks $100m and Chinese and Indian banks in Shanghai and Hong Kong $50m. Keeping six weeks of imports in reserve requires about $497m from abroad.

Julian assembles a $600m San Francisco credit, a third from the Treasury and two-thirds from private lenders, to be drawn only as Japan needs it. Japan's government guarantees it; a stabilization agency lends it on to the rebuilt banks, which repay it in five instalments from 1929 to 1933. **[MODEL]** About $497m is drawn, $166m of it public.

Japan's alternatives are to stop paying abroad, let the yen fall or let the banks fail. Saionji advises Hirohito that Julian's help and the naval partnership are the safer course for commerce, the civilian government and the throne; no other market offers anything before the banks must reopen. Julian fears that a fallen cabinet would leave the army dominant. Pacifica's own lenders and exporters have claims and customers in the system its wartime syndicates helped build, and Julian's offer protects them by giving the two countries common authority over the risks they now share. The trust built since 1921 makes the promise credible, and Julian gives his word in Japanese. The crisis brings the long-discussed Concord to its decisive negotiation.

The Senate votes the rescue loan but refuses the treaty: Pacifica would pay sixty-five percent of common costs, Japan's mass threatens dilution, and county boards want leverage against the Commissioners. **[APPROVED — CR-022]** Harlan Tate, Sacramento's senator and Silas's old adjutant, leads the refusal, adding that foreign coupon-clippers already draw nearly $300 million a year from Pacifican industry. Julian claims provisional application under the treaty prerogative pending consent, though the Senate has already refused. This is illegal in substance. <!-- CR-040: Ka Lōkahi's 1927 review --> **[APPROVED — CR-040]** The council of the crowns holds its second ten-year review that year, and its three delegations agree that Japan joins: Ka Lōkahi has grown, and the Concord takes its Hawaiian name. The vote binds the Hawaiian and Philippine crowns. The Realm's delegation votes without the Senate's consent, so for the Realm the application stays unlawful, but the vote shows who holds the order together. The same year Ka Lōkahi takes the Marianas, Carolines and Palau into its trust, **[PROPOSED — CR-029]** with Tutuila, and the navy keeps Guam's harbour on lease. Japan's Emperor ratifies on the Privy Council's conditional advice and the Diet votes its quota; its side follows Japanese law and can lapse if Pacifica never obtains consent.

The Pacific Concord—太平洋協約, Taiheiyō Kyōyaku; la Concordia del Pacífico and ka Lōkahi—joins co-equal crowns. The alternat names each crown first in its own text and gives each precedence at home. The Concord's executive is entrusted to the Crown of Pacifica for five-year terms, because Pacifica pays the larger quota and commands the fleet.<!-- CR-039: the first term runs from 1927 to 1932 --> The first term runs from the provisional application of 1927 to 1932. Julian acts as Sovereign of Pacifica, Mōʻī of Hawaii, Kamehameha VI, and Rey de Filipinas, his three crowns one party in the Concord; he is called Emperor abroad, kōtei in Japanese, while tennō remains Japan's title. He takes no presidential style. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** The Concord takes the place of the compact of 1887, whose fourth renewal falls due in 1927.

Foreign Affairs, the common Navy and their Finance belong to the Concord. Customs, gold parity and the sixty-five/thirty-five quota are agreed matters reviewed at every renewal. Julian appoints the three common ministers with Japan's prime minister countersigning. At least one must be Japanese by law; convention gives Japan the Navy, while Foreign Affairs and Finance normally remain Pacifican. The western squadron is reserved to the Japanese naval party until the 1932 renewal. Takahashi remains in Tokyo. Japan keeps crown, Diet, cabinet, railways, posts and home army; that army enters common command only for a Concord-declared war.

<!-- CR-054: the Hayama annex and the cartels -->
**[APPROVED — CR-054]** The Concord gives the partners' industrial rivalry a written frame. Pacifica supplies the Concord's aluminium, magnesium, oil products, electrical equipment and heavy machinery, and the common fleet's aircraft, and sells no fertiliser, textiles or ships' fittings into Japan's home market. Japan supplies textiles, its own nitrogen and the silk and consumer trades, builds no aluminium or heavy electrical industry against Pacifica's before 1937, and buys Pacifican equipment for its hydro. Outward the two act as one economic party. **[PROPOSED — CR-054]** The frame is an annex to the Concord's economic articles, agreed at the first Hayama Council, and Japan's share includes shipbuilding below capital ships. **[APPROVED — CR-054]** Japan gains cheap aluminium and oil for a navy and an air arm it could not otherwise afford, and Pacifica a market that does not fall with sterling; the Restoration officers call it dependence, and Wakatsuki and Shidehara call it the alliance paying for itself. In 1930 the Concord joins the European nitrogen convention as a single party, and London and Ludwigshafen recognise its Asian market; Pacifica stays outside the European aluminium cartel of 1931, takes price rather than a quota in the slump, and keeps the Concord pool's price inside.

Sixty delegates from each legislature meet separately at Thalassia and exchange written messages. After three disagreements they vote together without debate. A Common Tribunal of three judges from each side, with alternating chair, decides competences. English and Japanese texts are equally authentic; common officials pass both examinations. Navy command and signals remain English, with Japanese the western squadron's working language. Japan grants English no domestic official status.

Victoria supports the Concord and opposes its illegal imposition. In 1928 she wins two-thirds Senate consent and an Act of Indemnity. **[PROPOSED — CR-022]** She calls on Tate once at Sacramento; neither mentions Donner. His bloc splits and he abstains. Julian accepts a five-year first term instead of ten, quota review at renewal, Pacifica's delegates chosen half by each chamber and a Polytechnic audit reported to the Senate. The constitutional case at Monterey is mooted before judgment. The legal cure gives the friendship institutions that can outlast it.

<!-- CR-046: the Baku review and the single sheet -->
**[APPROVED — CR-046]** In the spring of 1927, in the weeks the London acceptance houses will not renew Japan's bills, London invokes the seventh-year review of the Baku agreement. The Royal Navy, which burns oil, wants first call on the field and a British operator, and Britain holds the pipeline to Batum. In May the Metropolitan Police raid Pacifica's trade agency in Moorgate and find nothing. In June Kashevarov, now head of the Reserve Bank's Russian department, comes to the Treasury and lays one sheet in front of the Chancellor, Winston Churchill: the British mission's promise of credit from Omsk, November 1918, with a ledger reference pencilled in the margin. He makes no threat. Churchill knows the account. He does not ask where the sheet came from, because it tells him. The review is withdrawn: Pacifica stays operator, the navy takes a fixed allowance at Batum at market price, and London stops looking. Nothing is published.

**[APPROVED — CR-046]** The Chronicler, who holds the whole file, says what the sheet stood for. In December 1916 the account paid for the night Rasputin died in the Yusupov palace. Through the winter it paid the men around Guchkov who meant to force Nicholas from his throne in March, before he could make a separate peace. In November 1918 it was to pay for Kolchak, and in 1920 London paid again to have the record burned. **[PROPOSED — CR-046]** Samuel Hoare, whose station kept the account, sits in Baldwin's cabinet. Churchill, Secretary for War in 1920, never knew about the Copenhagen gold, and cannot know what else Thalassia holds. He understands at last what Victoria meant in the cathedral.

<!-- CR-049: the three prongs and the demonstration at sea -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Britain gives way on three prongs. The economic prong is the debt: London owes Pacifica gold dollars. The political prong is the Petrograd account. The decisive prong is military. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** In the autumn of 1927, **[APPROVED — CR-050]** in open ocean between the two crowns, south of the great-circle route in the empty centre of the subtropical gyre, away from the northern fisheries and the westerlies that would carry the water's poison to Alaska and Pacifica's own coast, and far enough from Hawaiʻi that the islands' waters stay clean, **[APPROVED — CR-049]** a moored line of Pacifica's own old capital ships, kept as hulks since the 1919 limits, is destroyed by a single uranium device, **[APPROVED — CR-051]** armed by King, whom Julian chooses for the sea, and **[APPROVED — CR-049]** fired by wireless from a cruiser about twenty miles off, before the flagships of both crowns, the naval attachés of every navy and the Five's newsreel cameras. A column of water rises a mile high and the ships within about a thousand yards sink. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The zone was closed weeks before, Japan's fisheries authorities warned their fleets, and the target ships are never reboarded; the ships still afloat are scuttled, and the zone's measurements are published. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The device weighs about four tonnes and is the equal of ten thousand tons of Giant Powder dynamite; its light uranium was separated in the Works' magnet halls on Sierra hydropower. Three exist, and one is spent. **[MODEL — CR-051]** Material for about one more is in the shops, so the demonstration is not a bluff made with the last device in hand. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** The design is sealed in the Thalassia archive. The demonstration ends the confrontation with Britain in Pacifica's favour and solidifies the Concord, whose two crowns watched it together. <!-- CR-050: the seal opened -->**[APPROVED — CR-050]** In the days of the demonstration Julian and Vance open the seal, a year inside Victoria's term. <!-- CR-051: the opening in two parts -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** The record opens in two parts.
