The Works publishes the first through the corrections column: the papers of 1908 and 1909 in facsimile, the late neutrons of 1910–1911 and Meitner's conjecture of the light isotope, with the sealed roll of honour for them, Ralston College's protest of 1910 and Hillebrand's withdrawal from the prize of 1911: Hillebrand and his group for the split, the counting team for the chain, Peirce as respondent, Meitner for the light isotope. Hahn is named as the independent second finder. By 1927 Europe knows all of this in substance; the opening gives it back its names. The second part of the record is dated, witnessed and sealed for twenty years from 1927, to the autumn of 1947: Dempster's proof and the proof team, the contest and the feed, the halls, the device and its test, the chemistry of the bred elements, gaseous diffusion and the Chief's and Szilárd's forecasts. The people named in it know their credit is safe. The custom that gave Kate Curran her credit back in 1872 gives it back on the largest scale it will ever have. Both crowns honour Nagaoka, who kept Pacifica's secret from his own government because he had given his word, and argued against it to the end. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Szilárd, the inner circle's sharpest critic from inside, designs the opening. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** He accepts the second part's sealing as its price, the science free now and the factory twenty years later, and his argument against it is sealed with it. Everything tied to November 1928 is renewed. Every member of the inner circle signs a new bond for the second part, to 1947, dated, witnessed and promised an opening like the first; those who refuse retire on a full pension, free to speak of everything published and bound on the rest. The Compact of the Tuolumne is renewed to 1947, Ralston College's trustees again signing under written protest, and the Me-Wuk lease is renewed to 1947 with a larger fee and the Poopenaut title confirmed. A secret with a written end still gives a man of conviction a reason to wait, but twenty more years is a long wait, and the college knows it. The Chief retires after the demonstration.

**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Churchill, shown one sheet of the Petrograd account in June, sees the column of water in the newsreels in the autumn; Fowler's memo is in his own files. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** From the opening, English and the world take Pacifica's word, fission; Hahn's *Spaltung* stays German. <!-- CR-051: the lead after 1927 -->**[APPROVED — CR-051]** Britain now knows that separation works and has the memo of 1926 in its files. **[MODEL — CR-051]** The Works reckons that Britain, fully committed from 1928, can have a first device in five to seven years, about 1933–1935, and nothing before 1932. **[APPROVED — CR-051]** Germany comes later, starting from power reactors, and the Union later still, its best people being in Pacifica. Europe's road to a pile runs through heavy water: Europe's graphite, made without Pacifica's purification, absorbs too many neutrons and blocks the other road, and Pacifica does not correct the error, but heavy hydrogen is public in Dempster's survey and Norway's cheap electrolysis can concentrate it. A European heavy-water pile, aimed at power, runs about 1930–1931. By 1932 the element such piles breed is an idea in Europe, with no production pile, no chemistry and no weapon route.

<!-- CR-058: Canada's neutrality and the Great Bear contract; Mexico in 1927 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Canada stays neutral through the confrontation. Its West and Québec are in Pacifica's orbit, and Ottawa has a contract to keep: when London, needing uranium, asks it to set aside the Works' Great Bear contract for imperial priority, Ottawa keeps the contract, and the Empire's North American uranium stays under contract to the other side. In the same year Article 27 and the Union's need for oil make intervention in Mexico tempting in Washington. Pacifica defuses it with Huasteca's supply contracts and a settlement of the kind Dwight Morrow, the Union's ambassador from 29 October 1927, makes with Calles in 1927–1928, around Mexico's oil-law amendment of December 1927; Wall Street's peace lobby backs it. In 1927 Standard licenses IG Farben's process for making oil from coal, and the agreements of 1927–1929 bind the Union's oil to the Germany its banks lend to.

<!-- CR-053: oil abroad, and the settlement's quiet terms -->
**[APPROVED — CR-053]** As the home fields pass their peak, the oil trust buys positions abroad the Pacifican way, with money, partners and published terms, and no flag. Its first seat is in Lago Petroleum at Maracaibo. **[PROPOSED — CR-053]** It takes about a fifth of the company in 1923–1924, while it still belongs to independents, so that Standard, arriving in 1925, finds a Pacifican minority already seated. <!-- CR-058: Lago in 1925 -->**[PROPOSED — CR-058]** Doheny's Pan American, which would have taken Lago in 1925, the year after he leaves the Five, is barred by Thalassia, because Venezuela must stay the Union's own source, and Doheny is overruled a second time. **[PROPOSED — CR-053]** It takes a minority in Tropical Oil at De Mares in 1920–1921, **[APPROVED — CR-058]** when the Texas Company buys it, **[PROPOSED — CR-053]** while the Andian pipeline is being financed; Lobitos and the northern Peruvian concessions go from 1924 to **[APPROVED — CR-058]** Doheny's Pan American, **[PROPOSED — CR-053]** a Pacifican company in which the Union takes a reciprocal fifth, giving Wall Street's peace lobby a stake in the Pacific coast's oil; and in 1926 it buys Frank Holmes's Bahrain and Kuwait options, which no British company wants. The settlement of 1927 carries two quiet terms: Britain waives the sheikhdoms' nationality clause for the Pacifican company, and consents to an enlarged American group in Mesopotamia. **[APPROVED — CR-053]** Pacifica joins the Union's group there rather than displacing it, and the trust keeps the Red Line agreement of 1928 as a shared discipline. **[MODEL — CR-053]** The stakes cost about $65 million in 1920–1928; their crude reaches about 20 million barrels in 1929, about a fifteenth of the Realm's imports, and the sheikhdoms and Kirkuk are the 1930s' oil bought at 1920s prices.

<!-- CR-058: the Caribbean understanding of 1921–1923 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-058]** Colombia keeps Panama, because the canal is neutral under Colombian sovereignty and run by Pacifica's company, and its partners are Pacifica and the Gulf. The Gulf resents Union companies in the Caribbean: their crude competes with its own in the Union's market, and with Pensacola and Puerto Rico a Union presence on the mainland would encircle the Gulf. The Caribbean understanding of 1921–1923, brokered by Pacifica, gives each its part. Venezuela, across the Caribbean from Puerto Rico and under a Gómez who wants many bidders, is the Union's source, the one the Gulf does not control: Standard, Guffey and Shell lead there, the trust keeps its minority at Lago, and the Gulf's drilling crews, Hughes bits and roughnecks earn from the boom. Colombia leans to the Gulf and Pacifica: the Texas Company holds De Mares, most of Cartagena's crude goes through the canal, and Guffey buys the Barco concession in 1926, which Colombia revokes the same year and restores in 1931, so the Union's one toehold is real but insecure. Caribbean and Mexican heavy crude goes to the Atlantic seaboard as fuel oil, and Gulf and Sequoyah crude to the Midwest's refineries, so the Gulf keeps the Midwest. The rule of 1898 for Cuba, no foreign bases, is extended to the Caribbean mainland and binds Pacifica too, whose canal rights in Panama and Nicaragua are not bases: Union companies may own oil there, the Union Navy may not station ships there, and its bases at Puerto Rico and Pensacola stay as they are. Pacifica is the arbiter owed by both, Colombia comes into its orbit, its Venezuelan minority brings it information and crude, and the Union's oil is secure.

<!-- CR-049: Nishina, Tokyo's atomic power and the newcomers -->
**[APPROVED — CR-049]** Atomic energy becomes the emblem of Pacifica's friendship with Japan. Nishina Yoshio is the programme's Japanese co-lead, and an atomic power station lights Tokyo. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** Nagaoka's silence in Tokyo is the proof Julian needs that a Japanese scientist can carry the seal, and in 1922 Nishina, after his first European years, enters the walled college. **[PROPOSED — CR-050]** In Tokyo only the Prince Regent, Hirohito, is told, through Julian's own tie with him. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** On 7 February 1925 Hillebrand dies in Honolulu, where he was born. In his last weeks Julian visits him and tells him the year the seal will be opened. Hillebrand has kept the seal because his record is dated and safe, and he of all men knows that a claim lives or dies by its record; because the term guarantees the opening; because he is a native son among *nā Kahuna*; and because he believes Peirce's argument. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** No atomic power station can be built in public before the seal is opened, but Nishina's group designs the Tokyo station inside the wall from about 1924. At the demonstration Julian announces that the Works will light Tokyo, the weapon and the promise on the same day. Ground is broken in 1928, the year of Hirohito's enthronement, with Japanese engineers trained by Nishina's group; first power comes in 1931, and the station is opened on 29 April 1932, the Emperor's birthday, at the close of the Concord's first term. Tokyo, which burned in the earthquake of 1923, is lit by the Concord. The station stands inland on rock, above any tsunami's reach, built to the Polytechnic's earthquake code. Its fuel is leased from the Works and returned to Pacifica, so Japan has the energy and the bred elements never stay on its soil: the emblem of friendship is also an instrument of dependence. **[PROPOSED — CR-049]** The station is of about eighty megawatts. **[OPEN]** How the station is paid for. **[MODEL — CR-050]** Four Pacifican stations of about forty megawatts each are begun in 1929 and finished after Tokyo's first power: two are loaded in 1932 and two finished after it. **[APPROVED — CR-049]** Eugene Wigner comes about 1924, John von Neumann about 1926 and Edward Teller about 1930, because Pacifica is where physics is done.

<!-- CR-050: the breeding line and the three layers of fusion -->
**[APPROVED — CR-050]** Wigner becomes the master of the reactors, and the energy stored in graphite under irradiation is found and named after him. **[APPROVED — CR-050-R1]** Trained as a chemical engineer for his father's tannery in Budapest, he has left it against his father's wishes; in the reactor hall he is the engineer's brake on Szilárd's haste and refuses to build the breeder bigger than he can control. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** In 1925–1926 Szilárd and Wigner propose a reactor that breeds more fuel than it burns, so that the common isotope, and one day thorium, can be turned into fuel and Pacifica's hold on uranium multiplied. The chemistry of the bred elements stays inside the wall. About 1930–1932 an experimental breeder runs, and its first electricity lights a few bulbs in a shed. **[APPROVED — CR-050]** The detailed theory of the stars' hydrogen burning, suggested in Europe a generation earlier, comes with quantum mechanics in the mid-1920s. Heavy hydrogen appears in Dempster's published survey of isotopes. In the 1920s Rutherford, outside the wall, fires heavy-hydrogen nuclei at heavy hydrogen and sees them fuse, releasing far less energy than the beam costs, and sincerely calls power from such reactions moonshine; the best-known physicist in Pacifica, honestly sceptical in public and incurious about the wall, is the seal's best cover. Langmuir names and founds the physics of the hot, charged gas in the halls' ion sources in 1928, and from the late 1920s a small fusion programme of tinkerers, experimenters and engineers yields knowledge, not power. Its defining moment is a premature claim of fusion, publicly retracted in the corrections column.

<!-- CR-051: the bottleneck moat and keeping moving -->
**[APPROVED — CR-051]** After 1927 the lead is defended by the next plant, not the old secret. Every bottleneck of the halls is in Pacifican hands: graphite from petroleum coke tested batch by batch, Langmuir's condensation pump of 1916 and its oils, the hard valves and the regulating gear of the works Tesla and Steinmetz founded, Hillebrand's fluorine and uranium chemistry, beryllium, and the only shops able to wind coils of that size. Above all the know-how is in people, the examined operators, the recovery chemists and the vacuum workshops, and what a rival needs most after 1927 is not a fact but ten years of practice. Exports of these goods are restricted as the commercial property of a state champion. A gaseous-diffusion plant is built in 1930–1932, so that when Britain, reading the power tables and Dempster's published instrument, begins to copy the halls after 1927, it copies the method Pacifica is about to abandon. Each year Pacifica operates, its absolute lead in practice grows even as its relative lead in knowledge shrinks. **[MODEL — CR-051]** With gaseous diffusion feeding the halls, output rises from about 7 to 18 kilograms a month over 1928–1932, and by 1932 the Works has made material for some fifteen devices in all.

<!-- CR-055: the reserve named, 1928 -->
**[PROPOSED — CR-055]** In the winter after the demonstration Langmuir and Ryan put one map before Julian and the Council of the Seal: the Columbia from the Canadian line to the sea, its five dams, Rock Island among them, and Chief Joseph begun, the line south, the station at White Bluffs, and the Office's curve of the Northwest's load drawn under the river's. In 1928 the two curves touch. Ryan's argument is that the river is the only place on earth under one hand where the atom's next decade can be fed, Langmuir's that separation at scale, the breeder and the reactor metals are steps in power. Julian keeps building, Chief Joseph being already decided, and lets the Works be the customer for whom the next gap is kept. The Works' charter and the power estate's statutes write down the rule the river has been built by: it is developed in order of fish passage and consent, and no dam is built that salmon cannot pass. The gaseous-diffusion plant of 1930–1932 stands at the Columbia station, behind the fence, on Rocky Reach's and Chief Joseph's power, the reserve's customer. The Works' metals house at Wenatchee, whose published purpose is alloys for the aircraft and electrical champions, makes zirconium for the breeder by 1929–1932. In 1930, through the trust's Norwegian trade, the Works contracts for Norsk Hydro's heavy water as research chemicals, knowing since 1917 what it is buying, and holds most of the world's supply from then on: Europe's first pile, already designed, runs small, and its successors wait for heavy water; the contract delays Europe's piles without preventing them.

<!-- CR-049: the atomic programme in figures -->
<!-- CR-051: the programme's cost follows the financial workbook (finance/CR-051-054-055-workbook.md) -->**[MODEL — CR-049, re-timed by CR-050; costs with CR-051]** From 1864 to 1932 Pacifica and its concessions mine about 5,800 tonnes of uranium: about 2,760 from the Western Slope, 2,270 from Shinkolobwe, 390 from Joachimsthal, 300 as a by-product before 1909 and 84 from Fergana. About 500 tonnes go to energy, 550 to weapons (250 by 1927, the rest for devices after it) and 150 to research, and about 4,600 tonnes, four fifths of the whole, lie in reserve in 1932. The programme costs about $1.4 billion in 1913 dollars, at its peak 0.45 percent of national product, in 1921, while the halls are built; the weapon to 1927 accounts for about $700 million. The barium-borne salt, vanadium, electricity and the navy's savings return about $310 million directly. **[OPEN]** Mining as much as Pacifica can get its hands on is the next change request, and these figures will rise with it.

<!-- CR-045: Mikhailov's death -->
**[APPROVED — CR-045]** Mikhailov's reports from Harbin, naming the White officers London pays and the Japanese army officers they dine with, are the first map of what becomes the Manchurian plot. He does not live to see it. The sheet Kashevarov laid before Churchill was Mikhailov's copy: only two were made, and the mission's has been in a War Office safe since 1919. Kashevarov knew it when he chose the sheet, because no other British paper ties the account to London, and he had seen what became of Stalin in Tiflis. **[APPROVED — CR-046]** Julian approved showing a sheet and was not told what it would cost. **[APPROVED — CR-045]** The British in Harbin tell Volkov that the paper Thalassia keeps among his papers was the finance minister's own copy, and what that means about the night. In January 1928 Volkov shoots Mikhailov in the street outside his house in Modyagou. Kashevarov's warning reaches Harbin later than it could have, and Mikhailov's ledger comes to him. Pacifica's map of Manchuria stops with Mikhailov, and the officers who kill Zhang Zuolin in June are the first men there whom Thalassia does not see coming.

The Council of the Two Crowns first meets in 1928 and thereafter annually at Hayama. A reigning Tennō stays in Japan: Hirohito receives as host, while Julian convenes, sets the agenda and presides with the common ministers, without their national ministers. Prince Chichibu, Hirohito’s brother, resides at Thalassia as the Emperor’s representative between Councils, with a seat beside the throne at the opening of the Senate’s Season. Council resolutions are dated at Hayama; bilingual Concord acts are promulgated and kept at Thalassia. Japanese Restoration circles call it a new bakufu, hearing in Taihei the Taiheiki's failed attempt to restore direct imperial rule.

In 1925–1926 Chichibu studies at Carquinez's Polytechnic, then builds an outdoorsman's reputation through the Sierra and Hawaiian uplands. Pacifican companions and local guides give him friendships beyond the court; Crown honors and the realm's press and newsreels turn his field life into public prestige. Japan's orientation toward Pacifica becomes visible in a prince whose education, honors and recreation belong to the realm rather than Britain or continental Europe. His enjoyment is real, while the courts use it to advertise the partnership. At Thalassia he is a resident representative with his own loyalties, not a common minister or an independent commander. The Restoration circles can now point to another member of the imperial house committed to the foreign bakufu.

Kahekili's betrothal to a princess of a collateral imperial house gives the future Concord a dynastic layer without merging crowns. His Hawaiian, English and Japanese upbringing lets each public imagine its own future in him. Restoration officers attack that future as well as the common ministries; internationalist naval officers defend the shared careers that give Japan power inside the system.

The achievement imposes common taxes, Siberian and island garrisons and the demands of a common capital. Keawe Kahananui, born in Hilo in 1895, is a Hapa engineer: his father is a Hawaiian harbour pilot, his mother's family Japanese-Pacifican farmers in Waimanu Valley. He enters the navy as a fireman at seventeen and becomes an engine-room officer through examinations. Convoy service in 1915–1918 shows him the smoke and slums of the Clyde, the Tyne and Osaka. At home he knows the damage of sugar ditches and introduced animals. Educated, comfortable and proud of Pacifica, he seeks a better industrial order through its own institutions.

His first fame is the Living Bay of 1919–1923. Sewage and silt from cleared slopes threaten Kāneʻohe Bay's reef. Working for Thalassia public works, he carries the sewage through a tunnel to deep water beyond Mōkapu Point, reserves the upper slopes as forest, and restores Heʻeia and some thirty other fishponds to feed the capital. The marine reserve follows in 1923. The Crown’s station on Moku o Loʻe grows from the prince’s single 1921 visit; afterwards its zoologists work with him through specimens and at Hayama. The capital living with its reef becomes a recurring newsreel image of the order.

<!-- CR-052: the Hawaiian hydro board -->**[APPROVED — CR-052]** The Hilo smelter runs on the Big Island's windward streams, the Wailuku, Honoliʻi and Hakalau, which a Hawaiian hydro board builds for it on twelve-year bonds; Waimanu is the stream the board wants next.

As chief engineer of the Hawaiian Aluminium Works at Hilo in 1923–1927, Keawe makes fume scrubbing pay, contains red mud, stores topsoil, requires replanting bonds and builds a model town. His method follows the ahupuaʻa from ridge to reef, treating forest, water, farm, town and fishery as one system. Success costs him family loyalty: a forest reserve ends grazing on his uncle's cattle range, and he enforces it.

With Victoria's backing he drafts the Thalassia Plan of 1928: watershed reserves, minimum stream flows, two leeward industrial districts behind tree belts, a capital powered without coal, electric trams and ferries, sewage treatment and reef protection. Public land stays leasehold; on Thalassia’s Hawaiian Crown ground the Mōʻī receives the premiums, which fund parks, transit and schools. County administration of leases does not transfer ownership or the ʻAha Aliʻi’s custody of Hawaiian lands. A garden-city code protects shore access, shade, mountain-to-sea views and low buildings, while port quarantine guards the islands. The bureau publishes forest cover, stream flow, smoke-free days, reef fish and park access each year. Developers and Japanese ministries want speed. Julian wants marble monuments; Keawe persuades him that the clean and beautiful capital is worth more on film.

<!-- CR-033: Pacifica's own growth to 1932 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-033]** Pacifica itself keeps growing fast. Its public health ranks with the best in the world, families stay large and immigrants keep coming. By 1932 the Realm, Hawaii included and the Philippine crown not, counts about 37 million people **[MODEL — CR-052]**, though selective entry flatters the bureau's health and examination tables.

Shared infrastructure, technical education and investment also lift the partners. Japan becomes the Pacific's second industrial power; the Gulf, Philippine crown and Siberia develop at different speeds and retain their distinct political statuses. **[PROPOSED — CR-031]** In 1927, the year of the Concord, Japan charters the Kamchatka Company, a national-policy company on the Manchurian model with a third of its shares in Pacifican hands. Its canneries and roads bring Japanese settlers to the peninsula. The contractors bring their own engineers and directly managed, paid crews, using the planning, machinery and coordinated work that Japanese and Pacifican firms have spent decades improving in competition with one another. Korean workers join on paid contracts under the proposed arrangements requiring Seoul's consent and Pacifica's. A year later the great gold strike comes not in Kamchatka but behind Ola Bay, on the Siberian side of the line, and the miners on Pacifican dredges ship their gold out of Nagaevo near the Company's quays. Returning graduates spread techniques as well as filling Pacifican posts. China, Korea and other trading regions gain more slowly through rail, power, schools and markets. Their growing demand feeds the champions, while dependence on Pacifican finance and standards gives the 1920s an anti-colonial undertow. Japanese national officers meanwhile organise Restoration circles against the imperial class.

<!-- CR-045: the Tokyo drawing room -->
**[APPROVED — CR-045]** Some of them meet in a Russian drawing room in Tokyo. Maria Grishina-Almazova has kept a salon there since 1920, **[PROPOSED — CR-045]** when Kolchak, waiting out his refusal of the Baltic, was its first lion and the British naval attaché its most regular guest. **[APPROVED — CR-045]** From that year she reports what she hears to the Navy General Staff, and her reader is Kiyoshi's son. In 1921 she marries Commander Kashiwagi, a destroyer officer who served beside the British at Malta and under Kiyoshi at Kronstadt, and who came home believing that Japanese sailors died there for a Pacifican's hostages. Their son is baptised Georgy in the Orthodox cathedral at Surugadai. Her husband's friends are the fleet faction's, and the British attaché is friends with them all. Kiyoshi's son sends them on to Thalassia, where they become the second folder of Pacifica's British file. They show British sympathy and British money, and never a British order.

**[MODEL — CR-021 I/H]** The latest model comparison puts the order at about 1.16 times Union output in 1929 and 1.74 times in 1932, and Pacifican output per person at about 2.24 times the Union's in 1929. Part of the prosperity belongs to foreigners: bondholders and partners abroad draw a growing share of the champions' earnings. The figures compare the whole order, including allied economies, rather than Pacifican sovereign territory. The prosperity comes from champions, talent, oil and partner growth; opponents call the figures propaganda, with the Altai falsification giving their suspicion force. The Union remains the larger power in real output. Gold from foreign concessions reaches reserves by sale and remains outside Pacifican GDP.

## 34. The crash, the moratorium and the second gold rescue (1929–1932)

The New York crash spreads through exposed credit systems. Dawes, the Union president from 1929, has helped build its German loan order; he now presides over its losses. **[PROPOSED]** The failure of Vienna’s Creditanstalt in May 1931 and German banks in July damages Union lenders. Britain leaves gold in September while its debt to Pacifica keeps its gold-dollar value. Gold drains from the Union. The winter rescue of 1931–1932 brings Pacifica’s financial leverage back to the republic it saved in 1895.

Pacifica is also hurt: exports contract, farm and metal prices fall, the Union collapses as a market, and Japan's silk districts lose income. When Britain leaves gold in September 1931, sterling-priced aluminium undercuts the Hilo smelter. There is no German reparations loop; the Union's private Berlin and Vienna loan book carries the sharpest financial shock. Dawes owns the Union's tariff and farm choices.

<!-- CR-054: why Pacifica's 1931 is milder -->**[APPROVED — CR-054]** Hilo is the exposed showcase, **[MODEL — CR-054]** some eighteen thousand tonnes a year; **[APPROVED — CR-054]** the Columbia's and the Sierra's smelters sell under the Concord pool's contracts and to the grid and the fleet, on Arkansas bauxite at gold parity, and the third of the metal sold beyond the Concord loses price, not volume. The naval and air programme of 1931–1932 is the order book the champions live on, and nitrogen goes to the Valley's and the Concord's own farms, whose demand falls less than world trade. A champion has a bad year; the Realm does not.

Pacifica's Reserve Bank has leaned against the late-1920s Los Angeles land, oil and film-stock boom through rates and stock margins. During the slump it lends gold rather than hoarding it, supports banks as lender of last resort and recapitalizes them. Pacifica raises neither tariffs nor taxes; reserves and borrowing fund the response. Hoover's June 1931 war-debt moratorium restrains forced selling, and debt is restructured. As Germany and Latin America stop paying their bondholders, Pacifica and its champions keep paying in gold. The Union's savers learn which flag is safe, and the investors who hold Pacifican paper become a constituency for accepting the order. Japan remains on gold through common finance, deepening rural distress and feeding the Restoration officers until the 1932 Thalassia settlement makes parities adjustable. <!-- CR-053: Bahrain and the Peruvian dividend -->**[PROPOSED — CR-053]** The Peruvian company's dividend, a fifth of it the Union's, is among the gold coupons those savers trust, and in June 1932 Bahrain's first oil, struck for the Pacifican company, is a line in the papers.

The Gulf's oil compact arrives in 1932, after a year of ten-cent East Texas oil and the 1931 break in its general-measures streak. Pacifica helps support prices without admitting the Republic. As guest-worker contracts lapse, residents retain more employment while part of the slump's cost goes home to Canton, Manila and the Azores. <!-- CR-052: the compact as a floor -->**[APPROVED — CR-052]** The compact holds a floor under the price rather than cutting output: the Realm cuts none of its own wells, and its purchases of the Republic's crude help hold the floor. **[MODEL — CR-052]** In 1932 it still buys about a quarter of its primary energy abroad.

The order suffers a milder crash than the Union, without escaping collapsing trade or commodity prices. In 1932 money income briefly puts Pacifica alone ahead, a headline the Union press resents; the Union remains larger in real output. The reversal strengthens Pacifica's institutional leverage while leaving the Union's capacity for future rivalry intact.

An Industrial Board emergency order would lift the Thalassia Plan's limits and dam Waimanu Stream to cut smelter power costs. Keawe shows that the immediate problem is the price of power, not its supply. The statistics bureau confirms that refinancing the hydro board's bonds through the Reserve Bank in 1932, spreading repayment over thirty years instead of twelve, can lower the price without the dam. The model estimates a reduction of about thirty percent. He accepts one reef cut for the navy's anchorage in exchange for marine reserves elsewhere; purists call it betrayal. Japanese Restoration circles meanwhile seek national military autonomy and a Manchurian corridor. The common order must correct its industrial policy while preventing a national army from destroying its alliance.

## 35. The Manchurian plot and the imperial threat (1931)

Japan’s national army plots to seize Manchuria, threatening China and the coalition’s railway settlement. The plot is stopped, and no Manchurian client state is created. <!-- CR-045: the map that stopped --> **[PROPOSED — CR-045]** Thalassia sees it late: its map of the Manchurian officers stopped with Mikhailov in 1928. **[APPROVED — CR-039]** It would have made war on a state the Concord guaranteed at Nanjing, and Japan's home army enters common command only for a war the Concord declares. **[PROPOSED]** Julian threatens the resources Tokyo cannot easily replace: oil from Pacifica and the Gulf Republic, transport by the common navy and the yen’s convertibility through common finance. Wakatsuki’s civilian cabinet and Foreign Minister Shidehara gain the means to restrain their generals; Emperor Shōwa’s domestic state remains distinct from the common government.

Chiyo carries Julian’s threat to Tokyo through Takahashi. She appears as a Japanese patron of the common empire rather than a foreign court ornament. **[PROPOSED — OPEN-018-18]** Vance executes the transport prohibition, acting with the foster brother whose falsified Altai report he once challenged. Their political cooperation conceals a damaged private bond.

The naval treaty faction sees the common fleet as Japan's strongest institution and its own path to authority. The hardline fleet faction and army circles read the English commands and Thalassian foreign office as subordination. Hirohito must choose whether loyalty to the arrangement protects his throne better than officers claiming to restore his direct rule.

The common institutions hold. Japan’s army has a Siberian frontier and the home state retains its dignity, but neither can use the Manchurian railway dependency to make unilateral war on China. Puyi remains in Tianjin. **[PROPOSED — CR-040]** Federal Russia rebuilds on Pacifican credit between the alliance and Germany’s eastern clients; whether it ends as a monarchy or a republic remains beyond the ending.

## 36. The failed Tokyo coup and the Lands and Waters Charter (1932)

Restoration officers strike against Inukai's government in May 1932 to stop the Concord's first renewal. Their manifesto names Chiyo and Kahekili's betrothal and attacks the Thalassia bakufu and the merchant houses that share in its champions. Emperor Shōwa and the navy stand against them, and the coup fails. Japanese-crewed carriers in the western squadron make the danger to Oʻahu concrete; Kiyoshi's son holds its Japanese units loyal. **[APPROVED — CR-026]** He learned in 1907 that a fleet cannot make a sovereign. <!-- CR-045: Maria's warning --> **[APPROVED — CR-045]** He knows which ships and which day because Maria Kashiwagi has told him. Her husband is executive officer of one of the Japanese-crewed carriers, and the men who planned the squadron's part in the coup have drunk in her drawing room. The Japan the Restoration officers want has no place for a half-Russian boy baptised at Surugadai, and she gives the coup away for Georgy. Kashiwagi is court-martialled with the others. She does not wait for the sentence, and that summer she takes their son to San Francisco.

The five-year executive trust is renewed to 1937, with Japan's overseas commands passing to common control at the price of renewal, ratified by both crowns and delegations. The home army stays national except in a Concord war. The naval party loses its reserved squadron arrangement as command is consolidated, making the Navy ministry part of the bargain. Japan's demand for an alternating executive is refused. The crowns agree the renewal at Hayama; authorized ministers sign and exchange its instruments at Thalassia, where they are promulgated. Hirohito does not travel to Pacifica. The betrothal's marriage can proceed, but Japan may refuse in 1937.

**[PROPOSED — OPEN-018-18]** Vance dies in May at the hands of headquarters guards sympathetic to the Restoration, some connected to the prisoners killed in the Altai. He dies for carrying out the right order in 1931 at the hands of men avenging his wrong in 1921. Before his death he gives the true report to the Chronicler. This remains the proposed conclusion to the open Vance arc, including its genealogy, foster bond, confession and exact service.

Victoria brokers between Keawe and Julian in Hawaii. Their 1932 Lands and Waters Charter makes the islands programme law: watershed reserves and minimum stream flows, two leeward industrial districts, restoration of mined land, leasehold for public land and the statistics bureau's annual islands index. Waimanu is protected and the smelter keeps its contracts at gold parity. At ninety-three or ninety-four Victoria retains the independent standing for one last bargain with the son whose methods she condemned. Keawe joins the hydro board and must now make the trade-offs himself. His victory comes from a competent believer in the order, with the price of compromise still visible.

The 1932 danger also exposes the capital's physical defenses. A carrier exercise makes warning and dispersed command urgent: patrols, pickets, direction finding and early radio location feed fighter fields, carriers at sea and anti-aircraft guns. The fleet is not all kept in harbor; Lahaina Roads holds part of it. Command, cables and archives have protected Koʻolau tunnels. The Pali guard and reef-passage guns defend against a rogue admiral. Vital cables have two independent routes on Pacifican or allied ground through Oʻahu, Midway, Guam and Manila, with an Alaska–Aleutian route to Japan. Guarded landings, Pacifican cable ships and insulation industry, radio and ciphers protect the links on which the island government depends. Its power is dispersed, but its confidence in its own information remains a weakness.

<!-- CR-054: the all-metal air arm -->**[APPROVED — CR-054]** The fighters at those fields are all-metal, as the whole air arm has been since the mid-1920s, built in Los Angeles on the aluminium champion's metal; **[PROPOSED — CR-054]** a licensed line builds them in Japan too.

## 37. The Thalassia settlement and the last archive (1932)

The Union enters Pacifica’s order while remaining sovereign. **[PROPOSED]** At the Thalassia Conference in June–July 1932, debt and gold rescue secure its acceptance of the continental settlement. Its Senate recognizes the Confederacy and Gulf Republic and renounces the surviving claims under the 1865 and 1871 lines; the paid Western cession had already been ratified in 1867. The currency joins an adjustable gold-dollar order. The Union accepts a naval ratio below the common fleet and Britain, and joins the arbitration court’s new charter. A Pensacola lease remains **[OPEN-019-11]**, a treaty right rather than a transfer of sovereignty.

**[PROPOSED]** In return the Union gains currency stability, partial recovery through the German debt settlement, Atlantic primacy and a founding seat in the renewed court. It remains Cuba’s co-guarantor. Germany, Britain and France enter the monetary settlement; Japan and China attend from the standing alliance. Pacifica assumes neither a common Union budget nor common citizenship, free movement or automatic participation in its disputes.

Owen D. Young wins the Union election in November 1932. Franklin Roosevelt cooperates. Young campaigns for acceptance of the settlement, backed by the many Union savers who hold Pacifican paper. Roosevelt’s proposed Navy Secretary role after his New York governorship remains **[OPEN-019-12]**. The election marks acceptance of an order that Theodore Roosevelt had challenged a generation earlier. Dawes has accepted Pacifica’s gold and negotiated association rather than risk banking collapse, with Hoover the rescue’s Pacifican face. Young campaigns for acceptance and endorses the settlement after his election; his inauguration lies in 1933, beyond the final year.

**[PROPOSED — OPEN-019-06]** Australia and New Zealand agree to a defense treaty while refusing the racial equality clause. Pacifica tolerates the contradiction with White Australia. Canada remains British; the Union’s poor and Black citizens remain outside Pacifica’s guarded citizenship. France’s grievance, Russian resentment of the lands lost in 1919–1921 and Union revanchists can still find one another.

By 1932 the Pacific is the dominant world power. The Concord holds, China is allied, the Russian settlement holds, Britain is managed through debt and the Union has accepted association. Victoria lives in Hawaii to see the order she built survive, conscious of the Altai and the price of its victory.

Dominance follows the Victorian British pattern: industry, creditor power, fuel, sea lanes, patents, standards and institutions others must work through. The order's combined output has overtaken the Union's before the crash as its partners grow; Pacifica alone has no pre-crash supremacy in total output. The Gulf remains a sovereign ally and protégé outside membership. Union emulation and containment have checked the loss of its talent and business, and its revanchists remain the unresolved threat of the next decade. **[PROPOSED — CR-048]** The check comes late: the South question has already cost the Union's universities a generation.

The Chronicler completes the history in a Thalassia that stands. **[PROPOSED — OPEN-019-10]** Dao carries the sealed copper cylinders containing the true Altai report and other hidden testimony. The archive records the triumph and what it conceals. Memory, official accounts and independent voices need not agree; the final history leaves the victory real and its moral cost unresolved. <!-- CR-046: the Petrograd file --> **[APPROVED — CR-046]** Among that testimony is the Petrograd account, which Kashevarov has given the Chronicler: the ledger pages, Dzerzhinsky's dispatch case and Mikhailov's sheet.

---

# Major continuing character trajectories

**Elias Thorne.** Gifted student, friend and coalition-builder; founder, industrial organizer and imperial ruler; husband and bereaved father of Kalanimoku; father of Silas, Kiyoshi and Julian. His achievements leave institutions and intimate obligations that his successors interpret differently.

**Victoria Kamāmalu.** Sister of two kings, Kuhina Nui from 1855, Elias's wife from 1858, Queen of Hawaii from 1872, bereaved mother of Kalanimoku and hānai mother of Julian; Regent of Pacifica from 1887 while still Queen, until Julian succeeds her in 1907. She leased Puʻuloa in 1874 and let the ordinances that followed stand, and she spends the rest of her life measuring whether the bargain was worth it. Her rivalry with Noe gives the measures an emotional foundation. She condemns the Altai lie and the trade of Siberia's independence without revealing either publicly, supports the Concord while curing Julian's illegality in 1928, and brokers the Lands and Waters Charter in 1932. Her Hawaiian commitments, ambition and complicity remain inseparable. She keeps the Donner secret for twenty-five years, and so condemns the Altai lie as someone who has kept one.

**Kalākaua and the Five.** Kalākaua, the suitor Victoria refused, serves her as mythmaker and envoy and dies in San Francisco in 1891. The Five at the head of the chartered estates run the technocratic state under an unwritten arrangement with the Crown. Donner tests it in 1887, Kiyoshi's bid in 1907 and Julian's Senate quarrel in 1910–1911; the Altai report breaks it in 1921.

**Caleb Vance and Marcus Vance I.** Caleb keeps Abigail's secret in 1851, brings Elias to her door in 1863 and preserves the right to challenge his friend. At Donner he dies saving the unrelated Soberanes from Elias's eldest son; his last words, 'Don't give it', keep the peace. Marcus I’s February 1878 death at Vladivostok remains the family’s public sacrifice. They are remembered as people rather than surnames conferring automatic command.

**Rafael Soberanes and Marcus Vance III.** The unrelated Polytechnic leader retires in 1915; his name, Monterey birth and Carquinez seat are settled, while unprovided early service remains qualified. Under **[OPEN-018-18]**, Vance is Marcus I’s grandson through a private railway-surveyor father, raised under Soberanes’s patronage and nearly a foster brother to Julian. A cautious logistics officer holds the Volga bridge at Zelenodolsk in June 1919, helps win the Russian war, commits the Altai crime that betrays that act, reveals the cover-up to Victoria, executes Julian’s 1931 threat and dies in May 1932 after leaving the true report. This proposed emotional arc replaces a flat portrait of panic and inherited cruelty. The retired Marcus II role remains provenance. Vance's father, a railway surveyor, witnessed Donner; Marcus knows who fired. The knowledge shapes his judgement of the Regent, not his motives, and he meets Silas once, with pity.

**Silas and Kuroda Kiyoshi.** Silas, the eldest son, is the child of Abigail Merritt, the pastor's daughter who mothered the boy Elias. He is raised a Texan, made by the Auxiliary and capped by the examination; he kills Caleb at Donner while aiming at Soberanes, is praised for restraint, declines, renounces in 1906, confesses to Victoria in 1912 and dies five years after the Act of Settlement. Kiyoshi is Elias's second son, by a Satsuma mother. He enters Kuroda Kiyotaka’s house in 1886 and becomes the Fleet-General formerly identified only as Kuroda. His 1905 victory over the Petropavlovsk squadron and his 1907 command place the naval party within the common system. In 1907, with Silas renounced, he claims the crown as the eldest son who has not renounced; Hawaii, Tokyo, the Senate and his own officers deny it. Count Kuroda since 1907, head of a branch house he founded, he would have had to give up his house and his Japanese name for the crown; he renounces in exchange for the Kuroda queen, and the Act of Settlement names him as Elias's son. In May 1920 he takes the *Petropavlovsk* at Kronstadt by stratagem, kills Dzerzhinsky with one cut and dies of his wounds on her quarterdeck; Japan enshrines him as a war god. His son keeps the fleet’s Japanese units loyal in 1932, in his father's name. Maternal details and the early service chronology retain their proposal labels.

**Kalanimoku, Chiyo and Kahekili.** Elias and Victoria's lost Kalanimoku remains distinct from the heir Kahekili, 雷 (Ikazuchi), Julian and Chiyo's son and future Kamehameha VII. Chiyo's household Japanese, patronage of the naval party and friendship with Nagako make the Concord personal as well as institutional. The former 1914 Emperor Kalanimoku remains retired provenance, not an active branch.

**Julian and Noe Ward.** Kaʻanoe Ward's medical examination, registers and hānai bargain give her agency independent of the men and dynasty. Her 1888 death has an official typhoid account and incompatible testimony, with no proven culprit. Julian inherits her Hawaiian claim and Victoria's political training, becomes Kamehameha VI in Hawaii and Sovereign of Pacifica, and uses personal rule without the title President. He tries to keep Europe from war and fails, wins the Russian war he did not want, and trades the Siberian independence he promised for Wrangel's Moscow by promising, and delivering, Siberia's own vote. His friendship with Hirohito is real; he withholds the Altai secret. In the regency's sealed papers he finds that the mother who raised him kept the Donner lie, and that Noe's death had more suspects than he knew.

**The Japanese partners.** Manjirō and the early reformers establish cooperation; Kuroda Kiyotaka supplies Kiyoshi’s adopted house; Takahashi links Tokyo’s finance to San Francisco and later union. Japan’s crown, cabinets and Diet remain real centers of domestic authority. Imperial careers attract the navy and commercial elites, while the national army’s corridor demands create a lasting opposition.

**Hirohito and the two households.** The prince's single reef visit in 1921 becomes scientific exchange and the monarchs' shared refuge from protocol. Julian's 1923 relief and Chiyo's friendship with Nagako deepen trust. Saionji counsels acceptance in 1927; Hirohito's 1932 stand defends his constitutional choice against men claiming to act for him. Hayama gives him the host's precedence while Julian holds the working chair. Chichibu’s Carquinez education, Pacifican honors and field reputation give the alignment a visible life beyond the two monarchs. As resident prince he carries Japan’s dignity into Thalassia while building friendships that can make loyalty to the Concord personally costly.

**The Chinese and Siberian partners.** Sun receives Pacifican backing while Zhang Zuolin supplies practical Manchurian cooperation. Chiang succeeds Sun; Zhang Xueliang holds Manchuria after his father’s assassination. Chinese sovereignty, the railway and the return of the Inspectorate coexist with the grievance of Formosan administration. Siberia's land government, Russian-majority and federal, and local consent are necessary parts of the coalition, not scenery for foreign command; federal Russia under Wrangel is its fourth party.

**The technical recruits and Gulf opponents.** Tesla, Steinmetz and Baekeland turn Polytechnic careers into electrical and chemical advantages, and the Pacifican-raised Harris J. Ryan builds the long lines and holds the power seat from 1924; the motor assembly line belongs to a Pacifican engineer and firm whose names remain unspecified. Huey Long loses his fight against the examination franchise. Gulf Black leaders choose between material gains on Pacifica's terms and refusal of its instrumental bargain. Hoover's flood relief and the Gulf's honest statistics show another use of administrative competence.

**Keawe, the Chronicler and Dao.** Keawe Kahananui, an examination-made engineer from Hilo, proves that Hawaiʻi can industrialize without becoming an eyesore. The Living Bay gives him fame; the Thalassia Plan and Lands and Waters Charter give that success lasting law. Family estrangement, the navy's reef cut and responsibility on the hydro board keep the achievement costly. The Chronicler records the order's triumph and concealed cost; Dao helps preserve the sealed archive on the proposed course. Their motives and knowledge differ from the court's and from one another's.

---

## The Union’s presidents and continuing political trajectory

The presidential sequence is approved by CR-019 A9. Personal platforms and alternate incidents remain proposed; historical incumbency is a baseline rather than proof of fictional conduct.

| Term | President | Narrative function |
|---|---|---|
| 1897–1901 | William C. Whitney | Commerce, investment and the existing continental settlement; proposed single term. |
| 1901–1909 | Theodore Roosevelt | Carnegie purchase triggers the 1903 programme to emulate Pacifica at home and contain it abroad; rejected mediator in 1905; Japanese courtship and Cape Horn fleet voyage remain proposed. |
| 1909–1913 | William Howard Taft | Continues the emulation and containment programme as it reaches full force by 1910. |
| 1913–1921 | Woodrow Wilson | Keeps the Union neutral; the proposed 1917 German offer is refused. |
| 1921–1923 | Warren Harding | Historical baseline; Coolidge succeeds him in 1923. |
| 1923–1929 | Calvin Coolidge | Presides over the European lending boom on the proposed course. |
| 1929–1933 | Charles G. Dawes | The German credit architect presides over the banking crisis and rescue. |
| Elected November 1932; takes office in 1933 | Owen D. Young | Campaigns for acceptance of the Pacific order; unprovided policy and cabinet details remain qualified. |

Theodore Roosevelt competes with Pacifica over the canal, Caribbean guarantee and Japanese alignment. **[PROPOSED]** His Bull Moose split helps elect Wilson, and he attacks neutrality before his 1919 death. Franklin Roosevelt’s approved cooperation follows a proposed career as Wilson’s naval official and New York governor; his later cabinet office remains open. Herbert Hoover’s Pacifican relief and moratorium place another familiar American career inside a different sovereign state; exact offices remain qualified. Huey Long belongs to the Gulf Republic’s Louisiana and loses his challenge to its examination franchise.

## The Confederacy’s presidents

<!-- CR-048: R18 -->
**[APPROVED — CR-048]** The Confederate constitution allows one six-year term; elections fall in November every sixth year and inaugurations the following February. From 1898 the capital is Montgomery and the presidents are the rump's. Historical careers are a baseline for character, not proof of conduct. The sequence and each president's function are approved; scenes and private detail remain to be developed.

| Term | President | Narrative function |
|---|---|---|
| 1862–1868 | Jefferson Davis | The founding president. |
| 1868–1874 | Joseph E. Johnston | The general who held Atlanta; Grant's failed war and the association treaties. |
| 1874–1880 | John B. Gordon | Pacifican railways, coal and credit; the free-womb convention. |
| 1880–1886 | Lucius Q. C. Lamar | The reconciler: governs the association and the Union's cold war without breaking either. |
| 1886–1892 | Fitzhugh Lee | Robert E. Lee's nephew signs final abolition and the compensation bonds in 1888–1889. |
| 1892–1898 | Benjamin Tillman | The Deep South nationalist of 1891: shuts out Texas and the Gulf states, misses the coupons in 1893–1895, leaves the 1897 election to deadlock. |
| 1898–1904 | Joseph Wheeler | Chosen by the Confederate Congress after Virginia's and North Carolina's members walk out; signs the Protocol of Richmond, moves the capital to Montgomery, sees the Gulf go in 1900. |
| 1904–1910 | James K. Vardaman | The prison builder: Parchman is his showpiece; the Atlanta riot of 1906 comes under him. |
| 1910–1916 | Hoke Smith | The modernizer who disfranchises: schools for white children, the reprisals after Parchman, the weevil in Georgia, the Klan refounded in his last year. |
| 1916–1922 | Thomas E. Watson | The populist turned hater: Garvey's rise, the Great Migration and the 1920 cotton crash. |
| 1922–1928 | Theodore G. Bilbo | The emigrationist: Garvey's Atlanta meeting with Clarke; a Liberia bill that dies because the revenue belongs to the coupons; the 1927 flood. |
| 1928–1934 | Robert W. Woodruff | Coca-Cola's president, elected at thirty-seven when the planters and demagogues have run out of answers; faces the crash and the coupons when the history ends. |

# Explicitly unresolved canonical decisions

1. **The Missouri and Taos upbringing, narrowed by CR-066:** Elias's mother's name and the Colonel's name remain open; the Bible's rescue in 1837 (a creditors' sale or the fire) awaits Missouri's exemption law. Deliberately unresolved: the Colonel's actual Napoleonic service and the credibility of his claimed rank; what he did in January 1847; who set the fire of 1837.
2. **Resolved by CR-022 and CR-066:** the pastor's daughter is Abigail Merritt; the source of the marriage story is her own papers; the pastor preaches at Independence, Missouri, and his denomination is proposed as Methodist.
3. **Texas:** Elias's position during the Council House Fight and his and Caleb's exact experiences on the 1841–42 Santa Fe expedition and subsequent captivity.
4. **The 1846 founding:** the final Santa Barbara convention date, leadership of the Californio coalition, reconciliation of Pico and Castro, and substantive obligations to Indigenous polities (narrowed by CR-033: treaties of 1851–52 and sole-buyer purchase; terms OPEN).
5. **The naval confrontation, narrowed by CR-028:** Cahuenga, the 1847 suspension, the Querétaro recognition, Trist's California article, the Buchanan–Vallejo Convention and the 1848 recognitions are proposed. Still open: the Olompali casualties (including whether Caleb is wounded), Sloat's exact referral, the commission's survey, and how the debt is serviced before the first royalties.
6. **Gold and public finance:** precise discovery location, named Sonoran and Miwok/Yokuts collaborators, mining claims, private land titles, Indigenous rights, public royalty receipts and the state's credit system (narrowed by CR-033: register title, old-grant confirmation and the Land Fund; by CR-034: the State Assay Office and the royalty share for the school, whose amount stays OPEN).
7. **California's legal consolidation:** transitional courts, common-law reception and retained Mexican rules, Indigenous claims (narrowed by CR-033: the compact of protection and pre-emption; terms OPEN), incorporation and patent statutes, citizenship/franchise, and the scope of Elias's revenue office under President Vallejo.
8. **Resolved by CR-027:** Victoria Kamāmalu holds the Kuhina Nui from 1855, succeeds Lot in 1872 and leases Puʻuloa in 1874; the February rising replaces the massacre. Still open: when Victoria learns of Kiyoshi, the Five's proposed members beyond Soberanes, Turner and Tesla, and scene detail of the 1874–1876 ordinances.
9. **The lost child:** Kalanimoku’s name is settled; individuality, birth year, age at death, accident circumstances and medical consequences for Victoria remain open.
10. **The other sons:** Silas is the eldest (1851, Abigail Merritt; CR-022). Kiyoshi is the second son (1859, Satsuma mother); his 1886 Kuroda adoption and marriage, Fleet-General identity and 1920 death are settled. Proposed maternal biography, early dates and service retain their labels; his branch house and 1907 countship, the Kāneʻohe anchoring and his renunciation are settled by CR-026, and CR-040 moves the bid and renunciation to 1907.
11. **Resolved by CR-021 F1:** Kaʻanoe (Noe) Ward, c.1860 Lahaina birth, physician, health board, 1886 hānai bargain and contested autumn 1888 death. Guilt remains genuinely unproved, rather than an undecided identity.
12. **Resolved by CR-021, redated by CR-040:** the 1907 Act of Settlement, Julian’s Hawaiian style Kamehameha VI and 1907 Realm accession, the succession rule, and Kahekili / Ikazuchi as future Kamehameha VII, born about 1916 after daughters. Exact birth dates and scene detail remain unspecified.
13. **Julian’s upbringing:** CR-021 settles his four-language education and 1905–1906 Tokyo year. Individual tutors, friendships and scenes remain to develop within that chronology.
14. **Japanese alternate treaties:** the credible diversion or reshaping of Perry's 1853–54 mission, Satsuma and shogunal negotiations, and the precise clauses of the **1858 Treaty of Edo**; the 1860 *Kanrin Maru* visit is a subsequent exchange.
15. **The American wars:** exact Eastern assignments, audited campaigns, casualties and pre-1865 alliance clauses; the approved 1864 electoral defeat, 1865 peace framework, 1867 ratification, Western line, 1870–1871 war and later continental outcomes now constrain those details. McClellan's connective sequence and several treaty mechanics remain proposed. Northwestern membership dates and Native agreements still require instruments.
16. **Victoria’s conclusion:** she is alive, out of power and in Hawaii in 1932. Her backing of the Thalassia Plan and brokerage of the Lands and Waters Charter are settled by CR-020; final scenes and the private treatment of her relationship with Julian remain to be developed.
17. **The Chronicler:** identity, access to sources, unreliable testimony and how later independent character perspectives challenge his account.
18. **Military and finance:** numerical and engineering evidence for the American wars, Japanese war finance, Siberian coalition (railway capacity: CR-030), champions, migration, common institutions and creditor order remains under audit. CR-020 names the replacement financial model and reports its Story and Stress cases. Those MODEL results do not independently validate fleets, resources, labor capacity or bank balance sheets; see [war_base_assumptions.md](war_base_assumptions.md).
19. **Resolved by CR-021:** Confederation organs and three-language acts; life Providentorship, protected enabling provisions and, as amended by CR-027, the legal Puʻuloa lease of 1874 and the illegal ordinances of 1874–1876; Constitution of the Realm, counties, estates, graded autonomy, plural franchise, ordinances, the joined crowns and Concord consent/cure. The ignored judgment of 1876 remains a legal grievance.
20. **Logan's Kentucky beginnings:** exact birth year and holler, family, early practical work, adolescent migration and Carquinez apprenticeship.
21. **Resolved by CR-021 C:** English–Spanish California, three-language confederal publication, English Realm administration, entrenched historic-land languages, granted county languages and bilingual Concord institutions. Scene-level founding negotiations remain to be written.

22. **Resolved by CR-021 G, as amended by CR-029:** the Formosan Revenue Convention, Chinese commissions, revenue order, joint administration and technical schools; the Philippine purchase, its status before 1907 and the Philippine crown of 1907 (dated 1914 before CR-040); the Pacific islands. All OPEN-029 entries are decided; the rite of the Manila accession follows B7.
23. **Northern Pacific sequence, resolved by CR-031:** Russia retains the northern coast until 1921. The Okhotsk coast west of Ola Bay, Nikolayevsk, Chukotka and the Arctic islands east of the Kara Sea then pass to Siberia, a federal land of Russia, with Okhotsk to the Sakha land; Kamchatka and the Koryak coast become a republic under Japanese protection. Japan retains its southern gains and the Transbaikalian protectorate and holds the majority of the Siberian coast's fishing lots by convention; Siberian ships pass the straits. The Commander Islands and the Arctic leases go to Pacifica. Detailed survey mapping is an implementation task; a fortification freeze is **[OPEN-031-11]**.
24. **CR-016 implementation:** OPEN-016-04 is resolved by Noe’s approved biography and contested evidence; the uncertainty is culpability. The regency, adoption, life offices and constitutional designation are fixed by CR-021. Silas's family and date, Donner responsibility and the 1888 restriction are decided by CR-022; the Vance arc retains its qualifications; Soberanes’s unspecified early biography and service remain qualified.

25. **CR-018 details:** OPEN-018-18 still covers the revised Vance arc; CR-039 settles the 1905 signing place at Urajio. CR-021 resolves the Concord, legal instruments and crown mechanics; unrelated war strengths and operational details remain qualified. Hirohito is not told the Altai secret.

26. **CR-019 implementation:** OPEN-019-03 is decided by the presidential sequence. OPEN-019-09 is superseded by CR-020's Lands and Waters Charter. CR-020 settles Hoover's Pacifican identity, flood relief and moratorium, while exact relief-to-treasury offices under OPEN-019-04 remain qualified. OPEN-019-01–02, 05–08 and 10–13 remain: peace venue/date and broker, British payment in kind, Dominions treaty, London raid, German crown, archive handling, Pensacola lease, Roosevelt's office and the privacy of Victoria's 1917–1918 objection. Unrelated proposed mechanics and numerical feasibility retain their qualifications.

27. **CR-020 decision record:** OPEN-020-01–15 and 17–23 are decided; OPEN-020-16 is superseded by the revised skilled-entry ceiling. No OPEN-020 decision remains outstanding. Section [E of CR-020](CR-020.md#e-decisions-19-october-and-the-questions-still-open) records the choices. Names and scene details not supplied by those decisions, including the 1911 engineer and firm, remain unspecified without reopening the approved outcomes.

28. **CR-021 decisions:** OPEN-021-01–17 are approved. Revision 9 explicitly confirms uncapped tongue votes and Chichibu as the resident prince, with Pacifican education, honors and an outdoorsman reputation. It resolves the nationality convention, Hayama/Thalassia signing procedure, Dawes/Young sequence, northern coastline and dynastic wording. Noe’s death remains ambiguous by express author decision; unprovided decorations and scene details remain to develop.

29. **CR-049, narrowed by CR-050:** CR-050 settles the secrecy (the sealed envelope, the walled college, the term, the opening in 1927), when Japan is told (Nishina in 1922), the Tokyo station's dates and Hillebrand's successor (Langmuir). CR-051 gives the material made by 1932 (MODEL: enough for some fifteen devices in all) and Europe's and the Union's response to 1932. Still open from CR-049: how many devices are assembled after 1927; how the drift between Julian and Victoria plays out; names of the two new elements (the barium-borne one is the element the world prizes); the expedition episodes and their dangers; the Tokyo station's financing; and how Pacifica mines and buys more uranium than its four sources (the next change request).

30. **CR-050 and CR-050-R1:** thorium (a later CR); the Me-Wuk and Kootzaduka'a leaders' names (archival research); the PROPOSED details kept with local labels (Nagaoka's ring model and the proof test he leads, atomic number in the late 1880s, Vienna's institute in the 1890s, the knock-ons to Europe's theory and institutions after 1907, the dated steps of the health programme, the *kapu* register, Hirohito as the only one told in Tokyo, the Tokyo station's size).

31. **CR-051:** the founding date of Pacifica's Birmingham works, the route by which Sibert reaches the Polytechnic (by the late 1870s); whether the Senate's budget committee is told anything; the leaders of the Columbia bands in 1919–1920 and the compacts for the Works' dams on the Pit and the Feather with the Pit River peoples and the Maidu; the dependence strategy after 1927 (a separate CR); whether Victoria lives to object to the second part's term; whether a figure as famous as Hoover suits the ore office. The production schedule, the valley's cap, the halls' power and the lead after 1927 are MODEL.

32. **CR-052:** the Boulder reach's consent and terms; the Development Fund's share of power investment and the royalty's size; the halls' draw after 1927; the industrial-output derivation that would let the energy workbook read the investment share; a world oil-price response to Pacifica's early output (until then oil revenue before 1920 is an upper bound). Ezra Scattergood (the hydro board's model) and Edith Clarke (Ryan's successor in 1934) are PROPOSED for later use. Figures are MODEL.

33. **CR-053:** whether the Gulf Republic's trust fronts the Lago purchase; the split of the American group's share in Mesopotamia; the entitlement form (crude at cost against equity, or a purchase contract).

34. **CR-054:** the alumina refinery's site; the licensed aircraft line in Japan; what Japan withholds in return; whether the division of labour is an annex to the Concord's economic articles or an understanding recorded at Hayama (the first Council of the Two Crowns meets in 1928; CR-054 dated the annex 1927).

35. **CR-055:** the peoples' leaders and councils for the five reaches; the diffusion plant's proportions between the arsenal and the stations' fuel; whether Kroll is recruited for the reactor metals; the dams after 1932, Clarke's succession in 1934 and the *Tribune*'s question of 1935, all beyond the book's horizon.

36. **CR-058:** the name, place and signatories of the river grant of 1871; the western-waters treaty's year and its commission's name; Great Bear's year, company, volumes and route; the Columbia compact's year and whether its half is paid in power or gold, and its effect on the Columbia's firm power; the year of the first fur-seal convention; Huasteca's volumes, the Crown's share and its place in the financial workbook; the Union's fuel modelled in the financial workbook; the Canadian brigade's duties and its 1919 strength; the expedition's field strength (MODEL); Guffey's Venezuelan company; BC's anti-Asian laws against Pacifica's Japanese and Chinese citizens. Doheny's place among the Five is settled by CR-058-R1 (Turner's seat, about 1907–1924); his successor in 1924 is open. The Irish lever of the later 1920s is decided and belongs to the confrontation with Britain (a later CR).

The remaining questions are intentionally open. The history in the numbered chapters above can be read continuously without consulting revision instructions.
